# Pathway capabilities: the 9, the 18 and benchmark keys by tier

Generated by `tools/render-pathway-benchmarks-md.py`; do not hand-edit. Re-run after `tools/key-benchmark-tiers.py` or after editing `catalogue/context/pathway-evidence/`.

- **The 9:** each pathway's capabilities, 3 per tier (`catalogue/context/pathway/<pathway>.json`).
- **The 18:** two evidence examples per capability, one **conceptual** (theory, concept or model) and one **applied** (practice, real world): 6 per tier (`catalogue/context/pathway-evidence/<pathway>.json`). Draft for review.
- **Attribution:** whether a benchmark lands on the capability. **direct** = a direct benchmark matched it; **indirect only**; **UNATTRIBUTED** = no benchmark matched it (every-tier keys do not count).
- **Benchmark keys:** universal graduate competencies and subject statements (Psychology, the one authored subject map, keyed as a draft) that key into the pathway at each tier.

Tier basis: **match** = matched a capability at that tier; **every tier** = progression (direct, no match) or floor (indirect, no match: weakest, review these).

## Starting, Progressing and Succeeding

### Introductory

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Explain what excelling at university means | L3 | l3/knowledge.3 "Be aware of the nature of the area of study or work." | **UNATTRIBUTED** | `starting-progressing-succeeding#i1` |
| 2 | Recognise and follow the conventions of academic practice | L4 | l4/autonomy.2 "within broad but generally well-defined parameters" | **UNATTRIBUTED** | `starting-progressing-succeeding/c1` |
| 3 | Adopt a growth mindset towards academic challenge | L5 | 4.12.b7 "develop existing skills and acquire new competences" | **UNATTRIBUTED** | `starting-progressing-succeeding/01` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

1. **Explain what excelling at university means**
    - *Benchmark:* none matched at this tier (**UNATTRIBUTED**)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Excelling characterised through levels of understanding.* **Learning outcome:** The student can define and explain excelling as a staged progression from meeting minimum requirements to demonstrating mastery, in their own words.
          You set out what excelling in your studies actually involves, using a staged model of understanding that moves from describing isolated facts to relating ideas and finally extending them to new situations. You identify which stage your own recent work has reached and what moving up a stage would look like. This produces a clear, reasoned account of what excelling means, rather than a vague sense of trying harder. **Evidence:** A short written account of excelling, naming your current stage of understanding and the next one. *(Grounding: model: SOLO taxonomy (Biggs and Collis 1982); benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
        - *Applied: Excelling in a role explained to someone new.* **Learning outcome:** The student can identify and communicate, to someone unfamiliar with a role, the practical difference between adequate performance and excelling, using a concrete example of each.
          You are asked by someone new to a role what doing really well in it looks like, beyond simply meeting the basic requirements. You explain that it means understanding why tasks are done a certain way and connecting them to a bigger purpose, not just completing a checklist. You give one concrete example of each level so the distinction is clear and useful to them. **Evidence:** A short explanation given to a newcomer, with one example of basic performance and one of genuine excelling. *(Grounding: professional: Explains what strong performance in a role looks like to someone new to it.; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Excelling defined through deep and surface learning.* You read a short account of deep and surface approaches to learning and use it to explain what excelling in your studies means: seeking meaning and connecting ideas, not simply collecting marks or memorising facts. You note one moment from your own recent work that fits each approach, then state which one better describes genuine excelling and why. This shows you can define excelling using a recognised distinction rather than a vague impression. **Evidence:** A short written explanation of excelling, using the deep and surface distinction, with one example of each. *(Grounding: model: Deep and surface approaches to learning (Marton and Saljo 1976))*
        - *Applied: Interview with a more experienced learner on excelling.* You interview someone further along in their studies about what doing well looked like for them, then write a short summary comparing their answer with what you expected before you started. This shows you can explain what excelling means in practice, for instance discovering that doing well involved reading beyond what was set and contributing actively, not only achieving good results. **Evidence:** Interview notes and a short summary comparing your expectations with what an experienced learner told you. *(Grounding: professional: Learns what doing well looks like in a role by talking to someone more experienced.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: What excelling means, explained through deep and surface approaches.* You read a short account of deep and surface approaches to learning (Marton and Saljo 1976) and use it to write a paragraph on what excelling at university means: seeking meaning and connecting ideas, not only collecting marks or memorising. You add one example of each approach from your own first weeks. This shows you can explain what excelling involves (for example, a foundation-year design student contrasting copying a tutor's sketch style with understanding why it works). **Evidence:** A short written explanation of excelling at university, using deep and surface approaches, with two examples from your own study. *(Grounding: model: Deep and surface approaches to learning (Marton and Saljo 1976))*
        - *Applied: Interview with an experienced student about doing well.* You interview a second- or third-year student, a student ambassador or your personal tutor about what doing well at university looked like for them, then write a short summary comparing their answer with what you expected from school or college. This shows you can explain what excelling at university means in practice (for example, a criminology student learning that excelling meant reading beyond the list and speaking in seminars, not only grades). **Evidence:** Interview notes and a half-page summary comparing your expectations of university with what an experienced student told you. *(Grounding: practice: conversation with a student ambassador or personal tutor)*
2. **Recognise and follow the conventions of academic practice**
    - *Benchmark:* none matched at this tier (**UNATTRIBUTED**)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Conventions of a field identified before adopting them.* **Learning outcome:** The student can identify the conventions that govern practice in an unfamiliar field and explain why those conventions exist.
          Before producing a piece of work in an unfamiliar field, you identify the conventions it follows, such as how evidence is presented, what tone is used and how sources are acknowledged, rather than assuming your usual habits will transfer. You explain why these conventions exist for that field specifically. This produces a clear account of recognising conventions as learned practice rather than something fixed or universal across every setting. **Evidence:** A short account naming the conventions of an unfamiliar field and explaining why they exist there. *(Grounding: model: Academic literacies (Lea and Street 1998); benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
        - *Applied: New role's conventions followed from the outset.* **Learning outcome:** The student can observe and follow the unwritten conventions of a new setting when making their first contributions to it.
          On joining a new team or group, you work out its unwritten conventions, such as how communication is phrased, how decisions are recorded and what tone is expected, by observing before acting and asking when unsure. You then follow these conventions in your own early contributions rather than relying on habits from elsewhere. This shows you recognising and adopting the practices a community expects from someone new to it. **Evidence:** A short record of the conventions observed in a new setting and how your first contributions followed them. *(Grounding: professional: Learns and follows the conventions of a profession or organisation on joining it.; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Own writing annotated against field conventions.* You annotate a piece of your own writing against the conventions typically expected in your field, such as referencing style, structure and tone, then explain why these are practices specific to that field rather than general skills you learn once and apply everywhere. This shows you recognising and following conventions, noticing, for instance, that one type of document uses set sections and an impersonal style while another allows a more personal voice. **Evidence:** An annotated piece of your writing, marking each convention followed and noting what your field expects. *(Grounding: model: Academic literacies (Lea and Street 1998))*
        - *Applied: Document written to match a group's house style.* You find out the conventions expected in a setting outside your usual studies, such as a club, group or role you take part in, and compare them with the ones you already know. You then follow these new conventions in a real task, such as writing minutes or a message on behalf of the group. This shows you can recognise and follow conventions when joining a new community, not only within familiar settings. **Evidence:** Your finished document alongside the style guide or template, with a note on the conventions you followed. *(Grounding: professional: Learns and follows the conventions of a profession or organisation on joining it.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Own writing annotated as disciplinary practice.* You annotate one piece of your writing against your discipline's conventions (referencing style, structure, tone, use of evidence), then explain, using the academic literacies model (Lea and Street 1998), why these are practices of a particular discipline rather than general study skills learned once. This shows you recognising and following the conventions of academic practice (for example, a chemistry student noticing that lab reports use set sections and an impersonal style, unlike an English essay). **Evidence:** An annotated piece of your writing marking each convention followed, with a short note on what your discipline expects. *(Grounding: model: Academic literacies (Lea and Street 1998))*
        - *Applied: Real document written to an organisation's house style.* You find the conventions expected in a setting outside your course, such as a society, volunteering role or part-time job, and compare them with your discipline's. You then follow them in a real task, like writing minutes or a client email. This shows you can recognise and follow conventions when joining a community (for example, a politics student writing their first press release for the students' union in its house style). **Evidence:** Your finished document beside the house style guide or template, with a note on the conventions you followed. *(Grounding: professional: Learns and follows the conventions of a profession or organisation on joining it.)*
3. **Adopt a growth mindset towards academic challenge**
    - *Benchmark:* none matched at this tier (**UNATTRIBUTED**)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Challenge reframed as opportunity rather than threat.* **Learning outcome:** The student can describe how reframing a difficult task as an opportunity to develop a skill reflects a growth mindset.
          Faced with a task that feels beyond your current ability, you deliberately describe it to yourself as an opportunity to build a new skill rather than a test you might fail. You name the specific skill at stake and one small action that would start building it today. This produces a short, honest record of choosing a growth response to challenge instead of avoiding or dismissing it outright. **Evidence:** A short written record naming a challenge, the skill at stake, and one action taken today. *(Grounding: model: Mindset theory (Dweck 2006); benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
        - *Applied: Difficult task treated as a learning opportunity.* **Learning outcome:** The student can respond to a challenging task by treating it as a chance to learn and acting on feedback received before it is finished.
          Given a task that is harder than anything you have handled before, you choose to treat it as a chance to develop rather than a risk to avoid, and you actively seek feedback while still partway through, not only once it is finished. You note what that feedback let you fix early. This shows you adopting a growth response to challenge in a way that changes the outcome, not just your attitude towards it. **Evidence:** A short note on a hard task, the feedback sought midway, and the early fix it allowed. *(Grounding: professional: Treats new and difficult work as a chance to learn and seeks feedback to improve.; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: A setback reframed in fixed and growth terms.* You take a piece of work that went less well than hoped and write two short accounts of it: one assuming your ability is fixed, and one assuming it grows with effort and better strategy. Comparing the two, you set one concrete, effort-based next step. This shows you adopting a growth mindset towards a real setback rather than only towards challenge in the abstract, turning a difficult result into a plan. **Evidence:** Two contrasting reflections on one setback, fixed and growth framed, ending with a concrete next step. *(Grounding: model: Mindset theory (Dweck 2006))*
        - *Applied: A stretch challenge taken on with feedback sought.* You take on a challenge you would normally avoid because it feels too difficult, and partway through you ask someone for feedback on how it is going. You record what the feedback told you and what you changed as a result. This shows you treating difficult work as a chance to learn and improve, rather than avoiding it or waiting until the end to find out whether your approach was working. **Evidence:** A record of the challenge taken on, the feedback sought partway through, and what you changed afterwards. *(Grounding: professional: Treats new and difficult work as a chance to learn and seeks feedback to improve.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: A setback rewritten in fixed and growth mindset terms.* You take a piece of work that went less well than you hoped and write two short accounts of it, one in fixed-mindset terms and one in growth-mindset terms, drawing on Dweck's (2006) distinction between believing ability is fixed and believing it grows with effort and good strategy. You then set one effort-based next step. This shows you adopting a growth mindset towards academic challenge (for example, a mathematics student reframing a low first-term problem sheet mark). **Evidence:** Two contrasting reflections on one setback, fixed and growth framed, ending with a concrete next step you then took. *(Grounding: model: Mindset theory (Dweck 2006))*
        - *Applied: A stretch challenge taken on with feedback sought midway.* You take on a challenge you would usually avoid, such as a hard optional module, a coding competition or a presentation for your society, and ask for feedback partway through. You record what you did with that feedback. This shows you treating difficult work as a chance to learn (for example, a music student entering a composition competition and asking a tutor for comments on a draft score). **Evidence:** A record of the challenge, the feedback you asked for and received, and what you changed as a result. *(Grounding: professional: Treats new and difficult work as a chance to learn and seeks feedback to improve.; professional: Vitae RDF B1: personal qualities)*

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

4. **Set and sustain mastery-oriented goals**
    - *Benchmark:* none matched at this tier (**UNATTRIBUTED**)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: A mastery goal set and tracked over time.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse the difference between a mastery goal and a performance target, and define what achieving a mastery goal would involve.
          You set yourself one goal defined purely in terms of understanding or capability, rather than a mark or an external target, and you write down in advance what achieving it would actually look like. Over several weeks you log short, dated notes on your progress and what kept you committed when motivation dipped. This produces a clear, dated record of pursuing mastery deliberately rather than drifting towards whatever is simply easiest to measure. **Evidence:** A mastery goal statement with weekly dated progress notes and a comment on sustained motivation. *(Grounding: ing:doc:self-regulated-learning-goal-orientation-pintrich-2000; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
        - *Applied: A mastery goal sustained in a real role.* **Learning outcome:** The student can set a mastery goal that goes beyond required targets and sustain progress towards it over time without external prompting.
          In a role where you are given set targets, you additionally set yourself a mastery goal that goes beyond what is measured, such as deepening a skill rather than hitting a quota, and you keep working at it even when no one is checking. You review your own progress at set intervals and record what sustained your effort. This shows you can set and sustain a mastery goal unprompted, in a live setting with real demands. **Evidence:** A mastery goal set in a real role, with interval reviews and a note on sustained effort. *(Grounding: professional: Sets development goals beyond targets and keeps working towards them.; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Goals rewritten from performance to mastery focus.* You take three goals you are currently working towards and rewrite each so it focuses on mastery, understanding or genuine skill, rather than on performance, such as beating others or simply hitting a target grade. Over the following month you track your progress against the rewritten goals and note what kept you working at them. This shows you setting and sustaining mastery-oriented goals rather than ones driven mainly by comparison or marks. **Evidence:** Original and rewritten goals with each orientation labelled, plus a month of dated progress notes. *(Grounding: ing:doc:self-regulated-learning-goal-orientation-pintrich-2000)*
        - *Applied: Development goal pursued beyond what was set.* In a role outside your formal studies, such as a club, team or part-time position, you set yourself a development goal that goes beyond the targets you have been given, and you work towards it over several weeks, reviewing progress with someone who oversees your work there. This shows you can set and sustain a mastery goal in a real setting, not only when a goal is handed to you. **Evidence:** A goal statement, dated progress notes and a short comment from someone overseeing your work. *(Grounding: professional: Sets development goals beyond targets and keeps working towards them.; professional: Vitae RDF B2: self-management)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Goals rewritten from performance to mastery orientation.* You rewrite three of your current goals so they focus on mastery (understanding or skill) rather than performance (beating others or just reaching a grade), using Pintrich's (2000) mastery and performance goal orientations. You track progress against them for a month and note what kept you going. This shows you setting and sustaining mastery-oriented goals (for example, a physiotherapy student turning 'get 70 in anatomy' into 'explain the muscles of the shoulder without notes'). **Evidence:** Original and rewritten goals with each orientation labelled, plus a month of dated progress notes. *(Grounding: ing:doc:self-regulated-learning-goal-orientation-pintrich-2000)*
        - *Applied: Development goal pursued in a coaching or work role.* In a part-time job, sports club or volunteering role, you set yourself a development goal beyond the targets you are given and work towards it over a term, reviewing it with a supervisor or coach. This shows you can set and sustain a mastery goal outside the classroom (for example, a sport science student coaching a youth team who sets out to learn to plan a full training session, not just run drills). **Evidence:** A goal statement, termly review notes and a short comment from your supervisor or coach on your progress. *(Grounding: professional: Sets development goals beyond targets and keeps working towards them.; professional: Vitae RDF B2: self-management)*
5. **Build self-efficacy and manage stress and anxiety under pressure**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Independent and self-directed learning (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Independent working planned to manage pressure.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain how structuring independent work into manageable stages helps sustain confidence and reduce stress under pressure.
          You take full responsibility for managing a demanding piece of work without relying on someone else to structure it for you, and you deliberately build in strategies that keep your confidence steady, such as breaking the task into stages you know you can complete and tracking small wins along the way. You record how working independently in this planned way changed your stress levels compared with previous, less structured attempts. This demonstrates managing your own learning under pressure. **Evidence:** A short record showing how working independently, staged into small wins, kept confidence steady under pressure. *(Grounding: model: Sources of self-efficacy (Bandura 1977); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/independent-and-self-directed-learning)*
        - *Applied: Confidence sustained while working independently under pressure.* **Learning outcome:** The student can manage their own learning independently under pressure, adjusting their approach when they notice confidence or stress levels changing.
          Given a task to manage on your own, with a genuine deadline and no one checking your progress day to day, you set up your own structure for staying on track and your own way of noticing early if stress is building. You adjust your approach partway through when you notice confidence dipping, rather than waiting for someone else to intervene. This shows you managing your own learning and performance under pressure, independently and effectively. **Evidence:** A short account of adjusting your own approach midway through independent, pressured work to protect confidence. *(Grounding: professional: Keeps confidence and performance under pressure and copes with stress.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/independent-and-self-directed-learning)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Confidence plan built on four sources of self-efficacy.* You plan how to build confidence for a task you find daunting, using four recognised sources of self-efficacy: direct experience of succeeding at it, watching someone similar succeed, encouragement from others, and managing how nervous or calm you feel. You take one action under each source, then evaluate afterwards which made the most difference. This shows you building self-efficacy and managing pressure using a structured plan, not just hoping nerves settle. **Evidence:** A four-source self-efficacy plan with the actions taken and a short evaluation of what helped most. *(Grounding: model: Sources of self-efficacy (Bandura 1977))*
        - *Applied: Stress and coping log kept across a pressured period.* During a genuinely high-pressure period, such as several deadlines landing close together, you keep a brief daily log of your stress levels and the coping strategies you use, such as planning ahead, protecting sleep, talking to someone, or taking short breaks. Afterwards you review which strategies actually kept your confidence and performance steady. This shows you managing stress under real, sustained pressure rather than only describing strategies you intend to use. **Evidence:** A dated stress and coping log across the pressured period, with a short end-of-period review. *(Grounding: professional: Keeps confidence and performance under pressure and copes with stress.; professional: Tomlinson (2017): psychological capital)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Confidence plan built on four sources of self-efficacy.* You plan how to build confidence for an assessment you find daunting, using Bandura's (1977) four sources of self-efficacy: mastery experiences, vicarious experience, verbal persuasion, and physiological and emotional states. You choose one action under each, carry them out and evaluate which made most difference. This shows you building self-efficacy and managing stress under pressure (for example, an education student preparing for a first micro-teaching session by rehearsing short sections, watching peers and calming nerves beforehand). **Evidence:** A four-source self-efficacy plan with the actions you took and a short evaluation of which helped most. *(Grounding: model: Sources of self-efficacy (Bandura 1977))*
        - *Applied: Stress and coping log across a real deadline cluster.* During a high-pressure period, such as a cluster of deadlines or a placement assessment, you keep a brief stress log and use chosen strategies (planning, sleep, talking to someone, short breaks) to stay on track. Afterwards you review what kept your confidence and performance steady. This shows you managing stress under real pressure (for example, a journalism student meeting deadlines on the student newspaper during exam season). **Evidence:** A dated stress and coping log across the pressured period, with a short end-of-period review. *(Grounding: professional: Keeps confidence and performance under pressure and copes with stress.; professional: Tomlinson (2017): psychological capital)*
6. **Develop an academic voice and the confidence to own one's practice**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Voice grounded in command of core content.* **Learning outcome:** The student can distinguish their own interpretation of a topic from paraphrased source material, grounding that interpretation in broad, well-founded subject knowledge.
          You take a topic you know well and write a short piece stating your own interpretation of it, deliberately drawing on your broad, well-founded grasp of the core material to support claims you make in your own words, rather than paraphrasing a single source. You mark the sentences where the view is distinctly yours. This demonstrates a voice that stands on solid knowledge rather than borrowed phrasing or secondhand opinion. **Evidence:** A short piece on a familiar topic with your own sentences marked as distinct from paraphrased source material. *(Grounding: model: Writer identity and self as author (Ivanič 1998); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/subject-knowledge-and-understanding)*
        - *Applied: Expert view voiced with confidence in practice.* **Learning outcome:** The student can voice their own professional assessment of a situation, drawing on well-founded subject knowledge, and defend that assessment when questioned.
          Drawing on your broad, well-founded knowledge of a subject, you give your own assessment of a situation to colleagues or peers, rather than simply relaying facts, and you stand behind that assessment when it is questioned. You note afterwards which parts of your knowledge let you speak with genuine confidence and which parts you still need to strengthen. This shows the confidence to own a professional view grounded in real command of the material. **Evidence:** A short record of an assessment you gave and defended, and the knowledge gaps it revealed. *(Grounding: professional: Speaks and writes with a professional voice and owns their practice within the profession's standards.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/subject-knowledge-and-understanding)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Writing voice compared across early and recent work.* You compare an early piece of your writing with a recent one, focusing on how far each states your own position and stands behind its claims rather than only summarising what others have said. You annotate where your voice is stronger in the later piece and explain the specific choices behind that shift. This shows you developing a distinct voice and the confidence to own your own views within accepted conventions. **Evidence:** Two annotated extracts, early and recent, with a commentary on how your authorial voice developed. *(Grounding: model: Writer identity and self as author (Ivanič 1998))*
        - *Applied: Your own view presented and defended publicly.* You present your own view in a real setting beyond your usual coursework, such as an open talk, a panel or a group meeting, stating a position clearly and backing it with reasons rather than simply reporting what others think. You then handle questions and challenge on the spot. This shows the confidence to own your practice and voice in front of others, under genuine scrutiny rather than in a safely private draft. **Evidence:** Your notes or slides, the questions you were asked, and a short reflection on your responses. *(Grounding: professional: Speaks and writes with a professional voice and owns their practice within the profession's standards.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Early and recent writing compared for authorial voice.* You compare an early and a recent piece of your writing using Ivanič's (1998) idea of writer identity, especially the self as author: how far you state your own position and stand behind your claims. You annotate where your voice is stronger and explain the choices you made within the discipline's conventions. This shows you developing an academic voice and owning your practice (for example, a sociology student moving from summarising sources to arguing a position on them). **Evidence:** Two annotated extracts, early and recent, with a commentary on how your authorial voice has developed. *(Grounding: model: Writer identity and self as author (Ivanič 1998))*
        - *Applied: Own view presented and defended at a student conference.* You present and defend your own view in a real setting beyond the module, such as a departmental seminar, an undergraduate research conference or a course representative meeting, and handle questions on it. This shows the confidence to own your practice in your own voice (for example, an anthropology student presenting fieldwork findings at a student conference and answering challenge from staff). **Evidence:** Your abstract or slides, the questions you were asked and a short reflection on how you responded. *(Grounding: professional: Speaks and writes with a professional voice and owns their practice within the profession's standards.)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Independent and self-directed learning (direct; lands on `starting-progressing-succeeding/02`)
- Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect; lands on `starting-progressing-succeeding/c2`)

### Advanced

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | Articulate one's graduate capital for the move from student to graduate | L5 | 4.12.b8 "the qualities and transferable skills necessary for employment" | **UNATTRIBUTED** | `starting-progressing-succeeding/04` |
| 8 | Direct one's own learning and development, as a lifelong learner | L6 | 4.15.b9.3 "the learning ability needed to undertake appropriate further training" | direct | `starting-progressing-succeeding/c3` |
| 9 | Consolidate a graduate identity | L7 | 4.17.b6 "demonstrate self-direction"; 4.17.b7 "continue to advance their knowledge and understanding" | indirect only | `starting-progressing-succeeding#e3` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

7. **Articulate one's graduate capital for the move from student to graduate**
    - *Benchmark:* none matched at this tier (**UNATTRIBUTED**)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Readiness for transition mapped across personal assets.* **Learning outcome:** The student can synthesise distinct forms of personal advantage, such as knowledge, networks and resilience, into a reasoned account of their readiness for transition.
          You list the distinct kinds of advantage you bring into your next step, such as what you know, who you know, how well you fit a new setting, how you see yourself, and how well you cope with setbacks, and you find one piece of concrete evidence for each. You then rank them from strongest to weakest and explain why. This produces a reasoned map of your own readiness rather than a single, vague impression of confidence. **Evidence:** A ranked list of five kinds of personal advantage, each backed by one piece of evidence. *(Grounding: model: Graduate capital model (Tomlinson 2017); benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
        - *Applied: Personal advantages presented to a decision-maker.* **Learning outcome:** The student can justify their readiness for a genuine transition by presenting specific personal advantages, each supported by evidence, to a decision-maker.
          In a real conversation where someone is deciding whether to take you on, such as for a role, a place or a position of responsibility, you lay out the specific advantages you bring, backed by one concrete example for each, rather than listing generic strengths. You ask directly which of these landed most convincingly and which needs stronger evidence. This shows you can articulate your readiness for a genuine transition under real scrutiny. **Evidence:** A short record of advantages presented in a real decision conversation and which landed most convincingly. *(Grounding: professional: Presents own knowledge, networks, cultural fit, professional identity and resilience to an employer, with evidence.; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Personal capital audited across five recognised forms.* You audit yourself against a recognised model of the capital that supports a move into a career, covering knowledge, networks, cultural fit, identity and resilience, finding evidence for each from your studies, work and activities, and naming the weakest form. You then write a short plan to strengthen it. This shows you can articulate your own capital for the transition ahead, rather than relying on a vague sense of being ready or not. **Evidence:** A five-part capital audit with evidence under each form and a plan for the weakest one. *(Grounding: model: Graduate capital model (Tomlinson 2017))*
        - *Applied: Capital evidenced in a real application.* You use your capital audit when putting together a genuine application or going into a mock interview, giving evidence-backed answers about your knowledge, networks, fit, identity and resilience rather than generic statements, then you ask for feedback on how convincing those answers were. You revise at least one answer in light of that feedback. This shows you presenting your own capital to someone making a real judgement about your readiness. **Evidence:** A submitted application or recorded mock interview, with the feedback received and your revisions. *(Grounding: professional: Presents own knowledge, networks, cultural fit, professional identity and resilience to an employer, with evidence.; professional: Tomlinson (2017): graduate capital)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Graduate capital audit across Tomlinson's five forms.* You audit yourself against Tomlinson's (2017) graduate capital model, covering human, social, cultural, identity and psychological capital, finding evidence for each from your degree, work and activities and naming the weakest form. You then write a short plan to strengthen it. This shows you can articulate your graduate capital for the move from student to graduate (for example, an engineering student finding strong technical knowledge but few industry contacts, so weak social capital). **Evidence:** A five-part graduate capital audit with evidence under each form and a plan for the weakest one. *(Grounding: model: Graduate capital model (Tomlinson 2017))*
        - *Applied: Graduate capital evidenced in a real application or mock interview.* You use your capital audit in a real application or a mock interview with the careers service, giving evidence-backed answers on your knowledge, networks, fit, identity and resilience, then ask for feedback. This shows you can present your graduate capital to an employer (for example, a history student applying for a heritage trainee scheme and using museum volunteering as evidence). **Evidence:** A submitted application or recorded mock interview, with the careers adviser's feedback and your revisions. *(Grounding: professional: Presents own knowledge, networks, cultural fit, professional identity and resilience to an employer, with evidence.; professional: Tomlinson (2017): graduate capital)*
8. **Direct one's own learning and development, as a lifelong learner**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Independent and self-directed learning (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Own learning managed without external direction.* **Learning outcome:** The student can construct a self-directed plan for a substantial piece of learning, justifying the sources, sequence and standard of knowledge chosen without external direction.
          You take full ownership of a substantial piece of learning that nobody else has structured for you: you decide what to learn, in what order, from which sources, and how you will know when you have learned it well enough. You carry this through to completion and keep a short log of the decisions you made along the way and why. This demonstrates managing your own learning and working independently, with no one setting the steps for you. **Evidence:** A decision log for a self-structured piece of learning, from start to completed, independent outcome. *(Grounding: model: Andragogy (Knowles 1980); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/independent-and-self-directed-learning)*
        - *Applied: Independent work carried through unprompted and unsupervised.* **Learning outcome:** The student can independently plan, carry out and judge the standard of a substantial piece of work, directing their own learning without external oversight.
          At work, you take on a substantial task where no one tells you how to proceed, and you independently plan the steps, find what you need to complete it, and judge for yourself when it is done to a good standard. You keep a short account of the decisions you made without waiting to be told. This shows you managing your own learning and work independently, under real conditions with no one checking each step. **Evidence:** A short account of an independently planned and completed work task, with no step checked beforehand. *(Grounding: professional: Directs own practice, learning and development independently throughout a career.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/independent-and-self-directed-learning)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Development plan grounded in adult-learning theory.* You write a plan for the period right after you finish your current programme of study, drawing on a recognised view of adult learners as self-directing, to justify how you will identify your own needs, choose your own resources and judge your own progress without a formal course setting these for you. This shows you directing your own learning and development as a matter of deliberate design, not simply drifting once formal structure disappears. **Evidence:** A development plan stating your needs, chosen resources, milestones and how you will review progress. *(Grounding: model: Andragogy (Knowles 1980))*
        - *Applied: A learning gap closed through self-directed study.* You identify something your formal studies did not cover but that the path you want to follow genuinely needs, then you plan and complete that learning yourself, such as a short course, a module from a professional body, or a self-set project, and you keep a record of it as ongoing development. This shows you directing your own learning independently, closing a real gap rather than waiting for someone else to set the task. **Evidence:** A development record entry: the gap identified, the learning completed, and how you used it. *(Grounding: professional: Directs own practice, learning and development independently throughout a career.; professional: Vitae RDF B3: professional and career development)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Post-degree development plan grounded in andragogy.* You write a development plan for the year after your degree, using Knowles's (1980) andragogy, with its view of the adult learner as self-directing, to justify how you will identify your own needs, choose resources and judge your progress without a module to set them. This shows you directing your own learning and development (for example, a computing student planning how to learn a new programming framework through online courses and contributions to an open-source project). **Evidence:** A post-degree development plan stating needs, chosen resources, milestones and how you will review your own progress. *(Grounding: model: Andragogy (Knowles 1980))*
        - *Applied: Self-directed learning to close a career-relevant gap.* You identify a gap that your course does not cover but your intended career needs, then plan and complete the learning yourself, such as a short online course, a professional body module or a side project, and record it as continuing development. This shows you directing your learning independently (for example, a psychology student completing an introductory course in data analysis software before applying for research assistant roles). **Evidence:** A continuing development record entry: the gap, the learning completed, a certificate or output, and how you used it. *(Grounding: professional: Directs own practice, learning and development independently throughout a career.; professional: Vitae RDF B3: professional and career development)*
9. **Consolidate a graduate identity**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Identity grounded in command of core knowledge.* **Learning outcome:** The student can critique their own development by linking specific, well-founded subject knowledge to an emerging sense of themselves as a graduate.
          You write a short account of who you are becoming, as shown through the breadth and depth of what you now know in your field, picking out the core knowledge you can draw on confidently and without notes. You connect specific pieces of that knowledge to how you now see yourself and what you want to do with it. This demonstrates an identity consolidated through genuine command of subject matter, not borrowed labels or titles. **Evidence:** A short written account linking specific core knowledge to how you now see yourself as a graduate. *(Grounding: model: Self-authorship (Baxter Magolda 2001); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/subject-knowledge-and-understanding)*
        - *Applied: Graduate identity shown through command of knowledge.* **Learning outcome:** The student can present themselves to others as a graduate by drawing fluently on well-founded subject knowledge rather than listing qualifications alone.
          In a conversation with someone outside your studies, such as at an event or a first meeting in a new role, you describe yourself as a graduate by drawing directly on the broad, well-founded knowledge you now hold, rather than listing qualifications alone. You notice which parts of that knowledge you can speak about with real fluency and confidence. This shows a graduate identity grounded in substance, recognisable to someone meeting you for the first time. **Evidence:** A short reflection on describing yourself as a graduate through knowledge rather than qualifications alone. *(Grounding: professional: Presents a coherent account of oneself as a graduate that others in the field can recognise and test.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/subject-knowledge-and-understanding)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Reflective account tracing a path to self-authorship.* You write a reflective account tracing your own journey towards defining what you believe, who you are and what you are committed to, rather than continuing to rely on other people's formulas for what to think and do. You use specific, dated moments from your studies as evidence and state what kind of graduate you have become as a result. This shows you consolidating a graduate identity through deliberate, evidenced reflection rather than vague self-description. **Evidence:** A reflective account of about a thousand words on your development, citing dated moments from your studies. *(Grounding: model: Self-authorship (Baxter Magolda 2001))*
        - *Applied: Professional profile tested with someone established.* You write a short profile or personal statement for a genuine next step, such as applying for a role or a further place of study, presenting a coherent account of who you are as a graduate, and you test it with someone already established in that field. This shows a consolidated graduate identity that other people can recognise and respond to, rather than one that only makes sense to you, and you revise it based on their comments. **Evidence:** Your profile or statement, the comments you received and your revised version. *(Grounding: professional: Presents a coherent account of oneself as a graduate that others in the field can recognise and test.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Reflective essay tracing your path towards self-authorship.* You write a reflective essay tracing your journey towards self-authorship (Baxter Magolda 2001), from relying on others' formulas for what to think and do towards defining your own beliefs, identity and commitments. You use specific moments from your studies as evidence and say what kind of graduate you have become. This shows you consolidating a graduate identity (for example, a master's student in social policy describing how a dissertation on housing changed the work they want to do). **Evidence:** A reflective essay of about 1,500 words on your development towards self-authorship, citing dated moments from your studies. *(Grounding: model: Self-authorship (Baxter Magolda 2001))*
        - *Applied: Professional profile tested with a practitioner in the field.* You write a professional profile or personal statement for a real next step, such as a job, a doctoral application or professional registration, presenting a coherent account of who you are as a graduate, and test it with someone working in that field. This shows a consolidated graduate identity that others can recognise (for example, an architecture master's student revising their portfolio statement after a practice director's comments). **Evidence:** Your profile or statement, the practitioner's comments and your revised version. *(Grounding: practice: application for employment, further study or professional registration)*

- Universal: Independent and self-directed learning (direct; lands on `starting-progressing-succeeding/c3`)
- Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect; lands on `starting-progressing-succeeding/c3`, `starting-progressing-succeeding#e3`)

<a id="understanding-learning-assessment-feedback"></a>
## Understanding Learning, Assessment and Feedback

### Introductory

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Understand how learning works in a discipline's discourse community | L3 | l3/knowledge.3 "Be aware of the nature of the area of study or work." | direct | `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/01` |
| 2 | Tell formative from summative assessment and engage with varied forms | L4 | 4.10.b5 "within a structured and managed environment" | **UNATTRIBUTED** | `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/04` |
| 3 | Tell active, constructivist learning from passive reception | L5 | 4.12.b4 "an understanding of the limits of their knowledge" | direct | `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/02` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

1. **Understand how learning works in a discipline's discourse community**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Core knowledge traced to a community's shared practice.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain how a discipline's core knowledge is established and legitimised through participation in its community of practice, rather than through isolated facts.
          You take a core topic in a field and trace how its accepted knowledge came to be accepted: who established it, what evidence convinced the community, and how newcomers are taught to use it. Using the idea of legitimate peripheral participation, you show that broad, well-founded knowledge of the subject's content grows through taking part in the community that produces and tests it, not from isolated facts alone. **Evidence:** A short trace of one core topic showing how community practice established and tested the knowledge. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/subject-knowledge-and-understanding)*
        - *Applied: Core knowledge checked against a community of practitioners.* **Learning outcome:** The student can check their understanding of a core topic against how practitioners in the discipline actually apply and test it.
          You take a core idea you hold in a field and check it against how practitioners in that field actually use and test it, by asking a knowledgeable contact, reading a professional source or observing real practice, to see whether your understanding matches theirs. You note where your grasp was accurate and where it needed adjusting. This demonstrates broad and well-founded knowledge built through engagement with the field's working community. **Evidence:** A short note comparing your understanding of a core idea with how practitioners in the field use it. *(Grounding: professional: Learns how a profession or organisation works, its norms, people and ways of knowing, on joining it.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/subject-knowledge-and-understanding)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Map of a field as a community of practice.* You map a field of study as a community using the idea of legitimate peripheral participation: newcomers learn by taking part in real but limited ways and move towards full participation. You mark where you stand now as a newcomer and the activities, such as discussion, reading and writing, that move you closer to full participation. This demonstrates understanding of how learning works inside a field's community of practice. **Evidence:** A labelled diagram of a field's community showing your position and the activities that move you towards fuller participation. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991)*
        - *Applied: Notes from a live gathering on how a field argues.* You attend a real gathering of practitioners in a field, such as a seminar, a public talk or a professional meeting, and note how people there talk, argue and use evidence. You then write a short account of what this taught you about how the field builds and tests its knowledge. This demonstrates understanding a professional community from the inside. **Evidence:** Event notes and a short account of the community's ways of talking, arguing and using evidence. *(Grounding: professional: Learns how a profession or organisation works, its norms, people and ways of knowing, on joining it.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Map of your discipline as a community of practice.* You map your discipline as a community using Lave and Wenger's (1991) idea of legitimate peripheral participation: newcomers learn by taking part in real but limited ways and move towards full participation. You mark where you are now as a newcomer and the activities, such as seminars, reading and writing, that move you inward. This shows you understand how learning works in your discipline's discourse community (for example, a first-year archaeology student placing themselves relative to lecturers who excavate and publish). **Evidence:** A labelled diagram of your discipline's community showing your position and the activities that move you towards fuller participation. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991)*
        - *Applied: Notes from a research seminar on how the discipline argues.* You attend a real event in your discipline's community, such as a departmental research seminar, a guest lecture or a learned society meeting, and note how people talk, argue and use evidence there. You then write a short account of what this taught you about how the discipline learns. This shows you understanding a discourse community from the inside (for example, a physics student noting how a speaker's results were questioned at a colloquium). **Evidence:** Event notes and a short account of the community's ways of talking, arguing and using evidence. *(Grounding: professional: Learns how a profession or organisation works, its norms, people and ways of knowing, on joining it.)*
2. **Tell formative from summative assessment and engage with varied forms**
    - *Benchmark:* none matched at this tier (**UNATTRIBUTED**)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Formative and summative purposes distinguished by function.* **Learning outcome:** The student can distinguish formative from summative assessment by purpose, timing and the role each plays in improving or recording performance.
          You take two pieces of assessment, one intended to improve your work in progress and one intended to record a final result, and set out how each one's purpose, timing and use of feedback differs, using constructive alignment to show how the formative piece feeds into the summative one. This demonstrates differentiating formative from summative assessment and engaging with the varied forms each purpose produces. **Evidence:** A short comparison of two assessments setting out their differing purpose, timing and use of feedback. *(Grounding: ing:doc:constructive-alignment-biggs-1996; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
        - *Applied: Varied forms of judgement engaged with on their own terms.* **Learning outcome:** The student can engage appropriately with different forms of assessment, adjusting their approach according to whether a form is meant to improve or to record their work.
          Across a period of work, you take part in several different forms through which your work is judged, some formal and recorded, some informal and intended only to help you improve, and you adjust how seriously you treat each one to match its actual purpose. You record which form helped you improve most and why. This demonstrates differentiating formative from summative judgement and engaging with varied assessment forms. **Evidence:** A short record of different forms of judgement encountered, each labelled by purpose, with the most useful one identified. *(Grounding: professional: Uses practice runs, drafts and reviews as chances to improve before work is judged.; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Assessments sorted and aligned to outcomes.* You list the assessments in a unit of study, label each one formative or summative, and use constructive alignment to show which learning outcome each is designed to check. You then note how you will use the formative tasks to prepare for the summative ones. This demonstrates telling the two apart and engaging with varied assessment forms. **Evidence:** An assessment table labelled formative or summative, each linked to an outcome, with your plan for using formative tasks. *(Grounding: ing:doc:constructive-alignment-biggs-1996)*
        - *Applied: Optional practice run used to improve a judged piece.* You take part in a practice task you could skip, such as a draft submission, a trial run or a review by someone else, and use the feedback to change the final piece before it is judged. This demonstrates engaging with varied assessment forms and treating rehearsal as a chance to improve before the result counts. **Evidence:** Your practice draft with its feedback, beside the final piece showing the changes you made. *(Grounding: professional: Uses practice runs, drafts and reviews as chances to improve before work is judged.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Module assessments sorted and aligned to outcomes.* You list the assessments in one module, label each formative or summative, and use Biggs' (1996) constructive alignment to show which learning outcome each is designed to check. You then note how you will use the formative tasks to prepare for the summative ones. This shows you can tell the two apart and engage with varied forms (for example, a first-year marketing student seeing that a practice pitch prepares for the assessed group presentation). **Evidence:** A module assessment table labelled formative or summative, each linked to an outcome, with your plan for using the formative tasks. *(Grounding: ing:doc:constructive-alignment-biggs-1996)*
        - *Applied: Optional formative draft used to improve a marked piece.* You take part in a formative task you could skip, such as a draft submission, a practice quiz or a peer review, and use the feedback to change the final piece before it is marked. This shows you engaging with varied assessment forms and treating rehearsal as a chance to improve (for example, a film studies student submitting an essay plan for comment and restructuring the argument as a result). **Evidence:** Your formative draft with its feedback, beside the summative submission showing the changes you made. *(Grounding: professional: Uses practice runs, drafts and reviews as chances to improve before work is judged.)*
3. **Tell active, constructivist learning from passive reception**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Core content tested through a deep approach to study.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain how a deep, questioning approach to study produces more well-founded subject knowledge than passive, surface-level reading.
          You pick one piece of core subject content and study it twice, once by simply reading it and once by questioning it, relating it to what you already know and testing whether you can explain it unprompted. Drawing on the distinction between deep and surface approaches to learning, you show that broad, well-founded knowledge comes from the deep approach, and you set out the evidence of understanding each one produced. **Evidence:** A short comparison of what you could explain after a surface reading versus a deep, questioning study. *(Grounding: ing:doc:approaches-to-learning-marton-saljo-1976; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/subject-knowledge-and-understanding)*
        - *Applied: Well-founded knowledge built by active questioning on the job.* **Learning outcome:** The student can build well-founded knowledge by actively questioning procedures and explanations rather than accepting them at face value.
          Rather than accepting a procedure or explanation at face value, you actively question it, asking why it works, testing it against a case you know, and checking your revised understanding with someone experienced. You record what changed in your grasp of the underlying knowledge as a result. This demonstrates broad and well-founded knowledge built through an active rather than a passive approach to learning. **Evidence:** A short record of a procedure questioned, tested and checked, with what your understanding changed to. *(Grounding: professional: Takes an active part in own learning at work rather than waiting to be trained.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/subject-knowledge-and-understanding)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Two study sessions compared as deep and surface approaches.* You record two study sessions on the same material, one spent rereading and highlighting and one spent questioning, summarising in your own words and linking to earlier topics. Using the distinction between deep and surface approaches to learning, you explain which session was active and constructivist, and what you could explain afterwards that you could not before. This demonstrates telling active learning from passive reception. **Evidence:** Notes from both sessions side by side, with a commentary classifying each approach and what you could explain afterwards. *(Grounding: ing:doc:approaches-to-learning-marton-saljo-1976)*
        - *Applied: Learning a task actively instead of waiting to be shown.* In a role where you are developing a new skill, you notice a point where you were waiting to be told what to do, and instead learn it actively: asking questions, trying it out and checking your understanding against the result. You write up the contrast between the two approaches. This demonstrates distinguishing active from passive learning in practice. **Evidence:** A short before-and-after account of learning a task actively rather than passively. *(Grounding: professional: Takes an active part in own learning at work rather than waiting to be trained.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Two study sessions compared as deep and surface approaches.* You record two study sessions on the same reading, one spent rereading and highlighting and one spent questioning, summarising in your own words and linking to earlier topics. Using Marton and Saljo's (1976) distinction between deep and surface approaches, you explain which session was active and constructivist and what you could explain afterwards. This shows you can tell active learning from passive reception (for example, a philosophy student comparing their grasp of a Kant extract after each session). **Evidence:** Notes from both sessions side by side, with a short commentary classifying each approach and what you could explain afterwards. *(Grounding: ing:doc:approaches-to-learning-marton-saljo-1976)*
        - *Applied: Learning a work task actively instead of waiting to be shown.* In a job, volunteering or society role, you notice a point where you were waiting to be told what to do and instead learn it actively: asking questions, trying it and checking your understanding. You write up the contrast. This shows you distinguishing active from passive learning in practice (for example, a computing student in a summer IT support job learning a ticketing system by working through real requests rather than reading the manual). **Evidence:** A short before-and-after account of learning a task at work, confirmed by a note from your supervisor. *(Grounding: professional: Takes an active part in own learning at work rather than waiting to be trained.; professional: Vitae RDF B2: self-management)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (direct; lands on `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/01`, `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/02`)

### Intermediate

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 4 | Interpret learning outcomes and how activities align to them | L4 | l4/knowledge.3 "the nature and approximate scope of the area of study or work" | direct | `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/03` |
| 5 | Turn feedback into an action plan through reflection | L5 | l5/application.4 "Evaluate actions, methods and results."; l5/autonomy.1 "planning and developing courses of action" | **UNATTRIBUTED** | `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/06` |
| 6 | Apply self-regulated learning cycles to independent study | L6 | 4.15.b5 "the ability to manage their own learning" | direct | `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/05` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

4. **Interpret learning outcomes and how activities align to them**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.30: the learning outcomes they seek to develop and measure (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.31: Assessment criteria are clearly articulated and related to the module and course learning outcomes (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 4.5: Students will undertake a variety of assessments matched against the learning outcomes for their particular curricula of study (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Assessment criteria traced back to stated outcomes.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse how assessment criteria, activities and stated learning outcomes are aligned, identifying any mismatch between them.
          You take a set of assessment criteria and trace each one back to the specific learning outcome it is meant to measure, using constructive alignment to check that the criteria, the outcome and the activities that prepare for it all point the same way. Where the link is unclear, you note the mismatch. This demonstrates interpreting how assessment criteria are articulated and related to stated learning outcomes. **Evidence:** A short trace linking each assessment criterion to its learning outcome, with any mismatches noted. *(Grounding: ing:doc:constructive-alignment-biggs-1996; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.31)*
        - *Applied: Review criteria matched to agreed goals before work begins.* **Learning outcome:** The student can evaluate, before starting a task, whether the criteria it will be judged against clearly relate to its intended outcomes.
          Before starting a piece of work that will be judged, you check that the criteria it will be judged against are clearly related to the goals you have been set, and raise it if a criterion does not obviously connect to a stated goal. You keep a short note of the check and any clarification you sought. This demonstrates interpreting how judging criteria relate to intended outcomes. **Evidence:** A short note checking judging criteria against stated goals, with any clarification sought and received. *(Grounding: professional: Reads objectives and performance standards and sees how tasks and appraisal relate to them.; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.31)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Alignment grid tracing outcomes to activities and assessment.* You take the intended learning outcomes for a unit of study, pick out the verb in each, and trace how teaching activities and assessment are aligned to them, using the idea of constructive alignment. Where an outcome has no clear matching activity, you note how you will practise it yourself. This demonstrates interpreting outcomes and how activities align to them. **Evidence:** An alignment grid: each outcome, its verb, the matching activities and assessment, and your own actions for any gaps. *(Grounding: ing:doc:constructive-alignment-biggs-1996)*
        - *Applied: Everyday tasks mapped to stated objectives.* In a role with stated objectives or performance standards, you work out how your everyday tasks contribute to each one, and discuss the mapping with someone who reviews your work. This demonstrates reading objectives and seeing how tasks relate to them, in the same way you read and interpret learning outcomes. **Evidence:** A mapping of your tasks to stated objectives, annotated after discussion with a reviewer. *(Grounding: professional: Reads objectives and performance standards and sees how tasks and appraisal relate to them.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Alignment grid tracing outcomes to activities and assessment.* You take the intended learning outcomes for a module, pick out the verb in each, and trace how lectures, seminars and the assessment are aligned to them, using Biggs' (1996) constructive alignment. Where an outcome has no clear matching activity, you note how you will practise it yourself. This shows you interpreting outcomes and how activities align to them (for example, a civil engineering student linking 'design a beam' to tutorial calculations and the design project). **Evidence:** An alignment grid: each outcome, its verb, the matching activities and assessment, and your own actions for any gaps. *(Grounding: ing:doc:constructive-alignment-biggs-1996)*
        - *Applied: Everyday tasks mapped to role objectives with a supervisor.* In a job, placement or society role, you take the objectives or performance standards you are given, work out how your everyday tasks contribute to each, and discuss the mapping with your supervisor. This shows you reading objectives and seeing how tasks relate to them, as you do with learning outcomes (for example, a hospitality management student mapping hotel reception duties to the guest satisfaction targets in their appraisal). **Evidence:** A mapping of your tasks to your role objectives, annotated after discussion with your supervisor. *(Grounding: professional: Reads objectives and performance standards and sees how tasks and appraisal relate to them.)*
5. **Turn feedback into an action plan through reflection**
    - *Benchmark:* none matched at this tier (**UNATTRIBUTED**)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Reflection used to turn comment into a dated action plan.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse feedback through deliberate reflection to identify why a gap occurred and convert that analysis into a dated action plan.
          You take a piece of feedback and, instead of reacting to it immediately, pause to reflect on the thinking that led to the original decision, why the feedback differs from it, and what a better decision would look like. You then convert that reflection into an action plan with specific, dated steps. This demonstrates turning feedback into an action plan through deliberate reflection rather than quick, unexamined response. **Evidence:** A short reflection on one piece of feedback, with an action plan of specific, dated steps that followed. *(Grounding: ing:doc:reflective-practitioner-schon-1983; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
        - *Applied: Feedback converted into a tracked plan for improvement.* **Learning outcome:** The student can convert feedback received on their work into a tracked plan of dated actions and review progress against it over time.
          After receiving feedback on something you produced, you set aside time to reflect on it properly rather than responding at once, identify what it means for how you work, and write a short plan of dated actions. You check back against the plan after a set period to see what changed. This demonstrates converting feedback into a tracked plan for improvement through structured reflection. **Evidence:** A dated action plan drawn from one piece of feedback, with a later check against what changed. *(Grounding: professional: Acts on appraisal and colleague feedback through a development plan.; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Feedback turned into an action plan by reflection on action.* You take feedback on a completed piece of work and use reflection-on-action to look back at the decisions you made while producing it, asking why you made them and what the feedback suggests instead. You turn this into an action plan with specific, dated changes for the next piece of work. This demonstrates using reflective practice to turn feedback into improvement. **Evidence:** Annotated feedback, a reflection on your decisions and an action plan of specific, dated changes. *(Grounding: ing:doc:reflective-practitioner-schon-1983)*
        - *Applied: Feedback acted on through a short development plan.* You turn feedback from someone reviewing your work into a short development plan, act on it over several weeks and review the outcome with the person who gave the feedback. This demonstrates using feedback to plan improvement rather than filing it away unread. **Evidence:** Feedback notes, your development plan and a follow-up review recorded with the person who gave it. *(Grounding: professional: Acts on appraisal and colleague feedback through a development plan.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Feedback turned into an action plan by reflection-on-action.* You take feedback on a marked assignment and use Schon's (1983) reflection-on-action to look back at the decisions you made while writing it, asking why you made them and what the feedback suggests instead. You turn this into an action plan with specific, dated changes for the next assignment. This shows you using reflective practice to turn feedback into improvement (for example, a social work student reviewing comments on how they applied legislation in a case study). **Evidence:** Annotated feedback, a reflection on your decisions and an action plan of specific, dated changes. *(Grounding: ing:doc:reflective-practitioner-schon-1983)*
        - *Applied: Supervisor feedback acted on through a short development plan.* You turn appraisal or supervisor feedback from a job, placement or volunteering role into a short development plan, act on it over several weeks and review the outcome with the person who gave the feedback. This shows you using feedback to plan improvement beyond the classroom (for example, a retail management student acting on a shift manager's comments about handling customer complaints). **Evidence:** Feedback notes, your development plan and a follow-up review signed or emailed by your supervisor. *(Grounding: professional: Acts on appraisal and colleague feedback through a development plan.; professional: Vitae RDF B3: professional and career development)*
6. **Apply self-regulated learning cycles to independent study**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Independent and self-directed learning (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Independent study managed through a full regulation cycle.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain how setting goals, monitoring progress and evaluating outcomes form a self-regulated cycle that sustains independent study.
          You take a period of independent study and show how you manage it without direction from anyone else, by running it through a full cycle: setting your own goals and strategies, monitoring your progress as you go, and evaluating what worked once you have finished. You identify one adjustment the cycle produced. This demonstrates managing your own learning and working independently through a self-regulated cycle rather than by chance. **Evidence:** A short account of one independent study cycle, its goals, monitoring and the adjustment it produced. *(Grounding: model: Cyclical model of self-regulated learning (Zimmerman 2002); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/independent-and-self-directed-learning)*
        - *Applied: Self-directed work regulated without outside direction.* **Learning outcome:** The student can manage a piece of independent work by setting their own targets, tracking progress against them and adjusting their approach without external direction.
          You take on a piece of work with no set schedule of check-ins, and manage it yourself, setting your own targets, tracking progress against them at intervals you choose, and changing your plan when progress falls short. You keep a brief log others could use to see how you managed the work independently. This demonstrates managing your own learning and working independently through a deliberate, self-regulated cycle. **Evidence:** A brief self-management log showing targets set, progress tracked and changes made without outside direction. *(Grounding: professional: Plans own work and development, monitors progress against goals and adjusts approach.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/independent-and-self-directed-learning)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
