        - *Conceptual: Two documented cycles of self-regulated independent study.* You plan, monitor and review a block of independent study using a cyclical model of self-regulated learning: forethought, where you set goals and strategies, performance, where you monitor as you work, and self-reflection, where you evaluate results. You complete at least two full cycles and show how the second changed in light of the first. This demonstrates applying self-regulated learning cycles to independent study. **Evidence:** Two completed cycle records, covering forethought, performance and self-reflection, showing what changed between them. *(Grounding: model: Cyclical model of self-regulated learning (Zimmerman 2002))*
        - *Applied: Progress log kept across an independent project.* During a long independent piece of work, you set weekly goals, keep a progress log and adjust your approach at set review points, sharing the log with someone overseeing the work. This demonstrates applying self-regulated cycles to real independent work rather than only to short, closely supervised tasks. **Evidence:** A project progress log with weekly goals, monitoring notes and recorded adjustments, shared with an overseer. *(Grounding: professional: Plans own work and development, monitors progress against goals and adjusts approach.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Two documented cycles of self-regulated independent study.* You plan, monitor and review a block of independent study using Zimmerman's (2002) cyclical model of self-regulated learning: forethought (setting goals and strategies), performance (monitoring as you work) and self-reflection (evaluating results). You complete at least two full cycles and show how the second changed in light of the first. This shows you applying self-regulated learning to independent study (for example, a modern languages student preparing for an oral exam through weekly practice cycles). **Evidence:** Two completed cycle records, covering forethought, performance and self-reflection, showing what changed between the first and second. *(Grounding: model: Cyclical model of self-regulated learning (Zimmerman 2002))*
        - *Applied: Progress log kept across a live client brief.* During a long independent project, such as a dissertation, a self-directed lab project or a live client brief, you set weekly goals, keep a progress log and adjust your approach at set review points, sharing the log with your supervisor. This shows you applying self-regulated cycles to real independent work (for example, a product design student running a twelve-week brief for a local charity). **Evidence:** A project progress log with weekly goals, monitoring notes and recorded adjustments, shared with your supervisor. *(Grounding: professional: Plans own work and development, monitors progress against goals and adjusts approach.; professional: Vitae RDF B2: self-management)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Psychology 3.30: the learning outcomes they seek to develop and measure (direct; lands on `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/03`)
- Psychology 3.31: Assessment criteria are clearly articulated and related to the module and course learning outcomes (direct; lands on `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/03`)
- Psychology 4.5: Students will undertake a variety of assessments matched against the learning outcomes for their particular curricula of study (direct; lands on `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/03`)
- Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (direct; lands on `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/03`, `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/05`)
- Universal: Independent and self-directed learning (direct; lands on `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/05`)
- Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (indirect; lands on `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/05`)

### Advanced

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | Adapt to the demands of different types of assessment | L5 | l5/application.2 "Determine, adapt and use appropriate methods and skills." | direct | `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/c3` |
| 8 | Assess own and peers' work against marking criteria | L6 | l6/application.4 "Evaluate actions, methods and results and their implications." | direct | `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/c1` |
| 9 | Use feedback in dialogue with tutors to sustain development across a degree | L7 | 4.17.b7 "continue to advance their knowledge and understanding" | direct | `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/c2` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

7. **Adapt to the demands of different types of assessment**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.31: Assessment criteria are clearly articulated and related to the module and course learning outcomes (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Criteria compared across assessment types for consistency.* **Learning outcome:** The student can critique how published criteria for different assessment types encode different levels of cognitive demand, despite assessing related learning outcomes.
          You take the published criteria for two different types of assessment and compare how each criterion is articulated and related to the stated learning outcomes, noting where the demands differ even though both are checking related knowledge. Using a revised taxonomy of thinking skills, you identify the different level of thinking each form of assessment requires. This demonstrates adapting to differing demands while recognising the shared outcomes behind them. **Evidence:** A comparison of criteria across two assessment types, showing the differing demands and their shared outcomes. *(Grounding: model: Revised Bloom's taxonomy (Anderson and Krathwohl 2001); benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.31)*
        - *Applied: Preparation adapted once criteria for a form are read.* **Learning outcome:** The student can justify and adapt their approach to preparing for a given form of assessment based on a careful reading of its specific criteria.
          Before producing a piece of work, you read how the judging criteria are articulated for that particular form and check how they relate to the outcome you are meant to be meeting, then adjust your preparation accordingly rather than using your usual method regardless of form. You note one way your approach changed as a result. This demonstrates adapting to the demands of different types of assessment. **Evidence:** A short note on judging criteria read beforehand and the resulting change to your preparation. *(Grounding: professional: Adapts to the forms in which work is judged: reports, pitches, reviews and audits.; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.31)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Three assessment types compared by the thinking they demand.* Across three different types of assessment, such as a timed test, a reflective piece and a group report, you analyse the command verbs and criteria using a revised taxonomy of thinking skills, to identify the level of thinking each one demands, and adapt your preparation and writing to match. This demonstrates adapting to the demands of different types of assessment rather than treating every task the same way. **Evidence:** A comparison sheet for three assessment types: verbs, thinking levels, and how you adapted your approach for each. *(Grounding: model: Revised Bloom's taxonomy (Anderson and Krathwohl 2001))*
        - *Applied: Work produced for a form judged outside usual channels.* You produce work in a form judged by a real audience outside the usual process you are used to, such as a pitch, a brief report or a submission to an external body, and adapt your approach to that form's expectations. You note what differed from your usual way of presenting work. This demonstrates adapting to the forms in which work is judged. **Evidence:** The finished piece for the external form, the response it received, and a note on how you adapted. *(Grounding: professional: Adapts to the forms in which work is judged: reports, pitches, reviews and audits.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Three assessment types compared by the thinking they demand.* Across three assessment types, such as an exam, a reflective portfolio and a group report, you analyse the command verbs and criteria with Bloom's revised taxonomy (Anderson and Krathwohl 2001) to identify the level of thinking each demands, and adapt your preparation and writing to match. This shows you adapting to the demands of different types of assessment (for example, a politics student seeing that a policy brief asks them to evaluate and recommend, while an exam answer asks them to explain). **Evidence:** A comparison sheet for three assessments: verbs, Bloom's levels, and how you adapted your approach for each. *(Grounding: model: Revised Bloom's taxonomy (Anderson and Krathwohl 2001))*
        - *Applied: Briefing written for a real external audience.* You produce work for a real audience in a form judged outside the university, such as a pitch to a business, a report for a community group or a funding bid for a society, and adapt your approach to that form's expectations. You note what differed from academic genres. This shows you adapting to the forms in which work is judged (for example, an environmental science student writing a two-page flood-risk briefing for a parish council). **Evidence:** The finished piece for the external audience, their response, and a short note on how you adapted to the form. *(Grounding: professional: Adapts to the forms in which work is judged: reports, pitches, reviews and audits.)*
8. **Assess own and peers' work against marking criteria**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.31: Assessment criteria are clearly articulated and related to the module and course learning outcomes (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Own work judged against clearly articulated criteria.* **Learning outcome:** The student can justify a mark for a piece of work by evaluating it criterion by criterion against clearly articulated standards.
          You take a finished piece of your own work and a published set of criteria, and judge the work against each criterion in turn, writing a short justification for the mark you would give. You then compare your judgement with an expert mark to see where your reading of the criteria matched or differed. This demonstrates assessing work against criteria that are clearly articulated and related to stated standards. **Evidence:** A self-assessment against a published set of criteria, with justifications and a comparison to an expert mark. *(Grounding: model: Conditions for closing the gap through feedback (Sadler 1989); benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.31)*
        - *Applied: Peer work judged against shared, explicit standards.* **Learning outcome:** The student can critique a peer's work against shared, explicit standards and give criterion-by-criterion feedback rather than a general impression.
          You review a colleague's work against a shared, explicit set of standards, writing comments tied to each one rather than a general impression, and you ask for the same kind of review on your own work in return. You note any criterion where you and your reviewer read the standard differently. This demonstrates assessing work against clearly articulated criteria that are applied consistently by more than one reviewer. **Evidence:** Criterion-by-criterion review comments exchanged with a colleague, with any disagreement in reading the standard noted. *(Grounding: professional: Reviews own and colleagues' work against agreed standards before it is submitted or published.; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.31)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Exemplar and own draft marked using set conditions.* You mark a sample piece of work and then your own draft against published marking criteria, using a published model of the conditions needed to close a performance gap: knowing the standard, comparing your work with it and acting to close the gap. You justify each judgement with evidence from the text and compare your marks with an expert's. This demonstrates assessing work against criteria. **Evidence:** A marked sample and self-assessed draft with criterion-by-criterion justifications, plus a comparison with an expert mark. *(Grounding: model: Conditions for closing the gap through feedback (Sadler 1989))*
        - *Applied: Criterion-based review before work is released.* You act as a reviewer in a structured process, giving criterion-based comments on someone else's work before it is finished, and receiving the same kind of comments on your own. This demonstrates reviewing work against agreed standards, in the way colleagues check each other's work before it is released. **Evidence:** Your written review comments mapped to the criteria, and the responses or changes they led to. *(Grounding: professional: Reviews own and colleagues' work against agreed standards before it is submitted or published.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Exemplar and own draft marked using Sadler's conditions.* You mark an exemplar and then your own draft against the published marking criteria, using Sadler's (1989) conditions for improvement: knowing the standard, comparing your work with it and acting to close the gap. You justify each judgement with evidence from the text and compare your marks with a tutor's. This shows you assessing work against criteria (for example, a biomedical science student marking a lab report exemplar before self-assessing their own report). **Evidence:** A marked exemplar and self-assessed draft with criterion-by-criterion justifications, plus a comparison with the tutor's mark. *(Grounding: model: Conditions for closing the gap through feedback (Sadler 1989))*
        - *Applied: Criterion-based peer review in a team code review.* You act as a reviewer in a real process, such as structured peer review on your module, editing for a society publication or a team code review, giving criterion-based comments on others' work before submission and receiving them on your own. This shows you reviewing work against agreed standards, as colleagues do before work is released (for example, a software engineering student reviewing teammates' pull requests against the team's coding standards). **Evidence:** Your written review comments mapped to the criteria, and the responses or changes they led to. *(Grounding: professional: Reviews own and colleagues' work against agreed standards before it is submitted or published.; professional: Carless and Boud (2018): making judgements)*
9. **Use feedback in dialogue with tutors to sustain development across a degree**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Recurring feedback used to deepen core knowledge over time.* **Learning outcome:** The student can synthesise feedback received over an extended period to diagnose a recurring theme and explain how resolving it deepens their core subject knowledge.
          You trace one theme that keeps recurring in feedback on your work over an extended period, and use the four features of feedback literacy, appreciating, judging, managing affect and acting, to work out why the same issue keeps appearing and what change would finally resolve it. You show how addressing it has deepened your broad, well-founded grasp of the subject's core content. This demonstrates using feedback diagnostically to sustain development. **Evidence:** A trace of one recurring feedback theme, the four-feature analysis applied to it and the resulting change in understanding. *(Grounding: ing:doc:feedback-literacy-carless-boud-2018; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/subject-knowledge-and-understanding)*
        - *Applied: Dialogue with a reviewer used to build core knowledge.* **Learning outcome:** The student can sustain an ongoing dialogue with a reviewer across several rounds of feedback, agreeing actions that build their grasp of core material over time.
          Rather than treating each round of feedback as separate, you deliberately raise it in conversation with the person reviewing your work, asking how it connects to comments from earlier rounds, and agree specific actions before the next piece of work. Over several rounds you track how your grasp of the core material has broadened as a result. This demonstrates sustaining development through active dialogue rather than one-off responses to feedback. **Evidence:** A short record across several feedback rounds showing the dialogue, agreed actions and the resulting growth in understanding. *(Grounding: professional: Seeks feedback actively, discusses it with managers and colleagues and builds on it over time.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/subject-knowledge-and-understanding)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Feedback analysed across a course of study using four features.* You review feedback you have received over an extended period of study and analyse it using four features of feedback literacy: appreciating feedback, making judgements, managing affect and taking action. You identify recurring themes, show how you acted on them, and set out how you will keep using feedback once formal review ends. This demonstrates using feedback diagnostically to sustain development over time. **Evidence:** A cross-period feedback analysis with recurring themes, evidence of action taken and a plan linked to the four features. *(Grounding: ing:doc:feedback-literacy-carless-boud-2018)*
        - *Applied: Feedback meetings prepared for and led over a term.* You arrange and lead feedback conversations with someone who reviews your work over an extended period, bringing your own questions and a summary of what you have changed since the last meeting, and recording what you agree. This demonstrates seeking feedback actively and using dialogue to build on it over time rather than waiting for it to be offered. **Evidence:** Meeting records across a period showing your questions, the actions agreed and the changes made between meetings. *(Grounding: professional: Seeks feedback actively, discusses it with managers and colleagues and builds on it over time.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Cross-module feedback analysis using four features of feedback literacy.* You review the feedback you have received across your degree and analyse it with Carless and Boud's (2018) four features of feedback literacy: appreciating feedback, making judgements, managing affect and taking action. You identify recurring themes, show how you acted on them, and set out how you will keep using feedback beyond the course. This shows you using feedback diagnostically to sustain development (for example, a master's student in education tracing a recurring comment about critical synthesis). **Evidence:** A cross-module feedback analysis with recurring themes, evidence of action taken and a plan linked to the four features. *(Grounding: ing:doc:feedback-literacy-carless-boud-2018)*
        - *Applied: Feedback meetings you prepare for and lead across a term.* You arrange and lead feedback conversations with a tutor, supervisor or manager over a term, bringing your own questions and a summary of what you have changed since the last meeting, and you record what you agree. This shows you seeking feedback actively and using dialogue to build on it over time (for example, a postgraduate translation student meeting a dissertation supervisor fortnightly with a list of queries on drafts). **Evidence:** Meeting records across a term showing your questions, the actions agreed and the changes made between meetings. *(Grounding: professional: Seeks feedback actively, discusses it with managers and colleagues and builds on it over time.; professional: Carless and Boud (2018): appreciating feedback, taking action)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Psychology 3.31: Assessment criteria are clearly articulated and related to the module and course learning outcomes (direct; lands on `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/c3`, `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/c1`)
- Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (direct; lands on `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/c2`)
- Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (indirect; lands on `understanding-learning-assessment-feedback/c1`)

### Keys at every tier

**Progression:**

- Psychology 2.14: students to have an input into the design of the curriculum and assessment (direct)
- Psychology 3.19: opportunities for students to shape and co-create aspects of their learning and assessment (direct)
- Psychology 3.29: students are provided with clear, constructive feedback and feedforward (direct)
- Psychology 3.32: Assessments help students to monitor and reflect on their own development (direct)
- Psychology 1.31: Assessments facilitate attainment and authentic demonstration of the key competencies (direct)
- Psychology 3.28: Assessment and feedback enable students to develop and demonstrate a full range of knowledge, skills and attributes (direct)

**Floor:**

- Universal: Ethics, integrity and values (indirect)
- Universal: Oral or other communication to audiences (indirect)

<a id="finding-using-sources"></a>
## Finding and Using Sources

### Introductory

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Explain how academic searching differs from everyday searching | L3 | l3/application.3 "Use appropriate investigation to inform actions." | **UNATTRIBUTED** | `finding-using-sources#i1` |
| 2 | Use the library discovery service to locate scholarly sources | L4 | l4/application.3 "Initiate and use appropriate investigation to inform actions." | direct | `finding-using-sources/02` |
| 3 | Follow a staged search, starting from the reading list and what one already knows | L5 | 4.12.b4 "an understanding of the limits of their knowledge" | indirect only | `finding-using-sources/c1` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

1. **Explain how academic searching differs from everyday searching**
    - *Benchmark:* none matched at this tier (**UNATTRIBUTED**)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Explanation of how structured searching differs from casual searching.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain how structured, scholarly searching differs from everyday searching in the terms chosen, the sources consulted, and the checks applied before trusting a result.
          You compare a casual search of the kind used to settle an everyday question with a structured search aimed at scholarly sources, and set out in your own words what changes: the terms you choose, the places you look, and the checks you apply before trusting a result. You explain why a structured search takes longer but produces material you can defend, using a recognised model of information literacy to frame the stages involved. **Evidence:** A written explanation, with a short diagram or list, of the stages that separate structured searching from casual searching. *(Grounding: model: SCONUL Seven Pillars of Information Literacy (SCONUL 2011); benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
        - *Applied: Structured search explained to someone unfamiliar with it.* **Learning outcome:** The student can describe, for someone unfamiliar with structured searching, the practical difference between everyday searching and a scholarly search, using one worked example.
          Someone asks why you are taking longer over a search instead of just using a quick web search engine. You explain, in plain terms, that a structured search uses specific terms, trusted sources and a check on currency and authority, and that this is what separates it from an everyday search. You give one concrete example of a term you refined and why the refined version returned better material. **Evidence:** A short written or spoken explanation of structured versus everyday searching, with one worked example of a refined search term. *(Grounding: professional: Explains the difference between a quick everyday search and a structured search to someone who has not done one before.; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Search results compared against a structured information-literacy model.* You run the same question twice, once through a general web search engine and once through a scholarly search tool, then compare the first ten results from each. Using a structured information-literacy model, you note which stages the scholarly search demanded more of you, such as scoping terms carefully and evaluating sources, and explain the difference in a short paragraph. **Evidence:** A side-by-side table of the two result lists, with notes against the model's stages and a short written explanation. *(Grounding: model: SCONUL Seven Pillars of Information Literacy (SCONUL 2011))*
        - *Applied: Everyday finds and citable sources sorted for one task.* You are asked to find information for a short piece of public-facing writing on a topic you do not know well. A quick web search mostly returns adverts, opinion and news, while a scholarly search turns up sources you could cite and check. You sort your finds into the two groups and explain in plain terms why the second kind of search is different, and when each kind is good enough for the job. **Evidence:** A short source list split into everyday and citable finds, with a brief written explanation of the difference and when each is used. *(Grounding: professional: Distinguishes citable, checkable sources from general information before using them in written work.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Web and library search compared against the Seven Pillars.* You run the same question twice, once on a general web search engine and once on the library search, and compare the first ten results. Using the SCONUL Seven Pillars of Information Literacy (SCONUL 2011), you note which pillars the academic search asked more of you, such as Scope and Evaluate, and explain the difference in a short paragraph (for example, an environmental science question on microplastics in rivers returning blogs on the web and peer-reviewed studies in the library). **Evidence:** A side-by-side table of the two result lists, with notes against the pillars and a 150-word explanation of the difference. *(Grounding: model: SCONUL Seven Pillars of Information Literacy (SCONUL 2011))*
        - *Applied: Leaflet sources sorted into everyday and academic finds.* Volunteering at a youth club, you are asked to find information for a leaflet on vaping. You show the leader that a quick web search mostly returns adverts, news and opinion, while NHS guidance and research studies give sources you could cite and check. You explain in plain terms why the second kind of search is different and when each is good enough (for example, a forum post being fine for slang terms but not for health risks). **Evidence:** The draft leaflet with its source list split into everyday and academic finds, plus the youth leader's comment. *(Grounding: practice: youth club volunteering)*
2. **Use the library discovery service to locate scholarly sources**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Information and sources (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Scholarly sources located and selected as appropriate to a question.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain how a dedicated discovery service narrows a broad enquiry to scholarly sources appropriate to a given subject and question.
          Given an unfamiliar question, you use a dedicated search tool rather than a general one, choosing terms and filters that narrow results to material suited to the question's level and subject matter. You explain, stage by stage, how your search moved from a broad starting point towards sources you judged appropriate, drawing on an established model of how people search when they do not yet know exactly what they need. **Evidence:** A written account of the search, its stages and the final list of sources judged appropriate to the question. *(Grounding: ing:doc:information-search-process-kuhlthau-1991; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/information-and-sources)*
        - *Applied: Appropriate sources located quickly using a dedicated search system.* **Learning outcome:** The student can use a library discovery service to locate and select scholarly sources appropriate to a specific question within a working session.
          Asked to find material to support a decision, you go straight to a dedicated search system rather than a general web search, choosing filters and terms that match the kind of source the question needs. You locate several appropriate items within a working session and note briefly why each one fits, so someone checking your work can see the selection was not accidental. **Evidence:** A short list of sources found through a dedicated search system, each with a one-line note on why it fits. *(Grounding: professional: Finds reliable information quickly with an organisation's knowledge systems and subscription services.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/information-and-sources)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Search diary mapped to the six stages of the search process.* You use a scholarly discovery search tool for a piece of work and keep a short diary structured around six stages: initiation, selection, exploration, formulation, collection and presentation. At each stage you record what you searched, what you found and how you felt, including the uncertainty that typically appears during exploration before a narrower focus forms and searching becomes easier. **Evidence:** A one-page search diary with the six stages as headings, screenshots of results and a list of five scholarly sources found. *(Grounding: ing:doc:information-search-process-kuhlthau-1991)*
        - *Applied: Subscription database search carried out using discovery-service skills.* You are asked to find recent, reliable material on a specific question using an organisation's own subscription database rather than the library discovery service you learned on. You apply the same approach: a subject search, filters narrowing results to serious published sources, and a check that you can access the full text, so you can show you locate reliable information quickly with whatever system is in front of you. **Evidence:** A short search record listing the database used, the filters applied and the sources found in one session. *(Grounding: professional: Finds reliable information quickly with an organisation's knowledge systems and subscription services.; framework: SCONUL Seven Pillars: Gather)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Discovery service search diary mapped to Kuhlthau's six stages.* You use the library discovery service for a first assignment and keep a short diary under Kuhlthau's (1991) six stages: initiation, selection, exploration, formulation, collection and presentation. You record what you searched, what you found and how you felt at each stage, including the uncertainty Kuhlthau describes during exploration (for example, a first-year business student searching for sources on the gig economy who feels lost until a narrower focus on delivery riders forms). **Evidence:** A one-page search diary with stage headings, screenshots of discovery service results and a list of five scholarly sources located. *(Grounding: ing:doc:information-search-process-kuhlthau-1991)*
        - *Applied: Placement search on a firm's subscription legal database.* On a work placement at a solicitors' firm, you are asked to find recent commentary on a change to tenancy law. You use the firm's subscription database the way you learned to use the library discovery service: a subject search, filters for practitioner and academic journals, and a check of full-text access. This shows you can locate reliable sources quickly with an organisation's own systems (for example, finding two journal commentaries and the relevant statute in one session). **Evidence:** A short search record listing the database, the filters applied and the sources found, initialled by your placement supervisor. *(Grounding: professional: Finds reliable information quickly with an organisation's knowledge systems and subscription services.; professional: SCONUL Seven Pillars: Gather)*
3. **Follow a staged search, starting from the reading list and what one already knows**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Independent and self-directed learning (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Independent search sequence planned before asking for help.* **Learning outcome:** The student can describe a staged search sequence that begins with existing knowledge and set reading before seeking further help.
          Given a new topic, you set out, before doing anything else, what you already know, which sources you intend to check first and in what order, and only ask for help once that sequence runs out. You keep a short record showing the stages you worked through on your own, which demonstrates that you can manage a piece of your own learning independently rather than waiting to be directed through every step. **Evidence:** A written search sequence with its stages, plus a note on which stage you reached unaided before asking for help. *(Grounding: ing:doc:information-search-process-kuhlthau-1991; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/independent-and-self-directed-learning)*
        - *Applied: Independent search carried out before raising a question with others.* **Learning outcome:** The student can carry out a staged search independently, starting from a reading list and prior knowledge, before raising questions with others.
          Given an open task, you check what is already available to you, follow up the sources that material points to, and only raise a question with someone else once those avenues are exhausted. You keep a brief note of what you checked and in what order, which shows you can work and manage your own search independently rather than relying on direction at every stage. **Evidence:** A brief note listing the sources checked in order and the point at which you raised a question with someone else. *(Grounding: professional: Starts a search from what the organisation and its people already hold before looking further.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/independent-and-self-directed-learning)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Staged search plan built from prior knowledge and a set reading list.* Before searching further, you write down what you already know about a topic and pick the two most useful items from a set reading list. You then follow a staged search: the reading list first, the references those items cite next, and a wider search tool last. You link each stage to a recognised model of the search process, showing how starting from prior knowledge moves you from an uncertain first stage towards a workable focus. **Evidence:** A staged search plan with a what-I-already-know section, reading list items, followed-up references and new sources, labelled by stage. *(Grounding: ing:doc:information-search-process-kuhlthau-1991)*
        - *Applied: Staged search starting from what a team already holds.* Asked to gather ideas on a topic for a piece of work, you start with what your own team or organisation already holds, such as past documents, a colleague's experience and an existing handout, then follow up the sources those materials cite before searching more widely. This shows a staged search beginning from existing knowledge rather than an open search from nothing, and narrows what you still need to find. **Evidence:** A short summary of what was already held, the sources followed up from it, and the gap the new search filled. *(Grounding: professional: Starts a search from what the organisation and its people already hold before looking further.; framework: SCONUL Seven Pillars: Identify, Scope)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Staged search plan starting from prior knowledge and reading list.* Before searching, you write down what you already know about the topic and pick the two most useful items from your reading list. You then follow a staged search: the reading list, the references in those items, then the discovery service. You link each stage to Kuhlthau's (1991) model, showing how prior knowledge helps you move from initiation to selection (for example, a psychology student starting an essay on eyewitness memory from a set textbook chapter). **Evidence:** A staged search plan with a 'what I already know' box, reading list items, followed-up references and new sources, each stage labelled. *(Grounding: ing:doc:information-search-process-kuhlthau-1991)*
        - *Applied: Teaching assistant search starting from what the school holds.* Working as a teaching assistant, you are asked to gather ideas for a reading intervention. You start with what the school already holds, such as past plans, staff knowledge and a training handout, then follow up the sources those cite before searching further. This shows a staged search that begins from existing knowledge (for example, finding that a colleague trialled paired reading last year, which narrows what you need to look for). **Evidence:** A short summary of what the school already held, the sources you followed up and the gap your new search filled. *(Grounding: professional: Starts a search from what the organisation and its people already hold before looking further.; professional: SCONUL Seven Pillars: Identify, Scope)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Information and sources (direct; lands on `finding-using-sources/02`)
- Universal: Independent and self-directed learning (indirect; lands on `finding-using-sources/c1`)

### Intermediate

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 4 | Identify keywords and synonyms from the task | L4 | l4/knowledge.2 "Analyse, interpret and evaluate relevant information and ideas." | **UNATTRIBUTED** | `finding-using-sources#d1` |
| 5 | Plan a search strategy with keywords and Boolean operators | L5 | l5/application.2 "Determine, adapt and use appropriate methods and skills." | **UNATTRIBUTED** | `finding-using-sources/01` |
| 6 | Refine a search in cycles, reflecting on the results each time | L6 | l6/application.2 "Determine, refine, adapt and use appropriate methods and skills." | **UNATTRIBUTED** | `finding-using-sources#d3` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

4. **Identify keywords and synonyms from the task**
    - *Benchmark:* none matched at this tier (**UNATTRIBUTED**)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Keywords and synonyms identified before a search begins.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse a task description to identify its component concepts and generate appropriate keywords and synonyms for each one.
          Given a task description, you identify the separate ideas within it, then generate keywords, synonyms and alternative spellings for each one before running a single search. You justify which terms you expect to be most productive and which you expect to narrow results too far, showing that identifying search language is a distinct step that happens before searching rather than something worked out by trial and error alone. **Evidence:** A written list of task concepts with keywords and synonyms for each, and a short justification of the choices made. *(Grounding: model: PICO framework for structuring questions (Richardson et al. 1995); benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
        - *Applied: Search terms built from a task's own wording and common usage.* **Learning outcome:** The student can apply a task's own wording and common alternative terms to build a search vocabulary that extends beyond the original phrasing.
          Given a short task, you list the exact words it uses, then add synonyms, abbreviations and the terms other people commonly use for the same idea, so a search is not limited to one way of phrasing the question. You note which added term made the biggest difference to the results, giving a concrete reason for widening your search language beyond the task's original wording. **Evidence:** A short list of original and added search terms, with a note on which addition improved the results most. *(Grounding: professional: Builds a keyword list from a brief plus the language people actually use about a topic.; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Concept grid breaking a task into keywords and synonyms.* You break a task's question down into its separate concepts using a structured framework for turning questions into searchable terms. For each concept you list keywords, synonyms, spelling variants and related terms, and explain why you kept or dropped each one. This shows you can turn an ordinary task description into language a search tool can actually use. **Evidence:** A concept grid with headings for each part of the question, keyword and synonym lists, and notes on rejected terms. *(Grounding: model: PICO framework for structuring questions (Richardson et al. 1995))*
        - *Applied: Keyword list built from a brief and everyday language.* Given a short brief, you pull out the obvious keywords it uses, then add the words people actually use when talking about the same topic in everyday language, such as reviews or informal posts. You test which of these terms actually finds useful material and which return mostly noise. This shows you can identify keywords and synonyms from a real task rather than relying on the brief's exact wording. **Evidence:** A keyword and synonym list drawn from a brief and everyday language, with a note on which terms worked best. *(Grounding: professional: Builds a keyword list from a brief plus the language people actually use about a topic.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: PICO concept grid with keywords and synonyms.* You break an assignment question into concepts using the PICO framework (Richardson et al. 1995): population, intervention, comparison and outcome. For each concept you list keywords, synonyms, spelling variants and related terms, and explain why you kept or dropped each one. This shows you can turn a task into searchable language (for example, in physiotherapy, 'older adults', 'elderly' and 'aged 65 and over' as population terms for a question on balance training and falls). **Evidence:** A concept grid under the four PICO headings, with keyword and synonym lists and notes on the terms you rejected. *(Grounding: model: PICO framework for clinical questions (Richardson et al. 1995))*
        - *Applied: Keyword list built from a brief and customer language.* On a marketing internship, you are asked to find what customers and trade press say about a product. You pull keywords from the brief, then add the words customers actually use in reviews and social posts, and test which terms find useful material. This shows you can identify keywords and synonyms from a real task (for example, 'sustainable', 'eco-friendly', 'green' and 'refill' for a refillable cleaning product). **Evidence:** A keyword and synonym list drawn from the brief and customer language, with a note on which terms found the most useful results. *(Grounding: practice: marketing internship)*
5. **Plan a search strategy with keywords and Boolean operators**
    - *Benchmark:* none matched at this tier (**UNATTRIBUTED**)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Search strings built and explained using Boolean logic.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain how Boolean operators and truncation combine keywords into a precise, purposeful search strategy.
          Given a topic with several components, you build search strings that combine the components with AND, bring in alternative wordings with OR, and remove unwanted meanings with NOT, adding truncation where a word has several endings. You explain each choice in terms of a recognised stage of the search process, where a settled focus allows precise, purposeful searching rather than a broad, unfocused look. **Evidence:** A set of Boolean search strings with a short written explanation of the reasoning behind each operator used. *(Grounding: ing:doc:information-search-process-kuhlthau-1991; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
        - *Applied: Reproducible search strategy planned and recorded for others.* **Learning outcome:** The student can plan and record a reproducible search strategy using keywords and Boolean operators so that another person could repeat it.
          Before searching, you plan a strategy using keywords and Boolean operators, then record every string, filter and date used so that someone else could rerun the exact same search and get the same results. You note the result count at each stage and briefly explain why you added or removed a term, turning what could be an informal look into a planned, reproducible piece of work. **Evidence:** A search log recording each string, filter, date and result count, written so another person could repeat it. *(Grounding: professional: Plans and documents searches for evidence, policy, competitors or prior art.; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Boolean search strategy placed within the search process.* You write a search strategy that combines concepts with AND, synonyms with OR, and excludes unwanted terms with NOT, adding truncation and limiters such as a date range. You place this plan within an established stage of the search process, where having a focused perspective lets you search with real purpose, and explain how your search strings reflect that focus rather than being guesswork. **Evidence:** A written search strategy with search strings, limiters and reasons for each choice, plus a results screenshot. *(Grounding: ing:doc:information-search-process-kuhlthau-1991)*
        - *Applied: Documented search strategy for a real decision.* Before a team moves ahead with a plan, you plan and document a search on a specialised database to check whether something similar already exists. You set out the keywords, Boolean strings and category codes used, then record dates and result counts so someone else could rerun the same search later. This shows a planned, reproducible strategy that supports a real decision rather than an informal look. **Evidence:** A documented search log with strings, databases, dates and result counts, shared with whoever needs to see it. *(Grounding: professional: Plans and documents searches for evidence, policy, competitors or prior art.; framework: SCONUL Seven Pillars: Plan)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Boolean search strategy placed at Kuhlthau's formulation stage.* You write a search strategy that combines concepts with AND, synonyms with OR and excludes noise with NOT, adding truncation and limiters such as date range. Drawing on Kuhlthau (1991), you place the plan at the formulation stage, where a focused perspective lets you search with purpose, and explain how the strings reflect that focus (for example, in criminology: ('knife crime' OR 'weapon carrying') AND (youth OR adolescen*) AND prevention). **Evidence:** A written search strategy with search strings, limiters and reasons for each choice, plus a screenshot of results from one database. *(Grounding: ing:doc:information-search-process-kuhlthau-1991)*
        - *Applied: Documented prior-art search for a product design live brief.* On a product design live brief, you plan and document a prior-art search on a patent database before your team develops a new folding bike lock. You set out the keywords, Boolean strings and classification codes, then record dates and hit counts so someone else could rerun the search. This shows a planned, reproducible strategy for a real decision (for example, finding an expired patent that frees your team to use a hinge design). **Evidence:** A documented search log with strings, databases, dates and hit counts, shared with the client or module tutor. *(Grounding: professional: Plans and documents searches for evidence, policy, competitors or prior art.; professional: SCONUL Seven Pillars: Plan)*
6. **Refine a search in cycles, reflecting on the results each time**
    - *Benchmark:* none matched at this tier (**UNATTRIBUTED**)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Search cycles judged and adjusted through reflection.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse search results from successive cycles to judge what is working and what change should be carried forward.
          You treat a search as a sequence of rounds rather than one attempt, and after each round you judge explicitly what worked, what returned too much noise and what the results suggest you should try next. You keep a short note after each round stating the change you are making and the reason for it, so that the refinement can be traced rather than appearing as an unexplained shift in approach. **Evidence:** A round-by-round record stating what each search returned, the judgement made and the change carried forward. *(Grounding: model: Berrypicking model of information seeking (Bates 1989); benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
        - *Applied: Search terms adjusted each cycle based on a stated reason.* **Learning outcome:** The student can refine a search across repeated cycles, adjusting terms or sources for a stated reason based on each round's results.
          Running searches to support an ongoing piece of work, you treat each attempt as one cycle in a sequence rather than a final answer. After every cycle you state in writing what the results were missing, change your terms or sources accordingly, and carry that reasoning into the next cycle. This shows a search that improves through reflection rather than one that stays the same no matter what the results show. **Evidence:** A log of search cycles, each with the stated gap identified and the specific change carried into the next cycle. *(Grounding: professional: Reviews search results with someone else involved in the work and adjusts terms for the next cycle.; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Search refined across rounds using a model of evolving queries.* You run a search in at least three rounds, and after each one you record what the results showed and what you changed as a result. You draw on a recognised model of information seeking, which describes a search query evolving as you read rather than staying fixed from the start, and you judge for each round whether the change you made actually helped. **Evidence:** A three-round search reflection showing each query used, a sample of results and the change made after each round. *(Grounding: model: Berrypicking model of information seeking (Bates 1989))*
        - *Applied: Search cycles refined with regular review from a colleague.* Supporting a piece of ongoing work, you run searches on a regular cycle, such as weekly, and review the results each time with someone else involved in the work. Each review identifies what was missing from the previous round, and you adjust your terms or the places you search for the next cycle. This shows reflection on results and real adjustment, rather than repeating the same search unchanged. **Evidence:** A shared search log or update notes showing each cycle's query, results and the feedback that shaped the next one. *(Grounding: professional: Reviews search results with someone else involved in the work and adjusts terms for the next cycle.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Three-round search reflection using Bates' berrypicking model.* You run a search in at least three rounds, and after each round you record what the results showed and what you changed. You use Bates' (1989) berrypicking model to explain why your query evolved as you read, rather than staying fixed, and judge whether each change helped. This shows you refine a search through reflection (for example, a music education search moving from music and wellbeing to group singing with older adults after early results pointed to choirs). **Evidence:** A three-round search reflection showing each query, a sample of results, what you learned and the change you made. *(Grounding: model: Berrypicking model of information seeking (Bates 1989))*
        - *Applied: Weekly search cycles as a student research assistant.* As a paid student research assistant on a sociology project about remote working, you run weekly searches for the team. Each week you review the results with the lead researcher, note what was missing, and refine your terms and databases for the next cycle. This shows you reflect on results and adjust in a real project (for example, adding 'hybrid working' after the first cycle missed most studies published since 2020). **Evidence:** A shared search log or weekly update emails showing each cycle's query, results and the lead researcher's feedback. *(Grounding: practice: student research assistant role)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- none matched; see the every-tier keys below

### Advanced

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | Extend a search by citation searching, databases and Google Scholar | L5 | l5/application.3 "Use relevant research or development to inform actions." | direct | `finding-using-sources/04` |
| 8 | Evaluate sources for currency, authority and relevance to own knowledge | L6 | 4.15.b5 "make use of scholarly reviews and primary sources" | direct | `finding-using-sources/03` |
| 9 | Search comprehensively, judging when the discourse is covered | L7 | 4.17.b1 "a critical awareness of current problems and/or new insights"; l7/application.3 "Design and undertake research" | direct | `finding-using-sources#e3` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

7. **Extend a search by citation searching, databases and Google Scholar**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Information and sources (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Comprehensive source coverage built through citation tracking.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain how backward and forward citation tracking across multiple tools builds comprehensive, appropriate coverage of a topic.
          Rather than treating one database as the end of a search, you follow a key source both backwards through what it cites and forwards through what later cites it, then cross-check the result against a second search tool. You select only the sources that are genuinely appropriate to the question at hand, discarding material a tool returns that does not actually fit, and explain your selection in each case. **Evidence:** A written account of backward and forward citation tracking, with the final selected sources and reasons for inclusion. *(Grounding: ing:doc:information-search-process-kuhlthau-1991; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/information-and-sources)*
        - *Applied: Sources selected as appropriate after a search extended across tools.* **Learning outcome:** The student can extend a search using citation tracking and additional databases, then select only the sources appropriate to the question.
          Given a question that needs solid evidence behind it, you run an initial search, then extend it with citation tracking and at least one further tool, and from everything found you select only the sources genuinely appropriate to the question, setting aside the rest with a brief reason. This shows a search that goes beyond a single tool and ends in a deliberately selected, appropriate set of sources. **Evidence:** A short record of the tools used, the sources selected as appropriate and the reasons given for excluding others. *(Grounding: professional: Searches beyond the first tool to cover the evidence comprehensively.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/information-and-sources)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Citation map extended backward and forward from one source.* Starting from one key source, you search backwards through its reference list and forwards through citation-tracking tools to see what later work builds on it, recording what each route adds that the other misses. You frame this using a stage of the search process where having a clear focus lets you gather material comprehensively, and explain why citation searching suited that particular point in your work. **Evidence:** A citation map showing the seed source with backward and forward links, and a table of what each route added. *(Grounding: ing:doc:information-search-process-kuhlthau-1991)*
        - *Applied: Evidence search extended beyond a single tool for full coverage.* Asked for current evidence on a specific question, you search one database first, then extend the search with a second database, forward citation tracking and relevant published guidance, noting what each addition contributed. This shows you can search beyond the first tool so the final evidence picture is properly covered rather than resting on whatever the first search happened to surface. **Evidence:** An evidence summary recording the tools used in sequence and what each one added that the previous tool missed. *(Grounding: professional: Searches beyond the first tool to cover the evidence comprehensively.; framework: SCONUL Seven Pillars: Scope, Gather)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Citation map from one seed paper, backward and forward.* Starting from one key paper, you search backwards through its reference list and forwards through 'cited by' in Google Scholar and a subject database, recording what each route added. You frame this with Kuhlthau's (1991) collection stage, when a clear focus lets you gather comprehensively, and explain why citation searching suited that point in your search (for example, in chemistry, tracing a landmark paper on a catalyst forward to its recent applications). **Evidence:** A citation map showing the seed paper with backward and forward links, and a table of new sources each tool found. *(Grounding: ing:doc:information-search-process-kuhlthau-1991)*
        - *Applied: Ward evidence summary extended beyond the first database.* On a nursing placement, the ward manager asks for evidence on pressure ulcer prevention. After searching one database, you extend the search with a second database, forward citation searching and national guidance, and note what each added. This shows you can search beyond the first tool so the evidence is properly covered (for example, finding a recent Cochrane review only by following citations forward from an older trial). **Evidence:** An evidence summary for the ward with a record of the tools used and what each added, signed by your practice supervisor. *(Grounding: professional: Searches beyond the first tool to cover the evidence comprehensively.; professional: SCONUL Seven Pillars: Scope, Gather)*
8. **Evaluate sources for currency, authority and relevance to own knowledge**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Information and sources (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Reasoned judgement reached on a source's currency and authority.* **Learning outcome:** The student can evaluate a source's currency, authority and relevance against existing knowledge to reach a reasoned, defensible judgement.
          Given a source you are unsure whether to trust, you set out its currency, its authority and its relevance to what you already know, weigh these three against each other, and state a reasoned judgement on whether to use it. You show your reasoning clearly enough that someone else could check it and reach their own view, rather than simply stating a verdict without explaining how you got there. **Evidence:** A short written judgement on one source covering currency, authority and relevance, with the reasoning set out. *(Grounding: ing:doc:information-search-process-kuhlthau-1991; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
        - *Applied: Reasoned decision made on which sources to rely on.* **Learning outcome:** The student can weigh competing sources for currency, authority and relevance and justify a reasoned decision on which to rely on.
          Given several sources that disagree with each other, you weigh each one for currency, authority and relevance to the decision at hand, then reach a reasoned judgement on which to rely on and which to set aside. You state your reasoning in a way that someone checking your decision afterwards could follow and test, rather than leaving the choice unexplained. **Evidence:** A short written decision naming the sources relied on, those set aside and the reasoning behind the choice. *(Grounding: professional: Judges whether information is reliable and relevant enough to base a decision on.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Evaluation matrix tracking how relevance judgements change.* You evaluate a set of sources for a substantial piece of work against currency, authority and relevance, adding a column on how each one relates to what you already know. Using a recognised model of how a search develops over time, you note how your judgement of a source's relevance shifted as your own understanding grew, and explain why you kept some older sources and dropped some newer ones. **Evidence:** An evaluation matrix scoring each source for currency, authority and relevance, with notes on judgements that changed. *(Grounding: ing:doc:information-search-process-kuhlthau-1991)*
        - *Applied: Source checks completed before a piece goes public.* Before a piece of writing is published, you check every source behind its claims, judging each for currency, authority and relevance to the specific claim it supports. You decide which sources can carry the piece as it stands and which need replacing, and you make at least one change, such as swapping an outdated figure for a current one. This shows reliability judgements made under a real deadline. **Evidence:** A source-checking sheet for one piece with a verdict on each source, alongside the version that was published. *(Grounding: professional: Judges whether information is reliable and relevant enough to base a decision on.; framework: ACRL Framework: Authority Is Constructed and Contextual)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Source evaluation matrix tracking changing relevance judgements.* You evaluate eight sources for a dissertation proposal against currency, authority and relevance, adding a column on how each relates to what you already know. Using Kuhlthau's (1991) stages, you note how your judgements of relevance changed between exploration and formulation, and why you kept or dropped sources (for example, in architecture, keeping an older daylight study because later work still builds on it). **Evidence:** An evaluation matrix of eight sources scored for currency, authority and relevance, with notes on how your judgements changed over time. *(Grounding: ing:doc:information-search-process-kuhlthau-1991)*
        - *Applied: Source checks before publishing a student newspaper story.* As an editor on the student newspaper, you check every source in a story on rising rents before it is published. You judge each for currency, authority and relevance to the claim it supports, and decide which can carry the story and which need replacing. This shows you can judge whether information is reliable enough to act on (for example, replacing a 2019 letting agency survey with current official rental price data). **Evidence:** A source-checking sheet for one published article with your verdict on each source, alongside the final published piece. *(Grounding: professional: Judges whether information is reliable and relevant enough to base a decision on.; professional: ACRL Framework: Authority Is Constructed and Contextual)*
9. **Search comprehensively, judging when the discourse is covered**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Reasoned judgement reached on when a search is sufficiently complete.* **Learning outcome:** The student can justify a judgement on when a search is sufficiently comprehensive, weighing evidence such as repeated or diminishing results.
          Rather than stopping a search when you run out of time, you set out the evidence for treating the discourse as covered, such as further searches returning only sources you already hold, or reference lists adding nothing new. You weigh this evidence and reach a stated judgement on completeness, explaining your reasoning so someone else could check whether the search really was comprehensive. **Evidence:** A written statement of the evidence for completeness, the judgement reached and the reasoning behind it. *(Grounding: model: PRISMA 2020 reporting guideline (Page et al. 2021); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
        - *Applied: Judgement made on when evidence-gathering is complete enough.* **Learning outcome:** The student can decide and justify when evidence-gathering is complete enough, recording the indicators and reasoning behind that stopping point.
          Given a task that depends on gathering evidence, you weigh indicators such as repeated results, exhausted reference trails and diminishing new material, and reach a stated judgement on when to stop rather than continuing indefinitely or stopping arbitrarily. You record the reasoning behind that judgement so someone relying on your evidence can see it was a deliberate decision, not a default caused by running out of time. **Evidence:** A short written judgement stating when the evidence-gathering stopped, the indicators used and the reasoning given. *(Grounding: professional: Agrees a stopping rule for an evidence scan in advance and reports what the search may have missed.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Literature search reported with a justified stopping point.* For a substantial piece of work, you run a documented search across several sources and report it using a recognised reporting standard that tracks how many results came from where and what was excluded. You then set out, in writing, when and why you judged the material covered, such as new searches returning only items you already had, or key reference lists adding nothing further. **Evidence:** A reporting diagram, the full search details for each source used, and a short statement justifying where the search stopped. *(Grounding: model: PRISMA 2020 reporting guideline (Page et al. 2021))*
        - *Applied: Evidence scan completed against an agreed stopping rule.* Asked to gather evidence to inform a decision, you agree in advance, with whoever needs the result, a rule for when the scan is complete, then search academic and other published material and report what the scan covers and what it may have missed. This shows a judgement about sufficient coverage that is made deliberately and agreed beforehand, rather than being decided by running out of time. **Evidence:** An evidence scan report with its coverage and limitations stated, and a note confirming the stopping rule was agreed. *(Grounding: professional: Agrees a stopping rule for an evidence scan in advance and reports what the search may have missed.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: PRISMA-reported literature search with a justified end point.* For a dissertation literature review, you run a documented search across several databases and report it with a PRISMA 2020 flow diagram (Page et al. 2021). You then set out when and why you judged the discourse covered, such as new searches returning only studies you already had and key reference lists adding nothing new (for example, in a pharmacy review of deprescribing in care homes). **Evidence:** A PRISMA flow diagram, the full search strings for each database and a 300-word statement justifying where your search stopped. *(Grounding: model: PRISMA 2020 reporting guideline (Page et al. 2021))*
        - *Applied: Evidence scan with an agreed stopping rule for a transport authority.* On a placement with a regional transport authority, you lead an evidence scan on e-scooter safety to inform a policy decision. You search academic and grey literature, agree a stopping rule with your manager in advance, and report what the scan covers and what it may have missed. This shows independent judgement about when coverage is enough (for example, noting that most studies came from cities with very different road layouts). **Evidence:** The evidence scan report with its coverage and limitations section, and an email from your manager agreeing the stopping rule. *(Grounding: practice: placement with a regional transport authority)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (direct; lands on `finding-using-sources/03`, `finding-using-sources#e3`)
- Universal: Information and sources (direct; lands on `finding-using-sources/04`, `finding-using-sources/03`)
- Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect; lands on `finding-using-sources/03`)

### Keys at every tier

**Progression:**

- Psychology 3.17: retrieve and organise information found in a range of sources (direct)
- Psychology 4.8: systematically reviewed and evaluated key psychological literature (direct)
- Psychology 4.9: conducted in-depth reviews of psychological literature (direct)

**Floor:**

- Universal: Ethics, integrity and values (indirect)
- Universal: Oral or other communication to audiences (indirect)

<a id="referencing"></a>
## Referencing, Citation and Academic Integrity

### Introductory

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Explain why academic work acknowledges its sources | L3 | l3/knowledge.3 "Be aware of the nature of the area of study or work." | direct | `referencing/c1` |
| 2 | Construct accurate in-text references and a references list in one style | L4 | 4.10.b4 "accurately and reliably" | direct | `referencing/01` |
| 3 | Record and organise sources as they are found, so every citation can be traced | L5 | l5/autonomy.2 "Exercise autonomy and judgement within broad parameters." | direct | `referencing/c2` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

1. **Explain why academic work acknowledges its sources**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Information and sources (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Explaining why selected sources must be named.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain why academic work names its sources, showing how acknowledgement lets a reader judge the quality of information used and trace claims to their origin.
          Working from a short set of possible sources, you select the ones most relevant to a question and explain why naming them matters rather than simply listing them at the end. You set out how acknowledging where information comes from lets a reader judge the quality of what you selected and trace a claim back to its origin. You treat this naming as part of showing your selection was deliberate, not an afterthought added once the writing was finished. **Evidence:** A short written explanation linking selected sources to the reasons they were chosen and named. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/information-and-sources)*
        - *Applied: Justifying chosen sources to an uninvolved colleague.* **Learning outcome:** The student can identify the sources used in a piece of work and explain to someone unfamiliar with it why those sources were selected and acknowledged.
          You are asked to justify why you relied on particular sources of information when completing a piece of work. You explain, to someone who was not involved, which sources you selected, why they were appropriate to the task, and why you named them openly rather than presenting their conclusions as your own. This shows that selecting and acknowledging sources appropriate to the work is part of how you earn a reader's trust. **Evidence:** A short written justification of the sources selected and acknowledged, reviewed by a colleague. *(Grounding: professional: Explains why a report or piece of advice names the evidence it rests on and where it comes from.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/information-and-sources)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Linking source acknowledgement to joining a knowledge community.* You write a short explanation of why serious written work names its sources, using the idea that newcomers join any community of practice by taking part in its shared customs. Acknowledging sources is one such custom: it signals honesty about where an idea came from and lets a reader trace it back to the original argument it builds on. You treat this as a mark of trust between writer and reader, not a formality that exists only to avoid being penalised. **Evidence:** A one-page note linking source acknowledgement to joining a knowledge community, with written feedback. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991)*
        - *Applied: Tracing the sourced claims in a public-facing document.* You take a real document written for a general audience and trace where its figures and claims come from, checking each one against its original source. You then explain, in a short note, why naming that evidence matters to the people who rely on the document, showing that acknowledging sources is about accountability and trust rather than something done only to earn a mark. **Evidence:** An annotated document marking each claim and its source, with a short note on why acknowledgement matters to readers. *(Grounding: professional: Explains why a report or piece of advice names the evidence it rests on and where it comes from.; professional: ICAI Fundamental Values (2021): honesty, trust)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Community of practice note on why sources are acknowledged.* You write a short explanation of why academic work names its sources, using Lave and Wenger's (1991) idea of legitimate peripheral participation: newcomers join a community of practice by taking part in its customs, and acknowledging sources is one of those customs. You link this to trust and to letting readers trace ideas (for example, why a history essay on the 1832 Reform Act credits the historians whose arguments it builds on). **Evidence:** A one-page explanatory note, with tutor comments, linking the acknowledgement of sources to joining an academic community of practice. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991)*
        - *Applied: Tracing the sources behind a community leaflet.* You take a real document from a part-time job or volunteering role and find where its figures and claims come from (for example, a food bank leaflet quoting NHS portion guidance). You then explain to your supervisor why naming that evidence matters to the people who read it, showing you understand that acknowledging sources is about accountability and trust, not only marks. **Evidence:** The annotated leaflet marking each claim and its source, with a short note on why acknowledgement matters to its readers. *(Grounding: professional: Explains why a report or piece of advice names the evidence it rests on and where it comes from.; professional: ICAI Fundamental Values (2021): honesty, trust)*
2. **Construct accurate in-text references and a references list in one style**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Information and sources (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Converting a mixed source list into one consistent style.* **Learning outcome:** The student can describe the elements a reference needs and explain how converting sources into one consistent citation style lets a reader trace every claim.
          Given a short list of sources found during research, you select the details each one needs, such as author, date, title and publisher, and convert every entry into one consistent citation style. You check each in-text citation against its matching reference so a reader could trace every claim back to where it was found. This demonstrates using sources appropriate to the task accurately and consistently, not only locating them. **Evidence:** A worked references list converted into one consistent style, checked against its in-text citations. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/information-and-sources)*
        - *Applied: Standardising citations across a shared piece of work.* **Learning outcome:** The student can identify inconsistent citations in a piece of writing and correct them so every citation and reference entry matches one consistent style.
