          You are handed a draft containing citations in several inconsistent formats and asked to bring it into line with one required style before it goes out. You check each source is still correctly identified, correct any missing details, and make every citation and reference entry consistent. This shows that you can find, select and use sources accurately even when you did not collect them yourself. **Evidence:** A corrected draft with every citation standardised, plus a short note listing the changes made. *(Grounding: professional: Attributes sources in reports, proposals and published material in the style the organisation or field requires.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/information-and-sources)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Annotating a references list to show what each element does.* You build the in-text citations and references list for a short piece of work in one required style, then annotate three entries to explain how each element, such as author, date, title and publisher, helps a reader find the source. Drawing on the idea that learning these conventions is part of moving from the edge of a knowledge community towards fuller participation, you treat accuracy here as more than a formatting rule to satisfy. **Evidence:** An annotated references list and matching in-text citations, checked against a style guide, with margin notes. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991)*
        - *Applied: Matching a required house style for sourced claims.* You attribute sources in a document written for a real reader, following a required house style rather than a familiar set of conventions you already know well. You keep the style consistent throughout and check that every citation leads back to its source, showing that you can construct accurate references when someone else has set the format rather than when you chose it yourself. **Evidence:** A submitted document with its source notes, the house style guidance followed, and checking notes. *(Grounding: professional: Attributes sources in reports, proposals and published material in the style the organisation or field requires.; professional: SCONUL Seven Pillars: Manage (recall: cites sources in suitable styles))*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Annotated references list showing what each element does.* You build the in-text citations and references list for a first assignment in your department's required style, then annotate three entries to explain how each element (author, date, title, publisher, page) helps a reader find the source. Drawing on Lave and Wenger (1991), you note that learning these conventions is part of moving from the edge of your subject community towards fuller participation (for example, Harvard author-date in a business studies report). **Evidence:** An annotated references list and matching in-text citations from an assignment, checked against the style guide, with margin notes. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991)*
        - *Applied: Footnoted campaign briefing in an organisation's house style.* You attribute sources in a document written for a real reader, following that organisation's house style rather than a module handbook (for example, a students' union campaign briefing where the officer asks for footnoted links to council data). You keep the style consistent and check that every citation leads to its source, showing you can construct accurate references when someone else sets the format. **Evidence:** The submitted briefing with its source notes, plus the house style guidance you followed and your checking notes. *(Grounding: professional: Attributes sources in reports, proposals and published material in the style the organisation or field requires.; professional: SCONUL Seven Pillars: Manage (recall: cites sources in suitable styles))*
3. **Record and organise sources as they are found, so every citation can be traced**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Information and sources (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Independent and self-directed learning (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Logging sources at the point of selection, not afterwards.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain why recording a source at the point it is selected, rather than afterwards, keeps every citation traceable and organised for later use.
          As you find and select sources for a piece of work, you record each one immediately, noting enough detail that it could be traced and used again later. You organise the log so related sources sit together, rather than treating recording as a step to complete once the writing is finished. This shows that selecting sources appropriate to a task includes keeping a usable record of the selection itself. **Evidence:** A source log built during research, organised by theme, with full details recorded at point of selection. *(Grounding: model: SCONUL Seven Pillars of Information Literacy (SCONUL 2011); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/information-and-sources)*
        - *Applied: Building a traceable record while gathering evidence.* **Learning outcome:** The student can keep an organised, traceable record of sources while gathering evidence for a task, so each one can be found and checked again later.
          While gathering evidence for a task, you keep a running record of where each source came from and how it was used, organised well enough that a colleague could pick it up without asking you. You treat this record as part of selecting sources properly, not as paperwork added once the real work is done. This shows that finding and using sources appropriate to a task includes keeping them traceable throughout. **Evidence:** A running evidence record built during the task, reviewed by a colleague for completeness. *(Grounding: professional: Keeps a record of the sources and evidence used, so that any figure or claim can be traced.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/information-and-sources)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Mapping a source log to an information literacy model.* You set up a log for every source you plan to use in a piece of work and map your routine to a recognised information literacy model, which treats organising and citing information as a distinct skill rather than an afterthought. You record full details, page numbers and the date you accessed each source as you read, not afterwards, so that every citation you later write can be traced back to where it came from. **Evidence:** A source log or reference manager export, with a short commentary mapping the routine to the model. *(Grounding: model: SCONUL Seven Pillars of Information Literacy (SCONUL 2011))*
        - *Applied: Keeping a traceable record of evidence behind a piece of work.* In a role where you produce work others will check, you keep a record of where every figure or claim came from, organised so a colleague could find it quickly. You log sources as you use them rather than reconstructing the list afterwards, showing the same traceability habit that accurate referencing depends on, and you can point to a specific figure and its source on request. **Evidence:** An evidence log or index from the work, with a note confirming it was used to check figures. *(Grounding: professional: Keeps a record of the sources and evidence used, so that any figure or claim can be traced.; professional: Concordat to Support Research Integrity: accountability)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Source log mapped to the SCONUL Manage pillar.* You set up a source log for a project and map your routine to the Manage pillar of the SCONUL Seven Pillars of Information Literacy (SCONUL 2011), which covers organising information and citing it properly. You record full details, page numbers and access dates as you read, not afterwards, so every later citation can be traced (for example, a psychology lab report drawing on twelve journal articles). **Evidence:** A source log or reference manager library export, with a short commentary mapping your recording routine to the Manage pillar. *(Grounding: model: SCONUL Seven Pillars of Information Literacy (SCONUL 2011))*
        - *Applied: Placement evidence log behind a component specification.* On a placement or in a job, you keep a record of where every figure in a piece of work came from, so a colleague could check it (for example, an engineering placement where you log the standards and supplier datasheets behind a component specification). You organise the record so items can be found quickly, showing the same traceability habit that referencing needs. **Evidence:** An evidence log or shared-drive index from the placement, with a supervisor's note confirming it was used to check figures. *(Grounding: professional: Keeps a record of the sources and evidence used, so that any figure or claim can be traced.; professional: Concordat to Support Research Integrity: accountability)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Information and sources (direct; lands on `referencing/c1`, `referencing/01`, `referencing/c2`)
- Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect; lands on `referencing/c1`)
- Universal: Independent and self-directed learning (indirect; lands on `referencing/c2`)

### Intermediate

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 4 | Acknowledge quotation and paraphrase correctly, avoiding plagiarism | L4 | l4/application.2 "Identify, adapt and use appropriate methods and skills." | indirect only | `referencing/02` |
| 5 | Reference trickier source types accurately | L5 | l5/application.2 "Determine, adapt and use appropriate methods and skills." | direct | `referencing#d2` |
| 6 | Use citation to show the scope of research and engagement with the discourse | L6 | 4.15.b5 "make use of scholarly reviews and primary sources" | direct | `referencing/c3` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

4. **Acknowledge quotation and paraphrase correctly, avoiding plagiarism**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Demonstrating subject knowledge through accurate paraphrase.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse a paraphrase against its original source to check that no distinctive wording survives unattributed, showing genuine understanding rather than rearranged phrasing.
          Given a passage central to a topic, you paraphrase it in your own words and compare your version against the original to check no distinctive phrasing has survived unmarked. Where your understanding is incomplete, the paraphrase tends to drift back towards the source's wording, so you treat that drift as a signal to reread the passage rather than patch the sentence. This links accurate paraphrase to genuinely understanding the subject's core content, not just rearranging words. **Evidence:** A paraphrased passage with the original attached and notes on where understanding was checked. *(Grounding: model: Patchwriting (Howard 1995); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/subject-knowledge-and-understanding)*
        - *Applied: Showing understanding by paraphrasing a technical passage for others.* **Learning outcome:** The student can paraphrase a technical passage accurately for a non specialist reader, marking any direct quotation and crediting the source throughout.
          You are asked to explain a technical passage to someone without specialist background, in your own words, while being honest about which parts are a close paraphrase and which are a direct quotation worth keeping intact. Doing this well depends on genuinely understanding the core content, since a shaky grasp shows up as wording that drifts back towards the original. You credit the source either way, showing both your understanding and your honesty. **Evidence:** A written explanation for a general reader, with the source credited and quoted passages marked. *(Grounding: professional: Reports others' words and findings accurately, marking what is quoted and putting the rest in own words with attribution.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/subject-knowledge-and-understanding)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Checking a draft paragraph for patchwriting.* You compare a paragraph you drafted from a source with the original wording, checking for patchwriting, where a writer stays too close to the source's sentence structure while changing only a few words. You mark any patchwritten phrases, then either quote them properly with quotation marks and a page reference or rewrite them as a genuine paraphrase with a citation, so the final version reflects your own understanding of the material. **Evidence:** Side-by-side drafts: the source, the first attempt with patchwritten phrases marked, and the corrected version. *(Grounding: model: Patchwriting (Howard 1995))*
        - *Applied: Marking quotation and paraphrase in a shared summary.* You report someone else's words and findings accurately in a short item written for other people, marking clearly what is quoted and putting the rest in your own words with credit. You decide, claim by claim, when an exact quotation best serves the reader and when a paraphrase does, and you credit the original source either way, so nothing in the summary could be mistaken for your own finding. **Evidence:** A written summary with the source attached and the quoted and paraphrased passages highlighted. *(Grounding: professional: Reports others' words and findings accurately, marking what is quoted and putting the rest in own words with attribution.; professional: ICAI Fundamental Values (2021): honesty)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Patchwriting check and revision of a drafted paragraph.* You compare a paragraph you drafted from a source with the original, using Howard's (1995) idea of patchwriting, where a writer stays too close to the source's wording and sentence structure. You mark any patchwritten phrases, then either quote them with quotation marks and a page number or rewrite them as a genuine paraphrase with a citation (for example, a law student summarising a Supreme Court judgment). **Evidence:** Side-by-side drafts: the source, your first attempt with patchwritten phrases marked, and the revised version with correct quotation and attribution. *(Grounding: model: Patchwriting (Howard 1995))*
        - *Applied: Ward newsletter summary with quotation and paraphrase marked.* You report someone else's words and findings accurately in a real setting, marking what is quoted and putting the rest in your own words with credit (for example, a nursing student writing a ward newsletter item that summarises a new NICE guideline). You decide when an exact quotation is needed and when a paraphrase serves the reader better, and you credit both. **Evidence:** The newsletter item, with the source attached and the quoted and paraphrased passages highlighted for comparison. *(Grounding: professional: Reports others' words and findings accurately, marking what is quoted and putting the rest in own words with attribution.; professional: ICAI Fundamental Values (2021): honesty)*
5. **Reference trickier source types accurately**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Information and sources (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Selecting the right template for an awkward source type.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse an unfamiliar source type to identify which elements a reader needs to locate it, rather than forcing it into a standard template.
          Given a source that does not fit the usual pattern, such as a dataset, a translated text or a piece of software, you work out which elements a reader would need to find it again, rather than forcing it into a template built for a book or an article. You select and record only the details that genuinely identify that source, showing that using sources appropriate to the task includes adapting how you cite the ones that do not fit easily. **Evidence:** A short worked reference for an awkward source type, with notes on the elements selected and why. *(Grounding: model: MLA core elements template (Modern Language Association 2021); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/information-and-sources)*
        - *Applied: Referencing an unfamiliar source type under time pressure.* **Learning outcome:** The student can reference an unfamiliar source type correctly under time pressure, checking guidance and recording the decision so the approach can be repeated.
          You are given a source you have not had to cite before, such as a recorded interview, a dataset or an exhibition, and asked to reference it correctly before a deadline. You check the available guidance, select the elements that identify that source, and record your decision so a colleague could follow the same approach next time. This shows that finding and using sources appropriate to the task extends to ones that are unfamiliar. **Evidence:** A completed reference for an unfamiliar source type, with a short note on the decision made. *(Grounding: framework: SCONUL Seven Pillars: Manage (recall: cites sources in suitable styles); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/information-and-sources)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Breaking an unfamiliar source into its core elements.* You reference an awkward source, such as a translated work, a chapter inside an edited collection, a dataset or an online post, by working through a core elements template covering author, title, container, other contributors, version, number, publisher, date and location. You then convert the entry into a required citation style, checking that every element that exists for that source type has been captured correctly rather than guessed at. **Evidence:** A worked table breaking an unusual source into its core elements, with the final reference in a required style. *(Grounding: model: MLA core elements template (Modern Language Association 2021))*
        - *Applied: Recording how an unusual source was credited.* On a project where sources do not fit a standard pattern, such as a set of guidelines, a licensed image, an interview recording or an online exhibit, you credit each one, checking a style guide or asking someone with more experience when the format is unclear. You record each decision so it can be repeated consistently, showing that you can handle unfamiliar source types accurately rather than skipping or guessing at them. **Evidence:** A project credits page plus a decision note recording how each unusual source type was referenced. *(Grounding: framework: SCONUL Seven Pillars: Manage (recall: cites sources in suitable styles))*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Core elements breakdown of awkward source types.* You reference awkward sources, such as a translated novel, a chapter in an edited book, a dataset and a social media post, by working through the core elements template in the MLA Handbook (Modern Language Association 2021): author, title, container, other contributors, version, number, publisher, date and location. You then convert each entry into your required style, showing you understand what each element does (for example, in a modern languages essay). **Evidence:** A worked table breaking each tricky source into its core elements, with the final reference in your required style. *(Grounding: model: MLA core elements template (Modern Language Association 2021))*
        - *Applied: Credits page and decision note for a live design brief.* On a live brief or society project, you credit sources that do not fit a standard pattern (for example, a design student crediting a client's brand guidelines, a licensed stock image, a podcast interview and an online exhibition). You check the style guide or ask a librarian, record each decision, and apply it consistently, showing you can handle unfamiliar source types accurately. **Evidence:** The project's credits or references page, plus a decision note recording how each unusual source type was referenced and why. *(Grounding: practice: live design brief for an external client)*
6. **Use citation to show the scope of research and engagement with the discourse**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Information and sources (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Showing research scope through a reference list's range.* **Learning outcome:** The student can evaluate a reference list to judge whether its range, including opposing views, reflects the genuine scope of the research undertaken.
          You review a piece of your own writing and ask what its reference list reveals about the scope of your research, rather than only whether each citation is correctly formatted. You check whether the sources span enough of the relevant conversation, including views that disagree with your own, and treat gaps in that range as a sign your research was narrower than the sources appropriate to the task required. **Evidence:** A short review of a reference list's range, noting where coverage was too narrow and how it was widened. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/information-and-sources)*
        - *Applied: Demonstrating research scope through cited evidence.* **Learning outcome:** The student can gather and cite sources that both support and challenge a position, so a reader can judge the breadth of research behind a recommendation.
          Before presenting a recommendation, you check that the sources you have gathered show the genuine scope of your research, not just the first few that supported your initial view. You deliberately seek out sources appropriate to the task that challenge your position, and you cite them alongside the supporting evidence, so a reader can see the breadth of what you considered before reaching a judgement. **Evidence:** A citation list for a recommendation showing both supporting and challenging sources considered. *(Grounding: professional: Shows the range and quality of the evidence behind a recommendation.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/information-and-sources)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Mapping a reference list into strands of engagement.* You analyse your own reference list and sort the citations into strands, such as foundational works, recent studies, opposing views and primary sources. Drawing on the idea that joining a knowledge community means engaging with its different conversations, you read what your citations tell a reader about which of those conversations you have joined, and you add sources where a strand is thin or missing before finishing the work. **Evidence:** A citation map of a reference list grouped by strand, with a note on gaps filled before submission. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991)*
        - *Applied: Showing the evidence base behind a real recommendation.* You prepare a recommendation for a genuine decision and set out the range and quality of the evidence behind it, distinguishing strong, directly relevant sources from weaker or more tangential ones. You explain to the reader why that evidence base is enough to support the decision, and where it remains thin, so your citations do more than list what you happened to read. **Evidence:** A recommendation with an evidence summary table and feedback on whether it informed the decision. *(Grounding: professional: Shows the range and quality of the evidence behind a recommendation.; professional: ICAI Fundamental Values (2021): trust)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Citation map showing which conversations your essay joins.* You analyse the references list of your own essay and sort the citations into strands: foundational works, recent studies, opposing views and primary sources. Using Lave and Wenger's (1991) community of practice idea, you explain what your citations tell a reader about which conversations in the field you have joined, and add sources where a strand is missing (for example, a sociology essay on gig work that cited only one side). **Evidence:** A citation map of your essay's references grouped by strand, with a revision note on the gaps you filled before submission. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991)*
        - *Applied: Evidence-backed recommendation for a sports club.* You prepare a recommendation for a real decision and show the range and quality of the evidence behind it (for example, a sport science student on placement at a rugby club advising on hamstring injury prevention, citing systematic reviews, a governing body guideline and the club's own injury data). You explain to the reader why that evidence base is enough for the decision, and where it is thin. **Evidence:** The recommendation paper with its evidence summary table, and the coach's feedback on whether it informed the decision. *(Grounding: professional: Shows the range and quality of the evidence behind a recommendation.; professional: ICAI Fundamental Values (2021): trust)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Information and sources (direct; lands on `referencing#d2`, `referencing/c3`)
- Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect; lands on `referencing/02`)

### Advanced

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | Uphold honesty and trust in academic work | L5 | 4.12.b8 "the exercise of personal responsibility" | direct | `referencing/03` |
| 8 | Uphold fairness, respect, responsibility and courage in academic conduct | L6 | 4.15.b9.1 "the exercise of initiative and personal responsibility" | direct | `referencing/04` |
| 9 | Carry integrity into graduate and professional identity | L7 | 4.17.b6 "at a professional or equivalent level" | direct | `referencing#e3` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

7. **Uphold honesty and trust in academic work**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Ethics, integrity and values (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Weighing an ethical issue raised by a weak result.* **Learning outcome:** The student can justify a course of action when a weak or disappointing result raises an ethical choice between honest reporting and presenting a stronger impression.
          Faced with a result that is weaker or messier than expected, you set out the ethical issue it raises: whether to present it as stronger than it is, to quietly drop it, or to report it honestly with its limitations explained. You reason through each option against the responsibility to let a reader trust what you report, and you justify the choice you make rather than simply following the easiest path available. **Evidence:** A short reasoned note setting out the ethical issue raised by a weak result and the choice made. *(Grounding: model: ICAI Fundamental Values of Academic Integrity (ICAI 2021); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/ethics)*
        - *Applied: Acting on an ethical responsibility to report accurately.* **Learning outcome:** The student can justify acting to correct an unsupported figure or claim in shared work, upholding honesty even where the error would otherwise go unnoticed.
          You notice that a figure or claim in a piece of shared work is not well supported, and you recognise this as an ethical responsibility rather than a minor detail to let pass. You raise it, explain why it matters, and help correct the record, even though staying quiet would have been easier and probably gone unnoticed. This shows acting on an ethical responsibility rather than only recognising that one exists. **Evidence:** A record of the issue raised, the correction made, and confirmation from someone else involved. *(Grounding: professional: Reports work, results and sources honestly so that colleagues and clients can rely on them.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/ethics)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Analysing one honesty decision against a values framework.* You write a short case analysis of one integrity decision in your own work, using a framework that names honesty, trust, fairness, respect, responsibility and courage as fundamental values. You focus on honesty and trust: what you did when a result was weaker than expected or a source could not be verified, and how that choice protected a reader's ability to rely on your work afterwards. **Evidence:** A case analysis of one integrity decision, with the relevant extract, linked to honesty and trust. *(Grounding: model: ICAI Fundamental Values of Academic Integrity (ICAI 2021))*
        - *Applied: Correcting an overstated figure before it was relied on.* In a piece of work, you report something honestly when it would have been easier not to, such as telling a team that a figure in a shared document was overstated and correcting it before anyone relied on it. You record what you reported and why, showing that you understand colleagues and clients need to be able to trust what you tell them, even when the honest answer is inconvenient to deliver. **Evidence:** A corrected record or message, with a short note confirming what happened and when. *(Grounding: professional: Reports work, results and sources honestly so that colleagues and clients can rely on them.; professional: Concordat to Support Research Integrity: honesty, transparency and open communication)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Case analysis of an honesty decision in your own work.* You write a case analysis of one integrity decision in your own work, using the ICAI Fundamental Values of Academic Integrity (ICAI 2021), which name honesty, trust, fairness, respect, responsibility and courage. You focus on honesty and trust: what you did when a result was weaker than expected or a source could not be checked, and how your choice protected the reader's ability to rely on your work (for example, a chemistry lab report with an anomalous result). **Evidence:** A case analysis of one integrity decision, with the relevant extract from your submitted work, linked to honesty and trust. *(Grounding: model: ICAI Fundamental Values of Academic Integrity (ICAI 2021))*
        - *Applied: Correcting an overstated figure during an internship.* In a workplace or project, you report work honestly when it would be easier not to (for example, a computing student on a summer internship telling the team that test coverage figures in a release note were overstated, and correcting them). You record what you reported and why, showing you understand that colleagues and clients need to be able to rely on what you say. **Evidence:** The corrected record or message to the team, with a short note from your line manager confirming what happened. *(Grounding: professional: Reports work, results and sources honestly so that colleagues and clients can rely on them.; professional: Concordat to Support Research Integrity: honesty, transparency and open communication)*
8. **Uphold fairness, respect, responsibility and courage in academic conduct**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Ethics, integrity and values (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Reasoning through a fairness issue in shared work.* **Learning outcome:** The student can justify a response to a fairness issue in shared work, weighing the discomfort of speaking up against the responsibility that courage requires.
          You identify a fairness issue in a piece of shared work, such as one contributor's input going unacknowledged, and reason through what fairness, respect and responsibility require of you as the person who noticed it. You weigh the discomfort of raising the issue against the responsibility to act, and set out the course of action that courage requires, even where staying silent would be the easier choice. **Evidence:** A short reasoned note analysing a fairness issue against the values of respect, responsibility and courage. *(Grounding: model: ICAI Fundamental Values of Academic Integrity (ICAI 2021); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/ethics)*
        - *Applied: Acting with courage to correct an unfair outcome.* **Learning outcome:** The student can raise an unacknowledged contribution with those involved, treating them with respect, and help secure a fairer outcome even where the conversation is uncomfortable.
          You notice that someone's contribution to shared work has gone unacknowledged, and you recognise this as an ethical responsibility rather than someone else's problem to solve. You raise the issue with the people involved, treating them with respect even where the conversation is uncomfortable, and you help put in place a fairer outcome. This shows acting on an ethical responsibility for fairness rather than only noticing that one exists. **Evidence:** A record of the fairness issue raised, the response from those involved, and the outcome agreed. *(Grounding: professional: Treats colleagues' work fairly and with respect, takes responsibility for own work and raises concerns when something is wrong.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/ethics)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Analysing group conduct against four integrity values.* You analyse a group project against four values named in an integrity framework: fairness, respect, responsibility and courage. You set out how credit for ideas was shared, how you responded when someone else's work was presented without full credit, what you owned when something went wrong, and whether you raised a concern you could have let pass, judging where your conduct met each value and where it fell short. **Evidence:** A structured analysis of group conduct against four values, with evidence such as meeting notes. *(Grounding: model: ICAI Fundamental Values of Academic Integrity (ICAI 2021))*
        - *Applied: Raising a concern about an uncredited shared resource.* In a group or team setting, you take responsibility for the fair treatment of other people's work and raise a concern when something is wrong, such as noticing that a shared resource is being used without its creator's permission. You help agree a fair fix, and you record the steps taken and how you treated the people involved with respect throughout, rather than letting the issue drop because raising it felt uncomfortable. **Evidence:** A dated record of the concern raised, the response and the outcome, with confirmation from someone involved. *(Grounding: professional: Treats colleagues' work fairly and with respect, takes responsibility for own work and raises concerns when something is wrong.; professional: ICAI Fundamental Values (2021): fairness, respect, responsibility, courage)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Group project conduct analysed against four integrity values.* You analyse a group project against four ICAI (2021) values: fairness, respect, responsibility and courage. You set out how credit was shared, how you responded to others' ideas, what you owned when things went wrong, and whether you raised a concern you could have ignored (for example, an architecture studio group where one member's drawings were presented without credit). You judge where your conduct met each value and where it fell short. **Evidence:** A structured analysis of your group project conduct against the four values, with evidence such as meeting notes or contribution records. *(Grounding: model: ICAI Fundamental Values of Academic Integrity (ICAI 2021))*
        - *Applied: Raising a concern about an uncredited shared resource.* In a society role, placement or job, you take responsibility for fair treatment of other people's work and raise a concern when something is wrong (for example, as a course representative in education studies, you tell staff that a revision guide is being shared without its author's permission, and help agree a fair fix). You record the steps you took and how you treated the people involved with respect. **Evidence:** A dated record of the concern raised, the response and the outcome, with confirmation from a staff member or society officer. *(Grounding: professional: Treats colleagues' work fairly and with respect, takes responsibility for own work and raises concerns when something is wrong.; professional: ICAI Fundamental Values (2021): fairness, respect, responsibility, courage)*
9. **Carry integrity into graduate and professional identity**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Ethics, integrity and values (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Recognising which ethical habits carry into a profession.* **Learning outcome:** The student can critique their own habits of honesty and attribution against the ethical expectations of a profession, identifying where those expectations will tighten.
          You examine your own habits around honesty, attribution and care for other people's work, and ask which of these count as ethical responsibilities that a profession will also expect of you, rather than rules that applied only to assessed coursework. You identify where the expectations will tighten, such as higher stakes for a dishonest claim, and where your existing habits already meet what the role will require of you. **Evidence:** A short written comparison of personal ethical habits against the expectations of a named professional role. *(Grounding: model: Communities of practice (Wenger 1998); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/ethics)*
        - *Applied: Carrying an ethical responsibility into a new professional role.* **Learning outcome:** The student can recognise and act on an ethical responsibility in a new professional role without being asked, and explain the habit to someone newer than themselves.
          Starting in a role where your work affects other people for the first time, you recognise an ethical responsibility you previously only met for assessment purposes, such as checking claims before passing them on, and you act on it without being asked. You explain the habit to someone newer than you, showing that the responsibility has become part of how you work rather than a rule you follow only when checked. **Evidence:** A short account of the ethical habit carried into the new role, with a note from someone who observed it. *(Grounding: framework: ICAI Fundamental Values (2021): honesty, trust; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/ethics)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Mapping personal integrity habits onto a professional code.* You write a short statement linking your own record of academic integrity to the code of conduct of a profession you intend to enter, drawing on the idea that learning within a community shapes identity as well as knowledge. You identify which of your current habits, such as careful attribution and honest reporting, carry across into that code, which will need to change, and what the profession will expect of you that your current habits do not yet cover. **Evidence:** A professional identity statement mapping integrity practices onto a named professional code. *(Grounding: model: Communities of practice (Wenger 1998))*
        - *Applied: Setting up an integrity routine for a team you join.* In a setting where you hold real responsibility for the first time, you set up a source-checking and attribution routine for the team's work, then teach it to someone newer than you. You treat this as carrying your integrity commitment into how you work as a professional, not only as something that applied to assessed work, and you can point to the routine being used by someone other than yourself. **Evidence:** The written routine, examples of its use, and a note confirming someone else adopted it. *(Grounding: framework: ICAI Fundamental Values (2021): honesty, trust; framework: Concordat to Support Research Integrity: accountability)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Professional identity statement linking integrity to a code.* You write a professional identity statement that links your record of academic integrity to the code of conduct of the profession you are entering, drawing on Wenger's (1998) account of communities of practice, in which learning shapes identity as well as knowledge. You identify which habits carry across, which change, and what the profession will expect of you (for example, a postgraduate pharmacy student mapping referencing and data honesty to the GPhC standards for pharmacy professionals). **Evidence:** A professional identity statement mapping your integrity practices to a named professional code, suitable for a portfolio or interview. *(Grounding: model: Communities of practice (Wenger 1998))*
        - *Applied: Source-checking routine set up for a policy team.* You carry an integrity commitment into a setting where you hold real responsibility (for example, a master's student in social research on a policy internship who sets up a source-checking and attribution routine for the team's briefings, then trains a new intern in it). You show that integrity is now part of how you work as a professional, not only how you meet assessment rules. **Evidence:** The routine or protocol you wrote, examples of its use in briefings, and a reference or note from your supervisor. *(Grounding: practice: postgraduate policy internship)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Ethics, integrity and values (direct; lands on `referencing/03`, `referencing/04`, `referencing#e3`)
- Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect; lands on `referencing#e3`)

<a id="criticality"></a>
## Criticality

### Introductory

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Map and question the scholarly discourse around a topic | L3 | l3/knowledge.4 "awareness of different perspectives or approaches" | **UNATTRIBUTED** | `criticality/02` |
| 2 | Apply critical thought to the choices made when searching, reading and planning | L4 | l4/knowledge.2 "Analyse, interpret and evaluate relevant information and ideas." | direct | `criticality/c1` |
| 3 | Treat being critical as a habit of mind, not a switch for assessment | L5 | 4.12.b4 "an understanding of the limits of their knowledge" | direct | `criticality#i3` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

1. **Map and question the scholarly discourse around a topic**
    - *Benchmark:* none matched at this tier (**UNATTRIBUTED**)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Critical map of positions within a field of debate.* **Learning outcome:** The student can describe the main positions within a scholarly debate and identify which questions about it remain unresolved.
          You take a topic currently debated within a field and build a map of the main positions people take, noting where they draw on evidence and where they simply assert a view. For each position you write down at least one question that is still open, rather than treating the debate as already settled. This demonstrates mapping a body of discourse and interrogating it rather than only summarising what has been said. **Evidence:** A short written map of positions, each tagged with an open question still to resolve. *(Grounding: ing:doc:delphi-report-facione-1990; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
        - *Applied: Questioning the state of a field before acting.* **Learning outcome:** The student can map the key positions in a field of debate and note the open questions before acting on any of them.
          Before committing to a course of action, you first establish what is already being said within the relevant field: who holds which position, where views converge, and where they conflict. You note the questions that remain unresolved so that any decision you make is built on an accurate picture rather than on one persuasive source. This demonstrates mapping a discourse and questioning it before acting on it. **Evidence:** A short note identifying key positions in a field and the questions still open, used to inform a decision. *(Grounding: professional: Scans a field, market or policy area to find its key players, positions and open questions before acting.; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Discourse map tagged with critical being domains.* You build a short map of the positions taken within a debate on a topic, grouping sources by the stance they argue and adding one open question to each group. You tag each question as aimed at the claim itself, at who it affects, or at an assumption you noticed yourself making. This shows you can map a body of discourse and interrogate it, rather than simply listing where the material came from. **Evidence:** An annotated map with grouped positions, one question per group, and a short key explaining the tags. *(Grounding: ing:doc:critical-being-barnett-1997)*
        - *Applied: Mapping sources and stakeholders before acting on an issue.* You are asked to find out what is being said about an issue before any decision is made on it. You map the main voices, such as published reports, a briefing and a couple of research articles, note where they agree and disagree, and write down the questions they leave open. This shows you can identify who is saying what about a topic and question it before anyone acts on it. **Evidence:** A briefing note mapping sources and positions, with written sign-off from whoever requested it. *(Grounding: professional: Scans a field, market or policy area to find its key players, positions and open questions before acting.; professional: ESCO thinking skills: acquiring and managing information)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Discourse map tagged with Barnett's domains of critical being.* You build a one-page map of the scholarly debate on a topic, grouping authors by the position they take and adding one question to each group. Using Barnett's (1997) idea of critical being across knowledge, self and world, you tag each question as aimed at the claim itself, at who it affects, or at what you had assumed. This shows you can map a discourse and question it, not just list sources (for example, three positions on screen time and early language development in education studies). **Evidence:** An annotated one-page discourse map with author groups, a question on each group and a short key explaining the Barnett tags. *(Grounding: ing:doc:critical-being-barnett-1997)*
        - *Applied: Source and stakeholder map for a food bank coordinator.* You volunteer with a local food bank and the coordinator wants to know what is being said about food insecurity among students. You map the main voices, such as university reports, a charity briefing and two research articles, note where they agree and disagree, and write down the questions they leave open. This shows you can find who is saying what and question it before anyone acts (for example, asking whether the published figures include part-time and commuting students). **Evidence:** A two-page briefing note with a map of sources and positions, signed off by the volunteer coordinator. *(Grounding: professional: Scans a field, market or policy area to find its key players, positions and open questions before acting.; professional: ESCO thinking skills: acquiring and managing information)*
2. **Apply critical thought to the choices made when searching, reading and planning**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Judgement reached through weighing conflicting evidence.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain how weighing the strength and relevance of conflicting evidence leads to a reasoned judgement rather than a quick preference.
          You are given two pieces of evidence that point in different directions and, instead of picking the one you find easiest to believe, you set out the strength and relevance of each, then explain which argument the evidence best supports. You state the judgement you reach and the reasoning that gets you there, so someone else could follow and test it. This demonstrates evaluating evidence and arguments to reach a reasoned judgement. **Evidence:** A short written judgement setting out the evidence weighed, the reasoning used and the conclusion reached. *(Grounding: ing:doc:delphi-report-facione-1990; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
        - *Applied: Recommendation after weighing the evidence.* **Learning outcome:** The student can identify which of two conflicting pieces of evidence better supports a course of action and state the reasoning behind that choice.
          You are asked to recommend a course of action and given information that does not all point the same way. You weigh the strength and relevance of each piece before making your recommendation, state which evidence carries most weight and why, and note what would change your mind. This demonstrates evaluating evidence and arguments critically to reach a judgement someone else can act on. **Evidence:** A short recommendation stating the evidence weighed, the judgement reached and what would change it. *(Grounding: professional: Weighs competing evidence or arguments before recommending a course of action.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Decision log tagged with critical-thinking dispositions.* Before starting a piece of work, you keep a log of each choice you make: the terms you search, the filters you apply, and which sources you read, and in what order. Beside each choice you note which disposition from an established critical-thinking framework it reflects, such as open-mindedness or diligence in seeking relevant information. This shows critical thought operating before any writing begins. **Evidence:** A decision log of about ten entries, each tagged with a named disposition and a one-line reason. *(Grounding: ing:doc:delphi-report-facione-1990)*
        - *Applied: Planning note questioning a task's brief.* You are given a piece of work to complete and, before gathering anything, you write down its scope, the sources you intend to trust, and the assumptions built into how the task was given to you. This shows you apply critical thought to searching and planning from the outset, rather than only when something goes wrong, and you can point to what you flagged and why. **Evidence:** A short planning note listing scope, chosen sources and flagged assumptions, with written comment from whoever set the task. *(Grounding: professional: Questions the sources, scope and assumptions of a piece of work from the start, not only at review.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Search and reading decision log tagged with Delphi dispositions.* While preparing a first-year essay, you keep a log of each choice you make: the search terms, the filters, which sources you read and in what order. Beside each choice you note which disposition from Facione's (1990) Delphi report it reflects, such as open-mindedness or diligence in seeking relevant information. This shows critical thought at work before any writing starts (for example, in sport science, choosing to read a study that challenges the textbook view on static stretching). **Evidence:** A search and reading decision log of about ten entries, each tagged with a Delphi disposition and a one-line reason. *(Grounding: ing:doc:delphi-report-facione-1990)*
        - *Applied: Planning sheet questioning a manager's brief at a café.* In a part-time café job, your manager asks you to find out whether switching to compostable cups is worth it. Before collecting anything, you write down the scope of the question, the sources you will trust, such as council guidance and an independent review, and the assumptions you notice in the brief. This shows you apply critical thought to searching and planning from the start (for example, noting that the most persuasive figures come from the cup supplier itself). **Evidence:** A one-page planning sheet listing scope, chosen sources and flagged assumptions, with your manager's written comment on it. *(Grounding: professional: Questions the sources, scope and assumptions of a piece of work from the start, not only at review.)*
3. **Treat being critical as a habit of mind, not a switch for assessment**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Habitual evaluation of everyday evidence and claims.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain why evaluating evidence and arguments critically should be a standing habit rather than a response reserved for assessment.
          You adopt a standing habit of evaluating evidence and arguments wherever you meet them, not only when a piece of work is being judged. When you notice a claim, whether in a conversation, a report or a passing comment, you pause to ask what evidence supports it and whether the reasoning holds up before accepting or acting on it. This demonstrates evaluating evidence and arguments critically as an ongoing disposition rather than a switch turned on for assessment. **Evidence:** A short written account of a claim you queried, the evidence you checked, and the judgement you reached. *(Grounding: model: Gibbs' Reflective Cycle (Gibbs 1988); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
        - *Applied: Evaluating claims as routine professional practice.* **Learning outcome:** The student can routinely question the evidence behind everyday claims and judge whether the reasoning holds up before relying on them.
          In your day-to-day work, you treat evaluating evidence and arguments as a standing habit rather than a step you perform only when a piece of work is being reviewed. Whenever a claim is made, a figure is quoted or a plan is proposed, you ask what evidence backs it and whether the reasoning stands up before you rely on it. This demonstrates reaching reasoned judgements through critical evaluation as an everyday working habit, not an occasional exercise. **Evidence:** A brief record of a claim checked during routine work, the evidence considered, and the judgement reached. *(Grounding: professional: Treats evaluating evidence and arguments as a standing habit built into everyday work, not reserved for formal review.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Weekly reflections on everyday critical questioning.* Over several weeks you write short reflections on moments when you questioned something outside any formal assessment: a claim in conversation, a statistic doing the rounds online, an assumption behind a decision. Working through each stage of a recognised reflective cycle, you ask why you questioned it and what you would do differently next time. This shows being critical becoming an ordinary habit of mind rather than something switched on only for marked work. **Evidence:** Several dated reflective entries following a recognised cycle, with a closing paragraph on what has changed. *(Grounding: model: Gibbs' Reflective Cycle (Gibbs 1988))*
        - *Applied: Raising questions within an ordinary team meeting.* As a member of a group you belong to, you start questioning its everyday claims: figures quoted in updates, assumptions behind plans, statements made in passing. You raise these questions in routine meetings where nothing is being formally assessed, and you check claims before anyone acts on them. This shows criticality operating as a standing habit in ordinary settings, not only when work is being marked. **Evidence:** Meeting notes showing the questions you raised, any checks you ran, and what changed as a result. *(Grounding: professional: Treats everyday claims and routine decisions as worth questioning, not only formally assessed work.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Weekly reflections on everyday questioning using Gibbs' Reflective Cycle.* Over four weeks you write short reflections, using Gibbs' (1988) Reflective Cycle, on moments when you questioned something outside assessment: a news story, a claim in a lecture, a statistic in a group chat. The analysis and conclusion stages show you asking why you questioned it and what you would do next time. This shows being critical becoming a habit, not something saved for marked work (for example, a psychology student questioning a viral post about learning styles). **Evidence:** Four dated reflective log entries following the six stages of Gibbs' cycle, with a closing paragraph on what has changed. *(Grounding: model: Gibbs' Reflective Cycle (Gibbs 1988))*
        - *Applied: Questions raised as a student society committee member.* As a society committee member, you start questioning the society's own habits: claims in recruitment posts, figures in the treasurer's report, assumptions behind event plans. You raise these questions at ordinary meetings where nothing is being assessed, and you check claims before they are acted on. This shows criticality as a habit of mind in everyday settings (for example, asking where the claim that most members prefer evening events came from, then running a quick poll). **Evidence:** Meeting minutes showing the questions you raised, the poll results and a short note on the decisions that changed. *(Grounding: practice: student society committee)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (direct; lands on `criticality/c1`, `criticality#i3`)

### Intermediate

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 4 | Recognise the critical thinking skills of analysis and evaluation | L4 | 4.10.b1 "an ability to evaluate and interpret these" | direct | `criticality/01` |
| 5 | Analyse and evaluate a scholarly interpretation | L5 | 4.12.b5 "undertake critical analysis of information" | direct | `criticality/03` |
| 6 | Read a journal article strategically by its structure and method | L6 | 4.15.b5 "make use of scholarly reviews and primary sources"; 4.15.b3.2 "describe and comment upon particular aspects of current research" | direct | `criticality/04` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

4. **Recognise the critical thinking skills of analysis and evaluation**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 1.20: Course content emphasises critical thinking, ethics, evidence-based decision making, and collaboration (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 2.5: critically evaluating the ethical behaviour of others (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.16: critically and reflectively evaluate psychological theory and research (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 4.10: demonstrated the use of critical-thinking skills and the ability to evaluate the rigour (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.32: lead to the progressive development of transferable skills and competencies, such as of critical thinking (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Judging the rigour of a piece of work.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse a piece of work by distinguishing what was done from how rigorously it was carried out, then evaluate each separately.
          Given a piece of work to review, you first separate the analytical task, working out what was done and how the parts fit together, from the evaluative task, judging how rigorously it was carried out. You examine the method or reasoning behind the work and state how far it holds up, rather than simply describing what the work contains. This demonstrates the critical thinking skills of analysis and evaluation applied to judging rigour. **Evidence:** A short written judgement on a piece of work, separating the analysis from the evaluation of its rigour. *(Grounding: ing:doc:bloom-anderson-krathwohl-2001; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/4.10)*
        - *Applied: Assessing the rigour of a piece of work.* **Learning outcome:** The student can evaluate the rigour of a piece of work by analysing its method before judging how far its conclusions can be trusted.
          You are asked to check whether a piece of work can be relied on. You analyse how it was carried out, what was measured and how, before judging how rigorous that method actually is and what confidence it supports. You report your judgement separately from your analysis so that others can see how you reached it. This demonstrates critical thinking skills of analysis and evaluation used to judge the rigour of real work. **Evidence:** A short report analysing how a piece of work was carried out and judging how rigorous it is. *(Grounding: professional: Recognises when a brief asks for analysis, for a judgement between options, or for both.; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/4.10)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Past questions classified by analysis or evaluation.* You collect eight past tasks or questions you have encountered and classify each by the process it demands, separating analysis, breaking something down, comparing it, organising its parts, from evaluation, checking it, critiquing it, judging it. You justify each classification from the words used to set the task and flag any that need both processes. This shows you can recognise analysis and evaluation when a task calls for them, even when the wording is not explicit. **Evidence:** A table of eight tasks showing the wording, the process identified, your justification, and two borderline cases noted. *(Grounding: ing:doc:bloom-anderson-krathwohl-2001; ing:doc:delphi-report-facione-1990)*
        - *Applied: Sorting requests into analysis or judgement.* You receive a handful of requests before starting work on them. For each one you note whether it is asking for analysis, working out how something functions and why it fails, for a judgement between options, or for both, and you confirm your reading with whoever set the request. This shows you can recognise analysis and evaluation even when the brief does not use formal academic wording. **Evidence:** Your annotated copies of several requests, plus a short exchange confirming what each one actually asked for. *(Grounding: professional: Recognises when a brief asks for analysis, for a judgement between options, or for both.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Past questions classified by revised Bloom's taxonomy.* You collect eight past essay and exam questions from your programme and classify each by the process it demands in Anderson and Krathwohl's (2001) revised Bloom's taxonomy, separating analyse (break down, compare, organise) from evaluate (check, critique, judge). You justify each classification from the steer words and flag questions that need both. This shows you can recognise analysis and evaluation when a task asks for them (for example, in law, 'analyse the reasoning in Donoghue v Stevenson' against 'to what extent was the decision justified'). **Evidence:** A table of eight questions showing the steer word, the Bloom process, your justification and a tutor's comment on two borderline cases. *(Grounding: ing:doc:bloom-anderson-krathwohl-2001; ing:doc:delphi-report-facione-1990)*
        - *Applied: Annotated placement requests sorted into analysis or judgement.* On an engineering placement you receive three requests from your supervisor. Before starting, you note for each whether it asks for analysis (how a system works and why it fails), a judgement between options, or both, and you confirm your reading with your supervisor. This shows you recognise analysis and evaluation when the brief does not use academic steer words (for example, 'can you look into the pump' turning out to mean 'recommend which replacement to buy'). **Evidence:** Your annotated copies of the three requests and a short email exchange confirming what each one asked for. *(Grounding: professional: Recognises when a brief asks for analysis, for a judgement between options, or for both.; professional: Revised Bloom's taxonomy: Analyse, Evaluate)*
5. **Analyse and evaluate a scholarly interpretation**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 2.5: critically evaluating the ethical behaviour of others (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.16: critically and reflectively evaluate psychological theory and research (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 4.10: demonstrated the use of critical-thinking skills and the ability to evaluate the rigour (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Evaluating theory and research with reflection.* **Learning outcome:** The student can evaluate a theoretical interpretation by analysing the evidence and assumptions it rests on and reflecting on their own standpoint.
          You take a piece of theory or published research and evaluate it critically, examining the evidence it rests on and the assumptions built into it, then reflect on how your own background might shape your reading of it. You reach a judgement on how far the interpretation is supported and note what further evidence would strengthen or weaken it. This demonstrates critically and reflectively evaluating theory and research rather than simply reporting what it claims. **Evidence:** A short evaluation of a theory or study, noting assumptions, supporting evidence, and what would strengthen the judgement. *(Grounding: ing:doc:delphi-report-facione-1990; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.16)*
        - *Applied: Reflective evaluation of research behind a proposal.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse the research behind a proposal and evaluate how far the evidence supports it before reaching a justified conclusion.
          Given a proposal that rests on a particular theory or body of research, you evaluate how well the evidence supports it, reflecting on what assumptions the proposal takes for granted and where your own standpoint might affect your judgement. You set out your conclusion and the reasoning behind it so that someone else could check your thinking. This demonstrates critically and reflectively evaluating theory and research when acting on a real proposal. **Evidence:** A short evaluation of the research behind a proposal, with your conclusion and the reasoning that supports it. *(Grounding: professional: Analyses an argument, report or proposal and weighs it against the alternatives.; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.16)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Critique of an interpretation using core analytic skills.* You take one interpretation of a body of evidence and work through it using three core skills: analysis, to identify its claims and the evidence behind them; evaluation, to judge how strong its reasoning is; and inference, to say what follows from it and what the author leaves out. You then state your own view on how far the interpretation holds up. This shows you can weigh an interpretation rather than simply summarising it. **Evidence:** A short critique setting out the interpretation's claims, your judgement of its strengths and weaknesses, and your inference. *(Grounding: ing:doc:delphi-report-facione-1990; ing:doc:bloom-anderson-krathwohl-2001)*
        - *Applied: Memo weighing a report against the alternatives.* You are given a report that makes a claim in favour of one option, and you are asked whether it should be adopted. You analyse its claims, its sample and its method, then weigh it against the other options under consideration and state which is best supported by the evidence. This shows you can analyse and evaluate an argument made by someone else to support a real decision. **Evidence:** A one-page memo comparing a report with the alternatives, including written feedback on the decision taken. *(Grounding: professional: Analyses an argument, report or proposal and weighs it against the alternatives.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Critique of a historian's interpretation using Delphi core skills.* You take one scholarly interpretation and work through it with three core skills from Facione's (1990) Delphi report: analysis, to identify its claims and evidence; evaluation, to judge the strength of its reasoning; and inference, to say what follows and what the author leaves out. You then reach your own view on how far it holds. This shows you can weigh an interpretation, not just summarise it (for example, a historian's argument that the 1834 Poor Law was driven chiefly by cost). **Evidence:** A two-page critique sheet mapping the interpretation's argument, your judgement of its strengths and weaknesses, and your inference, with sources cited. *(Grounding: ing:doc:delphi-report-facione-1990; ing:doc:bloom-anderson-krathwohl-2001)*
        - *Applied: Memo weighing a vendor's wellbeing report against alternatives.* On a placement with a school's pastoral team, you are given a supplier's report claiming its wellbeing app reduces pupil anxiety. You analyse its claims, sample and method, then weigh it against two other options the school is considering, and say which is best supported. This shows you can analyse and evaluate an argument made by someone else for a real decision (for example, noting that the report's only study was funded by the supplier and had no comparison group). **Evidence:** A one-page memo comparing the report with two alternatives, with your placement supervisor's written feedback. *(Grounding: professional: Analyses an argument, report or proposal and weighs it against the alternatives.)*
6. **Read a journal article strategically by its structure and method**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Verifying findings by reading the method first.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse a study's method ahead of its findings to judge whether the approach actually supports the conclusions drawn.
          Rather than accepting a reported finding at face value, you read the method section of a piece of research before anything else, checking how the evidence was gathered and whether the approach actually supports the conclusion drawn. Only once you trust the method do you read the wider argument and reach your own judgement on the evidence presented. This demonstrates evaluating evidence and arguments critically by reading strategically rather than passively. **Evidence:** A short note on a study's method, stating whether the approach actually supports its reported conclusion. *(Grounding: ing:doc:delphi-report-facione-1990; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
        - *Applied: Checking a study's method before acting on it.* **Learning outcome:** The student can evaluate whether a published study's method is strong enough to support a decision before relying on its reported findings.
          Before relying on a published study to support a decision, you read its method and results closely, checking how the work was carried out and on what it was tested, then judge whether the approach is strong enough to support the claim. You report your judgement so others can see the reasoning, rather than passing on the headline finding uncritically. This demonstrates evaluating evidence and arguments critically to reach a reasoned judgement. **Evidence:** A short written judgement on whether a study's method is strong enough to support a decision based on it. *(Grounding: professional: Reads research and technical reports selectively and checks their method before relying on their findings.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Reading route recorded method first across articles.* You read several articles by their structure rather than front to back: abstract and conclusion first, then the method, then the results, returning to the introduction only if you still need it. For each method section you apply core skills of interpretation and evaluation to decide how far you can trust the findings. This shows you choose how to read strategically rather than working through every article in order. **Evidence:** A reading record for several articles showing the order read, a method summary, and a verdict on reliability. *(Grounding: ing:doc:delphi-report-facione-1990)*
        - *Applied: Method check before relying on a published study.* You are asked whether a published study supports a particular decision. You go straight to the method and results, check how the work was carried out and on what it was tested, and only then read the discussion. You brief whoever asked on whether the method actually justifies the claim being made. This shows you can check a study's method before relying on its findings. **Evidence:** Your annotated copy of a study with method notes, plus the summary you gave to whoever asked. *(Grounding: professional: Reads research and technical reports selectively and checks their method before relying on their findings.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Reading route record for three articles, method first.* You read three journal articles by their structure: abstract and conclusion first, then the method, then the results, and only then the introduction if you need it. For each method section you apply the Delphi skills of interpretation and evaluation (Facione 1990) to decide how far the findings can be trusted. This shows you choose how to read rather than reading front to back (for example, in nursing, checking whether a falls-prevention trial randomised wards or individual patients). **Evidence:** A reading record for three articles showing the order you read sections, a method summary and a verdict on how far you rely on each. *(Grounding: ing:doc:delphi-report-facione-1990)*
        - *Applied: Method check of a published study during a software internship.* During a summer internship in a software team, you are asked whether a published study supports adopting a new testing tool. You go straight to the method and results, check how the tool was tested and on what kind of code, and only then read the discussion. You brief the team on whether the method justifies the claim (for example, noting that the study used small open-source projects quite unlike your team's large, older system). **Evidence:** Your annotated copy of the study with method notes, plus the short written summary or slide you gave the team. *(Grounding: professional: Reads research and technical reports selectively and checks their method before relying on their findings.)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Psychology 1.20: Course content emphasises critical thinking, ethics, evidence-based decision making, and collaboration (direct; lands on `criticality/01`)
- Psychology 2.5: critically evaluating the ethical behaviour of others (direct; lands on `criticality/01`, `criticality/03`)
- Psychology 3.16: critically and reflectively evaluate psychological theory and research (direct; lands on `criticality/01`, `criticality/03`)
- Psychology 4.10: demonstrated the use of critical-thinking skills and the ability to evaluate the rigour (direct; lands on `criticality/01`, `criticality/03`)
- Psychology 3.32: lead to the progressive development of transferable skills and competencies, such as of critical thinking (direct; lands on `criticality/01`)
- Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (direct; lands on `criticality/01`, `criticality/03`, `criticality/04`)

### Advanced

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | Form and justify judgements on the relative importance of factors | L5 | 4.12.b3 "evaluate critically the appropriateness of different approaches" | direct | `criticality/c2` |
| 8 | Write critically, synthesising sources in the discipline's vocabulary | L6 | 4.15.b3.1 "to devise and sustain arguments"; l6/knowledge.2 "Critically analyse, interpret and evaluate complex information, concepts and ideas." | direct | `criticality/05` |
| 9 | Write with authority, aware of how own stance shapes the argument | L7 | l7/knowledge.2 "to produce modified conceptions"; 4.17.b4.1 "to evaluate critically current research and advanced scholarship in the discipline" | direct | `criticality#e3` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

7. **Form and justify judgements on the relative importance of factors**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Reasoned ranking of competing factors.* **Learning outcome:** The student can justify a ranking of competing factors by weighing the evidence and arguments for each and keeping the reasoning open to challenge.
          Given several factors that could explain an outcome, you weigh the evidence and arguments for each one critically, then rank them by relative importance and justify the order you reach. You state the reasoning behind your ranking clearly enough that someone else could follow it and challenge any step. This demonstrates evaluating evidence and arguments critically to reach a reasoned judgement on which factors matter most. **Evidence:** A short written ranking of competing factors with reasons, open to challenge at each step. *(Grounding: ing:doc:delphi-report-facione-1990; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
