        - *Applied: Recommending priorities after weighing the factors.* **Learning outcome:** The student can construct a recommendation that names the most important factor, justifies that priority, and states what evidence would change the judgement.
          Facing several possible explanations for a problem, you weigh the evidence and arguments behind each one and reach a judgement on which factor matters most, stating clearly what led you there. You put this judgement into a recommendation that names what should be addressed first and what evidence would change your mind. This demonstrates evaluating evidence and arguments critically to reach a reasoned judgement that someone else can act on. **Evidence:** A short recommendation naming the priority factor, the reasoning behind it, and what would change the judgement. *(Grounding: professional: Recommends one option over others and states why, so that a decision can be taken on it.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Ranked factors justified against explicit criteria.* You rank the factors that explain an outcome and justify the order against criteria you state in advance, such as the strength of the evidence, the size of the effect and its scope. You work at the level of judging each factor against those criteria rather than simply listing them, and you say what evidence would change your ranking. This shows you can form and justify a judgement on relative importance rather than present an unweighted list. **Evidence:** A ranked table of factors with stated criteria, a short justification, and a note on what would change it. *(Grounding: ing:doc:bloom-anderson-krathwohl-2001; ing:doc:delphi-report-facione-1990)*
        - *Applied: Recommending which factor to address first.* You investigate why an outcome has changed for the worse, weigh the possible causes against each other, and recommend which one should be tackled first, stating why and what you set aside. This shows you can form and justify a judgement that someone else will act on, rather than listing causes without ranking them. You make clear what evidence supports your recommendation and what would change it. **Evidence:** A recommendation report with ranked causes and reasons, plus a written response on the decision taken. *(Grounding: professional: Recommends one option over others and states why, so that a decision can be taken on it.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Ranked factor judgement built on explicit evaluation criteria.* You rank the factors that explain an outcome and justify the order against criteria you state in advance, such as strength of evidence, size of effect and scope. In Anderson and Krathwohl's (2001) terms you work at the level of evaluate, checking and critiquing each factor against those criteria rather than listing them. You also say what evidence would change your ranking (for example, in geography, ranking rainfall, land use and drainage as causes of repeated flooding in one town). **Evidence:** A ranked factor table with criteria and weightings, a 300-word justification and a note on what evidence would change the ranking. *(Grounding: ing:doc:bloom-anderson-krathwohl-2001; ing:doc:delphi-report-facione-1990)*
        - *Applied: Live brief recommendation on which factor to tackle first.* On a live brief for a small business, you investigate why repeat customers have fallen, weigh the possible causes, and recommend which one the owner should tackle first, stating why and what you set aside. This shows you can form and justify a judgement someone will act on (for example, for an independent bike shop, judging from complaints and sales data that slow repair turnaround matters more than price). **Evidence:** A recommendation report with ranked factors and reasons, and the client's written response on the decision they took. *(Grounding: professional: Recommends one option over others and states why, so that a decision can be taken on it.; professional: Revised Bloom's taxonomy: Evaluate)*
8. **Write critically, synthesising sources in the discipline's vocabulary**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Synthesis reaching a reasoned written conclusion.* **Learning outcome:** The student can synthesise several sources into a single reasoned argument, making explicit how strongly the evidence supports its conclusion.
          You bring together several sources that each make a partial point and combine them into a single piece of writing that reaches its own reasoned conclusion, rather than reporting each source separately. You weigh how strongly each source supports your position and make that reasoning visible in the text itself. This demonstrates evaluating evidence and arguments critically and writing the resulting judgement in a form others can follow. **Evidence:** A short piece of writing combining several sources into one argument, with reasoning shown throughout the text. *(Grounding: ing:doc:bloom-anderson-krathwohl-2001; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
        - *Applied: Written recommendation synthesising evidence into one conclusion.* **Learning outcome:** The student can construct a written recommendation that draws evidence from several sources together and justifies a clear conclusion for others to act on.
          You write a recommendation that pulls together evidence from several sources into one coherent argument, rather than presenting each source in isolation, and you reach a justified conclusion that states what should happen next. You make clear how strongly the evidence supports that conclusion and where it is more tentative. This demonstrates evaluating evidence and arguments critically and reaching a reasoned judgement that others can act on. **Evidence:** A written recommendation combining evidence from several sources into one justified conclusion, with its reasoning made explicit. *(Grounding: professional: Writes a recommendation that draws the evidence together and reaches a justified conclusion in the field's own terms.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Redrafting summary into synthesis across sources.* You redraft one section of a piece of writing from a source-by-source summary into a synthesis, grouping sources by the point they make and writing in the field's own terms rather than quoting each one in turn. You annotate the redraft to show where it moves from reporting what others said to building a new structure that carries your own position. This shows writing critically rather than stringing summaries together. **Evidence:** Before and after versions of one section, annotated to show the synthesis moves and your own position. *(Grounding: ing:doc:bloom-anderson-krathwohl-2001; ing:doc:critical-being-barnett-1997)*
        - *Applied: Briefing that synthesises evidence for decision makers.* You write a briefing for people who must act on it, drawing together published research, local data and established guidance rather than listing each source in turn. You lead with your conclusion, organise the evidence by theme instead of by where it came from, and use the terms your readers already work with. This shows you can write critically for an audience that needs a clear, usable conclusion. **Evidence:** A short briefing with an evidence synthesis table and written sign-off confirming its accuracy. *(Grounding: professional: Writes a recommendation that draws the evidence together and reaches a justified conclusion in the field's own terms.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Essay section redrafted from summary into synthesis.* You redraft one section of an essay from source-by-source summary into synthesis, grouping sources by the point they make and using the field's own terms. You annotate the redraft to show where it moves from evaluate to create in Anderson and Krathwohl's (2001) revised taxonomy, putting sources together into a new structure that carries your own position (for example, in design, combining usability and inclusive design studies into an argument about hospital wayfinding signs). **Evidence:** Before and after versions of one essay section, annotated to show the synthesis moves, the discipline terms used and your own position. *(Grounding: ing:doc:bloom-anderson-krathwohl-2001; ing:doc:critical-being-barnett-1997)*
        - *Applied: Policy briefing synthesising evidence for a council team.* On a placement with a council's public health team, you write a briefing for councillors that draws together research, local data and national guidance. You lead with your conclusion, organise the evidence by theme rather than by source, and use the terms the team works with. This shows you can write critically for readers who must act (for example, framing a briefing on hot food takeaways near schools in terms of health inequalities and upstream interventions). **Evidence:** A two-page policy briefing with an evidence synthesis table and your supervisor's sign-off on its accuracy. *(Grounding: professional: Writes a recommendation that draws the evidence together and reaches a justified conclusion in the field's own terms.)*
9. **Write with authority, aware of how own stance shapes the argument**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Writing authoritatively while declaring your own stance.* **Learning outcome:** The student can critique their own standpoint, explaining how it may have shaped which evidence they judged most persuasive in reaching a conclusion.
          When you reach a judgement on evidence and arguments, you write it up in a way that both states your conclusion with authority and makes visible the stance from which you reached it, rather than presenting your view as if it came from nowhere. You note where your own position might have shaped which evidence you found most persuasive. This demonstrates evaluating evidence and arguments critically while remaining accountable for the standpoint behind the judgement. **Evidence:** A short piece of writing that states a conclusion with authority and names the stance it was argued from. *(Grounding: model: Stance and engagement model of academic interaction (Hyland 2005); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
        - *Applied: Owning your stance when reporting a judgement.* **Learning outcome:** The student can justify a written judgement with authority while disclosing the standpoint it was argued from, so others can assess the reasoning accordingly.
          After weighing evidence and arguments to reach a judgement, you write it up with authority while being explicit about the position you argued from, rather than presenting the conclusion as neutral. You flag where your own stake in the matter might have shaped which evidence carried most weight for you, so readers can judge the argument accordingly. This demonstrates evaluating evidence and arguments critically while remaining transparent about the standpoint behind the reasoned judgement. **Evidence:** A short written judgement that states its conclusion clearly and discloses the standpoint the reasoning came from. *(Grounding: professional: States a personal or organisational position at the outset and shows where it may shape the reading of evidence.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Stance analysis revising hedges and boosters in writing.* You analyse a draft piece of writing for its stance, marking hedges, boosters, attitude markers and places where you make yourself visible as the author, using a recognised model of how writers signal their position. You revise so your stance is deliberate rather than accidental, then write a short commentary on how your own position shaped which arguments you pushed hardest. This shows authority built on knowing your stance rather than hiding it. **Evidence:** A colour-coded extract showing stance markers before and after revision, with a short positionality commentary. *(Grounding: model: Stance and engagement model of academic interaction (Hyland 2005))*
        - *Applied: Stating your position before writing with authority.* You research a practice you are closely involved in and write a report for people who will act on it. You state your position as an insider at the start, show where that position may have shaped how you read the evidence, and still make a clear case with authority. This shows you can write as someone with expertise while being open about where you stand. **Evidence:** The final report with its positionality section, plus written feedback on the argument from a reader. *(Grounding: professional: States a personal or organisational position at the outset and shows where it may shape the reading of evidence.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Stance analysis and positionality commentary on a dissertation chapter.* You analyse a draft dissertation chapter using Hyland's (2005) model of stance, marking hedges, boosters, attitude markers and self-mention. You revise so your stance is visible and deliberate, then write a commentary on how your own position shaped which arguments you made strongest. This shows authority that comes from knowing your stance, not hiding it (for example, a teacher researching behaviour policy noticing they boosted claims that matched their own classroom experience). **Evidence:** A colour-coded chapter extract showing stance markers before and after revision, with a 500-word positionality commentary. *(Grounding: model: Stance and engagement model of academic interaction (Hyland 2005))*
        - *Applied: Insider research report stating the author's position.* In a work-based master's project, you research a practice in your own organisation and write a report for senior leaders. You state your stance as an insider at the start, show where it may have shaped your reading of the evidence, and still make a clear case with authority. This shows you can write as an expert while being open about your position (for example, a charity fundraising manager reviewing the donor strategy they helped design). **Evidence:** The final report with its positionality section, plus written feedback from a senior leader or academic supervisor. *(Grounding: practice: work-based master's project in own organisation)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (direct; lands on `criticality/c2`, `criticality/05`, `criticality#e3`)
- Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect; lands on `criticality#e3`)

### Keys at every tier

**Progression:**

- Psychology 1.21: They think critically about different realities and form their own judgements (direct)
- Psychology 3.9: a critical appraisal of their use, limitations and historical origins (direct)
- Psychology 3.10: a critical understanding of the role of research design and enquiry (direct)
- Psychology 4.9: systematically analysed research paradigms, methods of enquiry, and measurement techniques (direct)
- Psychology 4.6: prevalence of critical insight (direct)

**Floor:**

- Universal: Independent and self-directed learning (indirect)
- Universal: Ethics, integrity and values (indirect)
- Universal: Oral or other communication to audiences (indirect)

<a id="academic-writing"></a>
## Academic Writing

### Introductory

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Interpret an assessment question's steer and keywords before writing | L3 | l3/knowledge.2 "Interpret and evaluate relevant information and ideas." | direct | `academic-writing/05` |
| 2 | Follow the writing cycle to plan and complete a first draft | L4 | l4/application.2 "Identify, adapt and use appropriate methods and skills."; 4.10.b4 "communicate the results of their study/work accurately and reliably" | direct | `academic-writing/c1` |
| 3 | Find and correct errors in own writing by proofreading and editing | L5 | l5/application.4 "Evaluate actions, methods and results." | direct | `academic-writing/06` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

1. **Interpret an assessment question's steer and keywords before writing**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Written communication (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Form analysis matched to a task's instruction.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain how a task's steer and keywords indicate the form of writing it requires, before drafting begins.
          You take a set task and identify the form it expects, such as an argument, a report or an analysis, by reading its steer and keywords closely rather than guessing from habit. You set out what producing that form well would involve before you begin drafting. A recognised account of writing development treats this kind of noticing as part of learning what a field expects writing to look like. This shows you can read a task's instruction to work out the form it requires. **Evidence:** A short note identifying the required form from a task's steer and keywords, written before drafting began. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/written-communication)*
        - *Applied: Brief read to identify the required output.* **Learning outcome:** The student can identify, from an instruction's wording, the form, audience and standard a piece of writing must meet before starting to write.
          Given an instruction to produce a piece of writing, you work out before starting what form it needs to take, who will read it and what standard it will be judged against, rather than beginning with whatever format feels familiar. You note this down and check it against the original instruction once a first section is drafted. This shows you can read an instruction accurately enough to produce the form that is actually required. **Evidence:** A short note setting out the required form and audience, checked against the instruction once drafting began. *(Grounding: professional: Reads a brief to establish what is being asked, by whom and against what criteria before starting work.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/written-communication)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Steer and keyword analysis of a set task.* Before you start writing, you take a set task and mark up its steer, the instruction word that tells you what to do, and its keywords. For each one you write a short note on what it is asking you to produce. A recognised account of how writers develop treats learning what a steer means as part of becoming familiar with a field's expectations, not a rule that works the same way everywhere. This shows you can interpret a task before you begin to write. **Evidence:** A marked-up task with the steer and keywords labelled and a short note on what each requires. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998)*
        - *Applied: Annotated brief checked before starting a task.* Before beginning a piece of written work, you annotate the brief: what is being asked, the length expected and the criteria it will be judged against. You then check your reading of it with whoever set the task before you start, rather than assuming you have understood it correctly. This shows you read a brief to establish what is being asked and by what standard before you begin. **Evidence:** The annotated brief with a dated note confirming your reading of it was checked before you started. *(Grounding: professional: Reads a brief to establish what is being asked, by whom and against what criteria before starting work.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Question analysis sheet read through the academic literacies lens.* You take one essay question and mark up its steer (the instruction word) and its keywords, then write two sentences on what each asks you to do. Using Lea and Street (1998), you note that knowing what a tutor means by a steer is part of academic socialisation into a subject, not a fixed rule (for example, what "assess" asks for in a geography question on flood management). This shows you can interpret a question before you start writing. **Evidence:** A marked-up assessment question with the steer and keywords labelled and a short note on what each asks for. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998)*
        - *Applied: Annotated brief for your first marked assignment.* Before starting your first marked assignment, you annotate the brief: the steer, the keywords, the word count and the marking criteria. You then check your reading with your tutor or at a writing support drop-in (for example, a first-year nursing reflection that asks you to "discuss" a care episode, not describe it). This shows you read a brief to find out what is being asked before you begin. **Evidence:** The annotated assignment brief with a dated note of the tutor's or adviser's confirmation of your reading. *(Grounding: professional: Reads a brief to establish what is being asked, by whom and against what criteria before starting work.)*
2. **Follow the writing cycle to plan and complete a first draft**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Written communication (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Staged draft built to match a required form.* **Learning outcome:** The student can describe planning, drafting and reviewing as distinct stages needed to produce a complete first draft in a required form.
          You use a staged process, plan first, then draft, then review, to produce a complete first version of a piece of writing in the form a task requires, rather than starting on finished sentences. At the planning stage you set out how the required form shapes your structure; only once a full draft exists do you check it against that form closely. A recognised model of how writers work treats planning and drafting as separate stages. This shows you can follow a writing cycle to reach a complete first draft. **Evidence:** A plan, a complete first draft and a short note on how the draft matches the required form. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/written-communication)*
        - *Applied: Working draft completed before refining the form.* **Learning outcome:** The student can plan a structure and produce a complete working draft of a document before refining it to match its required form.
          Given a task to produce a piece of writing in a particular form, you plan its structure first, then write a complete working draft before adjusting it to fit that form more precisely. You resist the urge to perfect the opening paragraph before the rest exists, since a full draft shows whether the structure actually works. This shows you can follow a staged process to produce a first complete draft in the form required, ready for focused revision. **Evidence:** A dated plan and a complete first draft, annotated to show where it was adjusted to fit the required form. *(Grounding: professional: Plans a document and gets a full working draft down before polishing it.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/written-communication)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Process log tracking stages of a first draft.* While producing a piece of writing, you keep a short log recording what you do at each stage: planning, drafting and reviewing, using the stages set out in a recognised model of how writers work. You add a note on which conventions of producing this kind of writing you were still learning as you went. This shows you can follow a staged process from an initial plan through to a complete first draft rather than writing everything in one unplanned sitting. **Evidence:** A one-page log with dated entries for planning, drafting and reviewing, attached to your plan and first draft. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998; model: Cognitive process theory of writing (Flower and Hayes 1981))*
        - *Applied: Report planned and drafted for a group.* Asked to produce a report for a group you belong to, you draft a plan of headings first, write a complete rough version in one sitting, and only then revise it for clarity and tone. You keep the plan, the unpolished draft and the finished version together so the stages stay visible. This shows you can plan a document and get a full working draft down before you start polishing it, rather than trying to perfect each sentence as you go. **Evidence:** Your dated plan, the unpolished first draft and the finished report, kept together as one record. *(Grounding: professional: Plans a document and gets a full working draft down before polishing it.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Writing process log mapped to the writing cycle.* You keep a short log while you write one essay, recording what you did at each stage of the writing cycle and labelling it with Flower and Hayes' (1981) planning, translating and reviewing. Using Lea and Street (1998), you add a note on which conventions you were still learning (for example, how to open a 1,000-word sociology essay). This shows you followed a set method from plan to a complete first draft. **Evidence:** A one-page process log with dated entries for each stage of the writing cycle, attached to your plan and first draft. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998; model: Cognitive process theory of writing (Flower and Hayes 1981))*
        - *Applied: Society end-of-year report planned and drafted.* As a committee member of a student society, you are asked to write the end-of-year report for the students' union. You make a plan of headings first, write a full rough draft in one sitting, and only then tidy it (for example, a film society report covering screenings, membership and spending). This shows you can plan a document and get a complete first draft down before polishing. **Evidence:** Your dated plan, the unpolished first draft and the final submitted report, kept together as one portfolio page. *(Grounding: professional: Plans a document and gets a full working draft down before polishing it.)*
3. **Find and correct errors in own writing by proofreading and editing**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Written communication (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Error review checked against a form's requirements.* **Learning outcome:** The student can distinguish correcting surface errors from checking a draft's structure against the form its writing is required to take.
          Reviewing a completed piece of writing, you separate correcting surface errors, spelling, punctuation and slips, from checking that the piece still follows the structure its form requires. You treat these as different kinds of check, since a sentence can be grammatically correct yet still fail to do what that form needs. A recognised account of writing development supports treating these as distinct skills rather than one general check. This shows you can proofread and edit with an eye on the form a piece of writing is meant to take. **Evidence:** A marked-up draft with corrections split into surface errors and structural changes, noting the form required. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/written-communication)*
        - *Applied: Final check against required form before sending.* **Learning outcome:** The student can proofread and edit a finished document, correcting surface errors and confirming it follows the structure its form requires.
          Before a finished document goes to its reader, you check it twice: once for surface errors such as typing slips and once to confirm it still follows the structure and style that this type of document requires. You correct anything that falls short rather than letting familiarity with the text stop you from seeing its errors. This shows you can proofread and edit a document so it is both free of errors and in the form it is actually expected to take. **Evidence:** The corrected document with a short list of surface and structural fixes made before it was sent. *(Grounding: professional: Checks and corrects documents before they go out.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/written-communication)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Two-pass review separating editing from proofreading.* You review a piece of your own writing in two separate passes: first an editing pass checking meaning and order, then a proofreading pass checking surface errors such as comma splices and typing slips. A recognised account of writing development notes that spotting surface errors is only part of this; editing also means asking what the kind of writing you are producing expects of its structure. This shows you can find and correct errors in your own work using two distinct kinds of check. **Evidence:** A marked-up draft with an error log sorting each correction into editing or proofreading, naming the error type. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998)*
        - *Applied: Document checked and corrected before circulation.* Asked to check a document before it is circulated, you read it once for sense and once for errors, keep to its existing style, and send back a list of corrections rather than just fixing it silently. Checking twice in this way catches problems that reading only once, quickly, tends to miss. This shows you can check and correct a document for both meaning and surface errors before it goes out to its intended readers. **Evidence:** The corrected copy of the document and a short note listing the errors found and fixed. *(Grounding: professional: Checks and corrects documents before they go out.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Two-pass error log separating editing from proofreading.* You review one of your drafts in two separate passes: an editing pass for meaning and order, then a proofreading pass for surface errors such as comma splices. Using Lea and Street (1998), you note that error-spotting alone matches their study skills model, and that editing also asks what your subject expects (for example, a psychology lab report). This shows you can find and correct errors in your own writing. **Evidence:** A marked-up draft with an error log sorting each correction into editing or proofreading, with the type of error named. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998)*
        - *Applied: Proofread volunteer handbook update for a food bank.* Volunteering at a local food bank, you are asked to check an updated volunteer handbook before it is printed. You read it once for sense and once for errors, keep to the organisation's style, and send back a list of corrections (for example, inconsistent opening times and a run-on sentence in the safety section). This shows you can check and correct a document before it goes out. **Evidence:** The tracked-changes copy of the handbook and a short email from the coordinator confirming the corrections were used. *(Grounding: professional: Checks and corrects documents before they go out.)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Written communication (direct; lands on `academic-writing/05`, `academic-writing/c1`, `academic-writing/06`)

### Intermediate

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 4 | Identify and structure the essay and other argued genres | L4 | 4.10.b4 "with structured and coherent arguments"; 4.10.b2 "in order to develop lines of argument and make sound judgements" | direct | `academic-writing/02` |
| 5 | Write clear sentences in the active voice | L5 | 4.12.b6 "effectively communicate information, arguments and analysis" | direct | `academic-writing/04` |
| 6 | Use the four pillars to review and structure own writing | L6 | l6/application.4 "Evaluate actions, methods and results and their implications."; 4.15.b8 "communicate information, ideas, problems and solutions to both specialist and non-specialist audiences"; 4.15.b5 "the ability to manage their own learning" | direct | `academic-writing/03` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

4. **Identify and structure the essay and other argued genres**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Written communication (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Argued form selected to match a task's purpose.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse a task to identify which argued form it calls for and explain how that form's structure differs from others.
          Given a task that calls for an argued piece of writing, you work out which form it actually requires, a sustained argument, a structured response to a problem, or something else, and identify how its expected structure differs from the others. You set out the sections or stages each form would need before choosing one and building your own piece around it. This shows you can identify the argued form a task requires and structure writing to match it, rather than applying one structure to every task. **Evidence:** A short comparison of two argued forms with a structure plan for the one chosen for the task. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/written-communication)*
        - *Applied: Right argued format matched to its reader.* **Learning outcome:** The student can select the argued form a task requires and structure a piece of writing so each part fulfils that form's purpose.
          Given a task that needs an argued case made to a particular reader, you decide which format that argument should take and structure it so each part does the job that format requires, rather than writing everything as flowing prose regardless of purpose. You check the finished piece against what that format is normally expected to contain before treating it as complete. This shows you can identify and produce the argued form of writing a task actually calls for. **Evidence:** The finished document checked against the sections or stages its chosen format is expected to contain. *(Grounding: professional: Chooses and follows the right document type (briefing, report, case note, proposal) for the reader and purpose.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/written-communication)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Structural map comparing two argued forms of writing.* You compare two different forms of argued writing and map out how each is typically structured, drawing on a recognised model that treats a form of writing as belonging to a group of readers and writers who share a purpose for it. You explain why each structure suits its purpose, then choose and justify the structure for a piece of your own writing. This shows you can identify an argued form of writing and account for its structure rather than following it without question. **Evidence:** A side-by-side structural map of two argued forms, annotated, with a paragraph justifying your own chosen structure. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998; model: Genre analysis and discourse community (Swales 1990))*
        - *Applied: Document type chosen to make a case.* Asked to make the case for a change, you decide whether a short briefing, a full report or a proposal best suits the reader and the decision they need to make, rather than defaulting to whichever format you know best. You explain your choice and structure the document to fit it, matching its sections to what that type of document is expected to contain. This shows you can identify the right argued form for a purpose and structure it accordingly. **Evidence:** The finished document with a short written rationale explaining why that document type was chosen. *(Grounding: professional: Chooses and follows the right document type (briefing, report, case note, proposal) for the reader and purpose.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Genre map comparing an essay and a problem question.* You compare two argued genres in your subject and map how each is structured, using Swales' (1990) idea that a genre belongs to a discourse community with shared purposes. With Lea and Street (1998), you explain why tutors expect each structure (for example, a law essay that argues a position versus a problem question that applies the law to facts). You then choose and justify the structure for your own assignment. **Evidence:** A side-by-side structural map of two genres, annotated, with a paragraph justifying the structure used in your own assignment. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998; model: Genre analysis and discourse community (Swales 1990))*
        - *Applied: Choosing the right document type on a placement.* On a placement in a software team, you are asked to make the case for changing a testing process. You decide whether a briefing note, report or proposal suits the reader, explain your choice and structure the document to fit it (for example, a two-page proposal for a team lead with a costed option). This shows you can identify an argued genre and structure it for its purpose. **Evidence:** The finished document with a short written rationale for the genre chosen and your supervisor's feedback on it. *(Grounding: professional: Chooses and follows the right document type (briefing, report, case note, proposal) for the reader and purpose.)*
5. **Write clear sentences in the active voice**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Written communication (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Sentence clarity checked against a form's expectations.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse sentence construction to judge when active voice better serves a piece of writing's required form and reader.
          Reviewing a piece of writing meant to be read quickly and acted on, you check each sentence for subject, verb and object order, and rewrite any that bury the main action in a long clause or hide who is responsible for it. You judge voice choice against what that form requires to be useful to its reader, rather than treating every passive sentence as automatically wrong. This shows you can write clear, active sentences suited to the form a piece of writing needs to take. **Evidence:** A rewritten extract with a short note on which sentences were changed to active voice and why. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/written-communication)*
        - *Applied: Active sentences rewritten for a quick reader.* **Learning outcome:** The student can rewrite passive sentences into clear, active ones so a reader can follow a document's point without delay.
          Given a document meant to be read quickly and acted on, you check each sentence for length and clarity, rewriting passive constructions into active ones so the person responsible for an action is always named near the start of the sentence. You test the result by timing how long it takes someone unfamiliar with it to understand the main point. This shows you can write clear, active sentences that let a busy reader understand a document in the form it actually requires, the first time. **Evidence:** The rewritten document with timing notes from a reader test and a short list of sentences changed. *(Grounding: professional: Writes in plain, active English that a busy reader understands first time.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/written-communication)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Sentence rewrite commentary on active and passive voice.* You take a paragraph of your own writing and rewrite it in subject, verb, object order, changing passive sentences to active ones wherever that makes the sentence clearer. You judge, sentence by sentence, where the passive voice is expected by the kind of writing you are producing and where it only hides who is doing what. This shows you can construct clear sentences and choose a voice deliberately rather than by habit. **Evidence:** A before-and-after paragraph with a commentary explaining each change and each passive sentence kept. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998)*
        - *Applied: Standard message rewritten in plain active English.* You rewrite a message that readers often misunderstand, shortening sentences, putting the person doing the action first and cutting unnecessary jargon, then ask someone else to test whether it now reads clearly. One sentence that previously hid who was responsible for an action becomes a short, direct statement naming them. This shows you can write plain, active English that a reader can understand the first time, without needing to reread it. **Evidence:** The old and new versions of the message side by side, with notes explaining each change made. *(Grounding: professional: Writes in plain, active English that a busy reader understands first time.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Sentence rewrite commentary on active and passive voice.* You take a paragraph of your own and rewrite it in subject, verb, object order, changing passive sentences to active ones where that makes them clearer. Using Lea and Street (1998), you judge where the passive is a disciplinary convention and where it only hides who did what (for example, a chemistry method versus the discussion section). This shows you construct clear sentences and choose voice deliberately. **Evidence:** A before-and-after paragraph with a commentary explaining each change and each passive sentence you chose to keep. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998)*
        - *Applied: Plain English customer email template at work.* In a part-time job in a customer contact centre, you rewrite a standard email that customers often misread. You shorten sentences, put the person doing the action first and cut jargon, then ask colleagues to test it (for example, a refund email that now says "We will refund you within five days"). This shows you write plain, active English a busy reader understands first time. **Evidence:** The old and new email templates side by side with notes on each change and your manager's approval. *(Grounding: professional: Writes in plain, active English that a busy reader understands first time.)*
6. **Use the four pillars to review and structure own writing**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Written communication (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Draft restructured to fit its required form.* **Learning outcome:** The student can evaluate a draft's structure against the form it is required to take and identify where it must be reorganised.
          You take a complete draft and check it section by section against the structure that its particular form is expected to follow, moving or cutting any paragraph that does not hold to one clear point. Where the expected structure differs from how you first organised the piece, you note why and restructure accordingly rather than leaving the mismatch in place. This shows you can review and restructure your own writing so it actually takes the form that kind of writing is required to take. **Evidence:** A before-and-after structure map of a draft with notes on each change made to fit its required form. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/written-communication)*
        - *Applied: Document restructured to suit its reader and form.* **Learning outcome:** The student can restructure a draft document so its sections match what its required form is expected to deliver to its reader.
          Given a finished draft of a document, you check whether its structure matches what that type of document is expected to deliver, then reorder or rewrite sections so each one makes a single point a reader can follow quickly. Where the form calls for conclusions or recommendations near the start rather than the end, you move them, explaining the change in a short note. This shows you can structure a piece of writing to fit the form a task actually requires, not just the order ideas occurred to you. **Evidence:** The restructured document with a short note explaining each change made to match its required form. *(Grounding: professional: Organises a document so that each section and paragraph makes one point the reader can follow.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/written-communication)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Reverse outline review against a structured framework.* You use a structured self-review framework with four linked elements to check a complete draft, reverse outlining it so that each paragraph's main point is stated in one line, then restructuring any paragraph that does not hold to a single point. You also note where a choice reflects what the particular piece of writing is expected to do, rather than a universal rule that applies everywhere. This shows you can evaluate and restructure your own writing using a systematic self-review process. **Evidence:** A reverse outline of a draft before and after revision, noting what changed against each element checked. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998)*
        - *Applied: Report restructured so a reader finds the point first.* Asked to write a report on feedback gathered from a group of people, you plan it so each section and paragraph makes one point, then move the main recommendations to the front because that is what this reader reads first. You explain, in a short note, how this differs from writing that builds gradually to a conclusion at the end. This shows you can organise a piece of writing so a busy reader can follow it without having to read the whole thing in order. **Evidence:** The finished report with a short note on its structure and feedback from the person it was written for. *(Grounding: professional: Organises a document so that each section and paragraph makes one point the reader can follow.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Reverse outline review using the four pillars.* You use the guide's four pillars of effective writing as a self-review lens on a full essay draft. You reverse outline it, checking that each paragraph has a topic sentence and one point, and restructure where it does not. Using Lea and Street (1998), you note which choices follow your subject's expectations (for example, an education essay on inclusive classroom practice). This shows you evaluate and restructure your own writing. **Evidence:** A reverse outline of your draft before and after revision, with notes against each pillar on what you changed. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998)*
        - *Applied: Restructured report for a museum live brief.* On a live brief for a local museum, you write a report on visitor feedback. You plan it so each section and paragraph makes one point, then move the recommendations to the front because the client reads those first (for example, a history module project on a new gallery trail). You explain how this differs from an essay that builds to its conclusion. This shows you can organise writing so a reader can follow it. **Evidence:** The client report with a short note on its structure and the museum contact's written response to it. *(Grounding: professional: Organises a document so that each section and paragraph makes one point the reader can follow.)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Written communication (direct; lands on `academic-writing/02`, `academic-writing/04`, `academic-writing/03`)

### Advanced

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | Recognise how writing conventions vary by discipline | L5 | l5/knowledge.3 "Be aware of the nature and scope of the area of study or work."; 4.12.b6 "in a variety of forms to specialist and non-specialist audiences and deploy key techniques of the discipline effectively" | direct | `academic-writing/01` |
| 8 | Integrate sources actively, own point first | L6 | 4.15.b3.1 "to devise and sustain arguments"; 4.15.b5 "make use of scholarly reviews and primary sources" | direct | `academic-writing/c2` |
| 9 | Write with disciplinary self-awareness | L7 | 4.17.b5 "communicate their conclusions clearly to specialist and non-specialist audiences"; l7/knowledge.3 "Understand the wider contexts in which the area of study or work is located." | direct | `academic-writing#e3` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

7. **Recognise how writing conventions vary by discipline**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Written communication (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Required form identified across differing conventions.* **Learning outcome:** The student can critique how writing conventions vary across disciplines and justify which convention a given form of writing actually requires.
          You compare how two fields expect a similar kind of claim to be written up, noticing that one favours tentative, hedged statements while the other states findings directly, and you work out what each convention is trying to achieve for its readers. You then identify which convention the form you are writing in actually requires, rather than assuming your usual style will transfer unchanged. This shows you can recognise that writing conventions vary and use that recognition to produce the form a particular piece of writing requires. **Evidence:** A short comparison of two sets of conventions with a note on which one the task at hand requires. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/written-communication)*
        - *Applied: Writing style adjusted to match required conventions.* **Learning outcome:** The student can identify the conventions a disciplinary context expects and adapt their own writing to construct a piece that meets them.
          Given a task that needs writing up in a form you are less familiar with, you identify its expected conventions, how direct or hedged its claims should be, how evidence is normally presented, before drafting, rather than writing in your usual style and hoping it transfers. You check a finished section against those conventions and adjust anything that does not fit. This shows you can recognise that writing conventions differ across contexts and adapt your own writing to match the one a task actually requires. **Evidence:** A drafted section checked against the expected conventions of its required form, with adjustments noted. *(Grounding: professional: Writes in the conventions of one's profession and adapts them for readers outside it.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/written-communication)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Comparative analysis of writing conventions across fields.* You collect three extracts of established writing from different fields and analyse their conventions: voice, use of evidence, structure and hedging. Drawing on a recognised account of how writing conventions develop, you argue that these differences reflect different ways of building knowledge in each field rather than one style being more correct than another. You then apply what you find to adjust your own writing. This shows you can recognise how conventions vary across fields and use that recognition to inform your own choices. **Evidence:** A comparative table of conventions across three annotated extracts, with a commentary on changes made to your own writing. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998; model: Genre analysis and discourse community (Swales 1990))*
        - *Applied: Technical and plain versions of the same findings.* Having produced a technical account of a piece of work in the conventions your field expects, you adapt it for readers outside that field, deciding what to keep, what to explain in plainer terms and what to drop entirely. One dense paragraph of specialist detail becomes a short plain-language summary of what the finding actually means for someone outside the field. This shows you can write in the conventions of your field and then adapt them deliberately for readers who do not share them. **Evidence:** The paired technical and plain-language documents with a reflective note on the conventions changed between them. *(Grounding: professional: Writes in the conventions of one's profession and adapts them for readers outside it.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Comparative analysis of writing conventions across three disciplines.* You collect three published extracts from different disciplines and analyse their conventions: voice, use of evidence, structure and hedging. Using Lea and Street's (1998) academic literacies model, you argue that these differences reflect each discipline's ways of making knowledge, not right or wrong styles (for example, as a joint honours biology and philosophy student comparing a results section with an argued paper). You then apply your findings to your own writing. **Evidence:** A comparative table of conventions across three annotated extracts, with a commentary on how you adapted your own writing. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998; model: Genre analysis and discourse community (Swales 1990))*
        - *Applied: Technical and public versions of the same findings.* On a placement with an environmental consultancy, you write up a habitat survey in the firm's technical format, then adapt it for a community consultation. You decide what to keep, what to explain and what to drop for each audience (for example, turning species tables into a plain summary of what the development means locally). This shows you write in a profession's conventions and adapt them for readers outside it. **Evidence:** The paired technical and public documents with a reflective note on the conventions you changed, signed by your supervisor. *(Grounding: professional: Writes in the conventions of one's profession and adapts them for readers outside it.)*
8. **Integrate sources actively, own point first**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Information and sources (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Sources selected and ranked to support one point.* **Learning outcome:** The student can justify the selection and ranking of sources by their relevance to a position, ensuring the writer's own point leads the argument.
          Having gathered several sources on a question, you select only those relevant to the point you intend to make, set aside the rest, and rank the ones kept by how directly they support or complicate your position. You then draft a paragraph that states your point first and brings in the strongest source as support, rather than summarising every source you found in turn. This shows you can find, select and use sources appropriate to a task while keeping your own point in charge of the writing. **Evidence:** A short list of sources ranked by relevance, with a paragraph showing your point stated before the sources. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/information-and-sources)*
        - *Applied: Relevant sources selected to support a recommendation.* **Learning outcome:** The student can select sources relevant to a recommendation and construct a response that states their own judgement before citing supporting evidence.
          Given a question to answer with evidence, you search out several possible sources, then select only the ones that are actually relevant to the recommendation you intend to make, discarding sources that are merely related to the topic. You draft your recommendation first and attach the selected sources as support rather than letting whatever you found first dictate your conclusion. This shows you can find, select and use appropriate sources while keeping your own judgement at the front of the writing. **Evidence:** The recommendation with its supporting sources listed, each one noted as directly relevant to the point made. *(Grounding: professional: Leads with one's own recommendation and cites the evidence that supports or qualifies it.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/information-and-sources)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Voice audit of paragraphs reviewing other sources.* You audit several paragraphs of a piece of writing that draws on other sources, marking your own claims and the sources in different colours. Using a recognised account of how writers establish their own authority against sources, you rewrite any paragraph that opens with a source so it instead opens with your own point, uses paraphrase rather than quotation, and brings in sources to support or qualify that point. This shows you can integrate sources actively, with your own point stated first. **Evidence:** Colour-coded before-and-after paragraphs with a commentary on how your voice and the sources now relate. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998)*
        - *Applied: Recommendation paper leading with your own point.* Asked to recommend a change to how a group does something, you write a short paper that leads with your recommendation, then cites the evidence, published findings and the group's own data, that support or qualify it. You check the finished draft to confirm that a reader encounters your point before any source is mentioned. This shows you can put your own point first and use evidence to back it in a real decision someone else has to act on. **Evidence:** The recommendation paper with its source list and a written response showing whether the change was adopted. *(Grounding: professional: Leads with one's own recommendation and cites the evidence that supports or qualifies it.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Voice audit of literature review paragraphs.* You audit three paragraphs of your literature review, highlighting your own claims and the sources in different colours. Drawing on Lea and Street's (1998) attention to authority and identity in student writing, you rewrite paragraphs led by sources so each opens with your point, uses paraphrase, and uses sources to support or qualify it (for example, a criminology review on desistance). This shows you integrate sources actively with your own point first. **Evidence:** Colour-coded before-and-after paragraphs with a commentary on how your voice and the sources' voices now relate. *(Grounding: ing:doc:academic-literacies-lea-street-1998)*
        - *Applied: Evidence-led recommendation paper for a sports club.* Working with a university sports club, you write a short paper recommending a change to how coaches monitor training load. You lead with your recommendation, then cite the research and the club's own data that support or qualify it (for example, injury records set against two sport science studies). This shows you can put your own point first and use evidence to back it in a real setting. **Evidence:** The recommendation paper with its reference list and the head coach's written response, showing whether the change was adopted. *(Grounding: professional: Leads with one's own recommendation and cites the evidence that supports or qualifies it.)*
9. **Write with disciplinary self-awareness**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Written communication (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Form chosen to fit a field's expectations.* **Learning outcome:** The student can critique how a field's conventions shape the form expected of its writing and justify choices that depart from or follow them.
          Given a task that could reasonably be written several ways, you work out which form your field expects for this kind of claim and reflect on why that form suits the kind of knowledge the field builds, rather than treating the choice as arbitrary. You note where you choose to follow that expectation and where you deliberately depart from it, giving a reason either way. This shows you can write with awareness of how your field shapes the form a piece of writing is expected to take. **Evidence:** A short reflective note explaining the form chosen for a piece of writing and the reasoning behind it. *(Grounding: model: Academic literacies (Lea and Street 1998); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/written-communication)*
        - *Applied: Writing choices explained for a wider readership.* **Learning outcome:** The student can construct a document that serves specialist and non-specialist readers, justifying which disciplinary conventions to keep, explain or drop.
          Asked to produce a piece of writing that specialists and non-specialists will both read, you decide, section by section, which conventions of your field to keep, which to explain and which to drop, and you can explain the reasoning behind each decision if asked. You check a finished draft against both kinds of reader before treating it as complete. This shows you can write with awareness of your field's conventions while still producing a form that a wider readership can follow. **Evidence:** A finished document checked against both specialist and non-specialist readers, with reasons for key choices noted. *(Grounding: professional: Adapts the same substantive content for specialist and non-specialist readers.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/written-communication)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Positioning statement on your voice as a writer.* You write a reflective statement on how you position yourself as the author of a substantial piece of writing, drawing on a recognised distinction between the person you are outside your writing, the version of yourself your writing projects and the role of author you take on within it. You show how your choices of voice and evidence either follow, or knowingly depart from, the conventions your field expects. This shows you can write with awareness of your own position as a writer within a field. **Evidence:** A short positioning statement with excerpts from your own writing annotated to show deliberate authorial choices. *(Grounding: model: Writer identity (Ivanic 1998); model: Academic literacies (Lea and Street 1998))*
        - *Applied: Writing adapted across specialist and general readers.* Working on a substantial piece of writing, you produce three versions for different readers: a full technical account, a plain summary for someone outside the field and a short abstract for a wider audience. You then reflect on how your writing moved between these groups of readers and what each version demanded of you, including how much uncertainty to state for each one. This shows you write with awareness of your field's conventions and of the wider readers that writing eventually reaches. **Evidence:** A set of three versions of the same piece of writing with a reflective note on the choices made for each reader. *(Grounding: professional: Adapts the same substantive content for specialist and non-specialist readers.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Writer's positioning statement for a master's dissertation.* You write a reflective statement on how you position yourself as an author in your dissertation, using Ivanic's (1998) distinction between the autobiographical self, the discoursal self and the self as author, and Lea and Street's (1998) academic literacies model (for example, a public health dissertation where you write as a former practitioner). You show how your choices of voice and evidence fit, or knowingly depart from, your discipline's conventions. **Evidence:** A 1,000-word positioning statement with excerpts from your dissertation annotated to show deliberate authorial choices. *(Grounding: model: Writer identity (Ivanic 1998); model: Academic literacies (Lea and Street 1998))*
        - *Applied: Writing portfolio for specialist and non-specialist readers.* On an industry-sponsored engineering project, you write for three readers: a technical report for the sponsor, a lay summary for site staff and a short conference abstract. You then reflect on how your writing moved between these communities and what each demanded of you (for example, how much uncertainty to state for each reader). This shows you write with awareness of your discipline and its wider context. **Evidence:** A portfolio of the three pieces with a reflective commentary on your disciplinary writing choices and the sponsor's feedback. *(Grounding: practice: industry-sponsored postgraduate project)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Information and sources (direct; lands on `academic-writing/c2`)
- Universal: Written communication (direct; lands on `academic-writing/01`, `academic-writing#e3`)
- Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect; lands on `academic-writing#e3`)

### Keys at every tier

**Progression:**

- Psychology 3.11: successfully completed a series of research reports throughout their course (direct)
- Psychology 3.12: critical and reflective thinking and writing (direct)
- Psychology 3.30: essays, research reports, reflective reports, presentations and examinations (direct)
- Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (direct)

**Floor:**

- Universal: Independent and self-directed learning (indirect)
- Universal: Ethics, integrity and values (indirect)
- Universal: Oral or other communication to audiences (indirect)

<a id="presenting"></a>
## Presenting

### Introductory

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Explain what a presentation tests and read its brief | L3 | l3/knowledge.2 "Interpret and evaluate relevant information and ideas." | direct | `presenting#i1` |
| 2 | Build a presentation to a brief through the five steps | L4 | 4.10.b4 "with structured and coherent arguments" | direct | `presenting/c1` |
| 3 | Practise to time and fix what does not work | L5 | l5/application.4 "Evaluate actions, methods and results." | **UNATTRIBUTED** | `presenting#i3` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

1. **Explain what a presentation tests and read its brief**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Oral or other communication to audiences (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Brief read for its hidden test.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain how a presentation brief defines its intended audience, required form and the ideas it is meant to make clear.
          Given a presentation task and its assessment criteria, you work out, before planning any content, what the task is actually testing: the ideas it wants explained, the audience it is aimed at and the form the explanation must take. You write this out as a short statement of intent and check it against the stated criteria rather than against habit. This demonstrates that communicating a subject clearly depends on matching form and content to audience before any talk is built. **Evidence:** A short written statement of what the brief tests, its intended audience and the required form of explanation. *(Grounding: model: Constructive alignment (Biggs 1996); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/oral-or-other-communication-to-audiences)*
        - *Applied: Audience and format checked before drafting.* **Learning outcome:** The student can identify the audience and form required by a presentation brief and use that to guide what is planned before any content is drafted.
          Before you start building a talk, you identify who will be listening, what they already know and what form of explanation will suit them best, whether that is a short briefing, a visual walkthrough or a question and answer session. You then draft your content to match that audience and form, rather than reusing material written for a different purpose. This shows you can communicate a subject clearly to different audiences and in different forms, which is the real test any presentation sets. **Evidence:** A short audience and format analysis used to shape a talk's content before it is drafted. *(Grounding: professional: Matches the content, length and format of an explanation to who is listening before drafting it.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/oral-or-other-communication-to-audiences)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Brief mapped to its learning outcomes.* You take a set task brief and its marking criteria and link each criterion to the outcome it is testing, using the idea of constructive alignment. You then write a short list, in your own words, of what the task is really asking you to show, separating the skill it rewards, such as applying evidence, from the topic it happens to use. This proves you can read a brief for what it tests, not just its subject matter. **Evidence:** An annotated brief with each criterion linked to a learning outcome, and a five point list of what it tests. *(Grounding: model: Constructive alignment (Biggs 1996))*
        - *Applied: Purpose notes checked before building a talk.* You are asked to give a short talk at a regular team meeting on a new process or update. Before preparing any content, you write down what the talk needs to achieve, who will be listening, how long you have and how you will know it has worked, then check this understanding with whoever set the task. This shows you can read a brief properly before building anything, rather than guessing at what is really being tested. **Evidence:** Written notes on purpose, audience and time, confirmed or corrected by whoever set the task. *(Grounding: professional: Checks the purpose, audience and time allowed for a talk against the person who set it before preparing content.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Presentation brief mapped to learning outcomes.* You take a presentation brief and its marking criteria and link each criterion to the learning outcome it tests, using Biggs' (1996) idea of constructive alignment. You then write a short list, in your own words, of what the presentation is really asking you to show. This shows you can read a brief for what it tests, not just its topic (for example, a sport science brief on warm-up routines that rewards applying evidence, not only describing it). **Evidence:** An annotated copy of the brief with each criterion linked to a learning outcome, and a five-point list of what it tests. *(Grounding: model: Constructive alignment (Biggs 1996))*
        - *Applied: Purpose notes for a team huddle talk at work.* Your supervisor at a supermarket asks you to give a five-minute talk at the morning team huddle on a new returns process. Before preparing, you write down what the talk needs to achieve, who will be listening, how long you have and how you will know it worked, and you check this with your supervisor. This shows you can read a brief before building anything (for example, the real test being that staff can process a return without calling a manager). **Evidence:** Your written notes on purpose, audience and time, with your supervisor's confirmation or correction. *(Grounding: practice: part-time retail job)*
2. **Build a presentation to a brief through the five steps**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Oral or other communication to audiences (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Five step structure justified against a sequence model.* **Learning outcome:** The student can describe a five step structure for building a presentation and explain how it guides an audience from attention to action.
          You take a presentation brief and build its structure through five set steps, producing a hook, a main body and a conclusion rather than a loose list of points. You then test that structure against a recognised sequence for moving an audience from attention to action, checking that your opening earns attention and your closing asks for something specific. This demonstrates communicating a subject clearly by giving it a shape an audience can follow, not simply a shape you find comfortable to write. **Evidence:** A labelled storyboard showing the five steps and the stage of the sequence model each section covers. *(Grounding: model: Monroe's Motivated Sequence (Monroe 1935); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/oral-or-other-communication-to-audiences)*
        - *Applied: Hook, body and close built to a brief.* **Learning outcome:** The student can build a presentation to a given brief using five set steps, producing an opening, a main body and a conclusion for a specific audience.
          You are given a brief for a talk, including its purpose, audience and time limit, and you build it through a hook that earns attention, a main body of a few clear points and a conclusion that asks for something specific. You check the finished structure against the original brief before delivering it. This shows you can communicate a subject clearly in a form built for a particular audience, rather than reusing a structure that suited a different occasion entirely. **Evidence:** A finished talk plan showing the hook, main points and conclusion, checked against the original brief. *(Grounding: professional: Prepares a talk or briefing to a set purpose, audience and time.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/oral-or-other-communication-to-audiences)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Storyboard built through five steps and checked against Monroe.* You build a short presentation to a set brief, working through five steps: a hook, a main body and a conclusion. You compare your structure with Monroe's Motivated Sequence, which moves through attention, need, satisfaction, visualisation and action, and note which stage your opening and close cover. This shows a structured build rather than content assembled at the last minute, with each part doing a clear job in moving an audience from interest toward action. **Evidence:** A storyboard or slide outline labelled with the five steps, the hook, main body and conclusion, and the matching Monroe stage. *(Grounding: model: Monroe's Motivated Sequence (Monroe 1935))*
        - *Applied: Talk planned and built to someone else's brief.* You are asked to give a short talk to visitors about a course or programme of study. You plan it to the brief you are given, noting its purpose, audience and time, then build a hook, three main points and a clear close. This shows you can take someone else's brief and turn it into a structured talk, opening with a question that catches attention before moving through your main points to a short, memorable ending that the audience can repeat afterwards. **Evidence:** Your plan and slides for the talk, with brief written feedback from whoever organised the session. *(Grounding: professional: Prepares a talk or briefing to a set purpose, audience and time.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Storyboard built through the five steps and compared with Monroe.* You build a short presentation to a set brief, working through the five steps your module teaches, with a hook, a main body and a conclusion. You compare your structure with Monroe's Motivated Sequence (Monroe 1935): attention, need, satisfaction, visualisation and action, and note which stages your opening and close cover. This shows a structured build, not slides assembled at the last minute (for example, an environmental engineering talk that opens with a flood photo before setting out drainage options). **Evidence:** A storyboard or slide outline labelled with the five steps, the hook, main body and conclusion, and the matching Monroe stage. *(Grounding: model: Monroe's Motivated Sequence (Monroe 1935))*
        - *Applied: Open day talk planned as a student ambassador.* As a student ambassador, you are asked to give a ten-minute talk to visiting sixth formers about life on your course. You plan it to the brief, with its purpose, audience and time, then build a hook, three main points and a clear close. This shows you can build a presentation to someone else's brief (for example, opening with a question about the most surprising thing you learned in first-year chemistry). **Evidence:** Your plan and slides for the open day talk, with brief written feedback from the outreach officer who organised it. *(Grounding: professional: Prepares a talk or briefing to a set purpose, audience and time.; professional: Vitae RDF D2: communication and dissemination)*
3. **Practise to time and fix what does not work**
    - *Benchmark:* none matched at this tier (**UNATTRIBUTED**)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Timed run reviewed for what did not work.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain how timing a rehearsal and identifying what did not work supports improving a presentation before it is delivered.
          You time a run of a talk and, straight after, identify the specific moment where it ran long or lost its thread, rather than making a vague judgement that it went badly. You name what did not work, decide on one change, and plan to test that change in the next run. This demonstrates checking your own performance against a clear measure, time, and using that check to decide what to fix rather than simply repeating the same run again unchanged. **Evidence:** A short note naming one problem from a timed run, the change planned and the next run's result. *(Grounding: model: Experiential learning cycle (Kolb 1984); benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
        - *Applied: Fix identified and tested in the next run.* **Learning outcome:** The student can time a rehearsal of a presentation, identify one specific problem it reveals and make a targeted change before the next attempt.
