          After timing a run of a talk, you identify one thing that did not work, whether it overran, lost the audience or missed a point, and you make a specific change before the next attempt. You keep doing this until the talk fits its time and holds together, rather than stopping once you feel ready. This shows practising to time and genuinely fixing problems, not just repeating a run and hoping it goes better by chance alone. **Evidence:** A short log of timed runs, each with one named problem and the change made before the next attempt. *(Grounding: professional: Times a rehearsal, acts on honest feedback and cuts content until it fits the slot given.; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Rehearsal log structured by an experiential cycle.* You rehearse a presentation at least three times, timing and recording each run. After each one you work through an experiential learning cycle: the run itself, watching the recording back, working out why a section did not work, and trying a change in the next run. This shows you check your own performance honestly and fix it, for example trimming a section that overran so the whole talk finishes inside its time limit on a later run. **Evidence:** A rehearsal log of three timed runs, noting what you changed after each and the final timing. *(Grounding: model: Experiential learning cycle (Kolb 1984))*
        - *Applied: Talk tested with others and cut to time.* Before an important talk, you run through it with a couple of other people, timing it and asking what was unclear. You fix the parts that do not work, then run it again until it fits the time you have been given. This shows practice to time and honest correction in a real setting, rather than assuming a first draft will be fine on the day, with the final run landing close to the limit you were actually set. **Evidence:** Before and after notes or a script with timings, plus a short comment from someone who watched a run. *(Grounding: professional: Times a rehearsal, acts on honest feedback and cuts content until it fits the slot given.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Timed rehearsal log structured by Kolb's learning cycle.* You rehearse a presentation at least three times, timing and recording each run. After each one you work through Kolb's (1984) experiential learning cycle: the run itself, watching the recording, working out why a section did not work, and trying a change in the next run. This shows you check your own performance and fix it (for example, a history student trimming a long background section on the causes of the First World War to finish inside ten minutes). **Evidence:** A rehearsal log of three timed runs, noting what you changed after each and the final timing. *(Grounding: model: Experiential learning cycle (Kolb 1984))*
        - *Applied: Society welcome speech tested and cut to time.* Before speaking at your society's welcome event, you run through your talk with two committee members, timing it and asking what was unclear. You fix the parts that do not work, then run it again until it fits the slot. This shows practice to time and honest correction in a real setting (for example, a drama society welcome speech that first ran at four minutes against a two-minute slot). **Evidence:** Your before and after notes or script with timings, plus a short message from a committee member on the changes. *(Grounding: practice: student society event)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Oral or other communication to audiences (direct; lands on `presenting#i1`, `presenting/c1`)

### Intermediate

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 4 | Design effective, accessible visual aids ("less is more") | L4 | 4.10.b2 "an ability to present, evaluate and interpret qualitative and quantitative data" | direct | `presenting/01` |
| 5 | Adapt delivery to different presentation formats | L5 | 4.12.b6 "in a variety of forms to specialist and non-specialist audiences" | direct | `presenting/03` |
| 6 | Open engagingly and manage own nerves | L6 | 4.15.b9.1 "the exercise of initiative and personal responsibility" | direct | `presenting/02` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

4. **Design effective, accessible visual aids ("less is more")**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Oral or other communication to audiences (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Slide clutter cut using a load reduction principle.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse how reducing visual clutter and applying accessibility standards improves an audience's ability to follow a presentation's visual aids.
          Given a slide deck full of dense text and small images, you apply a principle of cutting unnecessary cognitive load: removing text that only repeats speech, splitting busy slides into two simpler ones, and keeping a single idea per slide. You then check each redesigned slide against basic accessibility standards, including contrast and text size, before judging it finished. This demonstrates communicating a subject clearly by shaping visual aids around what an audience can actually take in during a talk. **Evidence:** A redesigned slide set with a short note explaining each cut or split and the accessibility checks applied. *(Grounding: model: Cognitive load theory (Sweller 1988); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/oral-or-other-communication-to-audiences)*
        - *Applied: Accessible visuals tested with real viewers.* **Learning outcome:** The student can redesign a set of visual aids to cut unnecessary content and meet accessibility standards, checking each change against how real viewers respond.
          You design the visual aids for a talk and test them on people who were not involved in making them, checking that text is large enough, contrast is strong enough and images make sense without your spoken explanation. You adjust anything that confused a viewer before the final version goes out. This shows you can communicate a subject clearly in a visual form to an audience that includes people with different needs, not just the audience you originally had in mind. **Evidence:** A tested slide set with a short log of viewer feedback and the changes made in response. *(Grounding: professional: Prepares presentations and slide decks for colleagues, clients or the public, accessible to the audience.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/oral-or-other-communication-to-audiences)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Slide redesign justified with cognitive load theory.* You redesign a cluttered slide deck using cognitive load theory: you cut text that repeats what you say, split dense slides into simpler ones, and keep one idea per slide to reduce unnecessary load on the audience. You also check accessibility, including colour contrast, font size, alternative text and captions, and justify each change in a short design note, for example turning a paragraph of figures into one labelled chart that makes the same point at a glance. **Evidence:** Before and after slides with a design rationale and a completed accessibility checklist, such as a built in checker's output. *(Grounding: model: Cognitive load theory (Sweller 1988))*
        - *Applied: Accessible slide loop designed for a public screen.* You are asked to design a slide loop for a screen in a busy public space. You choose large text, strong contrast and images with short captions that people can take in within a few seconds as they pass, and you test the loop with a few people before it goes live. This shows you can design visual aids that work for a real, mixed audience, for example replacing a dense block of text with three images and one short line each underneath. **Evidence:** The final slide loop, a note of the accessibility checks run, and feedback from whoever commissioned it. *(Grounding: professional: Prepares presentations and slide decks for colleagues, clients or the public, accessible to the audience.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Slide redesign justified with cognitive load theory.* You redesign a cluttered slide deck using cognitive load theory (Sweller 1988): you cut text that repeats what you say, split dense slides, and keep one idea per slide to reduce unnecessary load. You also check accessibility, including colour contrast, font size, alternative text and captions, and justify each change in a short design note (for example, a nutrition slide turning a paragraph of figures into one labelled chart). **Evidence:** Before and after slides with a design rationale and a completed accessibility checklist, such as the output of a built-in checker. *(Grounding: model: Cognitive load theory (Sweller 1988))*
        - *Applied: Accessible slide loop for a local museum entrance.* Volunteering at a local museum, you design a slide loop for the screen in the entrance hall. You choose large text, strong contrast and images with short captions that visitors can take in within seconds, and you test it with a few visitors and staff. This shows you can design visual aids that work for a real, mixed audience (for example, replacing a dense timeline of the town's mills with three photographs and one line each). **Evidence:** The final slide loop, a note of the accessibility checks you ran, and feedback from the volunteer manager. *(Grounding: professional: Prepares presentations and slide decks for colleagues, clients or the public, accessible to the audience.)*
5. **Adapt delivery to different presentation formats**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Oral or other communication to audiences (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Delivery choices tested against a rhetorical model.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse how the demands of a presentation format shape the pacing, level of detail and structure a speaker should choose.
          Given one set of content to present in two different formats, you use a model of the rhetorical situation, its exigence, audience and constraints, to work out what each format actually demands before you build it. You then justify, in writing, why the pacing, detail and structure differ between the two versions rather than letting the differences happen by accident. This demonstrates communicating a subject clearly by treating form as a deliberate choice rather than an afterthought bolted on late. **Evidence:** A short written justification comparing the delivery choices made for two different formats of the same content. *(Grounding: model: The rhetorical situation (Bitzer 1968); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/oral-or-other-communication-to-audiences)*
        - *Applied: Content rebuilt for a different delivery format.* **Learning outcome:** The student can adapt the same content for a different presentation format, rebuilding its pacing, detail and structure rather than simply shortening the original.
          You are asked to deliver the same material in a new format, for example turning a planned talk into a poster or a live briefing into a recorded video. You identify what the new format allows and restricts, then rebuild the pacing, level of detail and structure to suit it, rather than simply shortening the original. This shows you can adapt delivery to the occasion, communicating the same subject clearly whatever form the audience actually needs to receive it in on the day. **Evidence:** Two versions of the same content built for different formats, with a short note on what changed and why. *(Grounding: professional: Adapts delivery to the occasion: pitch, briefing, conference talk, poster, interview or online meeting.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/oral-or-other-communication-to-audiences)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: One project delivered in two formats via Bitzer.* You take one piece of work and deliver it in two different formats: a short individual talk and a poster session. Using the idea of the rhetorical situation, with its exigence, audience and constraints, you analyse what each format demands and justify the delivery choices made for each, for example telling the work as a story in the talk but as a two minute walk through at the poster, where visitors can interrupt and ask questions directly. **Evidence:** A recording of the talk and the poster, with a one page comparison of the delivery choices made for each format. *(Grounding: model: The rhetorical situation (Bitzer 1968))*
        - *Applied: Same update delivered live and as a recording.* You present the same update twice: once in person to a group and once as a short recorded video for people who were not there. You change the length, detail and pace for each audience and medium, and can explain why, for example cutting detailed figures from the recording and adding captions for people watching without sound. This shows you can adapt delivery to the occasion rather than reusing one version of the material for every audience you meet. **Evidence:** The in person slides and the recorded version, with a short note explaining how and why delivery changed. *(Grounding: professional: Adapts delivery to the occasion: pitch, briefing, conference talk, poster, interview or online meeting.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: One project delivered as talk and poster, analysed with Bitzer.* You take one piece of work and deliver it in two assessment formats: a ten-minute individual talk and a poster session. Using Bitzer's (1968) rhetorical situation, with its exigence, audience and constraints, you analyse what each format demands and justify the delivery choices you made for each (for example, a geography fieldwork project on coastal erosion, told as a story in the talk and as a two-minute walk-through at the poster). **Evidence:** A recording of the talk and the poster, with a one-page comparison of the delivery choices made for each format. *(Grounding: model: The rhetorical situation (Bitzer 1968))*
        - *Applied: Union update delivered in person and as an online video.* As a students' union course representative, you present the same update on library opening hours twice: in person to the union council, and as a short recorded video for students online. You change the length, detail and pace for each audience and medium, and explain why. This shows you adapt delivery to the occasion (for example, cutting the budget detail from the video and adding captions for students watching on phones). **Evidence:** Your council slides and the online video, with a short note explaining how and why you changed delivery for each. *(Grounding: professional: Adapts delivery to the occasion: pitch, briefing, conference talk, poster, interview or online meeting.)*
6. **Open engagingly and manage own nerves**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Oral or other communication to audiences (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Independent and self-directed learning (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Opening designed to earn attention from the first line.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain how a deliberately designed opening and a managed level of arousal help a speaker engage an audience from the first moment.
          Before building the rest of a talk, you design its opening line by line, testing more than one option against what will actually earn attention from a specific audience rather than what feels comfortable to say. You use an understanding of arousal and performance to pitch your energy deliberately, neither flat nor overexcited, and you rehearse the opening until it no longer needs notes. This demonstrates communicating a subject clearly by treating the first moment of a talk as a deliberate design choice, not luck. **Evidence:** A tested opening line with the reasoning for choosing it over an alternative, and a note on rehearsed delivery. *(Grounding: model: Yerkes-Dodson law (Yerkes and Dodson 1908); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/oral-or-other-communication-to-audiences)*
        - *Applied: Composure kept through an unplanned challenge.* **Learning outcome:** The student can open a presentation engagingly and stay composed when faced with an unplanned challenge from the audience, without losing its structure.
          While presenting, you are interrupted or challenged by someone in the audience in a way you had not planned for. You pause rather than rushing a reply, acknowledge the point fairly, and either answer briefly or agree to return to it later, then pick the talk back up without losing the audience. This shows you can stay composed under real pressure and keep communicating a subject clearly, rather than letting one difficult moment derail the rest of the talk entirely. **Evidence:** A short account of an unplanned challenge faced while presenting and how composure was kept, with feedback. *(Grounding: professional: Engages an audience from the start and stays composed when presenting under pressure.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/oral-or-other-communication-to-audiences)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Hooks tested and a nerves plan built on Yerkes-Dodson.* You draft and test two possible opening hooks for a talk and choose one, with reasons for the choice. You also write a nerves plan based on the Yerkes-Dodson law, which links how aroused you feel to how well you perform in an inverted U shape, so you aim to use some nervous energy rather than remove it, with breathing exercises and a well rehearsed first minute to get started, for example opening with a quick question to the room. **Evidence:** Two draft hooks with the reason for the final choice, a pre talk nerves plan and a short reflection afterwards. *(Grounding: model: Yerkes-Dodson law (Yerkes and Dodson 1908))*
        - *Applied: Engaging opening held under real challenge.* You are asked to speak to a group at an event where the audience includes people who may disagree with you. You open with a short story or question that gets their attention, then stay composed when someone challenges a point in front of everyone, for example pausing, thanking them for raising it and offering to talk afterwards rather than rushing a defensive answer. This shows you can open engagingly and manage your nerves when the pressure is real, not staged for practice. **Evidence:** A short video clip or written feedback on the opening and composure shown, with your own reflection added. *(Grounding: professional: Engages an audience from the start and stays composed when presenting under pressure.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Tested hooks and a nerves plan based on Yerkes-Dodson.* You draft and test two possible hooks for an assessed presentation and choose one with reasons. You also write a nerves plan based on the Yerkes-Dodson law (Yerkes and Dodson 1908), which links arousal to performance in an inverted U, so you aim to use some nervous energy rather than remove it, with breathing and a well-rehearsed first minute (for example, a psychology student opening with a quick show of hands on last night's sleep). **Evidence:** Your two draft hooks with the reason for your choice, a pre-presentation nerves plan and a short reflection afterwards. *(Grounding: model: Yerkes-Dodson law (Yerkes and Dodson 1908))*
        - *Applied: Season launch talk to parents as a volunteer coach.* As a volunteer coach at a junior rugby club, you speak to parents at the season launch. You open with a short story that gets their attention, then keep composed when a parent challenges the training schedule in front of everyone. This shows you can open engagingly and manage your nerves when the pressure is real (for example, pausing, thanking the parent and offering to talk afterwards rather than rushing an answer). **Evidence:** A short video clip or the head coach's written feedback on your opening and composure, with your own reflection. *(Grounding: professional: Engages an audience from the start and stays composed when presenting under pressure.)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Oral or other communication to audiences (direct; lands on `presenting/01`, `presenting/03`, `presenting/02`)
- Universal: Independent and self-directed learning (indirect; lands on `presenting/02`)

### Advanced

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | Present in professional formats (pitch, conference poster, viva-style interview) | L5 | 4.12.b8 "the qualities and transferable skills necessary for employment" | direct | `presenting/c3` |
| 8 | Work in a group, leading and resolving conflict | L6 | l6/autonomy.2 "Initiate and lead tasks and processes" | direct | `presenting/04` |
| 9 | Present and defend an argument with an academic voice under questioning | L7 | 4.17.b5 "communicate their conclusions clearly to specialist and non-specialist audiences" | direct | `presenting/c2` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

7. **Present in professional formats (pitch, conference poster, viva-style interview)**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Oral or other communication to audiences (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Case built with claim, data, warrant and rebuttal.* **Learning outcome:** The student can construct an argument for a professional pitch using claim, evidence, warrant and rebuttal so that each step of its reasoning is explicit.
          Given a position to argue in a short pitch, you build its case using a structured argument model: stating the claim plainly, backing it with specific data, making explicit the warrant that links the data to the claim, and preparing a rebuttal for the most obvious objection. You then check the finished case holds together without your slides as a prop. This demonstrates communicating a subject clearly by making every step of an argument visible rather than merely asserted on a slide. **Evidence:** A written case broken into claim, data, warrant and rebuttal, used to brief the final pitch. *(Grounding: model: Toulmin model of argument (Toulmin 1958); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/oral-or-other-communication-to-audiences)*
        - *Applied: Pitch defended under direct questioning.* **Learning outcome:** The student can deliver a pitch in a professional format and defend its weakest points under direct questioning, conceding fairly where the evidence is limited.
          You deliver a short pitch in a professional format and are then questioned directly on its weakest points by people who were not involved in preparing it. You hold your position where the evidence supports it, concede the parts that are genuinely weaker, and avoid repeating rehearsed lines when a specific question is asked. This shows you can present in a professional format and defend the substance of an argument, not just perform a fixed script regardless of the question. **Evidence:** A record of the pitch delivered and the questions faced, with a short note on how each was answered. *(Grounding: professional: Pitches to clients, presents at conferences and performs in selection or assessment interviews.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/oral-or-other-communication-to-audiences)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Pitch and poster built on Toulmin's argument model.* You prepare a short pitch for a competitive setting and build its core case using Toulmin's model of argument: the claim, the data behind it, the warrant linking them, and a rebuttal that answers the obvious objection. You then carry the same argument into a poster style format, judging what each format needs to cut or keep, for example trimming the data down to one key figure for the pitch while keeping fuller evidence available on the poster for anyone who asks. **Evidence:** A pitch outline and poster, annotated with the Toulmin elements, and written feedback from whoever judged them. *(Grounding: model: Toulmin model of argument (Toulmin 1958))*
        - *Applied: Set presentation delivered and defended to a panel.* You take part in a selection process that includes a set presentation to a panel, followed by questions in an interview style. You prepare the presentation to the brief given, deliver it as a professional performance rather than a rehearsed routine, and answer the panel's follow up questions by defending your reasoning rather than repeating slides. This shows you can present in a real professional format, treating the questioning as part of the test, not an afterthought to survive. **Evidence:** The presentation slides and preparation notes, with written feedback from the panel or a practice run record. *(Grounding: professional: Pitches to clients, presents at conferences and performs in selection or assessment interviews.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Enterprise pitch and poster built on Toulmin's argument model.* You prepare a three-minute pitch for an enterprise competition and build its core case with Toulmin's (1958) model: the claim, the data behind it, the warrant linking them, and a rebuttal that answers the obvious objection. You then carry the same argument into a conference-style poster, judging what each format needs to cut or keep (for example, a pitch for a campus food-waste app backed by a two-week trial in one hall of residence). **Evidence:** Your pitch deck and poster, annotated with the Toulmin elements, and the judges' feedback sheet. *(Grounding: model: Toulmin model of argument (Toulmin 1958))*
        - *Applied: Assessment centre presentation and panel interview.* You apply for a graduate scheme or placement that uses a presentation-based assessment centre. You prepare the set presentation, deliver it to the panel and answer their follow-up questions in interview style, treating it as a professional performance rather than a class exercise. This shows you can present in a real professional format (for example, a civil engineering applicant presenting a ten-minute case for a bridge maintenance plan and defending its costs). **Evidence:** Your assessment centre slides and preparation notes, with written feedback from the employer or a careers adviser's mock panel record. *(Grounding: professional: Pitches to clients, presents at conferences and performs in selection or assessment interviews.; professional: Vitae RDF D2: communication and dissemination)*
8. **Work in a group, leading and resolving conflict**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Teamwork and collaboration (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Team stage named and used to resolve conflict.* **Learning outcome:** The student can justify a response to team conflict by naming the group's stage of development rather than treating the friction as purely personal.
          When a team you are part of hits visible conflict, you use a recognised model of group development to name the stage the team is in, rather than treating the friction as simply personal, and you choose a response suited to that stage: clarifying roles, surfacing disagreement openly, or re-agreeing ground rules. You then record what changed afterwards. This demonstrates working effectively with others by diagnosing a team problem before trying to fix it blindly. **Evidence:** A short account naming the group's stage of development, the response chosen and the outcome recorded. *(Grounding: ing:doc:learning-in-teams-gibbs-1994; model: Stages of group development (Tuckman 1965); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/teamwork-and-collaboration)*
        - *Applied: Disagreement mediated to keep a team on track.* **Learning outcome:** The student can mediate a disagreement between team members, steering the group toward a decision both can accept while keeping the work moving.
          Two members of a team you are working in disagree sharply about how to proceed, and the disagreement threatens to stall progress. You bring both people together, let each set out their reasoning, and steer the group toward a decision that both can accept and that keeps the work moving. You follow up afterwards to check the agreement held. This shows you can lead and mediate within a real team, not simply avoid the conflict until it passes on its own. **Evidence:** A short record of the disagreement, how it was mediated, the agreed way forward and a later check in. *(Grounding: professional: Works in and leads teams, mediating disagreements to keep work on track.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/teamwork-and-collaboration)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Ground rules set and conflict worked through with Tuckman.* At the start of a group project you lead the team in agreeing ground rules and roles, drawing on established guidance for learning in teams. When conflict surfaces, you use Tuckman's stages of group development to name what is happening, whether storming or norming, and steer the team through it, then record afterwards what the agreed rules let you do. This shows leadership and conflict resolution grounded in a model rather than improvised on the spot under pressure. **Evidence:** The team's agreed ground rules, meeting notes showing the conflict and how it was resolved, and a short reflection. *(Grounding: ing:doc:learning-in-teams-gibbs-1994; model: Stages of group development (Tuckman 1965))*
        - *Applied: Budget dispute mediated as a team lead.* As the person coordinating a team of others over several weeks, you face a sharp disagreement between two members over how a budget should be split. You meet them together, set out the options openly, and agree a way forward that keeps the work on track rather than letting the disagreement stall it. This shows you can lead and mediate in a real team, for example dividing funds between two priorities once both sides had shown their figures and reasoning clearly. **Evidence:** Planning minutes, a short account of how the dispute was mediated, and a note or reference on your leadership. *(Grounding: professional: Works in and leads teams, mediating disagreements to keep work on track.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Team ground rules and a conflict worked through with Tuckman.* At the start of a group project you lead the team in agreeing ground rules and roles, drawing on Gibbs' (1994) Learning in Teams. When conflict surfaces, you use Tuckman's (1965) stages of group development to name what is happening and steer the team through it, then record what the rules let you do. This shows leadership and conflict resolution based on a model (for example, a computing team split over whether to rewrite or patch a prototype). **Evidence:** The team's agreed ground rules, meeting notes showing the conflict and how it was resolved, and a 400-word reflection on your leadership. *(Grounding: ing:doc:learning-in-teams-gibbs-1994; model: Stages of group development (Tuckman 1965))*
        - *Applied: Leading volunteers through a budget dispute at a charity event.* As the committee lead for a student society's charity event, you coordinate a team of volunteers over several weeks. When two members disagree sharply about the budget, you meet them together, set out the options and agree a way forward that keeps the event on track. This shows you can lead and mediate in a real team (for example, splitting the budget between venue and publicity after both sides showed their costings). **Evidence:** Event planning minutes, a short account of how you mediated, and a note or reference from the society's staff adviser. *(Grounding: professional: Works in and leads teams, mediating disagreements to keep work on track.; professional: Vitae RDF D1: working with others)*
9. **Present and defend an argument with an academic voice under questioning**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Oral or other communication to audiences (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Stance checked across a set of recorded answers.* **Learning outcome:** The student can critique the stance taken in a recorded argument, judging where language overclaimed or hedged relative to the strength of the evidence.
          After presenting an argument and fielding questions, you review your recorded answers against a model of stance: identifying where your language hedged more than the evidence required and where it overclaimed beyond what you could support. You rewrite the weakest answers so the confidence of the language matches the strength of the evidence behind it. This demonstrates communicating a subject clearly by controlling how authoritative you sound relative to what you can actually defend under questioning. **Evidence:** A short set of redrafted answers with the original and revised stance marked for each. *(Grounding: model: Stance and engagement model of academic interaction (Hyland 2005); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/oral-or-other-communication-to-audiences)*
        - *Applied: Challenge answered without losing the argument.* **Learning outcome:** The student can answer a direct challenge to the weakest part of an argument, acknowledging its limit while justifying why the overall position still holds.
          During questions after a presentation, someone directly challenges the weakest part of your argument. You answer by acknowledging the specific limit they have identified, then explaining why your overall position still holds given the evidence available, rather than either collapsing the argument or dismissing the challenge. This shows you can argue a position and answer a direct challenge from colleagues, clients or a panel while keeping both your credibility and the substance of the argument intact. **Evidence:** A short transcript or note of a direct challenge faced and the answer given, with the reasoning behind it. *(Grounding: professional: Argues a position and answers challenges from colleagues, clients or panels.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/oral-or-other-communication-to-audiences)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Recorded question session analysed for stance.* You present an argument and take questions, then review the recording using a model of stance and engagement: where you hedged, where you committed fully, and where you claimed the position as genuinely your own. You note any answers where you gave ground too quickly or overclaimed beyond the evidence, and you redraft those answers with the right degree of authority. This shows you can defend an argument in a confident, evidence matched voice rather than an accidental one. **Evidence:** The recorded presentation and questions, with three answers transcribed, annotated for stance and redrafted. *(Grounding: model: Stance and engagement model of academic interaction (Hyland 2005))*
        - *Applied: Position defended before a specialist audience.* You present your own work to an audience that includes people more expert in the area than you, and take their questions live. You hold your position where the evidence genuinely supports it, concede the limits openly where it does not, and keep a clear, steady voice when challenged rather than backing down or overstating your case. This shows you can argue a position and answer challenge in a real professional setting, not just a rehearsed classroom exchange. **Evidence:** The presentation materials, a record of the questions asked and your answers, and feedback from someone present. *(Grounding: professional: Argues a position and answers challenges from colleagues, clients or panels.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Recorded question session analysed for stance.* You present your dissertation argument and take questions, then review the recording using Hyland's (2005) model of stance: where you hedged, where you committed, and where you claimed the position as your own. You note answers where you gave ground too quickly or overclaimed, and redraft them with authority. This shows you can defend an argument in an academic voice (for example, a social policy student defending qualitative interviews to a sceptical examiner). **Evidence:** The recorded presentation and questions, with three answers transcribed, annotated for stance and redrafted. *(Grounding: model: Stance and engagement model of academic interaction (Hyland 2005))*
        - *Applied: Research presentation defended at a student conference.* You present your own research at a student or early-career conference and take questions from an audience that includes specialists. You hold your position where the evidence supports it, concede limits openly, and keep a clear academic voice when challenged. This shows you can argue a position and answer challenge in a professional forum (for example, a marine biology student defending a sampling method that a researcher in the audience questions). **Evidence:** The conference abstract and slides, a record of the questions and your answers, and feedback from the session chair or a mentor. *(Grounding: professional: Argues a position and answers challenges from colleagues, clients or panels.; professional: Vitae RDF D2: communication and dissemination)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Oral or other communication to audiences (direct; lands on `presenting/c3`, `presenting/c2`)
- Universal: Teamwork and collaboration (direct; lands on `presenting/04`)
- Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect; lands on `presenting/c2`)
- Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (indirect; lands on `presenting/c2`)

### Keys at every tier

**Progression:**

- Psychology 1.2: to interpret evidence and to disseminate to a range of different audiences (direct)
- Psychology 3.6: the ability to design, obtain, analyse and disseminate evidence (direct)
- Psychology 3.17: respond inclusively in a manner sensitive to the needs and expectations of a diverse range of audiences (direct)
- Psychology 3.30: research reports, reflective reports, presentations and examinations (direct)
- Psychology 4.9: disseminated psychological research findings to peers and academic supervisors (direct)
- Psychology 3.24: encompass both collaborative group work and independent research (direct)
- Psychology 4.10: imparted to peers, tutors, supervisors and mixed audiences a comprehensive grasp of psychology (direct)

**Floor:**

- Psychology 1.11: developing students' interpersonal skills, such as empathy, active listening and effective communication (indirect)
- Psychology 4.8: communicated psychological knowledge and research findings in a range of formats (indirect)
- Universal: Ethics, integrity and values (indirect)

<a id="revising-exams"></a>
## Revising and Exams

### Introductory

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Explain the forms of exam and what each demands | L3 | l3/knowledge.3 "Be aware of the nature of the area of study or work." | **UNATTRIBUTED** | `revising-exams#i1` |
| 2 | Make a simple revision plan from the time available | L4 | l4/autonomy.1 "Take responsibility for courses of action" | direct | `revising-exams#i2` |
| 3 | Manage exam anxiety and prepare in an organised way | L5 | l5/autonomy.2 "Exercise autonomy and judgement within broad parameters." | indirect only | `revising-exams/01` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

1. **Explain the forms of exam and what each demands**
    - *Benchmark:* none matched at this tier (**UNATTRIBUTED**)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Exam demands explained through format features.* **Learning outcome:** The student can identify and explain the demands of different exam formats, including what each tests and how marks are likely distributed.
          You take each form of exam you will face and break down what it is actually testing: whether it rewards quick recall, extended writing, applied problem solving or hands-on performance, and how marks are likely to be distributed across these. You set this out so that the demand of each format, not just its name, is clear. This shows you can explain what different exam forms require before you begin preparing for them. **Evidence:** A short breakdown of each exam form, naming what it tests and how marks are likely weighted. *(Grounding: benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
        - *Applied: Format briefing used before choosing a strategy.* **Learning outcome:** The student can produce a short briefing that identifies what each exam format demands and where preparation effort should be concentrated.
          Before you commit to a preparation strategy, you identify the exam formats ahead of you and note, for each, what it demands: timed recall, structured argument, calculation, or live performance. You use this breakdown to decide where to put most of your preparation effort and which formats need the most unfamiliar skills. This shows you explaining demands accurately enough to act on, not just describe. **Evidence:** A short briefing note naming each exam's demand and where you will concentrate preparation effort. *(Grounding: professional: Identifies what a task actually requires before committing resource to preparing for it.; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Assessment formats mapped by cognitive demand.* You list every timed assessment format you will sit, such as short answer, multiple choice, essay and practical tasks, and classify each by the level of thinking it mainly requires, from simple recall through to evaluation and judgement, using a recognised hierarchy of thinking skills. For each format you write one sentence naming what it actually demands of you. This shows you can explain the differing demands of assessment forms rather than treating them as the same. **Evidence:** A one-page table listing each assessment format, its cognitive level and a one-line description of its demand. *(Grounding: model: Revised Bloom's taxonomy (Anderson and Krathwohl 2001))*
        - *Applied: Format guide shared to check understanding.* You gather the format of each upcoming assessment from the guidance available to you and past papers, then write a short, plain guide explaining what each format asks you to do and how much time you are given. Sharing this guide with others preparing for the same assessments and answering their questions is a check on your own understanding, since a demand you cannot explain clearly is one you have not yet understood. **Evidence:** A shared format guide, with the date posted and a note of any questions it raised. *(Grounding: professional: Communicates procedural requirements clearly enough for others to follow unaided.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Exam forms mapped against Bloom's revised taxonomy.* You take the exams listed in your module handbooks (multiple choice, short answer, essay, open book, practical) and, using Bloom's revised taxonomy (Anderson and Krathwohl 2001), note which level of thinking each mostly asks for, from remembering to evaluating. This shows you can explain what each form demands rather than treating all exams alike (for example, a first-year biology student seeing that a multiple-choice paper tests recall and understanding, while a seen essay question asks them to analyse and evaluate). **Evidence:** A one-page table of your exam forms, each tagged with its Bloom's level and one sentence on what it demands. *(Grounding: model: Revised Bloom's taxonomy (Anderson and Krathwohl 2001))*
        - *Applied: Exam-format guide written for your course group.* You collect the format of each of your first exams from the module pages and past papers, then write a short guide for your course group or study society explaining what each format asks you to do and how long you get. Explaining it to others shows you understand the demands yourself (for example, a law student explaining the difference between a problem question and an essay question in a contract law paper). **Evidence:** A shared exam-format guide for your cohort, with the date it was posted and any questions peers asked. *(Grounding: practice: course study group or student society)*
2. **Make a simple revision plan from the time available**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Reflection and personal development (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Development plan built from reflection on past revision.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain how reflecting on past revision attempts informs a realistic plan for allocating the time available.
          You look back at how you prepared for a previous set of exams or tests and identify what left you short of time, then use that reflection to design a new plan that allocates the time you actually have across topics in order of need. The plan states which lesson from the past attempt shaped each choice. This demonstrates planning your development by reflecting honestly on previous learning and practice. **Evidence:** A short plan with each time allocation linked to a specific lesson from earlier revision. *(Grounding: benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
        - *Applied: Realistic plan drawn up after reviewing past attempts.* **Learning outcome:** The student can produce a revision plan that allocates available time across topics by priority, informed by review of a previous attempt.
          After reviewing how a previous revision attempt went, you draw up a new plan that fits the time actually available to you, naming the topics that take priority and the sessions you will drop if time runs short. You check the plan against your calendar before the first week begins and adjust anything that is not realistic. This shows planned development grounded in honest reflection on your own practice. **Evidence:** A written revision plan with priority topics marked and a note on what changed from last time. *(Grounding: professional: Builds a workable plan from honest review of what worked and what did not.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Spaced revision timetable explained by memory decay.* You draw up a revision timetable for the weeks before your assessments that returns to each topic more than once, with the gaps between repeats growing wider each time. You explain this spacing by reference to how memory for new material fades quickly unless it is revisited, so that each return to a topic is placed where recall would otherwise be weakest. This shows a simple plan built to fit the time you actually have. **Evidence:** A dated timetable with repeat sessions marked, plus a short note explaining why each topic recurs. *(Grounding: model: Forgetting curve and spaced repetition (Ebbinghaus 1885))*
        - *Applied: Revision plan fitted around real commitments.* You build your revision plan around a real week of commitments: fixed hours for work, travel, caring responsibilities or other demands on your time. You block out what is already taken, fill what remains with short revision sessions, and keep to the plan for a full week before reviewing it. This shows you taking responsibility for planning revision from the time genuinely available to you, not from an ideal week that does not exist. **Evidence:** A weekly planner showing fixed commitments, planned revision slots and ticks for sessions completed. *(Grounding: professional: Plans around existing commitments rather than assuming unlimited time is available.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Spaced revision timetable explained by the forgetting curve.* You draw up a revision plan for the weeks before your exams that returns to each topic several times at widening intervals, and you explain the spacing by reference to the forgetting curve (Ebbinghaus 1885), which shows memory fading quickly unless material is revisited. This shows you can make a simple plan that fits the time you actually have (for example, a sport science student revisiting physiology topics after one day, three days and a week). **Evidence:** A dated revision timetable with repeat sessions marked, plus a short note explaining why topics recur. *(Grounding: model: Forgetting curve and spaced repetition (Ebbinghaus 1885))*
        - *Applied: Revision plan built around a real working week.* You build your revision plan around a real week: lectures, a part-time job, travel and caring duties. You block out the hours that are already taken, fill what is left with short revision sessions and keep to the plan for a week. This shows you take responsibility for planning from the time genuinely available (for example, a business student fitting revision around three evening shifts at a supermarket). **Evidence:** A weekly planner showing fixed commitments, planned revision slots and ticks for the sessions you completed. *(Grounding: practice: part-time work alongside study)*
3. **Manage exam anxiety and prepare in an organised way**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Independent and self-directed learning (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Independent routine built to manage exam nerves.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain how self-directed techniques, tested and refined over time, can be used to manage anxiety before an assessment.
          You design your own routine for managing nerves in the run-up to an assessment, choosing and testing techniques such as timed practice, controlled breathing or a fixed pre-exam sequence without waiting to be told which to use. You track which elements of the routine actually reduce your anxiety over several attempts and drop the ones that do not. This demonstrates managing your own learning and working independently to control a high-pressure state. **Evidence:** A self-designed anxiety routine with a record of which elements were kept after several trials. *(Grounding: model: Yerkes-Dodson law of arousal and performance (Yerkes and Dodson 1908); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/independent-and-self-directed-learning)*
        - *Applied: Self-managed preparation routine for a pressured exam.* **Learning outcome:** The student can independently design and run an organised preparation routine that manages anxiety before and during a high-stakes assessment.
          Facing an assessment where the stakes are high, you build your own preparation routine rather than relying on someone else to organise it for you: a schedule for practice questions, a checklist for the day itself and a plan for managing nerves once you are in the room. You run the routine without supervision and adjust it based on how the previous attempt felt. This shows independent management of both learning and pressure. **Evidence:** A self-built preparation and exam-day routine, annotated with changes made after each attempt. *(Grounding: professional: Prepares for high-stakes assessments, licensing or professional exams, and manages the pressure of them.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/independent-and-self-directed-learning)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Anxiety log read against arousal and performance.* Before and during a timed practice test you keep a short log of how anxious you feel, then read it against a long-standing account of arousal and performance which holds that performance rises with nervous tension up to a point before it starts to fall. You note which preparation steps brought your nerves back into a range where you performed well. This shows you managing anxiety by preparing in an organised, trackable way. **Evidence:** A practice-test anxiety log rating nerves before and after, with the preparation steps that helped. *(Grounding: model: Yerkes-Dodson law of arousal and performance (Yerkes and Dodson 1908))*
        - *Applied: Day-of checklist used for a high-stakes test.* Before a real, high-stakes assessment you write and follow a checklist covering the day itself: route and travel time, equipment, identification, arrival time and a short calming routine for the first minutes once you are seated. Afterwards you note what reduced your anxiety and what did not help at all. This shows organised preparation used specifically to manage the pressure that a must-pass assessment brings. **Evidence:** An exam-day checklist with post-test notes on what reduced anxiety and what to change next. *(Grounding: professional: Prepares for high-stakes assessments, licensing or professional exams, and manages the pressure of them.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Mock-exam anxiety log read through the Yerkes-Dodson law.* You keep a short log of your nerves before and during a mock exam and relate it to the Yerkes-Dodson law (Yerkes and Dodson 1908), usually read as performance rising with arousal up to a point and then falling. You note which preparation steps brought your nerves back into a useful range. This shows you managing anxiety by preparing in an organised way (for example, a psychology student finding they performed better in a mock once they had practised timed answers). **Evidence:** A mock-exam anxiety log rating your nerves before and after, with the preparation steps that helped and those that did not. *(Grounding: model: Yerkes-Dodson law of arousal and performance (Yerkes and Dodson 1908))*
        - *Applied: Exam-day checklist used for a must-pass test.* Before a real exam you write and follow a checklist for the day: route and travel time, equipment, ID, arrival time and a short breathing routine for the first minutes in the hall. Afterwards you note what calmed you and what did not. This shows organised preparation used to manage the pressure of a high-stakes assessment (for example, a nursing student preparing for a drug calculation test that must be passed to continue on placement). **Evidence:** Your exam-day checklist with post-exam notes on what reduced your anxiety and what you will change next time. *(Grounding: professional: Prepares for high-stakes assessments, licensing or professional exams, and manages the pressure of them.; professional: Vitae RDF B2: self-management)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Reflection and personal development (direct; lands on `revising-exams#i2`)
- Universal: Independent and self-directed learning (indirect; lands on `revising-exams/01`)

### Intermediate

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 4 | Turn passive revision into active revision | L4 | l4/application.2 "Identify, adapt and use appropriate methods and skills." | direct | `revising-exams/02` |
| 5 | Target revision at the gaps in own knowledge | L5 | 4.12.b4 "an understanding of the limits of their knowledge" | direct | `revising-exams#d2` |
| 6 | Monitor own revision and change it when it is not working | L6 | l6/application.4 "Evaluate actions, methods and results and their implications."; 4.15.b5 "the ability to manage their own learning" | direct | `revising-exams#d3` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

4. **Turn passive revision into active revision**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Reflection and personal development (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Study methods reviewed and replaced through reflection.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse why passive revision methods fail to produce lasting recall and explain how active techniques improve retention.
          You look back over a block of revision done by rereading and recognise that very little of it has stuck, so you reflect on why a passive method failed and redesign your approach around methods that force retrieval, such as self-testing or explaining a topic aloud from memory. You record what changed and why. This demonstrates reflecting on your own learning and practice to plan a more effective approach to development. **Evidence:** A short reflection comparing a passive method against an active one, with the change you made. *(Grounding: benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
        - *Applied: Active revision adopted after reviewing what failed.* **Learning outcome:** The student can replace an ineffective passive study method with an active technique and evaluate the improvement in recall it produces.
          After noticing that a long session of rereading left you unable to recall much the next day, you plan a replacement approach built around active techniques, such as self-testing, summarising from memory or teaching the material to someone else, and you try it on the next topic. You compare recall between the two approaches and keep whichever produced better results. This shows planned development built on honest reflection about what is and is not working. **Evidence:** A short before-and-after comparison of a passive and an active method, with recall results for each. *(Grounding: professional: Uses active study methods to learn and retain new professional knowledge.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Notes converted to visual form and self-tested.* You take a set of notes you would normally just reread and convert them into a mind map and a set of picture-and-keyword flashcards, drawing on the idea that material held as both words and images is easier to recall than words alone. You test your recall after each version and compare the results. This shows you turning passive revision into active techniques and judging which conversion actually works best for you. **Evidence:** Original notes alongside the mind map and flashcards made from them, with self-test scores for each. *(Grounding: model: Dual coding theory (Paivio 1971))*
        - *Applied: Active methods tried on real procedural knowledge.* You take something you genuinely need to learn for a role you hold, such as a set of procedures or a body of product knowledge, and work through it using active methods such as self-testing or teaching it back, rather than simply rereading a manual. You note which method actually helped you recall the material when you needed it in practice. This shows active study methods chosen to retain knowledge that has to stick. **Evidence:** A short comparison of methods tried on real procedural material, with a note on which method stuck. *(Grounding: professional: Uses active study methods to learn and retain new professional knowledge.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Notes converted into visual revision using dual coding.* You take a set of notes you would normally reread and convert them into a mind map and picture-and-keyword flashcards, then explain your choices using dual coding theory (Paivio 1971), which holds that material held as both words and images is easier to recall. You judge which conversion worked best by testing yourself. This shows you turning passive revision into active techniques (for example, a history student turning notes on the causes of the First World War into a visual timeline). **Evidence:** Before-and-after pages: original notes beside the mind map and flashcards, with your self-test scores for each method. *(Grounding: model: Dual coding theory (Paivio 1971))*
        - *Applied: Active methods used to learn real workplace procedures.* You use active methods to learn something you need for work or volunteering, such as a set of procedures or product facts, and compare them with simply reading the manual. You note which method helped you recall the material on shift. This shows you choosing and adapting active techniques for real learning (for example, a computing student volunteering at a children's coding club who learns the club's safeguarding steps with self-made flashcards). **Evidence:** A short comparison of methods tried on workplace material, with a supervisor's note confirming you can recall the procedure. *(Grounding: professional: Uses active study methods to learn and retain new professional knowledge.; professional: Vitae RDF B3: professional and career development)*
5. **Target revision at the gaps in own knowledge**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Reflection and personal development (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Subject gaps mapped against core content expected.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse their own command of core subject content against what is expected, in order to identify genuine gaps in knowledge.
          You set out the core content you are expected to know for your subject and check your own command of each part against it, marking honestly where your knowledge is thin rather than assuming even coverage. The topics where the gap between what is expected and what you can currently produce is widest become your priority for revision. This demonstrates showing broad, well-founded knowledge by first finding out exactly where it is not yet broad or well-founded. **Evidence:** A content checklist with each topic marked against expected coverage and a ranked list of weak areas. *(Grounding: benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/subject-knowledge-and-understanding)*
        - *Applied: Preparation time targeted at weakest core content.* **Learning outcome:** The student can evaluate their own knowledge against expected core content and direct remaining preparation time at the weakest areas identified.
          You check your command of the core content expected of you against a checklist or syllabus, identify the two or three areas where your knowledge falls shortest of that standard, and direct most of your remaining preparation time there rather than spreading it evenly. You confirm with a reliable source that you have identified the right gaps before committing time. This shows targeted preparation driven by an honest read of where core knowledge is weakest. **Evidence:** A content checklist with weak areas marked and the revised time allocation that followed from it. *(Grounding: professional: Uses performance feedback to target scarce preparation time at genuine weaknesses.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/subject-knowledge-and-understanding)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Knowledge gaps ranked through timed self-testing.* You sit a set of practice questions under timed conditions before doing any further revision, mark your own answers against a model answer or mark scheme, and rank topics from weakest to strongest. You justify spending most of your remaining time on the weakest topics by drawing on the idea that testing yourself not only strengthens memory but also reveals what you do not yet know, more reliably than rereading does. This shows revision targeted at real gaps. **Evidence:** A topic gap audit: timed practice scores by topic, a ranked weakness list and the plan that follows. *(Grounding: model: Testing effect and retrieval practice (Roediger and Karpicke 2006))*
        - *Applied: Revision rebuilt around marks actually lost.* You use feedback from a real, marked assessment to find the two or three topics where you lost the most marks, and rebuild your remaining revision time around closing exactly those gaps rather than revising every topic evenly. You then check with someone else that you have read the feedback correctly before committing time to it. This shows you targeting your own weak points using evidence of where your subject knowledge is thinnest. **Evidence:** A marked assessment annotated with lost-mark topics, a targeted revision list and a note confirming the reading. *(Grounding: professional: Uses performance feedback to target scarce preparation time at genuine weaknesses.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Past-paper gap audit justified by the testing effect.* You sit past-paper questions under timed conditions before revising, mark them against the mark scheme and rank topics by weakness. You justify spending most time on the weakest by citing the testing effect (Roediger and Karpicke 2006): retrieval practice strengthens memory and also shows you what you do not yet know. This shows revision targeted at the limits of your knowledge (for example, an engineering student finding thermodynamics problems much weaker than statics). **Evidence:** A topic gap audit: past-paper scores by topic, your ranked list of gaps and the revised plan that follows from it. *(Grounding: model: Testing effect and retrieval practice (Roediger and Karpicke 2006))*
        - *Applied: Revision rebuilt around marks lost in a class test.* You use feedback from a real assessment, such as a marked class test or tutor comments, to find the two or three topics where you lost most marks, and rebuild your revision around them. You then check with a tutor or peer mentor that you have read the gaps correctly. This shows you targeting your own weak points rather than revising everything evenly (for example, a pharmacy student targeting calculation errors flagged in a formative test). **Evidence:** A marked class test annotated with lost-mark topics, your targeted revision list and notes from a tutor check-in. *(Grounding: practice: formative class test and tutor feedback)*
6. **Monitor own revision and change it when it is not working**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Reflection and personal development (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Revision adjusted after reflecting on recall evidence.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse recall evidence from their own revision to judge whether a method is working and explain the reasoning behind any change.
          You review how well a method of revision has actually worked by testing your recall rather than trusting how confident the method made you feel, and you reflect honestly on what the result tells you about your approach to learning. Where the evidence shows a method is not working, you plan a specific change and state the reasoning behind it before trying the next method. This demonstrates reflecting on learning and practice in order to plan development. **Evidence:** A short reflection comparing recall evidence for two methods, with the planned change and its reasoning. *(Grounding: benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
        - *Applied: Approach changed after reflecting on weak results.* **Learning outcome:** The student can evaluate recall evidence against expectation and apply a specific, reasoned change to their revision approach when results are weak.
          When a period of revision produces weaker recall than expected, you stop and reflect on what in your approach might explain it, rather than simply continuing with more of the same. You decide on one specific change, apply it for the next block of revision, and check afterwards whether recall improved. This shows planned development built on honest reflection about your own practice, not on assumption or habit. **Evidence:** A short record of a weak result, the change made in response and the recall outcome after. *(Grounding: professional: Monitors progress against evidence rather than a felt sense of improvement.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Revision journal tracked for recall over time.* Over two weeks you keep a revision journal, rating a few days after each session how well you can still recall what you covered, and you read the pattern through an account of monitoring your own thinking while you learn. Where a method is clearly not producing recall, you record the change you made and the reason for it. This shows you evaluating your own revision honestly and changing it when it is not working. **Evidence:** A two-week revision journal with recall ratings, a mid-point review and a recorded change of method. *(Grounding: model: Metacognitive monitoring (Flavell 1979))*
        - *Applied: Weekly quiz check-ins to monitor progress jointly.* With someone else preparing for the same assessment, you agree weekly check-ins where you quiz each other on the previous week's material and compare scores. When your scores stall rather than improve, you discuss why together, change your approach for the next week and report back on whether the change worked. This shows you monitoring revision against real evidence rather than a feeling of progress, and acting when it stalls. **Evidence:** A shared check-in record with weekly scores, the change agreed and the effect it had. *(Grounding: professional: Monitors progress against evidence rather than a felt sense of improvement.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Revision journal analysed as metacognitive monitoring.* You keep a revision journal over two weeks, rating how well you can recall each session's material a few days later, and analyse it using Flavell's (1979) account of metacognitive monitoring. Where a method is not producing recall, you record the change you made and why. This shows you evaluating your own revision and changing it when it is not working (for example, a geography student dropping highlighting in favour of drawing practice maps after low recall scores). **Evidence:** A two-week revision journal with recall ratings, a mid-point review and a recorded decision to change method. *(Grounding: model: Metacognitive monitoring (Flavell 1979))*
        - *Applied: Weekly quiz check-ins with a study partner.* With a study partner or peer-assisted learning group, you agree weekly check-ins where you quiz each other on the previous week's topics. When your scores stall, you discuss why, change your approach and report the result at the next check-in. This shows you monitoring revision against evidence and acting on it (for example, two architecture students preparing for a building technology exam who switch from sharing notes to drawing construction details from memory). **Evidence:** A shared check-in record with weekly quiz scores, the change you agreed and the effect it had. *(Grounding: practice: peer-assisted learning group)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (direct; lands on `revising-exams#d2`)
- Universal: Reflection and personal development (direct; lands on `revising-exams/02`, `revising-exams#d2`, `revising-exams#d3`)

### Advanced

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | Use kinaesthetic and role-play techniques to deepen engagement | L5 | l5/application.2 "Determine, adapt and use appropriate methods and skills." | direct | `revising-exams/03` |
| 8 | Apply an exam strategy across formats and on the day | L6 | 4.15.b9.2 "decision-making in complex and unpredictable contexts" | direct | `revising-exams/04` |
| 9 | Choose techniques by what works, judged by own recall | L7 | 4.17.b7 "continue to advance their knowledge and understanding"; l7/application.4 "Critically evaluate actions, methods and results" | direct | `revising-exams#e3` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

7. **Use kinaesthetic and role-play techniques to deepen engagement**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Reflection and personal development (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Physical rehearsal reflected on to plan development.* **Learning outcome:** The student can critique how physical rehearsal and role-play deepen engagement with material compared with reading-based methods, and justify when to use each.
          You try rehearsing a procedure or scenario physically, through movement or role-play, rather than only reading or writing about it, and you reflect afterwards on whether this engaged you with the material more deeply than your usual methods, noting specifically what you remembered that you had not expected to. Based on that reflection you plan whether and how to use physical rehearsal again for different material. This demonstrates reflecting on your own learning in order to plan further development. **Evidence:** A short reflection on a physical rehearsal session, with a decision about using it again. *(Grounding: benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
        - *Applied: Scenario rehearsal reviewed to plan the next one.* **Learning outcome:** The student can design and run a physical rehearsal of a scenario, then evaluate what it revealed to plan a second, targeted rehearsal.
          You physically rehearse a scenario or procedure with others, playing it through as if it were real rather than describing it on paper, and afterwards you review together what each person did well and where the rehearsal broke down. You use that review to plan a second rehearsal that specifically targets the points that broke down the first time. This shows rehearsal used as a deliberate, repeated method of development rather than a one-off exercise. **Evidence:** A rehearsal review listing what broke down, plus the plan for a second, targeted rehearsal. *(Grounding: professional: Rehearses procedures and scenarios physically, as in simulation training.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Kinaesthetic activity tested against plain rereading.* You design a revision activity that involves movement, such as acting out a sequence of steps or placing topic cards around a room and walking between them in order, and you explain your choice by reference to the idea that deeper, more meaningful processing of material leads to longer-lasting memory than shallow processing does. You test your recall afterwards against a session of plain rereading on a similar topic. This shows deliberate use of movement to deepen engagement. **Evidence:** A plan of the movement-based activity with recall results compared against a rereading session. *(Grounding: model: Levels of processing (Craik and Lockhart 1972))*
        - *Applied: Role-play rehearsal of a real scenario.* You run a role-play with others that rehearses a realistic scenario you might be tested on, taking turns playing each role and stopping afterwards to debrief on what went wrong and what would need to change in a real situation. You then use what the debrief surfaced to target further revision on the weak points it revealed. This mirrors physical rehearsal of procedures and shows role-play used deliberately to deepen engagement with difficult material. **Evidence:** A scenario outline, a debrief note listing errors found and the revision actions that followed. *(Grounding: professional: Rehearses procedures and scenarios physically, as in simulation training.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Kinaesthetic revision activity designed around levels of processing.* You design a kinaesthetic revision activity, such as acting out a process or placing topic cards around a room, and explain it with the levels of processing framework (Craik and Lockhart 1972), which links deeper, more meaningful processing to longer-lasting memory. You test recall afterwards against a session of plain rereading. This shows deliberate use of movement to deepen engagement (for example, a biochemistry student walking through the stages of the Krebs cycle laid out on the floor). **Evidence:** A plan of your activity with photos or a sketch, plus recall results compared with a rereading session. *(Grounding: model: Levels of processing (Craik and Lockhart 1972))*
        - *Applied: Peer role-play rehearsing a professional scenario.* You run a role-play with peers that rehearses a real professional scenario from your course, taking turns in each role and debriefing on what went wrong. You then use what came up to target further revision. This mirrors simulation training in practice and shows you using role-play to deepen engagement (for example, law students staging a mock client interview before an exam on professional conduct). **Evidence:** A scenario card or script, a debrief note listing the errors found, and the revision actions that followed. *(Grounding: professional: Rehearses procedures and scenarios physically, as in simulation training.)*
8. **Apply an exam strategy across formats and on the day**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Reflection and personal development (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Independent and self-directed learning (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Strategy adjusted across formats through reflection.* **Learning outcome:** The student can evaluate how an exam strategy performs across different formats and justify adjustments carried forward from one sitting to the next.
          After each exam you sit, you reflect specifically on how your strategy performed against that format: whether your time allocation matched how marks were distributed, whether you answered questions in the right order, and where panic or rushing cost you marks that your knowledge did not. You then carry a named adjustment forward into your strategy for the next, differently structured format rather than repeating an approach that already failed once. This demonstrates planning development through reflection on practice. **Evidence:** A short log of one strategy adjustment carried from each exam format into the next. *(Grounding: ing:doc:self-regulated-learning-goal-orientation-pintrich-2000; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
        - *Applied: Time plan carried across formats and refined.* **Learning outcome:** The student can independently apply and refine a time-management strategy across exam formats, adjusting it under pressure based on performance in each sitting.
