          You carry a single time-management strategy across every exam format you face, allocating minutes to match how marks are distributed and deciding in advance which questions to tackle first, then you perform to that plan under the real pressure of the exam room itself. After each sitting you review exactly where the plan held and where it slipped, and refine it before the next format rather than starting from nothing each time. This shows time managed against priorities under sustained pressure. **Evidence:** A single strategy sheet refined after each exam format, with timings and adjustments noted throughout. *(Grounding: professional: Plans time against priorities and performs to time under pressure.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Exam strategy reviewed through goal orientation.* You review how you approached each format of exam you have already sat, noticing where a focus on not doing worse than other people, rather than on showing what you actually know, led you to panic or to overspend time defending one answer. Drawing on a distinction between orientations aimed at mastering material and orientations aimed at comparative performance, you set a time plan for each upcoming format aimed squarely at demonstrating your own knowledge. This shows strategy applied across formats. **Evidence:** A written strategy per exam format with time allocations and a reflection linking it to goal orientation. *(Grounding: ing:doc:self-regulated-learning-goal-orientation-pintrich-2000)*
        - *Applied: Timing plan followed and reviewed after a real exam.* In a real, timed assessment you apply a plan made in advance: reading the whole paper first, allocating time according to how marks are distributed, answering your strongest questions before your weakest, and leaving time at the end to check your work. Afterwards you compare the plan against what actually happened and adjust it before the next format. This shows you performing to time under genuine pressure rather than losing the plan once the exam begins. **Evidence:** A pre-exam strategy sheet annotated afterwards with actual timings and changes for the next paper. *(Grounding: professional: Plans time against priorities and performs to time under pressure.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Exam strategy reviewed through mastery and performance goals.* You review your strategy across your exam formats using Pintrich's (2000) distinction between mastery and performance goal orientations, noting where a performance-avoidance focus, not wanting to do worse than others, led you to panic or overspend time on one question. You then set a time plan for each format aimed at showing what you know. This shows you applying a strategy across formats and on the day (for example, an economics student allocating minutes per mark across a mixed paper). **Evidence:** A written exam strategy for each format with time allocations, and a short reflection relating it to goal orientation. *(Grounding: ing:doc:self-regulated-learning-goal-orientation-pintrich-2000)*
        - *Applied: Timing plan used in a real exam and reviewed.* In a real exam or timed assessment, you apply a plan made in advance: reading the whole paper, allocating time by marks, answering your strongest questions first and leaving time to check. Afterwards you compare the plan with what happened and adjust it for the next format. This shows you performing to time under pressure (for example, a computing student in an exam mixing short coding questions with one long system design question). **Evidence:** Your pre-exam strategy sheet, annotated afterwards with actual timings and the changes you will make for the next paper. *(Grounding: professional: Plans time against priorities and performs to time under pressure.; professional: Vitae RDF B2: self-management)*
9. **Choose techniques by what works, judged by own recall**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Technique evidence weighed to reach a reasoned choice.* **Learning outcome:** The student can evaluate evidence of how effective a technique is, measured by their own recall, to reach a reasoned judgement about which method to use.
          You gather evidence on how well two or three revision techniques actually worked for you, measured by recall rather than by how confident each technique made you feel at the time, and you weigh that evidence directly against each other before settling on a method. You set out the comparison and the reasoning that led to your choice clearly enough that someone else could check it. This demonstrates evaluating evidence and arguments critically to reach a reasoned judgement. **Evidence:** A short comparison of techniques by recall evidence, with the reasoned choice and justification given. *(Grounding: benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
        - *Applied: Recommendation on technique argued from weighed evidence.* **Learning outcome:** The student can justify a recommended revision technique to someone else by weighing recall evidence against common assumptions, stating what would change the judgement.
          Asked to recommend a revision technique to someone facing a similar exam, you weigh the recall evidence from methods you have tried yourself against claims commonly made about what works, rather than simply repeating the most popular advice. You state which evidence carries most weight in your recommendation and note what would change your mind about it. This demonstrates evaluating evidence and arguments critically enough to reach a judgement someone else can act on. **Evidence:** A short recommendation stating the evidence weighed, the judgement reached and what would change it. *(Grounding: professional: Chooses methods using evidence of their own effectiveness rather than habit or popularity.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Revision techniques compared through delayed recall.* You revise comparable material using two or three different techniques, test your recall of each a week later rather than immediately, and interpret the results through the idea that methods which feel harder at the time, such as spacing sessions out or testing yourself, can produce better long-term retention than methods that feel easy and fluent in the moment. You choose which techniques to keep based on the delayed evidence, not on how comfortable they felt. This shows critical evaluation of your own methods. **Evidence:** A short write-up of methods tried, recall scores after a week and the techniques kept. *(Grounding: model: Desirable difficulties (Bjork 1994))*
        - *Applied: Technique choice argued from your own recall data.* When preparing for an important exam alongside other commitments, you choose your revision techniques by looking at your own recall results from earlier exams rather than by following whatever approach is most popular around you, and you set out that reasoning to someone whose judgement you trust. This shows independent, evidence-based judgement about how you personally learn best, rather than a choice made on habit or convenience. **Evidence:** A short technique rationale citing your own earlier recall results, with a response from someone you consulted. *(Grounding: professional: Chooses methods using evidence of their own effectiveness rather than habit or popularity.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Self-experiment on revision techniques using desirable difficulties.* You revise comparable material with two or three techniques, test your recall a week later, and interpret the results with the idea of desirable difficulties (Bjork 1994): methods that feel harder, such as spacing and self-testing, can give better long-term retention than methods that feel fluent. You choose techniques from the evidence, not from how they felt. This shows critical evaluation of your own methods (for example, a master's student in public health preparing for a statistics exam). **Evidence:** A short write-up of your self-experiment: methods, recall scores after a week, interpretation and the techniques you kept. *(Grounding: model: Desirable difficulties (Bjork 1994))*
        - *Applied: Technique choice for a professional exam, argued from recall.* When preparing for a professional exam alongside study or work, you choose techniques from your own recall data in earlier exams and explain the choice to a mentor or line manager. This shows independent, evidence-based judgement about how you learn best (for example, a trainee accountant on a graduate scheme choosing timed question practice that worked for them before, rather than the group's lecture-notes approach). **Evidence:** A technique rationale for your mentor or tutor, citing your recall results from earlier exams, with their written response. *(Grounding: practice: professional body exam taken during study or work)*

<a id="researching-research-methods"></a>
## Researching and Research Methods

### Introductory

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Explain how a research project differs from an essay | L3 | l3/knowledge.3 "Be aware of the nature of the area of study or work." | direct | `researching-research-methods#i1` |
| 2 | Formulate a research question, rationale and aims | L4 | l4/application.3 "Initiate and use appropriate investigation to inform actions." | direct | `researching-research-methods/01` |
| 3 | Narrow a topic to a question one can research | L5 | 4.12.b4 "an understanding of the limits of their knowledge" | direct | `researching-research-methods#i3` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

1. **Explain how a research project differs from an essay**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Research and enquiry methods (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Research cycle set against essay-based argument.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain how a research project's method-based cycle, posing a question, gathering evidence and reporting openly, differs from an essay's use of existing argument.
          You set out the stages a piece of research moves through: posing a question, choosing methods to gather evidence, analysing what is found, and reporting it openly, and you contrast this with how an essay is built from existing reading and argument. You identify which stages make a piece of work research rather than commentary, and why each stage affects how trustworthy the findings are. **Evidence:** A short written comparison setting out the research cycle's stages against how an essay is constructed. *(Grounding: model: Research Skill Development framework (Willison and O'Regan 2007); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/research-and-enquiry-methods)*
        - *Applied: Methods and findings explained to someone unfamiliar with research.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain to someone unfamiliar with research how a method-based enquiry differs from an opinion piece, covering how evidence was gathered, checked and reported with its limits.
          You explain to someone unfamiliar with research how a method-based enquiry differs from an opinion piece or essay, covering how the question was chosen, how evidence was gathered and checked, and how findings are reported honestly, including their limits. You show why following a method, rather than only reasoning from what you already believe, changes how trustworthy the conclusions are. **Evidence:** A short explanation, written for a non-specialist, of how a method-based enquiry differs from an argued essay. *(Grounding: professional: Explains how evidence gathered through a defined method differs from opinion or argument alone.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/research-and-enquiry-methods)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Research project brief compared with an essay brief.* You compare a short research project brief with an essay brief using a recognised framework for research skill development, mapping each task onto stages such as embarking and clarifying, finding and generating, and analysing and synthesising evidence. In a short table you show which stages each task emphasises, noting that the project requires you to generate new evidence rather than build an argument from existing reading alone. **Evidence:** A comparison table setting an essay brief against a project brief using the framework's stages, with a short explanation. *(Grounding: model: Research Skill Development framework (Willison and O'Regan 2007))*
        - *Applied: Annotated summary distinguishing an investigation from an essay.* You look at a real investigation and explain how it differs from writing an essay. You show that it starts from a question, collects evidence systematically, and reports what was found, rather than building a case from reading alone, and you note where the investigation's structure diverges from an essay's structure and why that matters for its findings. **Evidence:** A short annotated summary of a real investigation, labelling its question, evidence and findings, compared with an essay. *(Grounding: professional: Distinguishes a question-led investigation's structure from an argument built solely from reading.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Essay and project briefs compared against research skill facets.* You compare an essay brief with a small research project brief using the six facets of Willison and O'Regan's (2007) Research Skill Development framework, such as embarking and clarifying, finding and generating, and analysing and synthesising. In a short table you show which facets each task stresses, noting that the project asks you to generate your own evidence (for example, an extended project on recycling habits in your college). **Evidence:** A comparison table of an essay brief and a project brief against the framework's facets, with a short written explanation. *(Grounding: model: Research Skill Development framework (Willison and O'Regan 2007))*
        - *Applied: Annotated summary of a youth club members' survey.* You look at a real investigation in a community or workplace setting and explain how it differs from writing an essay (for example, a youth club's survey of members before changing its opening hours). You show that it starts from a question, collects evidence and reports what was found, rather than arguing a case from reading alone. **Evidence:** A short annotated summary of the real investigation, labelling its question, evidence and findings, compared with an essay you wrote. *(Grounding: practice: youth club or community group consultation)*
2. **Formulate a research question, rationale and aims**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.6: a range of qualitative, quantitative and mixed methods, as appropriate to the research question (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.16: generate and explore appropriate psychological hypotheses and research questions (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Research and enquiry methods (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Hypothesis and question generated from a problem area.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain how a testable research question, its rationale and its aims are generated from a broad problem area and checked against each other for consistency.
          Starting from a broad problem area, you generate a testable question or hypothesis, then set out the rationale for why it matters and the specific aims that will answer it. You check that each aim is something you can actually achieve with the time and evidence available, and you revise the question until rationale and aims line up with each other. **Evidence:** A worked example moving from a broad problem to a question, rationale and aims, with revision notes. *(Grounding: ing:doc:research-skill-development-willison-2007; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.16)*
        - *Applied: Question and aims specified for a commissioned enquiry.* **Learning outcome:** The student can formulate a workable research question, state its rationale and specify aims that can be checked for progress, within the resources realistically available.
          You are asked to scope a piece of work and must generate a workable question from a general concern, then state why it matters and what the aims will deliver. You check the question is something that can genuinely be explored with the resources available, and you set out aims that a commissioner could check progress against. **Evidence:** A specification document stating the question, rationale and aims, checked against the resources available. *(Grounding: professional: Defines a research or evaluation question for a client or policy need, with its purpose and scope.; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.16)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Proposal outline annotated against a research skill facet.* You draft a research question, rationale and aims for a first proposal, working through a stage of a recognised research skill development framework concerned with working out what knowledge is needed and why. You annotate how your rationale demonstrates that need and how each aim follows logically from the question, checking that nothing in the aims goes beyond what the question actually asks. **Evidence:** A one-page proposal outline with question, rationale and aims, annotated against the framework's relevant stage. *(Grounding: ing:doc:research-skill-development-willison-2007)*
        - *Applied: Research brief agreed to meet a defined need.* You define a question for a real need, setting out its purpose and scope, then turn a stated worry or problem into a specific question with aims that are agreed by whoever holds the need. You show how the need itself, not just your own interest, shaped the question, and you record the agreement in writing before moving on. **Evidence:** The agreed research brief showing the question, purpose, scope and aims, with written confirmation that it was agreed. *(Grounding: professional: Defines a research or evaluation question for a stated need, with its purpose and scope.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Proposal outline annotated against the embark and clarify facet.* You draft a research question, rationale and aims for a first proposal, working through the embark and clarify facet of Willison and O'Regan's (2007) Research Skill Development framework, which is about working out what knowledge is needed and why. You annotate how your rationale shows the need and how each aim follows from the question (for example, a history proposal on how local newspapers reported the 1926 General Strike). **Evidence:** A one-page proposal outline with question, rationale and aims, annotated against the embark and clarify facet. *(Grounding: ing:doc:research-skill-development-willison-2007)*
        - *Applied: Agreed research brief for a local gym.* You define a question for a real client need, with its purpose and scope (for example, a business student on a live brief who turns a local gym's worry about losing members into a question about why new members cancel within three months, with aims agreed with the manager). You show how the client's need, not just your interest, shaped the question. **Evidence:** The agreed research brief showing the question, purpose, scope and aims, with the client's email confirming them. *(Grounding: professional: Defines a research or evaluation question for a client or policy need, with its purpose and scope.; professional: GSR technical framework: identifying research needs, designing and specifying research)*
3. **Narrow a topic to a question one can research**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.6: a range of qualitative, quantitative and mixed methods, as appropriate to the research question (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.16: generate and explore appropriate psychological hypotheses and research questions (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Research and enquiry methods (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Testable question generated and refined from a broad area.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain how a broad area of interest is narrowed into a specific, testable research question by checking each version against the evidence it would require.
          You take a broad area of interest and generate a series of increasingly specific, testable questions, checking each one against what evidence would actually be needed to answer it. You refine the question until it is narrow enough to investigate properly, keeping a record of each version and the reason for narrowing it further. **Evidence:** A sequence of question versions from broad to specific, each annotated with the reason for narrowing it. *(Grounding: model: PICO question framework (Richardson et al. 1995); benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.16)*
        - *Applied: Enquiry scoped to fit real constraints on time and access.* **Learning outcome:** The student can narrow a broad research concern into a specific question that fits the time and access available, recording what has been excluded from the final scope.
          Given a broad concern and limited time, you generate a specific question that can realistically be explored with the access and resources you have. You check the question against what data collection would actually require, negotiate scope with whoever controls access, and document what has been excluded from the final enquiry and why. **Evidence:** A scoping document recording the narrowed question, the constraints considered and what was excluded. *(Grounding: professional: Negotiates the scope of an enquiry against the time and access actually available.; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.16)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Broad topic narrowed using a structured question framework.* You narrow a broad topic to a question you can actually answer by applying a structured framework that breaks a question into its population, intervention or focus, comparison and outcome. You show each step from topic to question and note what you deliberately left out and why, recognising the limits of what can be researched in the time available. **Evidence:** A topic-narrowing worksheet showing successive versions of the question, the framework's breakdown and your reasons for each cut. *(Grounding: model: PICO question framework (Richardson et al. 1995))*
        - *Applied: Scoped question agreed within real time and access limits.* You narrow a broad question from a real setting to one you can answer with the time and access you actually have, turning a general concern into something specific and measurable. You discuss the scope with whoever holds the setting, agree what is realistic, and record in writing what you decided not to cover and why. **Evidence:** Notes from the scoping conversation and the final question, with a list of what was left out and why. *(Grounding: professional: Negotiates the scope of an enquiry against the time and access actually available.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: PICO worksheet narrowing a broad topic.* You narrow a broad topic to a question you can answer by applying the PICO framework (Richardson et al. 1995): population, intervention, comparison and outcome. You show each step from topic to question and note what you left out and why, recognising the limits of what you can research in the time available (for example, a physiotherapy student narrowing exercise and back pain to one adult group and one outcome). **Evidence:** A topic-narrowing worksheet showing successive versions of the question, the PICO breakdown and your reasons for each cut. *(Grounding: model: PICO question framework (Richardson et al. 1995))*
        - *Applied: Scoped question agreed with a river trust coordinator.* You narrow a broad question from a real setting to one you can answer with the time and access you have (for example, a geography student volunteering with a river trust who turns plastic pollution in the river into a question about litter counts at three sites over six weeks). You discuss the scope with the volunteer coordinator and record what you decided not to cover. **Evidence:** Notes from the scoping conversation and the final question, with a list of what was left out and why. *(Grounding: practice: environmental charity volunteering)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Psychology 3.6: a range of qualitative, quantitative and mixed methods, as appropriate to the research question (direct; lands on `researching-research-methods/01`, `researching-research-methods#i3`)
- Psychology 3.16: generate and explore appropriate psychological hypotheses and research questions (direct; lands on `researching-research-methods/01`, `researching-research-methods#i3`)
- Universal: Research and enquiry methods (direct; lands on `researching-research-methods#i1`, `researching-research-methods/01`, `researching-research-methods#i3`)

### Intermediate

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 4 | Structure and write a literature review and a research report | L4 | 4.10.b4 "communicate the results of their study/work accurately and reliably" | direct | `researching-research-methods/02` |
| 5 | Write a research proposal that answers each of its questions | L5 | 4.12.b3 "knowledge of the main methods of enquiry" | direct | `researching-research-methods/c1` |
| 6 | Review the literature as critique, to place own research in it | L6 | 4.15.b3.2 "to describe and comment upon particular aspects of current research" | direct | `researching-research-methods#d3` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

4. **Structure and write a literature review and a research report**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.18: with a particular emphasis on conducting and reporting empirical research (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Research and enquiry methods (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Empirical findings reported through a structured report format.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse how a structured empirical report and a thematically organised literature review combine to justify and present a piece of research clearly.
          You conduct a small piece of empirical research and report it using a standard structure covering introduction, method, results and discussion, checking that each section does only the job it is meant to do. You write a literature review beforehand that groups existing studies by theme rather than listing them, so the report's introduction can justify the study clearly. **Evidence:** A structured empirical report with a preceding literature review, each section checked against its intended purpose. *(Grounding: ing:doc:research-skill-development-willison-2007; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.18)*
        - *Applied: Empirical findings reported for a non-specialist audience.* **Learning outcome:** The student can apply a clear reporting structure to present empirical findings and a preceding review of existing evidence to an audience unfamiliar with the research.
          You conduct and report a small piece of empirical work for an audience that did not run the study, structuring the report so the method and results are clear even to someone unfamiliar with the detail. You write a short preceding review of existing evidence so the reader understands why the question mattered before seeing your own findings. **Evidence:** A structured empirical report with a preceding evidence review, written for a non-specialist audience. *(Grounding: professional: Reviews existing evidence and reports findings for decision-makers.; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.18)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Literature review and report draft checked against two facets.* You write a short literature review and a project report, using two stages of a recognised research skill framework, one concerned with analysing and synthesising sources and the other with communicating findings clearly, to check each part. You show that the review groups sources by theme and critiques them rather than summarising one at a time, and that the report follows a clear, logical structure. **Evidence:** A literature review and report draft, annotated to show synthesis across sources and the purpose of each section. *(Grounding: ing:doc:research-skill-development-willison-2007)*
        - *Applied: Evidence review with an executive summary for decision-makers.* You review existing evidence and report findings for people who need to act on them, producing a short evidence review with an executive summary aimed at busy readers. You choose a structure that suits those readers while keeping the review accurate and properly sourced, and you check the summary still reflects the full review's conclusions fairly. **Evidence:** The evidence review and report with its executive summary, and feedback on its usefulness for decision-making. *(Grounding: professional: Reviews existing evidence and reports findings for decision-makers.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Thematic literature review and structured report draft.* You write a short literature review and a project report, using the analyse and synthesise and the communicate and apply facets of Willison and O'Regan's (2007) Research Skill Development framework to check each part. You show that the review groups sources by theme and critiques them rather than summarising one at a time, and that the report follows a clear structure (for example, an education report on homework policy with introduction, method, results and discussion). **Evidence:** A literature review and report draft, annotated to show synthesis across sources and the purpose of each report section. *(Grounding: ing:doc:research-skill-development-willison-2007)*
        - *Applied: Evidence review with executive summary for councillors.* You review existing evidence and report findings for people who need to act on them (for example, a public health student on placement with a local council producing a short evidence review on active travel schemes, with an executive summary for councillors). You choose a structure that suits busy readers while keeping the review accurate and properly sourced. **Evidence:** The evidence review and report with its executive summary, and feedback from the placement supervisor on its usefulness. *(Grounding: professional: Reviews existing evidence and reports findings for decision-makers.; professional: GSR technical framework: analysis and interpretation; leading, communicating and championing social research)*
5. **Write a research proposal that answers each of its questions**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.6: a range of qualitative, quantitative and mixed methods, as appropriate to the research question (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.16: generate and explore appropriate psychological hypotheses and research questions (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Research and enquiry methods (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Proposal sections tested against the methods they rely on.* **Learning outcome:** The student can evaluate whether each section of a research proposal, its question, method, timeline and resourcing, can actually generate the evidence the method promises.
          You write a research proposal and check that its chosen methods of enquiry can actually answer the question it sets, testing each section, question, method, timeline and resourcing, against what the evidence will need to show. Where a section's method would not generate evidence strong enough to answer its question, you revise the method rather than the question. **Evidence:** A proposal with each section tested against the evidence its chosen method could realistically produce. *(Grounding: ing:doc:research-skill-development-willison-2007; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/research-and-enquiry-methods)*
        - *Applied: Proposal defended against questions about its own methods.* **Learning outcome:** The student can defend a research proposal by explaining, for each section, why its chosen method fits the question and what evidence it will produce.
          You write a proposal for a piece of work and prepare to defend it against questions about whether its methods of enquiry will actually answer what it sets out to answer. For each section you can state the method used, why it suits the question, and what evidence it will produce, so a reviewer can check the plan holds together before any work begins. **Evidence:** A proposal document with a short defence note explaining why each method fits the question it answers. *(Grounding: professional: Writes a research specification or bid that sets out the question, method, outputs and plan.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/research-and-enquiry-methods)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Full proposal checked section by section for workability.* You write a full research proposal and check that each section answers its own question: what you will study, why, how, when and with what resources. You use a stage of a recognised research skill framework concerned with organising and managing a project to test whether your plan, timeline and methods are actually workable, and you revise any weak sections you find. **Evidence:** A complete research proposal with a checklist mapping each section to the question it answers, and a revision log. *(Grounding: ing:doc:research-skill-development-willison-2007)*
        - *Applied: Bid or specification written for a real commissioner.* You write a short bid or specification for a real commissioner, setting out the question, method, outputs, timescale and costs for a piece of work someone else needs done. You decide what can realistically be delivered within the time and budget on offer, and you justify those choices clearly to whoever will decide whether to fund the work. **Evidence:** The submitted bid or specification, with the commissioner's response or written feedback on it. *(Grounding: professional: Writes a research specification or bid that sets out the question, method, outputs and plan.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Full proposal checked section by section for workability.* You write a full research proposal and check that each section answers its question: what you will study, why, how, when and with what resources. You use the organise and manage facet of Willison and O'Regan's (2007) Research Skill Development framework to test whether your plan, timeline and methods are workable, and revise weak sections (for example, a criminology proposal on how young people perceive stop and search). **Evidence:** A complete research proposal with a checklist mapping each section to the question it answers, and a revision log. *(Grounding: ing:doc:research-skill-development-willison-2007)*
        - *Applied: Survey bid for a local wildlife trust.* You write a short bid or specification for a real commissioner (for example, an environmental science student responding to a local wildlife trust's call for a small hedgehog survey, setting out the question, method, outputs, timescale and costs). You decide what can realistically be delivered and justify those choices to the funder. **Evidence:** The submitted bid or specification, with the commissioner's response or written feedback on it. *(Grounding: professional: Writes a research specification or bid that sets out the question, method, outputs and plan.; professional: Vitae RDF C2 Research management: research strategy)*
6. **Review the literature as critique, to place own research in it**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Research and enquiry methods (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Own enquiry positioned against existing methods of research.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse how earlier research methods and findings fall short, using that critique to justify the specific approach taken in their own enquiry.
          You review how previous work in your area has approached its questions, examining the methods of enquiry used and where they fall short, then use that critique to justify the specific approach your own research takes. You show that your project is not simply repeating earlier work but responding directly to a gap or weakness you have identified in it. **Evidence:** A written critique of existing approaches that justifies the specific method chosen for your own enquiry. *(Grounding: model: Create a Research Space (CARS) model (Swales 1990); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/research-and-enquiry-methods)*
        - *Applied: New work positioned against the limits of earlier enquiry.* **Learning outcome:** The student can position a new piece of work against the limitations of earlier research, presenting clearly which gap in existing enquiry it is intended to close.
          Before starting a piece of work, you review the methods and findings of enquiries that came before it, identify where their approach fell short or left questions open, and position your own work to address that specific limitation. You present this positioning clearly enough that someone reviewing your plan can see exactly what gap it is meant to close. **Evidence:** A positioning brief identifying limits in prior enquiry and how the current work responds to them. *(Grounding: professional: Reviews prior work fairly and positions a new piece of enquiry against its identified gaps.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/research-and-enquiry-methods)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Literature review opening structured through three established moves.* You structure the opening of your literature review using an established model for creating a research space: establishing the territory, establishing a niche by showing a gap or problem in existing studies, and occupying that niche with your own study. You critique key studies' methods and limits honestly, to show why your own project is needed rather than merely interesting. **Evidence:** A literature review section annotated with the three moves, showing where critique of earlier studies creates the gap. *(Grounding: model: Create a Research Space (CARS) model (Swales 1990))*
        - *Applied: Positioning note building on prior work's limits.* In a real project, you review what has already been done and show how your own work adds to it, identifying what earlier work missed or left unresolved and positioning your own enquiry to fill that specific gap. You critique the earlier work fairly rather than dismissing it, and you use its limits to justify the approach you are taking. **Evidence:** A short positioning note summarising prior work, its limits and how your own project builds on it. *(Grounding: professional: Reviews prior work fairly and positions a new piece of enquiry against its identified gaps.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Literature review opening structured with the CARS moves.* You structure the opening of your literature review using Swales' (1990) Create a Research Space model: establish the territory, establish a niche by showing a gap or problem in existing studies, and occupy the niche with your own study. You critique key studies' methods and limits to show why your project is needed (for example, a marketing dissertation showing that research on trust in influencers rarely covers older consumers). **Evidence:** A literature review section annotated with the three moves, showing where critique of earlier studies creates the gap. *(Grounding: model: Create a Research Space (CARS) model (Swales 1990))*
        - *Applied: Positioning note building on earlier accessibility audits.* In a work or community project, you review what has already been done and show how your work adds to it (for example, a computing student on placement who reviews earlier accessibility audits of the company's app, identifies what they missed, and positions their own user testing to fill that gap). You critique the earlier work fairly and use it to justify your approach. **Evidence:** A short positioning paper or project initiation note summarising prior work, its limits and how your project builds on it. *(Grounding: practice: industry placement project)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Psychology 3.6: a range of qualitative, quantitative and mixed methods, as appropriate to the research question (direct; lands on `researching-research-methods/c1`)
- Psychology 3.16: generate and explore appropriate psychological hypotheses and research questions (direct; lands on `researching-research-methods/c1`)
- Psychology 3.18: with a particular emphasis on conducting and reporting empirical research (direct; lands on `researching-research-methods/02`)
- Universal: Research and enquiry methods (direct; lands on `researching-research-methods/02`, `researching-research-methods/c1`, `researching-research-methods#d3`)

### Advanced

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | Apply research ethics, including consent and anonymisation | L5 | 4.12.b8 "personal responsibility and decision-making" | direct | `researching-research-methods/03` |
| 8 | Choose and justify a methodology and methods for a question | L6 | l6/application.3 "where appropriate design relevant research"; 4.15.b2 "deploy accurately established techniques of analysis and enquiry" | direct | `researching-research-methods/c2` |
