**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

7. **Apply research ethics, including consent and anonymisation**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Research and enquiry methods (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Ethics, integrity and values (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Ethical issues identified and resolved before data collection.* **Learning outcome:** The student can justify the ethical decisions made before data collection, including consent, anonymisation and storage, as a reasoned judgement rather than a procedural formality.
          Before collecting any data, you identify the ethical issues your study raises, consent, anonymisation, storage and the risk of harm, and you set out how each will be handled and why that handling is appropriate. You treat this as a reasoned judgement you are responsible for, not a form to complete, and you can explain each decision if challenged. **Evidence:** A written ethical risk assessment identifying each issue raised and the reasoned response to it. *(Grounding: ing:doc:research-skill-development-willison-2007; model: ESRC Framework for Research Ethics (ESRC 2015); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/ethics)*
        - *Applied: Ethical responsibilities acted on throughout a piece of work.* **Learning outcome:** The student can act on ethical responsibilities throughout a piece of research, adjusting consent, anonymisation or storage arrangements when a specific concern is identified.
          Throughout a piece of work involving other people's data or participation, you act on your ethical responsibilities rather than treating them as a one-off approval step, checking consent remains valid, anonymisation holds, and storage stays secure as the work progresses. You can point to specific moments where you changed your approach because of an ethical concern you noticed. **Evidence:** A log of ethical checks made during the work, with at least one documented change made in response. *(Grounding: professional: Conducts research within an employer's or funder's ethics and data protection requirements.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/ethics)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Ethics application drafted for a study with human participants.* You prepare an ethics application for a study involving human participants, applying established research ethics principles to consent, anonymisation and data storage. You use a recognised scale of researcher independence to show that you are making these decisions yourself, with reasons, rather than simply following a fixed checklist handed to you by someone else. **Evidence:** Your ethics application, participant information sheet, consent form and anonymisation plan, with any requested changes recorded. *(Grounding: ing:doc:research-skill-development-willison-2007; model: ESRC Framework for Research Ethics (ESRC 2015))*
        - *Applied: Ethics decision record kept for a real piece of research.* You run a piece of research within an organisation's ethics and data protection rules, checking your plan with whoever is responsible for safeguarding and data protection before collecting anything, especially where participants might be vulnerable. You record each ethical decision and the reason behind it, and you report results so that no individual participant can be identified. **Evidence:** A decision record of ethical and data protection choices, with sign-off and the anonymised final report. *(Grounding: professional: Conducts research within an employer's or funder's ethics and data protection requirements.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Ethics application for an interview study with carers.* You prepare an ethics application for a study with human participants, applying the ESRC research ethics principles to consent, anonymisation and data storage. You use the levels of student autonomy in Willison and O'Regan's (2007) Research Skill Development framework to show you are making these decisions yourself rather than following set steps (for example, a nursing study interviewing family carers, with a consent form, a right to withdraw and a plan to remove identifying details). **Evidence:** Your ethics application, participant information sheet, consent form and anonymisation plan, with the panel's approval or requested changes. *(Grounding: ing:doc:research-skill-development-willison-2007; model: ESRC Framework for Research Ethics (ESRC 2015))*
        - *Applied: Ethics decision record for a youth academy sleep survey.* You run research within an organisation's ethics and data protection rules (for example, a sport science student on placement with a football academy surveying players' sleep, who checks the plan with the club's safeguarding lead and data protection officer because some players are under 18). You record each ethical decision and why you made it, and report results so no individual can be identified. **Evidence:** A decision record of ethical and data protection choices, with the data protection officer's sign-off and the anonymised report. *(Grounding: professional: Conducts research within an employer's or funder's ethics and data protection requirements.; professional: Vitae RDF C1 Professional conduct: ethics, principles and sustainability; legal requirements)*
8. **Choose and justify a methodology and methods for a question**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.6: a range of qualitative, quantitative and mixed methods, as appropriate to the research question (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.11: the collection of original quantitative, qualitative or mixed methods data (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.16: generate and explore appropriate psychological hypotheses and research questions (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 4.9: adopted multiple perspectives in psychology and systematically analysed research paradigms (indirect)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 2.1: Courses also develop in students, a range of research skills and methods of enquiry for investigating cognitions and behaviour (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Research and enquiry methods (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Qualitative, quantitative or mixed methods chosen for fit.* **Learning outcome:** The student can justify a choice between qualitative, quantitative and mixed methods by comparing the evidence each would produce against what the research question requires.
          Facing a research question, you consider qualitative, quantitative and mixed methods in turn and choose whichever combination actually fits the question, rather than defaulting to the method you find most familiar or comfortable. You justify the choice by explaining what kind of evidence each option would produce and why that evidence answers the question better than the alternatives. **Evidence:** A written justification comparing qualitative, quantitative and mixed options against the research question. *(Grounding: ing:doc:research-skill-development-willison-2007; model: Research onion (Saunders, Lewis and Thornhill 2019); benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.6)*
        - *Applied: Appropriate methods chosen and defended for a live question.* **Learning outcome:** The student can select and defend a combination of research methods for a live question, presenting the justification clearly enough for another person to scrutinise before work begins.
          Given a live question that needs answering, you weigh qualitative, quantitative and mixed approaches against what is actually achievable and what evidence each would yield, then choose and defend the combination that best fits the question rather than the easiest option available. You present this justification clearly enough for someone else to check your reasoning before work begins. **Evidence:** A methods justification document comparing qualitative, quantitative and mixed options against the live question. *(Grounding: professional: Selects and defends research methods that fit the question, the evidence needed and the constraints.; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.6)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Methodology justified through a layered decision model.* You justify your methodology using a recognised layered model of research design, working through its layers from research philosophy and approach to methodological choice, strategy, time horizon and techniques. You link each layer's choice directly to your question, checking that the methods you select will actually produce the kind of evidence the question needs. **Evidence:** A methodology section with a diagram of your choice at each layer and a written justification for each. *(Grounding: ing:doc:research-skill-development-willison-2007; model: Research onion (Saunders, Lewis and Thornhill 2019))*
        - *Applied: Methods compared and defended within real constraints.* You select and defend methods for a real project where time, cost and access are all limited, comparing two or more realistic options against what each would actually tell you. You explain the trade-offs of each option to whoever will use the findings, and you show that your final choice fits both the question and the decision the evidence needs to support. **Evidence:** A methods options paper comparing approaches against the question and constraints, with the final decision recorded. *(Grounding: professional: Selects and defends research methods that fit the question, the evidence needed and the constraints.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Methodology section justified layer by layer.* You justify your methodology using the research onion (Saunders, Lewis and Thornhill 2019), working through its layers from research philosophy and approach to methodological choice, strategy, time horizon and techniques. You link each choice to your question, and use the find and generate facet of Willison and O'Regan's (2007) Research Skill Development framework to show how your methods will produce the evidence needed (for example, a psychology project choosing mixed methods). **Evidence:** A methodology section with a diagram of your choice at each layer and a written justification for each. *(Grounding: ing:doc:research-skill-development-willison-2007; model: Research onion (Saunders, Lewis and Thornhill 2019))*
        - *Applied: Methods options paper for a junction congestion study.* You select and defend methods for a real project with limits on time, cost and access (for example, a civil engineering student on placement choosing between new traffic counts and existing sensor data to assess congestion at a junction, and explaining the trade-offs to the project lead). You show that your choice fits the question and the decision the evidence will serve. **Evidence:** A methods options paper comparing approaches against the question and constraints, with the project lead's decision recorded. *(Grounding: professional: Selects and defends research methods that fit the question, the evidence needed and the constraints.; professional: GSR technical framework: knowledge of research methods; identifying research needs, designing and specifying research)*
9. **Plan and scope a dissertation-length project**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Research and enquiry methods (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Extended enquiry scoped around its own methods of research.* **Learning outcome:** The student can construct an extended research plan that scopes timeline and resourcing around its chosen methods, with milestones and fallback options for stages at risk of delay.
          You plan an extended piece of research by first deciding which methods of enquiry the project will use, then scoping the work's size, timeline and resourcing around what those methods can realistically deliver. You set milestones tied to each stage of the method, and you build in fallback options for stages most likely to run into difficulty or delay. **Evidence:** An extended project plan with milestones tied to each stage of the chosen method, including fallback options. *(Grounding: ing:doc:research-skill-development-willison-2007; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/research-and-enquiry-methods)*
        - *Applied: Large-scale enquiry planned around realistic delivery constraints.* **Learning outcome:** The student can plan a large, independent research project against realistic time, budget and access constraints, agreeing in advance how major risks to delivery will be handled.
          You are responsible for planning a large, independent piece of enquiry and must scope its methods of research against what time, budget and access will realistically allow before committing to a plan. You build a schedule with clear milestones, identify the stages most at risk of slipping, and agree with stakeholders what would have to change if a major risk occurred. **Evidence:** A delivery plan for a large enquiry, with milestones, a risk register and an agreed response to major risks. *(Grounding: professional: Plans and manages a research project to time, scope and budget.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/research-and-enquiry-methods)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Dissertation plan built with scope, milestones and risks.* You scope and plan an extended, independent piece of research at a stage of a recognised autonomy framework where you set the question and methods yourself, rather than following a set brief. You produce a plan with milestones, risks and fallback options, and you justify in writing what is deliberately in scope and what is left out. **Evidence:** A dissertation plan with scope statement, milestone chart, risk register and a note of what is out of scope. *(Grounding: ing:doc:research-skill-development-willison-2007)*
        - *Applied: Extended project planned and managed within a fixed budget.* You plan and manage an extended research project for an organisation, working within a fixed budget and a deadline set by an external event. You agree the project's scope with the people who hold a stake in it, track progress against the plan, and renegotiate that scope openly whenever a new risk makes the original plan unworkable. **Evidence:** The project plan, budget tracker and progress reports, with a stakeholder note on delivery against the agreed scope. *(Grounding: professional: Plans and manages a research project to time, scope and budget.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Dissertation plan with scope, milestones and risks.* You scope and plan a dissertation at the open-ended upper levels of student autonomy in Willison and O'Regan's (2007) Research Skill Development framework, where you set the question and methods yourself. You produce a plan with milestones, risks and fallback options, and justify what is in and out of scope (for example, a master's dissertation in architecture on the adaptive reuse of former mill buildings). **Evidence:** A dissertation plan with scope statement, milestone chart, risk register and a supervisor-agreed note of what is out of scope. *(Grounding: ing:doc:research-skill-development-willison-2007)*
        - *Applied: Visitor research project delivered for a local museum.* You plan and manage a research project for an organisation within time and budget (for example, a master's student in museum studies leading visitor research for a local museum, with a fixed budget for participant vouchers and a deadline before an exhibition redesign). You agree scope with stakeholders, track progress, and renegotiate when risks arise. **Evidence:** The project plan, budget tracker and progress reports, with a stakeholder's note on delivery against the agreed scope. *(Grounding: professional: Plans and manages a research project to time, scope and budget.; professional: Vitae RDF C2 Research management: project planning and delivery; risk management)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Psychology 3.6: a range of qualitative, quantitative and mixed methods, as appropriate to the research question (direct; lands on `researching-research-methods/c2`)
- Psychology 3.11: the collection of original quantitative, qualitative or mixed methods data (direct; lands on `researching-research-methods/c2`)
- Psychology 3.16: generate and explore appropriate psychological hypotheses and research questions (direct; lands on `researching-research-methods/c2`)
- Psychology 4.9: adopted multiple perspectives in psychology and systematically analysed research paradigms (indirect; lands on `researching-research-methods/c2`)
- Psychology 2.1: Courses also develop in students, a range of research skills and methods of enquiry for investigating cognitions and behaviour (direct; lands on `researching-research-methods/c2`)
- Universal: Research and enquiry methods (direct; lands on `researching-research-methods/03`, `researching-research-methods/c2`, `researching-research-methods/04`)
- Universal: Ethics, integrity and values (direct; lands on `researching-research-methods/03`)

### Keys at every tier

**Progression:**

- Psychology 3.10: rigorous specialist training is required to engender a critical understanding of the role of research design (direct)
- Psychology 4.8: constructed and tested research hypotheses and undertaken supervised research investigating specific lines of enquiry, both individually and in groups (direct)

**Floor:**

- Psychology 3.7: the ability to conduct and interpret appropriate statistical tests (indirect)
- Psychology 3.3: the inherent difficulties associated with extrapolating research conducted with Western samples to other cultures (indirect)
- Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect)
- Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (indirect)
- Universal: Independent and self-directed learning (indirect)
- Universal: Oral or other communication to audiences (indirect)

<a id="reflective-practice"></a>
## Reflective Practice

### Introductory

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Apply the describe, reflect, take action cycle | L3 | l3/autonomy.2 "" | direct | `reflective-practice/01` |
| 2 | Balance positives and negatives | L4 | 4.10.b6 ""; l4/autonomy.2 "" | direct | `reflective-practice/02` |
| 3 | Notice what went well and what didn't in your own work | L5 | 4.12.b8 "" | **UNATTRIBUTED** | `reflective-practice#i3` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

1. **Apply the describe, reflect, take action cycle**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Applying knowledge to practice or problems (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Reflection and personal development (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Reflection linked to a development step.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain the describe-reflect-act cycle as a structured sequence that converts an account of an experience into a planned development step.
          After a learning experience you set out what happened, consider why it went as it did, and identify what you will do differently. You use a simple structure, description then reflection then action, so each step depends on the last. The resulting plan links clearly back to the reflection that produced it, showing that the exercise results in a next step rather than only an account of events. **Evidence:** A written reflection with a clear action point, structured as description, analysis and planned development. *(Grounding: model: ERA cycle (Jasper 2013); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
        - *Applied: Practical action plan from a real reflection.* **Learning outcome:** The student can apply the describe-reflect-act cycle to a genuine learning experience, producing a written account that ends in one specific planned action.
          Following a real task or incident you write a brief account of what happened, think about why, and commit to one thing to do differently next time. You then act on it in a later situation and note the result. This turns your own experience into a planned change in practice, not just a description of an event, meeting an expectation to reflect and plan ongoing development. **Evidence:** A short reflective note ending in one planned action, with a follow-up note on how it went. *(Grounding: professional: Writes a structured reflective account of a practice event, ending in actions.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Three-part reflection on a learning task.* You write a short reflection on a recent learning task using a three-part cycle: describe what happened, reflect on why it went as it did and how you felt, then set one action to try next time. You keep the account tied to a single specific event rather than a general summary, so each stage builds clearly on the one before it and the action follows logically from the reflection. **Evidence:** A one-page entry with three labelled sections, describe, reflect and take action, kept as a record. *(Grounding: model: ERA cycle (Jasper 2013))*
        - *Applied: Structured reflection on a real incident.* You apply the describe, reflect, take action cycle to an event from paid work or a community role. You write what happened, think through why it unfolded that way, set one practical action, then try it out the next time a similar situation arises. This shows you can carry the cycle from a written exercise into a live setting and follow it through to a change in what you do. **Evidence:** A short structured account of a real event, ending in one action, with a note on how it went. *(Grounding: professional: Writes a structured reflective account of a practice event, ending in actions.; professional: NMC revalidation: written reflective accounts)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: ERA cycle reflection on a first lab session.* You write a short reflection on a recent learning task using the ERA cycle (Jasper 2013): experience, reflection, action. You describe what happened, reflect on why it went as it did and how you felt, and end with one action you will take next time (for example, after your first practical in a foundation year biology lab, when your results did not match the expected pattern). **Evidence:** A one-page reflective entry with three labelled sections, describe, reflect and take action, with tutor feedback on it. *(Grounding: model: ERA cycle (Jasper 2013))*
        - *Applied: Structured account of a customer complaint at work.* You use the describe, reflect, take action cycle on an event from a part-time job or volunteering (for example, handling a customer complaint during a busy weekend shift in a cafe). You write what happened, think through why it unfolded that way, set one practical action, then try it on a later shift, showing you can apply the cycle to real experience. **Evidence:** A short structured reflective account of the work event, ending in an action, with a note on how the action went. *(Grounding: professional: Writes a structured reflective account of a practice event, ending in actions.; professional: NMC revalidation: written reflective accounts)*
2. **Balance positives and negatives**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Reflection and personal development (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Weighing strengths and weaknesses to plan development.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain why a review of personal learning or practice should weigh strengths and weaknesses evenly before identifying a priority for development.
          When reflecting on your own learning or practice, you deliberately set out what went well before you consider what did not, so the review covers both sides rather than only the problems. You use this balanced account to identify where effort should go next, turning an even-handed review of performance into a basis for planning further development rather than a one-sided judgement. **Evidence:** A balanced written review of one piece of performance, ending in a short development point. *(Grounding: model: Gibbs' Reflective Cycle, evaluation stage (Gibbs 1988); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
        - *Applied: Even-handed review feeding a development plan.* **Learning outcome:** The student can produce a balanced written review of their own performance, giving equal weight to strengths and weaknesses and ending with one development priority.
          After a real piece of work you write a short review that gives equal attention to what went well and what did not, supporting each point with a specific example rather than a general impression. You use the balance of strengths and weaknesses to set a development priority, showing that reflecting on practice leads directly into planning what you will work on next. **Evidence:** A short written review balancing strengths and weaknesses, ending with one development priority. *(Grounding: professional: Reviews own practice critically and fairly, and considers other ways of working.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Balanced strengths and weaknesses review.* You reflect on a piece of your own work using the evaluation stage of a structured reflective cycle, which asks what was good and what was less effective about the experience. You set out strengths first, then weaknesses, each backed by a specific comment or piece of feedback, so the review is neither wholly positive nor wholly negative but weighs both sides fairly before you draw a conclusion. **Evidence:** A two-column table of strengths and weaknesses, each linked to a specific comment, with a balanced summary. *(Grounding: model: Gibbs' Reflective Cycle, evaluation stage (Gibbs 1988))*
        - *Applied: Fair review of a session you led.* After leading a real activity or session, you review what you did fairly, setting out what worked well alongside what did not, and consider other ways you could have approached it. You record both sides honestly rather than favouring one, back each point with a specific observation, and suggest one alternative approach you would try next time. **Evidence:** A review sheet listing balanced strengths and areas to develop, with a note on an alternative approach. *(Grounding: professional: Reviews own practice critically and fairly, and considers other ways of working.; professional: HCPC standards of proficiency: reflect critically and consider alternative approaches)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Balanced evaluation table for a history presentation.* You reflect on a piece of assessed work using the evaluation stage of Gibbs' reflective cycle (Gibbs 1988), which asks what was good and what was bad about the experience. You set out strengths first, then weaknesses, each backed by a feedback comment, so the reflection is neither wholly positive nor wholly negative (for example, a first-year history presentation where your research was praised but you ran over time). **Evidence:** A two-column evaluation table of strengths and weaknesses, each linked to a specific feedback comment, with a short balanced summary. *(Grounding: model: Gibbs' reflective cycle, evaluation stage (Gibbs 1988))*
        - *Applied: Fair review of a coaching session you led.* After a real performance, you review what you did fairly and consider other ways you could have worked (for example, a sport coaching student reviewing a session run for an under-12s football club, noting that the warm-up engaged the group well but drill instructions were too long). You record both sides honestly and suggest one alternative approach for next time. **Evidence:** A session review sheet with balanced strengths and areas to develop, signed by the club's lead coach. *(Grounding: professional: Reviews own practice critically and fairly, and considers other ways of working.; professional: HCPC standards of proficiency: reflect critically and consider alternative approaches)*
3. **Notice what went well and what didn't in your own work**
    - *Benchmark:* none matched at this tier (**UNATTRIBUTED**)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Spotting what worked in your own work.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain why reviewing their own work means identifying specific strengths and weaknesses separately rather than forming one overall impression.
          Looking back at a piece of your own work, you separate out what went well from what did not, rather than treating the outcome as simply good or bad overall. You identify at least one specific strength and one specific weakness, each tied to a particular part of what you did, so the noticing is concrete rather than a vague overall feeling about how things went. **Evidence:** A short note identifying one specific strength and one specific weakness in a piece of your own work. *(Grounding: model: Reflection-in-action and reflection-on-action (Schon 1983); benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
        - *Applied: Noticing strengths and gaps after a task.* **Learning outcome:** The student can identify, in a piece of their own completed work, one specific strength and one specific weakness before receiving feedback from others.
          Straight after completing a real task, you take a moment to identify one thing in your own work that went well and one thing that did not, before anyone else gives you feedback. You record both briefly so you are forming your own view of your performance first, rather than only reacting to what others tell you about it later. **Evidence:** A brief self-written note naming one strength and one gap, made before receiving outside feedback. *(Grounding: professional: Notices patterns in own contribution across repeated sessions of practice.; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: In-action and on-action noticing.* You keep brief notes during and after a task, drawing on the distinction between noticing things as they happen and noticing them only once you look back. While working, you jot down moments where something clearly went well or badly, then later compare those in-the-moment notes with what stands out once you have had time to think. This surfaces things you would otherwise only half remember. **Evidence:** Paired notes from one task, in-the-moment jottings and a later look-back entry, with differences marked. *(Grounding: model: Reflection-in-action and reflection-on-action (Schon 1983))*
        - *Applied: Session-by-session noticing log.* Across a series of sessions doing the same kind of activity, you note straight after each one a thing that went well and a thing that did not in your own contribution, rather than focusing only on how others responded. Over several sessions you look back across the notes and spot patterns in your own approach, not just in isolated single events, building a clearer picture of your own habits. **Evidence:** A dated log of five or more sessions, each noting one strength and one weakness in your own contribution. *(Grounding: professional: Notices patterns in own contribution across repeated sessions of practice.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Paired in-action and on-action notes from one task.* You keep notes during and after a task, using Schön's (1983) distinction between reflection-in-action, thinking while you are doing something, and reflection-on-action, looking back afterwards. You jot down moments where you noticed something going well or badly as it happened, then compare them with what you notice later (for example, a computing pair-programming session where you spotted a bug early but lost time on testing). **Evidence:** Paired notes from one task: in-the-moment jottings and a later reflection-on-action entry, with the differences highlighted. *(Grounding: model: Reflection-in-action and reflection-on-action (Schön 1983))*
        - *Applied: Session log from school reading volunteering.* In a volunteering role, you note after each session what went well and what did not in your own contribution (for example, as a reading volunteer in a primary school, noticing that a child engaged more when you let them choose the book, but lost focus when you corrected every word). Over several sessions you spot patterns in your own work, not only in the child's. **Evidence:** A dated log of five or more volunteering sessions, each noting one thing that went well and one that did not. *(Grounding: practice: school reading volunteer scheme)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Applying knowledge to practice or problems (direct; lands on `reflective-practice/01`)
- Universal: Reflection and personal development (direct; lands on `reflective-practice/01`, `reflective-practice/02`)
- Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect; lands on `reflective-practice/01`)

### Intermediate

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 4 | Reflect critically rather than describe | L4 | l4/autonomy.2 "" | direct | `reflective-practice/c1` |
| 5 | Turn reflection into a dated action plan | L5 | l5/autonomy.1 ""; 4.12.b7 "" | direct | `reflective-practice/c2` |
| 6 | Reflect on motivation and amend strategies that aren't working | L6 | 4.15.b5 "" | direct | `reflective-practice/04` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

4. **Reflect critically rather than describe**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 1.23: critically reflect on the language and terminology used within the discipline (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Reflection and personal development (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Moving from description to critical judgement.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse a descriptive account of their own practice to identify the assumptions and reasoning underlying what happened, producing a critical judgement rather than a narrative.
          Given an account of your own learning or practice, you rewrite the descriptive parts so that each one is followed by a question about why things happened that way, what assumptions were at work, and what else might explain the outcome. The revised version makes a judgement supported by reasoning, rather than only reporting a sequence of events, directly extending reflection into planning what to do differently. **Evidence:** A short piece of writing where each descriptive statement is paired with a critical question and judgement. *(Grounding: model: Four types of reflective writing (Hatton and Smith 1995); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
        - *Applied: Critical account of a real decision.* **Learning outcome:** The student can evaluate a real decision by examining the alternatives considered and the reasoning behind it, justifying what they would do differently.
          After a real decision or action, you write an account that goes beyond what happened to ask why you chose that course, what alternatives existed, and how your reasoning stands up to scrutiny. You identify at least one thing you would do differently and justify the change. This shows reflection feeding directly into a judgement about your own development rather than stopping at a narrative. **Evidence:** A written account of a decision that states the alternatives considered and the judgement reached. *(Grounding: professional: Reflects critically on own practice and considers alternative approaches.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Reflective writing revised from descriptive to critical.* You take an earlier reflective entry and rewrite it using a framework of four types of reflective writing: description, descriptive reflection, dialogic reflection and critical reflection. You label which type each paragraph currently shows, then revise the descriptive passages so they question your reasons, weigh other possible explanations and consider the wider context, rather than simply recounting what happened. **Evidence:** Original and revised reflective entries, each paragraph labelled by type, with notes on what changed. *(Grounding: model: Four types of reflective writing (Hatton and Smith 1995))*
        - *Applied: Critical reflection discussed with a reviewer.* You reflect critically on a real piece of your own practice, asking why you acted as you did and what else you could have done, rather than only describing the sequence of events. Someone experienced in the area reviews your account and comments on its depth. You revise the entry to move beyond recounting what happened, judging it instead against the standard expected. **Evidence:** A reflective account reviewed by an experienced colleague, with their written comments on the depth shown. *(Grounding: professional: Reflects critically on own practice and considers alternative approaches.; professional: HCPC standards of proficiency: reflect critically and consider alternative approaches)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Reflective entry rewritten from descriptive to critical.* You take an earlier reflective entry and rewrite it using Hatton and Smith's (1995) four types of reflective writing: descriptive writing, descriptive reflection, dialogic reflection and critical reflection. You label the type each paragraph shows, then revise the descriptive passages so they question your reasons, weigh other explanations and consider the wider context (for example, a social work student's entry on a shadowed home visit that recounted events but never asked why the family seemed wary). **Evidence:** Original and revised reflective entries, with each paragraph labelled by type and a note on what changed between them. *(Grounding: model: Four types of reflective writing (Hatton and Smith 1995))*
        - *Applied: Critical placement reflection discussed with a practice educator.* You reflect critically on your practice in a real setting, asking why you acted as you did and what else you could have done (for example, a diagnostic radiography student on placement who rushed a patient's positioning, and reflects on time pressure, communication and the alternatives). You move beyond recounting the event to judging it against the standards expected of you. **Evidence:** A placement reflective account discussed with your practice educator, with their written comments on the depth of reflection. *(Grounding: professional: Reflects critically on own practice and considers alternative approaches.; professional: HCPC standards of proficiency: reflect critically and consider alternative approaches)*
5. **Turn reflection into a dated action plan**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Reflection and personal development (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Turning a reflection into a dated plan.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain how a reflection on learning or practice should be converted into one or more dated, checkable actions that follow logically from what was noticed.
          Once you have reflected on a piece of learning or practice, you convert the main point into one or more actions, each with its own date and a way of checking it has been done. You explain how each action follows from the reflection, so a reader can trace a line from what you noticed to what you plan to change, rather than the two sitting separately. **Evidence:** A short reflection paired with a dated action, each action traceable back to a specific point raised. *(Grounding: model: SMART objectives (Doran 1981); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
        - *Applied: Dated plan built from a real reflection.* **Learning outcome:** The student can construct a dated action plan from a real reflection, then record on the stated date whether the action was completed and what changed.
          After reflecting on a real piece of work, you set one or more dated actions that follow directly from what the reflection found, rather than generic good intentions. You record a date by which each action will be completed, then return to the plan on that date to note whether it was done and what difference it made, closing the loop between reflecting and acting. **Evidence:** A dated action plan drawn from a real reflection, with a follow-up note on completion and effect. *(Grounding: professional: Plans and records development actions from reflection on practice.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: SMART action plan from a reflection.* You turn a reflection on your own performance into an action plan using a framework of specific, measurable, assignable, realistic and time-related objectives. You rewrite vague intentions, such as managing time better, into dated actions, and for each one you justify why it follows from something the reflection identified, so the plan is clearly tied to what you learned about yourself. **Evidence:** An action plan table of dated objectives, each linked to a line in the reflection, with a review column. *(Grounding: model: SMART objectives (Doran 1981))*
        - *Applied: Dated targets after an observed session.* Following a real session that someone else observed, you turn the resulting feedback and your own reflection into a short set of dated targets covering the next few weeks. You decide which targets matter most and explain your reasoning, then record progress against each one at agreed review points, so the plan is checked rather than simply written and forgotten. **Evidence:** A dated development plan with progress notes, reviewed and initialled at each agreed check-in point. *(Grounding: professional: Plans and records development actions from reflection on practice.; professional: Social Work England professional standards 4.6)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: SMART action plan drawn from a reflection.* You turn a reflection into an action plan using SMART objectives (Doran 1981): specific, measurable, assignable, realistic and time-related. You rewrite vague intentions such as managing time better into dated actions, and justify why each one follows from what the reflection found (for example, an engineering student planning to book two lab slots by set dates before a design report deadline). **Evidence:** An action plan table of dated SMART actions, each linked to a line in the reflection, with a review column. *(Grounding: model: SMART objectives (Doran 1981))*
        - *Applied: Dated development targets after a mentor observation.* You turn reflection from a placement into a dated development plan that someone else reviews (for example, a trainee teacher on school placement who, after a mentor observation, sets dated targets on questioning and classroom routines for the next four weeks). You choose which targets matter most and explain why, then record progress at each review date. **Evidence:** The dated development plan with progress notes, initialled by your school mentor at each review. *(Grounding: professional: Plans and records development actions from reflection on practice.; professional: Social Work England professional standards 4.6)*
6. **Reflect on motivation and amend strategies that aren't working**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Reflection and personal development (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Adjusting a learning strategy using reflection.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse why a learning strategy is not working, distinguishing a drop in motivation from a flaw in the approach itself, to justify a specific change.
          When a way of working is not delivering the results you expect, you reflect on both your motivation and the strategy itself, rather than assuming the problem is only effort. You identify the point where the approach breaks down, design a specific change, and set out how you will know, from your own evidence, whether the new approach is actually working better than the old one. **Evidence:** A short written comparison of the old and new strategy, with evidence for which one worked better. *(Grounding: model: Cyclical model of self-regulated learning (Zimmerman 2002); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
        - *Applied: Reflecting on motivation to change an approach.* **Learning outcome:** The student can evaluate a real approach that is not delivering results, make one specific change, and judge from the outcome whether to keep it.
          In a real piece of work, you notice that your motivation has dropped or that a way of working is not producing good results, and you reflect on why before deciding what to change. You make one specific adjustment, keep working at it for a set period, then judge from the outcome whether to keep the change, drop it, or adjust it again. **Evidence:** A short reflective note on motivation and strategy, with the change made and its later outcome. *(Grounding: professional: Uses feedback and supervision to find learning needs and change how one works.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Self-regulation review of a failing strategy.* You analyse a strategy that is not producing the results you want, using a cyclical model of self-regulated learning with forethought, performance and self-reflection phases. You track your own motivation and your results over several attempts, identify where the cycle is breaking down, and change the strategy, justifying the change with evidence you have gathered yourself rather than a hunch. **Evidence:** A strategy review with tracking data over several attempts, analysis by phase, and the amended plan. *(Grounding: model: Cyclical model of self-regulated learning (Zimmerman 2002))*
        - *Applied: Changing a routine after feedback.* Using feedback or supervision in a real role, you find out what in your current approach is not working and change how you do it. You reflect on what was affecting your motivation at the time, make one concrete change to your routine, and record over the following weeks whether the change actually improved things, rather than assuming it has. **Evidence:** Feedback notes, a short follow-up reflection, and a record showing the effect of the changed approach. *(Grounding: professional: Uses feedback and supervision to find learning needs and change how one works.; professional: Vitae RDF B1 Personal qualities: self-reflection)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Self-regulation review of a failing revision strategy.* You analyse a study strategy that is not working using Zimmerman's (2002) cyclical model of self-regulated learning, with its forethought, performance and self-reflection phases. You track your motivation and results over a few weeks, find where the cycle breaks down, and change the strategy, justifying the change with your own evidence (for example, a mathematics student who swaps rereading lecture notes for timed problem sheets after weak tutorial scores). **Evidence:** A strategy review with several weeks of tracking data, your analysis by phase, and the amended plan with its results. *(Grounding: model: Cyclical model of self-regulated learning (Zimmerman 2002))*
        - *Applied: Changing a work routine after supervision feedback.* You use feedback or supervision in a real role to find out what is not working and change how you work (for example, a psychology student employed as a research assistant who learns in supervision that data entry errors come from rushing late in the day, and moves checking to the morning). You reflect on what was driving your motivation and record whether the change worked. **Evidence:** Supervision notes or feedback, your follow-up reflection, and a record showing the effect of the changed approach. *(Grounding: professional: Uses feedback and supervision to find learning needs and change how one works.; professional: Vitae RDF B1 Personal qualities: self-reflection)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Psychology 1.23: critically reflect on the language and terminology used within the discipline (direct; lands on `reflective-practice/c1`)
- Universal: Reflection and personal development (direct; lands on `reflective-practice/c1`, `reflective-practice/c2`, `reflective-practice/04`)
- Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect; lands on `reflective-practice/04`)
- Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (indirect; lands on `reflective-practice/c1`)

### Advanced

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | Choose and apply a fuller model (Gibbs, Kolb, Johns) | L5 | 4.12.b8 "" | direct | `reflective-practice/c3` |
| 8 | Use reflection for CPD and lifelong learning | L6 | 4.15.b9.3 ""; l6/autonomy.3 "" | direct | `reflective-practice/03` |
| 9 | Reflexivity: how your identity and values shape what you notice | L7 | 4.17.b8.3 ""; l8/autonomy.3 "" | direct | `reflective-practice/c4` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

7. **Choose and apply a fuller model (Gibbs, Kolb, Johns)**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.32: Assessments help students to monitor and reflect on their own development (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Applying knowledge to practice or problems (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Reflection and personal development (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Applying a chosen reflective model to a real problem.* **Learning outcome:** The student can justify the choice of one established reflective model over its alternatives for a given situation, then construct a reflection that works through every stage of that model.
          Faced with a specific experience that needs more than a quick note, you select one established reflective model from several available, explain briefly why its structure suits this particular situation better than the alternatives, and then work through every one of its stages in full. This shows applying a chosen framework of knowledge to a real situation, rather than describing the models in the abstract. **Evidence:** A worked reflection following every stage of one named model, with a short note on why it was chosen. *(Grounding: ing:doc:reflective-cycle-gibbs-1988; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/applying-knowledge-to-practice-or-problems)*
        - *Applied: Applying a required reflective structure in practice.* **Learning outcome:** The student can apply a required reflective structure fully to a genuine event, critique how closely their response matches a recognised model's stages, and note where the structure constrained their reflection.
          In a real setting you are often given a fixed reflective structure to use rather than a free choice of model. You apply it fully to a genuine event, then check that your answers actually match a recognised model's stages rather than drifting into description, and note any point where the required structure limited how deep your reflection could go. **Evidence:** A completed reflection in a required format, checked against a named model's stages, with notes on limits. *(Grounding: professional: Uses the reflective model the profession or employer expects, and justifies the choice.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/applying-knowledge-to-practice-or-problems)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Justified choice between three reflective models.* You compare three different reflective models, each with its own stages and emphasis, for example one with six structured stages, one with four stages built around experience and experimentation, and one that explores different kinds of knowing. You choose the model that best fits a particular experience, justify the choice, and apply it fully rather than following its stages only loosely. **Evidence:** A reflective piece with a short rationale comparing the three models and explaining your eventual choice. *(Grounding: ing:doc:reflective-cycle-gibbs-1988; ing:doc:experiential-learning-kolb-1984; ing:doc:structured-reflection-johns-1995)*
        - *Applied: Reflective template mapped to a named model.* You use whatever reflective template or form a real setting requires, even when its headings do not match any single published model exactly. After completing it for a real event, you map its prompts onto the stages of a named reflective model and note where the fixed form still allowed you to reflect in depth, showing you can work within an imposed structure rather than only a freely chosen one. **Evidence:** A completed reflective template with a short note mapping its prompts to a named model, with a reviewer comment. *(Grounding: professional: Uses the reflective model the profession or employer expects, and justifies the choice.; professional: HCPC standards of proficiency: reflect on and review practice)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Assessed reflection with a justified choice of model.* You write an assessed reflection after comparing three models: Gibbs' (1988) six-stage reflective cycle, Kolb's (1984) four-stage experiential learning cycle, and Johns' (1995) model of structured reflection, which draws on Carper's ways of knowing. You choose one, justify why it suits this experience, and apply it fully (for example, a midwifery student choosing Johns for an emotionally demanding birth, because its cue questions explore personal and ethical knowing). **Evidence:** An assessed reflective essay with a short rationale section comparing the three models and explaining your choice. *(Grounding: ing:doc:reflective-cycle-gibbs-1988; ing:doc:experiential-learning-kolb-1984; ing:doc:structured-reflection-johns-1995)*
        - *Applied: Employer reflection template mapped to a named model.* You use the reflective model or form your profession or employer expects for a real event, and justify how you applied it (for example, a paramedic science student completing the ambulance service's reflection template after a difficult call, and mapping its prompts to Gibbs' stages). Where the form is fixed, you show how you still reflected in depth within it. **Evidence:** A completed reflective template from placement, with a short note mapping its prompts to a named model, reviewed by your mentor. *(Grounding: professional: Uses the reflective model the profession or employer expects, and justifies the choice.; professional: HCPC standards of proficiency: reflect on and review practice)*
8. **Use reflection for CPD and lifelong learning**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Professional practice and employability (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Reflection and personal development (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Reflection sustained across a career-long record.* **Learning outcome:** The student can synthesise several linked cycles of reflection into a single account that demonstrates a trajectory of development sustained beyond the point of qualifying.
          You treat reflection as an ongoing record rather than a single exercise, using a cycle in which each completed experiment becomes the starting experience for the next round of reflection. Looking back across several linked cycles, you can show a trajectory of development in a specific area of practice, demonstrating preparation for a professional setting where development continues well beyond the point of qualifying. **Evidence:** A reflective record spanning several linked cycles, with a short summary of the development trajectory shown. *(Grounding: ing:doc:experiential-learning-kolb-1984; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/professional-practice-and-employability)*
        - *Applied: CPD evidence prepared for professional settings.* **Learning outcome:** The student can construct a running record of reflection on their own practice, in a professional format, linking each entry to a specific external standard.
          You keep a running record of reflection on your practice in the format expected in a professional setting, rather than only for a single piece of coursework. Each entry links what happened to a specific standard or expectation of that setting, and over time the collection demonstrates readiness for ongoing professional development, not just a one-off reflective skill shown for an assessment. **Evidence:** A running CPD record in a professional format, each entry linked to a specific external standard. *(Grounding: professional: Records reflection on learning and practice as CPD evidence, to a regulator's or employer's standard.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/professional-practice-and-employability)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Linked reflective cycles building a CPD record.* You build a reflective record over an extended period, using a four-stage experiential learning cycle, concrete experience, reflective observation, abstract thinking and active experimentation, to link entries together so each experiment becomes the starting point for the next cycle. The record shows cumulative development across repeated cycles, rather than a series of separate, unconnected reflections on single events. **Evidence:** A term-long log of linked reflective cycles, with a summary of how your practice changed across entries. *(Grounding: ing:doc:experiential-learning-kolb-1984)*
        - *Applied: CPD portfolio mapped to professional standards.* You record ongoing reflection as evidence of continuing professional development, in whatever format a relevant professional body or employer expects. You select entries that show real development over time, link each one explicitly to a named standard it demonstrates, and discuss a selection of them with a peer to check the development is genuine rather than only self-reported. **Evidence:** A CPD portfolio section with reflective entries mapped to named standards and a note of a peer discussion. *(Grounding: professional: Records reflection on learning and practice as CPD evidence, to a regulator's or employer's standard.; professional: NMC revalidation)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Term-long CPD log of linked Kolb cycles.* You build a reflective CPD record over a term, using Kolb's (1984) experiential learning cycle to link entries: concrete experience, reflective observation, abstract conceptualisation and active experimentation, with each experiment becoming the next experience. You show how repeated cycles add up to development over time rather than one-off reflections (for example, an accountancy student preparing for professional body CPD by tracking growth in client communication). **Evidence:** A term-long CPD log of linked cycles, with a summary showing how your practice changed across the entries. *(Grounding: ing:doc:experiential-learning-kolb-1984)*
        - *Applied: CPD portfolio section mapped to professional standards.* You record reflection as CPD evidence in the format a regulator or employer uses (for example, a nursing student writing practice reflective accounts in the style of NMC revalidation, or a graduate teaching assistant mapping reflections to the Advance HE Professional Standards Framework). You select entries that show development, link each to the relevant standard, and discuss them with a peer. **Evidence:** A CPD portfolio section with reflective accounts mapped to named standards, and a record of the peer discussion. *(Grounding: professional: Records reflection on learning and practice as CPD evidence, to a regulator's or employer's standard.; professional: NMC revalidation; professional: Advance HE PSF 2023 A5)*
9. **Reflexivity: how your identity and values shape what you notice**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 1.11: reflective skills, social and personal responsibility (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.17: reflect on and articulate personal strengths and areas for development (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Reflection and personal development (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (indirect)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Ethics, integrity and values (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Examining personal responsibility in reflective judgement.* **Learning outcome:** The student can critique a judgement they reached about a real situation by examining how their own identity, assumptions or social position may have shaped it, using a named approach to reflexivity.
          You take a judgement or interpretation you reached about a real situation and ask how your own identity, assumptions or social position might have shaped it, rather than treating the judgement as neutral. Using a named approach to reflexivity, you pick out which aspect of your own standpoint was most likely to be at work, and set out what a different standpoint might have noticed instead. **Evidence:** A short written analysis naming your standpoint, its likely effect, and what an alternative standpoint might show. *(Grounding: ing:doc:variants-of-reflexivity-finlay-2002; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/1.11)*
        - *Applied: Testing how personal values affect a decision.* **Learning outcome:** The student can justify a real decision by checking whether a personal value, rather than the available evidence, is driving the judgement, and set out how they will keep checking for this.
          Before acting on a real judgement, you pause to ask whether a personal value or assumption of yours, rather than the evidence in front of you, is driving the decision. You check this with someone else where possible, note what you found, and record how you will keep checking for this kind of influence in similar decisions, treating personal responsibility as part of making the judgement, not separate from it. **Evidence:** A short note recording a checked judgement, the value identified, and the plan to keep checking it. *(Grounding: professional: Reflects on own values and challenges their effect on practice.; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/1.11)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Naming which variant of reflexivity applies.* You examine how your own identity, values and position shape what you notice, choosing from several named variants of reflexivity, for example turning attention onto your own reactions, reflecting on the relationship with others involved, working collaboratively to check an interpretation, or stepping back to question the assumptions built into how you are looking at something. You show how your background led you to notice some things and miss others. **Evidence:** A reflexive commentary naming the variants used, with specific extracts showing their effect on what you noticed. *(Grounding: ing:doc:variants-of-reflexivity-finlay-2002)*
        - *Applied: Challenging a value shaping a real judgement.* In discussion with someone experienced in the area, you realise that one of your own values or assumptions has influenced a real judgement you made about a situation or a person. You record what you noticed, how you changed the judgement or asked someone else to check it, and what you will do to keep testing your own assumptions in similar situations in future. **Evidence:** An anonymised reflection naming the value identified, its effect on the judgement, and the action taken. *(Grounding: professional: Reflects on own values and challenges their effect on practice.; professional: Social Work England professional standards 4.8)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Reflexive commentary using Finlay's variants of reflexivity.* You examine how your identity, values and position shape what you notice, using Finlay's (2002) variants of reflexivity: introspection, intersubjective reflection, mutual collaboration, social critique and discursive deconstruction. You choose the variants that fit and show how your background led you to see some things and miss others (for example, a master's student in development studies reflecting on how being an outsider shaped interviews with farmers during fieldwork). **Evidence:** A reflexive commentary or methodological appendix naming the variants used, with field note extracts showing their effect. *(Grounding: ing:doc:variants-of-reflexivity-finlay-2002)*
        - *Applied: Supervision reflection on values shaping a risk judgement.* You challenge how your own values affect your practice and the people you work with (for example, a postgraduate social work student realising in supervision that their views about family stability influenced a risk assessment, and asking a colleague to review it). You record what you noticed, what you changed, and how you will keep checking your assumptions in future cases. **Evidence:** An anonymised supervision reflection showing the value identified, its effect on your judgement, and the action you took. *(Grounding: professional: Reflects on own values and challenges their effect on practice.; professional: Social Work England professional standards 4.8)*
