<a id="work-based-experiential-learning"></a>
## Work-Based and Experiential Learning

### Introductory

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Distinguish the types of practice- and work-based learning | L3 | l3/knowledge.3 "Be aware of the nature of the area of study or work." | direct | `work-based-experiential-learning/01` |
| 2 | Explain what learning in practice offers | L4 | l4/knowledge.1 "Use practical, theoretical or technical understanding" | direct | `work-based-experiential-learning#i2` |
| 3 | Set own learning goals for a practice experience | L5 | l5/autonomy.1 "Take responsibility for planning and developing courses of action" | direct | `work-based-experiential-learning#i3` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

1. **Distinguish the types of practice- and work-based learning**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 2.15: the opportunity to undertake work-based learning and/or placements (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.26: work-based learning (for example, placements and fieldwork) are also considered good practice (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Applying knowledge to practice or problems (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Professional practice and employability (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Classification of learning modes by depth of engagement.* **Learning outcome:** The student can identify and explain the different forms that learning through practice can take, distinguishing each by the level of responsibility it involves.
          Given a set of different ways you can learn by doing, such as observing, practising under guidance, and carrying out real tasks within a team, you sort them into a short classification based on how much genuine responsibility each one involves. You explain the reasoning behind each item's position in the classification, rather than simply listing the modes you have come across. This demonstrates broad and well-founded knowledge of the different forms that learning through practice can take. **Evidence:** A classification of learning modes by depth of engagement, with a reasoned note under each one. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/subject-knowledge-and-understanding)*
        - *Applied: Briefing note distinguishing real learning opportunities.* **Learning outcome:** The student can describe, for someone less experienced, the practical differences between distinct forms of learning through practice, using a real example of each.
          You are asked to brief someone newer than you on the different ways people learn while doing real work, so you draft a short note distinguishing observing, supervised practice, and full participation in a task, with an example of each drawn from your own experience. You check that the note would let a newcomer tell the forms apart and know what to expect from each one. This demonstrates well-founded, practical knowledge of the different forms that work-based learning takes. **Evidence:** A briefing note distinguishing forms of learning through work, each illustrated by a real example. *(Grounding: professional: Recognises the forms of learning at work (on-the-job training, shadowing, apprenticeship, continuing development) and uses them.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/subject-knowledge-and-understanding)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Types of practical learning sorted by participation.* You list the different forms of practical learning available to you, something you watch, something you rehearse under supervision, and something where you take on a real task within a group of experienced practitioners. You sort these using the idea of legitimate peripheral participation, marking which ones count as genuine participation in a community's work and which are only rehearsal. This lets you distinguish the types rather than treating all practical learning as the same. **Evidence:** A sorted table of practical learning types, each marked as participation or rehearsal, with a reason. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991)*
        - *Applied: Comparison of real opportunities for learning through work.* You find out about two or three real opportunities for learning through work that are open to you, each involving a different kind of task and a different level of responsibility, by speaking to someone who organises this kind of opportunity. You write a short comparison of what each one would involve and what you would learn from it. This shows you recognising and comparing different forms of learning at work, rather than taking the first one offered without thinking about what it teaches. **Evidence:** A short written comparison of two or three real opportunities, noting what each would teach you. *(Grounding: professional: Recognises the forms of learning at work (on-the-job training, shadowing, apprenticeship, continuing development) and uses them.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Practice-based learning types sorted by kind of participation.* You list the forms of practice- and work-based learning your course offers (laboratory work, simulation, placement, internship, volunteering, apprenticeship) and sort them using Lave and Wenger's (1991) idea of legitimate peripheral participation, noting which involve taking part in a real community of practice and which rehearse it. This shows you can distinguish the types (for example, a pharmacy student separating a simulated dispensary session from a community pharmacy placement). **Evidence:** A sorted table of practice- and work-based learning types, each marked as real participation or rehearsal, with an example. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991)*
        - *Applied: Comparison of real placement and internship opportunities.* You find out about two or three real opportunities open to you, such as a summer internship, a placement year, work shadowing or volunteering, by talking to the placements team or an employer, and write a short comparison of what each involves and what you would learn. This shows you recognising forms of learning at work (for example, a geology student comparing a field assistant role with a placement at an environmental consultancy). **Evidence:** A one-page comparison of real opportunities, with notes from your conversation with the placements team or employer. *(Grounding: professional: Recognises the forms of learning at work (on-the-job training, shadowing, apprenticeship, continuing development) and uses them.)*
2. **Explain what learning in practice offers**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.26: work-based learning (for example, placements and fieldwork) are also considered good practice (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Applying knowledge to practice or problems (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Explanation of why practice knowledge differs from theory.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain what direct engagement with practical situations adds to understanding that theoretical study alone does not provide.
          You are asked to explain, for someone who has only studied a subject in theory, what engaging with real practical situations actually adds to their understanding. Using a model of how understanding develops through direct experience, you set out what a learner notices in practice that written material alone cannot convey, and why that is the case. This demonstrates applying subject knowledge to practical situations by explaining what that application reveals about the subject itself. **Evidence:** A short written explanation of what direct practical experience adds beyond theoretical study. *(Grounding: model: Model of skill acquisition (Dreyfus and Dreyfus 1986); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/applying-knowledge-to-practice-or-problems)*
        - *Applied: Real situation used to explain practice's distinct value.* **Learning outcome:** The student can identify a specific practical situation and explain to others what applying subject knowledge within it revealed that study alone could not.
          You take a specific situation from a piece of real work and use it to explain, to someone less experienced, why applying subject knowledge to an actual problem taught you something that classroom examples had not. You point to the exact feature of the real situation, such as its unpredictability or its stakes, that made the learning possible. This demonstrates applying subject knowledge to practical situations and being able to explain clearly what that application adds. **Evidence:** A short explanation, built from one real situation, of what applying knowledge in practice adds. *(Grounding: professional: Draws out what direct experience in a real setting teaches that study alone does not.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/applying-knowledge-to-practice-or-problems)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Novice to expert explained through practice stages.* You explain what learning in practical settings offers that classroom learning alone cannot, using a model of skill acquisition that describes moving from a novice who follows fixed rules towards expertise built from direct experience of real, varied situations. You illustrate the early stages with an example of something you only fully understood once you tried it for real. This shows you explaining the distinctive value of learning in practice, rather than simply asserting that it matters. **Evidence:** A short explanation using stages of skill acquisition, illustrated by one example from your own experience. *(Grounding: model: Model of skill acquisition (Dreyfus and Dreyfus 1986))*
        - *Applied: Account of a lesson learned only through doing.* After a short period of hands-on experience, you write a brief account of one thing you learned there that no amount of reading or classroom teaching had taught you, and you explain what it was about being in the real situation that made the difference. This shows you can explain, in concrete terms, what learning in practice offers that other forms of learning do not, rather than repeating a general claim about practice being useful. **Evidence:** A short account naming one thing learned only through direct experience, and why the setting mattered. *(Grounding: professional: Draws out what direct experience in a real setting teaches that study alone does not.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: The value of practice explained with the Dreyfus stages.* You explain what learning in practice offers that classroom learning cannot, using the Dreyfus model of skill acquisition (Dreyfus and Dreyfus 1986), which describes moving from a novice who relies on rules towards expertise built on experience of real situations. You illustrate the early stages with your own practice examples. This shows you can explain the value of learning in practice (for example, a veterinary student contrasting learning handling rules in class with handling nervous animals on a farm placement). **Evidence:** A short explanation using the Dreyfus stages, illustrated by your own examples from a lab, simulation or placement. *(Grounding: model: Model of skill acquisition (Dreyfus and Dreyfus 1986))*
        - *Applied: Account of something learned only through work shadowing.* After a short practice experience, such as a day of work shadowing, a lab practical or a volunteering session, you write a brief account of something you learned there that lectures had not taught you, and why the setting made the difference. This shows you can explain what learning in practice offers (for example, a primary education student noticing how a class teacher manages the transitions between lessons). **Evidence:** A short post-experience account naming what you learned in practice and why the setting made it possible. *(Grounding: practice: work shadowing or volunteering session)*
3. **Set own learning goals for a practice experience**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.26: work-based learning (for example, placements and fieldwork) are also considered good practice (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Applying knowledge to practice or problems (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Goal set defined before starting a period of practice.* **Learning outcome:** The student can describe how to set specific, checkable learning goals before a period of practical experience, explaining what each goal is intended to develop.
          Before a period of learning through real practice, you draft a short set of personal learning goals and check each one is specific enough to know afterwards whether it was met. You note what knowledge or skill you expect each goal to build, and how this form of practical experience is widely recognised as a valuable route to that kind of development. This demonstrates setting purposeful goals for a form of learning regarded as good practice. **Evidence:** A dated set of personal learning goals written before a period of practical experience began. *(Grounding: model: SMART goals (Doran 1981); benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.26)*
        - *Applied: Learning goals set and checked against real progress.* **Learning outcome:** The student can set specific learning goals before a period of practical work and review progress against them at agreed points.
          Before taking on a piece of practical work, you set yourself two or three learning goals and arrange to check your progress against them partway through and at the end. You keep brief notes on what you actually achieved against each goal and adjust your approach where progress turns out to be slower than planned. This demonstrates taking responsibility for your own learning during a period of work widely recognised as valuable practice. **Evidence:** A short set of learning goals with midpoint and final notes on progress against each one. *(Grounding: professional: Agrees and reviews learning goals with whoever oversees a piece of practical work.; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.26)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Learning goals tested against SMART criteria.* Before starting a new piece of practical work, you write three learning goals for yourself and test each one against the SMART criteria, specific, measurable, achievable, relevant and time-bound, in their now common form. You revise any goal that fails one of these tests and note what evidence would show it has been met. This shows you setting your own learning goals for a practice experience, rather than leaving your intentions vague or unchecked. **Evidence:** Your draft and revised learning goals, each checked against SMART, with the evidence that would show success. *(Grounding: model: SMART goals (Doran 1981))*
        - *Applied: Goals agreed and reviewed with whoever oversees the work.* At the start of a period of practical work, you agree your learning goals with whoever is responsible for overseeing it, then review them together partway through and again at the end, noting which goals were met and why. This shows you taking responsibility for planning your own learning in a real setting, rather than waiting to be told afterwards what you should have been trying to learn. **Evidence:** A goals agreement with the person overseeing your work, plus notes from a midpoint and end review. *(Grounding: professional: Agrees and reviews learning goals with whoever oversees a piece of practical work.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Placement learning goals tested against SMART criteria.* Before a placement or practice experience, you write three learning goals and test each against the SMART criteria (Doran 1981), in their now common form: specific, measurable, achievable, relevant and time-bound. You revise any that fail and note how you will know each has been met. This shows you setting your own learning goals for a practice experience (for example, a quantity surveying student aiming to produce a bill of quantities for one small job by week four). **Evidence:** Your draft and revised learning goals, each checked against SMART, with the evidence you will use to show it was met. *(Grounding: model: SMART goals (Doran 1981))*
        - *Applied: Learning goals agreed and reviewed with a placement supervisor.* You agree learning goals with your placement supervisor or volunteering coordinator at the start, then review them together midway and at the end, noting which were met and why. This shows you taking responsibility for planning your own learning in a real setting (for example, a marketing student on a summer internship agreeing goals on campaign analytics with their line manager). **Evidence:** A goals agreement signed by your supervisor, with mid-point and end-point review notes. *(Grounding: practice: placement or volunteering with a supervisor)*

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

4. **Conduct oneself as a professional in a practice setting**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.21: ethical conduct in professional practice and social responsibility (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Applying knowledge to practice or problems (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Professional practice and employability (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Decision analysed against a community's standards of conduct.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse a decision taken in practice against the standards of ethical conduct and social responsibility expected of an established professional.
          You take a moment from a piece of practical work where you were unsure how to behave, and you analyse it using the idea that a newcomer learns a community's standards of conduct by watching and gradually taking part, rather than being handed a rulebook. You set out what a fully established member would have done and why, then compare it with your own choice. This demonstrates ethical conduct and social responsibility reasoned through carefully, not simply assumed. **Evidence:** A written analysis of one conduct decision, comparing your choice with an established member's likely approach. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.21)*
        - *Applied: Ethical judgement made and explained in real practice.* **Learning outcome:** The student can make and explain an ethical decision in a real practice situation, accounting for the professional standards and wider responsibilities involved.
          In a real situation where the ethical course of action was not obvious, you make a decision that respects the standards and responsibilities of your role, then write a short explanation of the reasoning behind it for someone else to check. You note what wider responsibility, beyond the immediate task, shaped your choice. This demonstrates ethical conduct in professional practice and a sense of social responsibility, acted on rather than only discussed afterwards. **Evidence:** A written explanation of one ethical decision in real practice, with the reasoning behind it. *(Grounding: professional: Meets the conduct, values and standards expected in the role.; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.21)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Professional norms observed and analysed as a newcomer.* You observe how experienced members of a group you are working within behave, in matters such as punctuality, confidentiality and how they communicate with others, and you analyse this using the idea that newcomers learn a community's standards of conduct by taking part in its work from the edge inward. You then record which norms you chose to adopt yourself, and why you made that choice. This shows you conducting yourself as a professional within that setting. **Evidence:** An observation record of professional norms in your setting, listing the norms you adopted and your reasons. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991)*
        - *Applied: Conduct standard met and recorded in a real situation.* During a piece of real work you meet a specific standard of conduct, such as confidentiality or basic safety practice, in an actual situation rather than a hypothetical one, and you record exactly how you handled it and who you checked with if you were unsure. This shows you meeting the conduct and values expected of you in that role, in a genuine and sometimes pressured moment, rather than only in principle. **Evidence:** A short log entry on the situation, the standard applied, and who you checked with, confirmed afterwards. *(Grounding: professional: Meets the conduct, values and standards expected in the role.; framework: QAA work-based learning guidance (2018): applying knowledge, skills and professional behaviours)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Observation of a setting's professional norms as a newcomer.* You observe how established members of a practice setting behave (punctuality, confidentiality, dress, how they speak to clients) and analyse it with Lave and Wenger's (1991) legitimate peripheral participation, explaining how newcomers learn a community's conduct by taking part. You then record which norms you adopted and why. This shows you conducting yourself as a professional in that setting (for example, a law student on a vacation scheme noting how trainees handle client confidentiality). **Evidence:** An observation record of professional norms in your setting, with the norms you adopted and your reasons. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991)*
        - *Applied: A conduct standard met in a real workplace situation.* During a placement or job you meet a conduct standard from the relevant code or policy, such as confidentiality, safeguarding or health and safety, in a real situation, and record how you handled it and who you consulted. This shows you meeting the conduct and standards expected in the role (for example, a sport science student working in a gym handling a member's health disclosure in line with the club's data policy). **Evidence:** A reflective log entry on the situation, referenced to the code or policy, countersigned by your supervisor. *(Grounding: professional: Meets the conduct, values and standards expected in the role.; professional: QAA work-based learning guidance (2018): applying knowledge, skills and professional behaviours)*
5. **Make effective use of a mentor on placement**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 2.15: the opportunity to undertake work-based learning and/or placements (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.26: work-based learning (for example, placements and fieldwork) are also considered good practice (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Mentoring relationship read through a participation model.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse a mentoring relationship in terms of stages of participation, identifying what support would help progress to the next stage.
          You examine your relationship with a mentor during a period of work-based learning through the idea that a newcomer develops by moving gradually from watching, to supervised attempts, to full participation in a community's work. You identify which stage you are currently at and what kind of support from your mentor would help you move to the next one. This demonstrates engaging seriously with the mentoring relationships that make this form of learning effective. **Evidence:** A short analysis of your current stage of participation and the support needed to progress. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.26)*
        - *Applied: Mentor's guidance turned into a concrete change in practice.* **Learning outcome:** The student can act deliberately on mentoring guidance received during practical work and evaluate what change it produced in their own performance.
          You take one piece of guidance your mentor gives you during a period of work-based learning and act on it deliberately in your next piece of work, then report back on what changed as a result. You note what you would ask your mentor differently next time, based on how useful that guidance turned out to be in practice. This demonstrates using mentoring actively to develop, making the relationship itself part of good practice. **Evidence:** A short note on one piece of mentoring guidance acted on, the change made, and the result. *(Grounding: professional: Uses supervision and mentoring to develop in a role.; benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.26)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Mentoring plan built around moving to full participation.* You plan how to make the most of a mentor during a period of practical experience, drawing on the idea that newcomers learn by working alongside more experienced members of a community and gradually moving from its edge towards full participation. You choose which tasks to watch first, which to attempt under guidance, and what questions to bring, then evaluate how far the relationship moved you towards fuller participation. This shows you making effective, planned use of a mentor. **Evidence:** A mentoring plan and a short end-of-experience evaluation of how the relationship developed your participation. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991)*
        - *Applied: Mentoring meetings prepared for and actively led.* You prepare for and take an active part in leading regular meetings with your mentor, bringing a short agenda, specific questions arising from your own practice, and a brief note of what you have done since the previous meeting. This shows you using mentoring to develop in a role by driving the relationship yourself, rather than waiting passively to be told what to do or think about next. **Evidence:** Mentoring meeting agendas and notes over time, with agreed actions and evidence they were followed up. *(Grounding: professional: Uses supervision and mentoring to develop in a role.; framework: QAA work-based learning guidance (2018): roles, responsibilities and support)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Mentoring plan for moving towards fuller participation.* You plan how to use your placement mentor, drawing on Lave and Wenger's (1991) view that newcomers learn by working alongside experienced members of a community of practice. You choose which tasks to observe, which to try under supervision and what questions to bring, then evaluate how the relationship moved you towards fuller participation. This shows you making effective use of a mentor (for example, a social work student planning joint home visits with their practice educator). **Evidence:** A mentoring plan and an end-of-placement evaluation of how the relationship developed your participation. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991)*
        - *Applied: Supervision meetings you prepare for and lead.* You prepare for and lead regular supervision meetings with your placement mentor, bringing a short agenda, questions from your practice and a note of what you have done since the last meeting. This shows you using mentoring to develop in the role rather than waiting to be told (for example, a trainee teacher bringing notes from an observed lesson to their school-based mentor and agreeing one change to try). **Evidence:** Supervision agendas and meeting notes across the placement, with agreed actions and evidence that they were followed up. *(Grounding: professional: Uses supervision and mentoring to develop in a role.; professional: QAA work-based learning guidance (2018): roles, responsibilities and support)*
6. **Test theory in practice and reflect on the result**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Applying knowledge to practice or problems (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Reflection and personal development (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Theoretical prediction checked against a real outcome.* **Learning outcome:** The student can state what a theory predicts for a real situation and evaluate any gap between that prediction and the actual outcome.
          You take a theory or principle from your subject, state clearly what it predicts should happen in a specific real situation, and then carry out the action to see whether the prediction holds. Where the result departs from what the theory predicted, you set out a plausible reason why, rather than discarding either the theory or the result without explanation. This demonstrates applying subject knowledge to practical situations and testing it honestly. **Evidence:** A written record of the theory's prediction, the real outcome, and your explanation of any gap. *(Grounding: ing:doc:experiential-learning-kolb-1984; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/applying-knowledge-to-practice-or-problems)*
        - *Applied: A taught method applied to a real problem and reviewed.* **Learning outcome:** The student can apply a taught method to a real problem encountered in practice and evaluate whether it produced the expected result.
          You take a method you have been taught and apply it to an actual problem you encounter in practice, rather than a set exercise, then review afterwards whether it produced the result the method promised. You identify one adjustment you would make to the method if you used it again in a similar situation. This demonstrates applying subject knowledge to a practical problem and reviewing honestly what worked. **Evidence:** A short review of one method applied to a real problem, with one adjustment identified for next time. *(Grounding: professional: Applies evidence and theory to practice and reviews what works.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/applying-knowledge-to-practice-or-problems)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: A theory tested at work through an experiential cycle.* You pick one theory relevant to your subject and test it in a piece of real work, using the later stages of an experiential learning cycle: you set out what the theory predicts, you try it out, and then you reflect on where the result matched or departed from the prediction. This shows you testing theory in practice and evaluating the result, rather than treating theory and practice as separate things that never meet. **Evidence:** A short record: the theory, your prediction, what you did, what happened, and your evaluation. *(Grounding: ing:doc:experiential-learning-kolb-1984)*
        - *Applied: Evidence-based method applied and its outcome reviewed.* In a piece of real work you apply an evidence-based method or guideline from your subject to an actual case, record what happened, and discuss with someone more experienced why it did or did not work as you expected. This shows you applying theory to practice and reviewing what works, treating the outcome as something to learn from rather than simply something to report. **Evidence:** A short case note on the method applied, the outcome, and notes from your review discussion. *(Grounding: professional: Applies evidence and theory to practice and reviews what works.; framework: HCPC standards of proficiency: reflect on and review practice)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: A course theory tested on placement through Kolb's cycle.* You pick one theory from your course and test it at work, using the abstract conceptualisation and active experimentation stages of Kolb's (1984) experiential learning cycle: you set out what the theory predicts, try it, then reflect on where the result matched or departed from it. This shows you testing theory in practice and evaluating the result (for example, a business student testing a theory of motivation when allocating tasks in a retail team). **Evidence:** A theory-test record: the theory, your prediction, what you did, what happened and your evaluation. *(Grounding: ing:doc:experiential-learning-kolb-1984)*
        - *Applied: Evidence-based method applied to a real case and reviewed.* On placement you apply an evidence-based guideline or method from your course to a real case, record the outcome, and discuss with your supervisor why it did or did not work as expected. This shows you applying theory to practice and reviewing what works (for example, an occupational therapy student using a falls-risk assessment tool with an older client). **Evidence:** An anonymised case note with the method applied, the outcome and your supervisor's comments on your review. *(Grounding: professional: Applies evidence and theory to practice and reviews what works.; professional: HCPC standards of proficiency: reflect on and review practice)*

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

7. **Apply an experiential learning cycle (Kolb) to work-based learning**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 2.15: the opportunity to undertake work-based learning and/or placements (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.26: work-based learning (for example, placements and fieldwork) are also considered good practice (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Applying knowledge to practice or problems (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Professional practice and employability (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Cycle used to turn one setback into a tested change.* **Learning outcome:** The student can construct a structured cycle of experience, reflection, generalisation and renewed action to explain how a specific professional setback produced learning.
          You take a specific moment of real difficulty from a piece of practical work and move it through a structured cycle of experience, reflection, general lesson and new attempt, then put the new attempt into practice and record what changed as a result. You check that each stage of the cycle is distinct, rather than collapsed into a single vague comment about the event. This demonstrates understanding a subject's professional settings well enough to learn deliberately from working within them. **Evidence:** A structured cycle write-up of one setback, its lesson, and the result of trying a new approach. *(Grounding: ing:doc:experiential-learning-kolb-1984; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/professional-practice-and-employability)*
        - *Applied: Development log built from cycles of real practice.* **Learning outcome:** The student can apply a structured learning cycle repeatedly across real practice, constructing a developmental record that shows genuine change in professional understanding over time.
          Over a period of practical work you keep a short log in which each entry moves through a real event, what you made of it, and a specific change you then tried, building a record that shows genuine development rather than a list of things that simply happened to you. You choose entries that show your professional understanding of the setting improving over time. This demonstrates being prepared for work through a disciplined habit of learning from it. **Evidence:** A development log of several cycle entries, each showing an event, a lesson, and a change tried. *(Grounding: professional: Learns from work experience through structured reflection and records it as development.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/professional-practice-and-employability)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: One real event taken through two full learning cycles.* You write up a significant event from a piece of real work through all four stages of an experiential learning cycle, the concrete experience itself, your reflection on it, the general principle you draw from that reflection, and the new approach you plan to try. You then show a second turn of the cycle once you have tried that new approach in practice. This shows you applying the cycle as a working tool rather than a label attached after the fact. **Evidence:** A two-cycle write-up of one real event, with each stage labelled and dated evidence of the second attempt. *(Grounding: ing:doc:experiential-learning-kolb-1984)*
        - *Applied: Structured reflective record kept in a recognised format.* Across a period of practical work you keep a structured reflective record, writing up several real experiences and showing how each one fed into an actual change in how you worked afterwards, set out in a format that a professional body in your field would recognise. This shows you learning from work experience through structured reflection and recording that learning as evidence of development, rather than leaving it unwritten and easily forgotten. **Evidence:** A set of dated reflective entries in a recognised format, confirmed by someone who witnessed the events. *(Grounding: professional: Learns from work experience through structured reflection and records it as development.; framework: NMC revalidation)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: One work event taken through two turns of Kolb's cycle.* You write up a significant event from a work-based experience through all four stages of Kolb's (1984) cycle, concrete experience, reflective observation, abstract conceptualisation and active experimentation, then show a second turn of the cycle when you tried the new approach. This shows you applying the cycle as a working tool, not a label (for example, a civil engineering student on a site placement working through a delayed concrete pour and the revised plan for the next one). **Evidence:** A two-cycle Kolb write-up of one work event, with each stage labelled and dated evidence of the second attempt. *(Grounding: ing:doc:experiential-learning-kolb-1984)*
        - *Applied: Reflective accounts kept in a regulator's format.* You keep a structured reflective record across a placement or job, writing up several experiences and showing how each fed into changes in your practice, in the form a regulator or employer would expect. This shows you learning from work through structured reflection and recording it as development (for example, a nursing student writing reflective accounts in the format used for NMC revalidation). **Evidence:** A set of dated reflective accounts in a regulator-style format, with your practice supervisor's confirmation that the events took place. *(Grounding: professional: Learns from work experience through structured reflection and records it as development.; professional: NMC revalidation)*
8. **Exercise and justify professional judgement in a work setting**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Applying knowledge to practice or problems (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Professional practice and employability (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Reasoned judgement built from competing considerations.* **Learning outcome:** The student can construct a reasoned professional judgement by weighing competing considerations against the standards of a role, stating what evidence would alter that judgement.
          Faced with a real decision at work where the available information points in more than one direction, you set out the competing considerations explicitly, weigh their relative importance against the standards of your role, and state the judgement you reach along with the reasoning that produced it. You note what evidence would have changed your decision. This demonstrates evaluating evidence and arguments critically to reach a reasoned judgement that someone else could follow. **Evidence:** A written judgement setting out the competing considerations weighed and the reasoning reached. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
        - *Applied: Judgement call defended against a challenge.* **Learning outcome:** The student can justify a professional judgement made in a real situation by responding to a genuine challenge to it, rather than simply restating the original decision.
          You make a judgement call in a real work situation and then have it genuinely questioned by someone else involved, so you explain, in person or in writing, the reasoning and standards behind your choice and respond to their specific concern rather than simply repeating your original decision. You note whether their challenge changed your view. This demonstrates reaching a reasoned judgement and defending it under real critical scrutiny. **Evidence:** A short account of one judgement call, the challenge raised against it, and your response. *(Grounding: professional: Makes and justifies decisions within the scope of a role and its standards.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Decision judged against an experienced practitioner's standard.* You take a decision you made during a piece of real work and analyse it by asking how someone with full standing in that field, rather than a newcomer, would have approached it, drawing on the idea that expertise is built by moving from the edge of a community's practice towards full participation in it. You set out the options you had, the standards that applied, and the knowledge you drew on, then justify your choice against what an experienced practitioner would accept. **Evidence:** A decision record setting out options, standards applied, your choice, and its justification. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991)*
        - *Applied: A judgement call made and justified in writing.* In a real situation at work where no fixed rule told you what to do, such as needing to reprioritise tasks or raise a concern, you make a decision that stays within the proper scope of your role, then justify it in writing with reference to the standards that apply. You note what you would do differently if a similar situation arose again. This shows professional judgement exercised in a genuinely unpredictable context, not a scripted one. **Evidence:** Your written justification of one judgement call, with the standards referenced and any outcome noted. *(Grounding: professional: Makes and justifies decisions within the scope of a role and its standards.; framework: QAA work-based learning guidance (2018): applying knowledge, skills and professional behaviours)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Decision record judged against the community's practice.* You analyse a decision you made at work by asking how full members of your community of practice would have made it, drawing on Lave and Wenger's (1991) account of learning as moving from peripheral to fuller participation. You set out the options, the standards and the knowledge you used, and justify your choice against what experienced practitioners would accept. This shows you exercising and justifying professional judgement (for example, a journalism student on placement deciding whether a source's claim was safe to publish). **Evidence:** A decision record: the situation, options considered, standards applied, your choice and its justification, reviewed by your supervisor. *(Grounding: ing:doc:situated-learning-lave-wenger-1991)*
        - *Applied: Written justification of a judgement call at work.* In a real work situation with no clear rule, you make a decision within your role's scope, such as reprioritising tasks or escalating a concern, and then justify it in writing to your supervisor with reference to the role's standards. This shows professional judgement in a complex, unpredictable context (for example, a final-year accounting student on an audit placement flagging an unusual transaction and explaining why). **Evidence:** Your written justification to your supervisor, with their reply and any outcome of the decision. *(Grounding: professional: Makes and justifies decisions within the scope of a role and its standards.; professional: QAA work-based learning guidance (2018): applying knowledge, skills and professional behaviours)*
9. **Reflect on own developing identity as a practitioner**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Reflection and personal development (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Developing identity traced across several reflective entries.* **Learning outcome:** The student can critique their own developing professional identity across a series of reflective accounts, naming what belief changed and why.
          Across a series of reflective entries written over time, you track how your sense of yourself as a practitioner has shifted, picking out moments where your values, confidence or ways of working changed in response to something that actually happened. You name what you believed at the start, what changed it, and what you believe now, rather than describing events without drawing a line between them. This demonstrates reflecting on learning and practice to plan ongoing development. **Evidence:** A short timeline of reflective entries tracking a shift in professional identity, with named turning points. *(Grounding: model: Reflection-in-action and reflection-on-action (Schon 1983); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
        - *Applied: Development plan built from a changed sense of identity.* **Learning outcome:** The student can construct a development plan from a genuine shift in professional identity, naming one resulting strength, one exposed gap, and a concrete action.
          Having noticed a genuine change in how you see yourself as a practitioner, you turn that insight into a short development plan, naming one strength the change has given you and one gap it has exposed, with a concrete step for addressing that gap. You set a date to check whether you actually acted on the step. This demonstrates reflecting on practice to plan future development, rather than treating reflection as an end in itself. **Evidence:** A short development plan naming a strength and a gap identified through reflection, with one action. *(Grounding: professional: Produces a professional self-statement that withstands scrutiny from someone more experienced in the field.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/reflection-and-personal-development)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Practitioner identity traced through reflection in and on action.* You write a critical reflection on how your sense of yourself as a practitioner has developed, identifying specific moments when you adjusted your approach in the middle of doing something, and other moments when you reflected afterwards on what had happened and why. You use these moments to state, with reasons, what kind of practitioner you now consider yourself to be. This shows genuine reflection on a developing professional identity, not a general account of experience. **Evidence:** A critical reflective piece citing dated moments of in-the-moment and after-the-event reflection. *(Grounding: model: Reflection-in-action and reflection-on-action (Schon 1983))*
        - *Applied: Practitioner statement discussed with someone more experienced.* You produce a short statement for a genuine professional purpose, tracing how your values and ways of working have changed through real practice, and you discuss a draft of it with someone considerably more experienced in the field, who challenges the claims you make about yourself. This shows you reflecting on your developing identity at a level serious enough to withstand scrutiny, rather than only writing about it privately for your own benefit. **Evidence:** Your practitioner statement, notes from the discussion with a more experienced person, and the revisions made. *(Grounding: professional: Produces a professional self-statement that withstands scrutiny from someone more experienced in the field.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Critical reflection on practitioner identity through reflective practice.* You write a critical reflection on how your identity as a practitioner has developed, drawing on Schon's (1983) reflective practitioner: you identify moments of reflection-in-action, when you adjusted in the moment, and reflection-on-action afterwards, and show how these built your own professional knowledge. You state what kind of practitioner you now see yourself as. This shows reflection on your developing identity (for example, a master's student in counselling reflecting on adapting a session plan as it unfolded). **Evidence:** A critical reflective essay on your practitioner identity, citing dated moments of reflection-in-action and reflection-on-action. *(Grounding: model: Reflection-in-action and reflection-on-action (Schon 1983))*
        - *Applied: Practitioner statement discussed with a senior practitioner.* You produce a practitioner statement for a real professional step, such as registration, chartership or a graduate scheme portfolio review, tracing how your values and ways of working have changed through practice, and you discuss it with a senior practitioner. This shows you reflecting on your developing identity at a professional level (for example, a landscape architecture student preparing a statement for a practice's graduate scheme). **Evidence:** Your practitioner statement, notes from the discussion with a senior practitioner and the revisions it led to. *(Grounding: practice: professional registration, chartership or portfolio review)*
