<a id="ai-technology"></a>
## AI and Technology

### Introductory

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Explain what generative AI is and is not | L3 | l3/knowledge.1 "Use factual, procedural and theoretical understanding" | direct | `ai-technology#i1` |
| 2 | Check the assessment guidance before using AI | L4 | l4/autonomy.2 "within broad but generally well-defined parameters" | direct | `ai-technology#i2` |
| 3 | Recognise what AI cannot be trusted for, and think first | L5 | 4.12.b4 "an understanding of the limits of their knowledge" | direct | `ai-technology#i3` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

1. **Explain what generative AI is and is not**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Digital skills (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Concept map of a digital tool's capabilities and limits.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain what a generative AI tool can and cannot reliably do, distinguishing genuine capability from assumption.
          Given a digital tool that is central to a field of work, you set out, in a short written piece, what the tool can reliably do, what it cannot do, and the boundary between the two. You draw on an established account of how the technology actually works, rather than on marketing claims or assumption, to support each point you make. This demonstrates using a core digital tool only after genuinely understanding its capabilities and limits. **Evidence:** A one-page concept map setting out the tool's genuine capabilities, its limits and the reasoning behind each. *(Grounding: model: Stochastic parrots critique of large language models (Bender et al. 2021); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/digital-skills)*
        - *Applied: Briefing explaining a digital tool's capabilities to colleagues.* **Learning outcome:** The student can brief others on what a digital tool can be trusted to do and what checks they should still carry out before relying on it.
          A digital tool that a field depends on is being introduced to people who will use it day to day, and you are asked to brief them before they start. You explain, in plain terms, what the tool can be trusted to do, what it cannot, and the checks they should still carry out themselves before relying on it. This demonstrates using the digital tools a field relies on competently and helping others do the same. **Evidence:** The briefing notes you prepared, with a short summary of the questions colleagues asked afterwards. *(Grounding: professional: Explains a digital tool's genuine capabilities and limits to colleagues before they rely on it.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/digital-skills)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Explainer distinguishing AI fluency from genuine understanding.* You write a short explainer, in your own words, setting out how a generative AI tool produces text by predicting likely words from patterns in its training data. Using a well known critique of large language models, you explain why fluent, confident output is not the same as checked, understood knowledge, and you give one example of a plausible but invented answer. This shows you can say clearly what generative AI is and is not. **Evidence:** A short explainer in your own words with one worked example of a fabricated AI answer. *(Grounding: model: Stochastic parrots critique of large language models (Bender et al. 2021))*
        - *Applied: Plain language answer on AI's limits for a visitor.* Someone unfamiliar with generative AI asks you, in a public facing role, whether people simply let a chatbot do their writing for them. You give a short, accurate answer explaining what generative AI is, what it cannot be relied on for, and that any setting using it sets its own rules on acceptable use. This shows you can explain generative AI's limits clearly to someone outside the field. **Evidence:** Your prepared talking points for explaining generative AI's limits, with a note confirming the conversation took place. *(Grounding: professional: Communicates the capabilities and limits of a technology clearly to a non-specialist audience.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Short explainer of generative AI and its limits.* You write a 300-word explainer, in your own words, on how a generative AI chatbot produces text by predicting likely words from patterns in its training data. Drawing on Bender and colleagues' (2021) "stochastic parrots" critique, you explain why fluent output is not the same as understanding or a checked fact (for example, why a chatbot can invent a plausible reference for a biology topic). This shows you can say what generative AI is and is not. **Evidence:** A 300-word explainer in your own words with one worked example of a fabricated or wrong AI answer. *(Grounding: model: Stochastic parrots critique of large language models (Bender et al. 2021))*
        - *Applied: Explaining AI to visitors as a student ambassador.* As a student ambassador at an open day, you are asked by a parent whether students just use AI to write essays. You give a short, accurate answer on what generative AI is, what it cannot be relied on for, and that universities set rules on its use (for example, explaining to a prospective computing student that AI predicts text rather than looking facts up). **Evidence:** Your prepared talking points and a short note from the ambassador coordinator who observed the conversation. *(Grounding: practice: student ambassador role at an open day)*
2. **Check the assessment guidance before using AI**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Digital skills (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Explanation of why a digital use rule fits its purpose.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain why a rule governing permitted digital tools for a task connects to what that task is meant to demonstrate.
          Given a rule governing which digital tools may be used for a piece of work, you set out, in a short written account, the reasoning that connects the rule to what the work is meant to demonstrate. You draw on an established model of aligning method with intended outcome to show the rule is not arbitrary or accidental. This demonstrates understanding the digital tools and technologies a field relies on, and why their use is sometimes restricted. **Evidence:** A short written account linking a digital use rule to its underlying purpose, using a named model. *(Grounding: model: Constructive alignment (Biggs 1996); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/digital-skills)*
        - *Applied: Checklist confirming permitted tools before starting a task.* **Learning outcome:** The student can draw up and resolve, in writing, a checklist of permitted and restricted digital tools before starting a task.
          Before beginning a piece of work, you draw up a short checklist of which digital tools and technologies are permitted, which are restricted, and where the boundary is unclear. You resolve any unclear point in writing before you start, rather than assuming, and you keep the checklist together with your finished work. This demonstrates using the digital tools and technologies a field relies on, within its stated rules. **Evidence:** Your checklist of permitted and restricted tools, with any written clarification you obtained before starting. *(Grounding: professional: Confirms which digital tools are permitted for a task before relying on any of them.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/digital-skills)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Alignment check between a task's rules and its outcomes.* Before using any AI tool on a piece of work, you read the task's AI guidance alongside what the task is actually assessing. Using a recognised model of aligning assessment with intended outcomes, you explain in a few sentences why the rule fits the skill being tested, for instance why drafting is restricted when the outcome is meant to be your own argument. This shows you check guidance first and understand why the rule exists. **Evidence:** A short table linking each part of the task's AI guidance to what it is assessing, with your explanation. *(Grounding: model: Constructive alignment (Biggs 1996))*
        - *Applied: Written clarification of unclear AI guidance before starting.* Before starting a piece of work, you find its guidance on AI use and note what is allowed and what is not. Where the wording is unclear, you put a specific question in writing to whoever set the task and keep the reply before you begin working. This shows you check the guidance before using AI and resolve doubt through a recorded exchange rather than assumption. **Evidence:** The guidance with your annotations, your written question and the reply you received before starting. *(Grounding: professional: Seeks written clarification on unclear rules before acting, rather than assuming.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Mapping a module's AI rule to its learning outcomes.* Before using any AI tool, you read your module's AI statement and its learning outcomes side by side. Using Biggs' (1996) constructive alignment, you explain in a few sentences why the rule fits what the task is assessing (for example, why a first-year economics essay bans AI for drafting because the outcome is your own argument). This shows you check the guidance first and understand why the rule exists. **Evidence:** A one-page table linking each part of the AI statement to a learning outcome, with your short explanation. *(Grounding: model: Constructive alignment (Biggs 1996))*
        - *Applied: Clarifying AI guidance with a tutor before an assignment.* Before starting a first-year business assignment, you find the AI statement for that task and note what is allowed and what is not. Where it is unclear, you email your tutor a specific question and keep the reply (for example, whether you may use AI to check your grammar in a group report). This shows you check the assessment guidance before using AI. **Evidence:** The task's AI statement with your annotations, your question to the tutor and the tutor's reply. *(Grounding: practice: first-year coursework)*
3. **Recognise what AI cannot be trusted for, and think first**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Digital skills (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Monitoring log of confidence before trusting a digital tool.* **Learning outcome:** The student can identify, before relying on a digital tool's answer, where their own understanding falls short and where the tool's output cannot be trusted.
          Before relying on a digital tool to answer a question in a field you are learning, you note your own level of confidence and understanding first. You then test the tool's answer against that starting point, using an established account of monitoring your own thinking, and record where the tool's output could not be trusted. This demonstrates judging where the digital tools a field relies on fall short, before using them. **Evidence:** A short monitoring log recording your starting confidence, the tool's answer and where it could not be trusted. *(Grounding: model: Metacognition and cognitive monitoring (Flavell 1979); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/digital-skills)*
        - *Applied: Record of a request a digital tool should not handle.* **Learning outcome:** The student can recognise a type of request that a standard digital tool should not be trusted to answer and set out what to do instead.
          You identify a type of request that the standard digital tools in your field should not be used to answer, because it needs judgement a tool cannot supply. You think the request through yourself, note exactly why the tool is unsuitable for it, and set out what you did instead of relying on the tool. This demonstrates recognising the limits of the digital tools and technologies a field relies on. **Evidence:** A short written record of the request, the reasoning for not using the tool, and the alternative you used. *(Grounding: professional: Identifies requests that fall outside what a standard digital tool should be trusted to answer.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/digital-skills)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Comparison of your own answer against an AI answer.* You answer a short question yourself first, then, where it is permitted, compare your answer with one produced by an AI tool. Using an established account of monitoring your own thinking, you note where you were confident, where the AI's answer was wrong or vague, and what kind of question it cannot be trusted to answer. This shows you recognise the limits of AI, and of your own knowledge, before relying on either. **Evidence:** A short log showing your own answer, the AI's answer, the checks you made and what you concluded. *(Grounding: model: Metacognition and cognitive monitoring (Flavell 1979))*
        - *Applied: Decision not to rely on AI for a specific request.* In a role where you support other people, someone asks you a question that needs individual, qualified judgement rather than a general answer. You recognise that an AI tool cannot be trusted to answer this kind of request responsibly, think through what you do and do not know yourself, and refer the person to someone qualified instead. This shows you know when AI should not be relied on. **Evidence:** A brief log entry recording the request, why you did not use AI, and who you referred it to. *(Grounding: professional: Recognises requests that need individual, qualified judgement rather than a general AI-generated answer.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Think-first comparison of your answer and an AI answer.* You answer a short study question yourself first, then, where permitted, compare your answer with a chatbot's. Using Flavell's (1979) idea of metacognitive monitoring, you note where you were confident, where the AI was wrong or vague, and what it cannot be trusted for (for example, current legal thresholds in a first-year law topic). This shows you recognise the limits of AI and of your own knowledge, and think before relying on it. **Evidence:** A short reflective log entry showing your own answer, the AI answer, the checks you made and what you concluded. *(Grounding: model: Metacognition and cognitive monitoring (Flavell 1979))*
        - *Applied: Deciding not to rely on AI in a gym job.* Working part time at a gym, a member asks you for a diet plan for a health condition. You recognise that a chatbot cannot be trusted for individual health advice, think through what you know and do not know, and refer the member to a qualified colleague (for example, someone managing diabetes asking about pre-training meals). This shows you know when AI should not be relied on. **Evidence:** A brief workplace log entry recording the request, why you did not use AI and who you referred the member to. *(Grounding: practice: part-time job in a gym)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Digital skills (direct; lands on `ai-technology#i1`, `ai-technology#i2`, `ai-technology#i3`)

### Intermediate

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 4 | Apply a critical-use workflow, AI as a critical friend | L4 | l4/knowledge.2 "Analyse, interpret and evaluate relevant information and ideas." | direct | `ai-technology/03` |
| 5 | Fact-check AI output and disclose its use | L5 | l5/knowledge.2 "Analyse, interpret and evaluate relevant information, concepts and ideas." | direct | `ai-technology#d2` |
| 6 | Decide, with reasons, when and when not to use AI | L6 | 4.15.b9.2 "decision-making in complex and unpredictable contexts"; l6/autonomy.3 "Exercise broad autonomy and judgement." | direct | `ai-technology/01` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

4. **Apply a critical-use workflow, AI as a critical friend**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Applying knowledge to practice or problems (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Digital skills (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Independent and self-directed learning (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Reasoned judgement after an AI tool questions a draft.* **Learning outcome:** The student can evaluate challenges raised by an AI tool against the actual evidence to reach a reasoned judgement that remains their own.
          You give an AI tool a piece of reasoning you have already drafted and ask it to challenge the weakest points, rather than to produce new content. You weigh each challenge against the evidence you actually have, decide which points genuinely need revising, and set out the reasoning behind each decision you reach. This demonstrates evaluating evidence and arguments critically to reach a judgement that is still your own. **Evidence:** A short written account of the AI's challenges, your evaluation of each, and your final reasoned position. *(Grounding: model: Critical friend (Costa and Kallick 1993); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
        - *Applied: Verified judgement reached with AI acting as challenger.* **Learning outcome:** The student can use an AI tool to question a piece of work, test each challenge against the evidence, and justify which points to revise.
          Before finishing a piece of work, you use an AI tool to question your reasoning and flag claims that look weak or unsupported. You test each flagged point against the actual evidence available, decide what to change and what to keep, and record a brief justification for each call you make. This demonstrates evaluating evidence and arguments critically, using AI to probe rather than replace your own judgement. **Evidence:** A short record of the AI's challenges, your evaluation of each, and the justification for your final decisions. *(Grounding: professional: Uses AI to draft, question and check work while keeping professional judgement and verifying output.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Prompt log questioning your own draft with AI.* Where it is permitted, you ask an AI tool to question a piece of work you have already drafted yourself, rather than to write it for you. Using an established idea of a critical friend who asks provoking questions and offers critique, you record each prompt, what the tool suggested, and whether you accepted or rejected it, with reasons. This shows you use AI critically while the thinking stays your own. **Evidence:** A prompt and decision log beside your draft, with your reasons for each accepted or rejected suggestion. *(Grounding: model: Critical friend (Costa and Kallick 1993))*
        - *Applied: Decision log for AI assisted review of finished work.* Using an AI tool that is approved for the task, you write a piece of work yourself, then use the tool to test it for tone, clarity or gaps. You check its suggestions against what you actually need, keep your own judgement on what should change, and record your reasoning for each decision you make. This shows you treat AI as a critical friend rather than a replacement for your own thinking. **Evidence:** A dated log of AI suggestions you kept or rejected, with your reasoning recorded for each. *(Grounding: professional: Uses AI to draft, question and check work while keeping professional judgement and verifying output.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Critical friend prompt log on your own draft.* Where your task permits it, you ask an AI tool to question a draft you have already written, not to write it. Using Costa and Kallick's (1993) idea of a critical friend who asks provoking questions and offers critique, you record each prompt, what the AI suggested and whether you accepted or rejected it, with reasons (for example, a geography essay where you rejected an invented statistic). This shows you use AI critically while the thinking stays yours. **Evidence:** A prompt-and-decision log beside your draft, with your disclosure statement and reasons for each accepted or rejected suggestion. *(Grounding: model: Critical friend (Costa and Kallick 1993))*
        - *Applied: Using permitted AI to review copy on a placement.* On a marketing placement where your manager allows a company-approved AI tool, you write campaign copy yourself, then use the tool to test it for tone and clarity. You verify its claims, keep your own judgement on what the client needs and tell your manager how you used it (for example, rejecting a suggested health claim the product cannot support). **Evidence:** A dated decision log of AI suggestions you kept or rejected, with your manager's note confirming the tool was approved. *(Grounding: professional: Uses AI to draft, question and check work while keeping professional judgement and verifying output.)*
5. **Fact-check AI output and disclose its use**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Digital skills (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Verification record for a digital tool's factual output.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse why a digital tool's factual claims require verification against an independent source before being accepted.
          A digital tool you are learning to use produces a factual claim as part of its output, and instead of accepting it, you check it against an independent, reliable source. Using an established method for verifying information away from where it was first encountered, you record what you found and whether the claim held up. This demonstrates using a digital tool a field relies on while still checking what it produces. **Evidence:** A short verification record showing the claim checked, the independent source used and the outcome. *(Grounding: model: Lateral reading (Wineburg and McGrew 2019); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/digital-skills)*
        - *Applied: Disclosed, verified use of a digital tool's output.* **Learning outcome:** The student can check a digital tool's factual output against an independent source and disclose clearly how and where the tool was used.
          After a digital tool produces output you intend to use, you check its factual claims against an independent source before relying on any of them. You keep a short record of what you checked and what you found, and you disclose clearly, in the finished work, exactly how and where the tool was used. This demonstrates using the digital tools a field relies on responsibly and transparently. **Evidence:** A verification record of the claims checked, plus the disclosure statement describing how the tool was used. *(Grounding: professional: Checks a digital tool's factual output against an independent source and discloses how the tool was used.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/digital-skills)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Lateral reading check of claims from an AI answer.* Where AI use is permitted, you take several factual claims from an AI tool's answer and check each one by lateral reading, leaving the output to see what independent, reliable sources actually say. You record which claims held up, which were wrong, and the source you will rely on instead of the tool. This shows you fact-check AI output rather than accepting fluent wording as proof. **Evidence:** A verification table listing each claim checked, the method used and the outcome, with sources noted. *(Grounding: model: Lateral reading (Wineburg and McGrew 2019))*
        - *Applied: Disclosure statement for fact checked AI assistance.* Where a task allows an AI tool to help explain something technical, you test every suggestion it gives against an authoritative, independent source before using it. You note anything that was wrong, outdated or unsupported, and write a clear disclosure statement setting out exactly how and where you used the tool. This shows you fact-check AI output and disclose its use honestly rather than silently. **Evidence:** Your disclosure statement and a short testing log showing each suggestion, how you checked it and the result. *(Grounding: professional: Tests AI-generated claims against an authoritative source before relying on them, and discloses the use honestly.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Lateral reading verification table for AI output.* Where AI use is permitted, you take five factual claims from an AI answer and check each one by lateral reading: leaving the output to see what reliable sources say, as described by Wineburg and McGrew (2019). You record which claims held, which were wrong and the source you will cite instead (for example, drug dosage claims in a pharmacy task). You then write a clear disclosure of how you used AI. **Evidence:** A verification table of claims, checks and outcomes, with the disclosure statement you attached to your work. *(Grounding: model: Lateral reading (Wineburg and McGrew 2019))*
        - *Applied: Fact-checked, disclosed AI use in a coding assignment.* In a computing assignment that allows AI to explain error messages, you test every AI suggestion against the official documentation before using it. You note what was wrong or outdated and write a disclosure statement in the format your department requires (for example, an AI explanation that relied on a deprecated library function). This shows you fact-check AI output and disclose its use honestly. **Evidence:** Your submitted disclosure statement and a testing log showing each AI suggestion, how you checked it and the result. *(Grounding: practice: permitted AI use in a programming assignment)*
6. **Decide, with reasons, when and when not to use AI**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Digital skills (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Policy based reasoning for using one digital tool over another.* **Learning outcome:** The student can weigh the goals and risks of a task against a stated policy to decide which digital tool, if any, is appropriate.
          Faced with a choice between two digital tools that a field commonly relies on, you set out, using a structured model of planning before acting, the goals the task needs to meet and the risks each tool brings. You weigh the options against a stated policy on acceptable use, and record which tool you chose and why you chose it. This demonstrates reasoned, policy-aware use of the digital tools a field relies on. **Evidence:** A short written comparison of the two tools, the policy applied and the reasoned choice you made. *(Grounding: model: Self-regulated learning cycle (Zimmerman 2002); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/digital-skills)*
        - *Applied: Documented decision to use or decline a digital tool.* **Learning outcome:** The student can decide, with reference to policy and a reasoned weighing of benefits against risks, whether to use or decline a digital tool for a task.
          You are asked whether a digital tool commonly used in your field should be used for a specific task. You check the relevant policy on its use, weigh the benefits against the risks it carries for this particular task, and record a clear decision with your reasoning, whether that decision is to use the tool or to decline it. This demonstrates using the digital tools a field relies on, with justified, policy-aware judgement. **Evidence:** A short written decision record stating the policy checked, the reasoning weighed and the final choice made. *(Grounding: professional: Decides whether and how to use a digital tool in work, within policy, and can justify the decision.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/digital-skills)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Forethought decision record on using AI for a task.* Before starting a substantial piece of work, you write a short decision record on whether and how to use AI. Using the forethought phase of an established model of self regulated learning, you set your goals, weigh what AI would help with against what it would take away from your own learning, and check the relevant rules first. You revisit the decision once the work is finished. **Evidence:** A one-page decision record with your reasons, the rule checked and a short review written afterwards. *(Grounding: model: Self-regulated learning cycle (Zimmerman 2002))*
        - *Applied: Justified decision on AI use within a group's policy.* Acting on behalf of a group, you decide whether to use an AI tool for a task involving other people's information. You check the relevant data policy, weigh time saved against privacy and accuracy risks, and explain your decision and reasoning to the people affected before going ahead with it. This shows you can decide, with reasons, when and when not to use AI within a stated policy. **Evidence:** The decision note you shared, citing the policy checked, and the group's record approving it. *(Grounding: professional: Decides whether and how to use AI tools in work, within the organisation's policy, and can justify the decision.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Forethought decision record on AI use for one task.* Before starting a substantial assignment, you write a decision record on whether and how to use AI. Using the forethought phase of Zimmerman's (2002) self-regulated learning cycle, you set your goals, weigh what AI would help and what it would take away from your learning, and check the rules (for example, a psychology report where you use AI only to test your search terms). You revisit the decision afterwards. **Evidence:** A one-page decision record with your reasons, the rule you checked and a short review written after submission. *(Grounding: model: Self-regulated learning cycle (Zimmerman 2002))*
        - *Applied: Society decision on AI for meeting minutes.* As secretary of a student society, you decide whether to use an AI tool to draft meeting minutes. You check the students' union's data policy, weigh time saved against members' privacy and accuracy, and explain your decision to the committee (for example, choosing not to upload recordings that include welfare discussions). This shows you can decide with reasons when and when not to use AI. **Evidence:** The decision note shared with the committee, citing the policy you checked, and the minutes approving it. *(Grounding: professional: Decides whether and how to use AI tools in work, within the organisation's policy, and can justify the decision. (Jisc digital capabilities: digital learning and development))*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (direct; lands on `ai-technology/03`)
- Universal: Digital skills (direct; lands on `ai-technology/03`, `ai-technology#d2`, `ai-technology/01`)
- Universal: Independent and self-directed learning (indirect; lands on `ai-technology/03`)

### Advanced

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | Avoid the GenAI misconduct risks, and never cite GenAI as a source | L5 | 4.12.b8 "the exercise of personal responsibility" | **UNATTRIBUTED** | `ai-technology/02` |
| 8 | Distinguish permitted support from unfair advantage | L6 | 4.15.b9.1 "the exercise of initiative and personal responsibility" | direct | `ai-technology/06` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

7. **Avoid the GenAI misconduct risks, and never cite GenAI as a source**
    - *Benchmark:* none matched at this tier (**UNATTRIBUTED**)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Written account of why AI output cannot be a source.* **Learning outcome:** The student can justify why presenting generative AI output as a citable source constitutes misconduct rather than a legitimate shortcut.
          You set out, in a short written account, why treating a generative AI tool's output as a citable source is a form of misconduct rather than a shortcut. You explain the difference between using AI to support your thinking and presenting its output as if it were evidence, and you identify two further risks the same misuse could create. This demonstrates avoiding GenAI misconduct risks and never citing GenAI as a source. **Evidence:** A short written account distinguishing AI support from AI as a false source, with two further risks identified. *(Grounding: model: Fundamental values of academic integrity (ICAI 2021); benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
        - *Applied: Tracing a primary source instead of citing an AI summary.* **Learning outcome:** The student can trace a claim generated by an AI tool back to its original source and cite that source instead of the AI output.
          You come across a claim that has clearly been generated by an AI tool and that someone intends to rely on as if it were a source. Rather than repeating or citing it, you trace the original material the claim refers to, check it directly, and cite that original material instead. This demonstrates avoiding GenAI misconduct risks and never treating generative AI output as a citable source. **Evidence:** A short note tracing the claim to its original source, with the citation you used in place of the AI output. *(Grounding: professional: Uses AI within professional and legal rules on authorship, confidentiality and attribution.; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Case analysis of GenAI misconduct scenarios against core values.* You analyse several short scenarios of generative AI use against a recognised set of core integrity values, including honesty, trust, fairness, respect, responsibility and courage. For each scenario, you judge whether it risks misconduct and explain why treating an AI tool's output as a citable source is never acceptable. This shows you can identify GenAI misconduct risks and the reasoning behind never citing AI as a source. **Evidence:** A written case analysis of several scenarios, with your judgement on each and the values each one engages. *(Grounding: model: Fundamental values of academic integrity (ICAI 2021))*
        - *Applied: Confidentiality check before using a public AI tool.* You are tempted to paste sensitive material into a public AI tool to get a quick summary of it. You check the relevant confidentiality policy, decline to do so once you see the risk, and when someone else's AI generated summary refers to a source, you trace and cite the original document rather than the AI's version of it. This shows you avoid GenAI misconduct risks and never treat AI output as a source. **Evidence:** A short decision record showing the policy checked and the original source you traced and cited instead. *(Grounding: professional: Uses AI within professional and legal rules on authorship, confidentiality and attribution.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Integrity case analysis of GenAI misconduct scenarios.* You analyse four short scenarios of GenAI use against the six fundamental values of academic integrity set out by the International Center for Academic Integrity (ICAI 2021): honesty, trust, fairness, respect, responsibility and courage. For each, you judge whether it risks misconduct and why citing GenAI output as a source is never acceptable (for example, a history student citing a chatbot's summary of a treaty instead of the treaty itself). **Evidence:** A written case analysis of four scenarios with your judgement on each and the integrity values it engages. *(Grounding: model: Fundamental values of academic integrity (ICAI 2021))*
        - *Applied: Confidentiality and attribution decision on a legal placement.* On a placement in a law firm, you are tempted to paste a client letter into a public AI tool to summarise it. You check the firm's policy, decline because of confidentiality, and when a colleague's AI summary names a case, you find and cite the judgment itself (for example, tracing a cited precedent to the law report). This shows you avoid GenAI risks and never treat its output as a source. **Evidence:** A decision record signed by your supervisor, showing the policy checked and the primary sources you traced and cited. *(Grounding: professional: Uses AI within professional and legal rules on authorship, confidentiality and attribution.)*
8. **Distinguish permitted support from unfair advantage**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Digital skills (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Reasoned judgement on a borderline assistive tool.* **Learning outcome:** The student can critique the evidence for and against a borderline assistive tool to reach a defensible judgement on whether its use is fair.
          Given a tool that sits on the boundary between fair support and unfair advantage, you weigh the evidence for each side, including what the task is meant to test and what the tool actually does, before reaching a judgement. You set out your reasoning so someone else could follow and challenge it, rather than simply asserting a conclusion without support. This demonstrates evaluating evidence and arguments critically to reach a reasoned judgement. **Evidence:** A short written judgement on the borderline tool, with the evidence weighed and the reasoning set out. *(Grounding: model: Universal Design for Learning (CAST 2018); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
        - *Applied: Recommendation on a disputed tool's fairness.* **Learning outcome:** The student can weigh what a task is meant to assess against what a disputed tool actually does, and justify a recommendation on its fairness.
          You are asked to recommend whether a specific tool should be allowed in a regulated task, and the evidence on it is mixed. You weigh what the task is meant to assess against what the tool can actually do for the person using it, state which consideration carries most weight, and make a justified recommendation. This demonstrates evaluating evidence and arguments critically to reach a judgement someone else can act on. **Evidence:** A short recommendation stating the evidence weighed, the judgement reached and the reasoning behind it. *(Grounding: professional: Judges whether a tool or aid is fair and permitted in a regulated assessment or practice.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/critical-evaluation-of-evidence-and-arguments)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Position paper distinguishing access support from replaced skill.* You write a short position paper distinguishing support that removes a barrier to doing a task from support that does the assessed work for you. Drawing on an established model of inclusive design, you argue that some tools can widen access without changing what is being assessed, while others may replace the very skill being tested. You test your distinction against two borderline examples you have chosen. **Evidence:** A short position paper with two borderline cases analysed and your reasoned judgement on each. *(Grounding: model: Universal Design for Learning (CAST 2018))*
        - *Applied: Guidance sheet judging fair support against unfair advantage.* People preparing for a regulated task ask you which tools are allowed for it. You compare the relevant rules against each tool they mention, judge which give fair support and which would hand an unfair advantage, and draft a short guidance sheet for someone with authority to check and approve. This shows you can distinguish permitted support from unfair advantage and put that judgement into a usable form. **Evidence:** The guidance sheet, with the checking authority's corrections, approval and a note of the rules used. *(Grounding: professional: Judges whether a tool or aid is fair and permitted in a regulated assessment or practice.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Position paper on assistive tools and fair assessment.* You write a short position paper distinguishing support that removes a barrier from support that does the assessed work for you. Using Universal Design for Learning (CAST 2018), you argue that tools such as speech-to-text can widen access without changing what is assessed, while a paraphrasing tool may replace the skill being tested (for example, in a law coursework essay). You test your distinction against two borderline cases. **Evidence:** A 1,200-word position paper with two borderline cases analysed and your reasoned judgement on each. *(Grounding: model: Universal Design for Learning (CAST 2018))*
        - *Applied: Peer guidance sheet on permitted tools for an exam.* As a peer-assisted learning leader in engineering, you are asked by students which tools are allowed in a coming open-book exam. You compare the exam regulations with the tools they mention, judge which give fair support and which an unfair advantage, and draft a guidance sheet for the module lead to check (for example, a permitted calculator versus an AI solver app). **Evidence:** The guidance sheet with the module lead's corrections and approval, and a note of the regulations you used. *(Grounding: professional: Judges whether a tool or aid is fair and permitted in a regulated assessment or practice.)*
9. **Weigh AI's costs and benefits as a responsible professional user**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Ethics, integrity and values (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Digital skills (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Ethical weighing of a technology's costs against its benefits.* **Learning outcome:** The student can synthesise a technology's ethical costs, such as environmental impact or effect on roles, with its practical benefits to reach a justified position.
          You are given a technology that raises a genuine ethical question, such as its environmental cost or its effect on people's work, and asked to weigh this against its practical benefits. Using an established framework for balancing social, environmental and economic considerations, you set out the competing factors and reach a reasoned position on whether the benefits justify the costs. This demonstrates recognising and acting on the ethical issues a technology raises. **Evidence:** A short written appraisal weighing the technology's ethical costs against its benefits, with a reasoned conclusion. *(Grounding: model: Triple bottom line (Elkington 1997); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/ethics)*
        - *Applied: Ethically justified recommendation on adopting a technology.* **Learning outcome:** The student can assess the ethical costs and practical benefits of adopting a technology and justify a recommendation that withstands disagreement.
          You are asked to recommend whether a technology should be adopted, and the ethical costs, including its environmental impact and effect on people's roles, are not trivial. You weigh these costs against the practical benefits, state clearly where you draw the line, and put forward a recommendation you can justify to someone who disagrees with it. This demonstrates recognising and acting on the ethical responsibilities a technology decision carries. **Evidence:** A short recommendation record stating the ethical costs weighed, the benefits considered and your justified decision. *(Grounding: professional: Assesses the costs, risks and sustainability of adopting a technology against its benefits.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/ethics)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Triple bottom line appraisal of your own AI use.* You appraise your own use of AI over a period of time using an established framework weighing people, planet and profit. You weigh time saved and quality gained against energy use, data and privacy risks, and the kind of practitioner the habit is shaping you into. You end with a short, justified personal policy on when you will and will not use the tool. **Evidence:** A reflective appraisal with a costs and benefits table under the three headings and your written personal policy. *(Grounding: model: Triple bottom line (Elkington 1997))*
        - *Applied: Options appraisal recommending whether to adopt an AI tool.* You are asked whether an organisation should adopt an AI tool for a routine task it currently handles another way. You weigh costs, data protection, environmental impact and effects on the people involved against the expected benefits, disclose any AI you used in producing the analysis, and make a justified recommendation. This shows you can weigh AI's costs and benefits responsibly before recommending its adoption. **Evidence:** The options appraisal with your recommendation, an AI-use disclosure and a record of the response you received. *(Grounding: professional: Assesses the costs, risks and sustainability of adopting a technology against its benefits.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Triple bottom line appraisal of AI in your own practice.* You appraise your own use of AI over one term using Elkington's (1997) triple bottom line of people, planet and profit. You weigh time saved and quality gained against energy use, data and privacy risks, and the effect on the graduate you want to become (for example, a master's architecture student deciding whether image generators belong in early concept work). You end with a justified personal policy. **Evidence:** A reflective appraisal with a costs and benefits table under the three headings and your written personal AI policy. *(Grounding: model: Triple bottom line (Elkington 1997))*
        - *Applied: Options appraisal of an AI chatbot for a small firm.* On a consultancy project for a small business, you are asked whether it should adopt an AI chatbot for customer enquiries. You weigh costs, data protection, environmental impact and effects on staff against expected benefits, disclose any AI you used in the analysis, and make a justified recommendation (for example, advising a family-run travel agent to trial the tool only for booking changes). **Evidence:** The options appraisal report with your recommendation, AI-use disclosure and the client's written response. *(Grounding: professional: Assesses the costs, risks and sustainability of adopting a technology against its benefits.)*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (direct; lands on `ai-technology/05`)
- Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (direct; lands on `ai-technology/06`)
- Universal: Ethics, integrity and values (direct; lands on `ai-technology/05`)
- Universal: Digital skills (direct; lands on `ai-technology/06`, `ai-technology/05`)

<a id="creativity"></a>
## Creativity

### Introductory

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Recognise creativity as a developable skill, related to critical thinking | L3 | l3/knowledge.4 "awareness of different perspectives or approaches" | direct | `creativity/01` |
| 2 | Use divergent and convergent thinking at the right stage of a task | L4 | l4/application.1 "Address problems that are complex" | direct | `creativity/02` |
| 3 | Overcome creative barriers such as fear of failure and perfectionism | L5 | l5/autonomy.2 "Exercise autonomy and judgement within broad parameters." | direct | `creativity/03` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

1. **Recognise creativity as a developable skill, related to critical thinking**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 1.11: critical thinking, problem-solving, data analysis, ethical awareness (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.16: apply critical-thinking skills to address complex problems to identifying creative and innovative solutions (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 4.10: demonstrated creative thinking when designing psychological research with relevance to contemporary psychological issues (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Enterprise and creativity (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Explaining why creativity counts as a buildable skill.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain why creativity functions as a skill that develops through practice rather than a fixed trait, linking it to critical thinking and judgement.
          You set out, in your own words, why creativity should be treated as a skill that improves with practice rather than a fixed gift some people have and others do not. You link this to critical thinking by showing how judging and selecting among ideas is itself part of being creative, not separate from it. You give one example of your own thinking becoming more creative through deliberate practice over time. **Evidence:** A short written explanation linking creativity's developability to critical judgement, with one personal example. *(Grounding: model: Four C model of creativity (Kaufman and Beghetto 2009); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/enterprise-and-creativity)*
        - *Applied: Treating creative thinking as normal in any role.* **Learning outcome:** The student can identify moments of creative thinking within everyday tasks and describe how judgement was used to decide whether an idea was worth pursuing.
          Across a period of regular work, you keep a short record of moments when you generated an idea rather than simply following existing steps. For each one, you note what prompted the idea and how you judged whether it was worth pursuing. This builds a pattern across several entries, showing that you approach creative thinking as something expected of anyone doing the work, not a rare talent held by a specialist. **Evidence:** A short record of several moments where an idea was generated and judged, kept over time. *(Grounding: professional: Approaches creative thinking as an expected part of any role, not a rare talent.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/enterprise-and-creativity)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Noticing small creative moments in everyday learning.* You read a short account of a model of creativity that ranges from small personal insights to widely recognised breakthroughs. Across a week you note three small moments where you had a new idea while working on something, and beside each you record the judgement you made about whether it was worth keeping. This shows you treat creativity as a skill you can notice and develop, not a fixed trait, and that you link each idea to a check rather than accepting it unexamined. **Evidence:** A short log of three small creative moments, each paired with the judgement made about it. *(Grounding: model: Four C model of creativity (Kaufman and Beghetto 2009))*
        - *Applied: Suggesting a small improvement in a part-time job.* In a part-time job you notice a recurring bottleneck and suggest a small change to how things are organised. You explain how you arrived at the idea, what you checked before raising it, and what happened once it was tried. This shows you treat creative thinking as an ordinary part of everyday work rather than a special talent reserved for a few people, and that you can back a suggestion with reasoning rather than instinct alone. **Evidence:** A brief written account of the idea raised, the checks made and the outcome once tried. *(Grounding: professional: Treats creative thinking as part of professional practice, not a specialist's talent. (Vitae RDF A3: creativity))*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Spotting everyday creativity in your own studying.* You read a short account of Kaufman and Beghetto's (2009) Four C model, which ranges from mini-c (personal insights while learning) to Big-C (eminent creativity). You then note three mini-c moments from your own week and the critical judgement that went with each (for example, a new way of laying out biology revision notes that you then tested). This shows you see creativity as a skill you can develop, linked to critical thinking. **Evidence:** A short reflective log listing three mini-c moments, each with the idea, how you judged it and what happened. *(Grounding: model: Four C model of creativity (Kaufman and Beghetto 2009))*
        - *Applied: Improvement idea suggested in a part-time café job.* In a part-time café job, you notice queues build at lunchtime and suggest a small change to how orders are taken. You describe how you came up with the idea and how you checked it would work before suggesting it (for example, a separate pre-order point for regular customers). This shows you treat creative thinking as part of everyday work, not a special talent. **Evidence:** A reflective log entry describing the idea, your checks and your supervisor's response to the suggestion. *(Grounding: professional: Treats creative thinking as part of professional practice, not a specialist's talent. (Vitae RDF A3: creativity))*
2. **Use divergent and convergent thinking at the right stage of a task**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.16: apply critical-thinking skills to address complex problems to identifying creative and innovative solutions (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Showing staged thinking solve a complex problem.* **Learning outcome:** The student can describe how divergent thinking to generate options and convergent thinking to select among them apply at different stages of solving a complex problem.
          Faced with a problem that has no obvious single answer, you first open up as many possible approaches as you can without ruling any out, then switch deliberately to narrowing them down against stated criteria. You label which stage you are in at each point and explain why you moved from one to the other. This demonstrates applying critical thinking to a complex problem by identifying a creative and workable solution. **Evidence:** A labelled record showing the open stage, the narrowing stage and the reasoning for switching between them. *(Grounding: model: Divergent and convergent thinking (Guilford 1967); benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.16)*
        - *Applied: Widening then narrowing options to solve a live problem.* **Learning outcome:** The student can identify the appropriate stage at which to widen a set of options before narrowing them against clear criteria to reach a workable solution.
          Given a problem with several possible solutions and no obvious best answer, you first gather as many options as you can from those involved before ruling anything out. You then agree criteria with the group and use them to narrow the list to one recommended solution, recording why the others were set aside. This demonstrates applying critical thinking to a complex problem to reach a creative, workable solution. **Evidence:** A short record of the options generated, the criteria agreed and the solution chosen with reasons. *(Grounding: professional: Generates options widely before narrowing to a decision. (Vitae RDF A3: creativity); benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.16)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Separating idea generation from judgement in an open task.* Working on a task with an open brief, you separate generating ideas from judging them, following a two-stage approach that moves from opening up widely to narrowing down on one option. You list at least fifteen possible approaches without judging them, then apply criteria you set in advance to narrow down to one. This shows you can use each kind of thinking at the right point rather than mixing them together too early. **Evidence:** Your unfiltered list of ideas, the criteria you set and a shortlist showing how you narrowed to one. *(Grounding: model: Double Diamond (Design Council 2005); model: Divergent and convergent thinking (Guilford 1967))*
        - *Applied: Running an idea session before deciding on one option.* Leading a short session with a group, you ask everyone to suggest as many options as possible for a shared task before anyone judges them. Once the list is complete, you agree criteria together, such as cost or likely impact, and use them to narrow down to one choice. This shows you generate options widely before narrowing to a decision, rather than settling on the first workable idea that comes up. **Evidence:** Photographed idea sheets, the agreed criteria and a note of the final decision reached. *(Grounding: professional: Generates options widely before narrowing to a decision. (Vitae RDF A3: creativity))*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Diverge then converge on a first-year design task.* On a set design task, you separate idea generation from judgement, following the Double Diamond's diverge and converge phases (Design Council 2005) and Guilford's (1967) distinction between divergent and convergent thinking. You first list at least fifteen ideas without judging them, then apply stated criteria to choose one (for example, concepts for a low-cost water filter in a first-year engineering module). This shows you use each kind of thinking at the right stage. **Evidence:** Your unfiltered idea list, the criteria you set and a scored shortlist showing how you narrowed to one idea. *(Grounding: model: Double Diamond (Design Council 2005); model: Divergent and convergent thinking (Guilford 1967))*
        - *Applied: Choosing a fundraising event for a student society.* As a member of a student society's fundraising team, you run a short session where members suggest as many event ideas as possible before anyone judges them. Then, with the group, you agree criteria such as cost and likely turnout and narrow to one event (for example, a charity quiz chosen from twenty ideas). This shows you generate options widely before narrowing to a decision. **Evidence:** Photographed idea sheets, the agreed criteria and the committee's note of the final decision. *(Grounding: professional: Generates options widely before narrowing to a decision. (Vitae RDF A3: creativity))*
3. **Overcome creative barriers such as fear of failure and perfectionism**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Enterprise and creativity (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Explaining how perfectionism was overcome to apply an idea.* **Learning outcome:** The student can explain how fear of failure and perfectionism inhibit creative action, and describe reasoning that helps move past such barriers.
          You identify a point where perfectionism, or a fear of getting it wrong, nearly stopped you from trying out an idea. You explain the thinking that was holding you back, the reasoning you used to move past it, and the small step you took to test the idea despite the risk of it not working. This demonstrates showing creativity in applying an idea rather than withholding it until it felt entirely safe. **Evidence:** A short written account of the barrier noticed, the reasoning used to move past it and the step taken. *(Grounding: model: Growth mindset (Dweck 2006); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/enterprise-and-creativity)*
        - *Applied: Testing an early idea despite the risk of it failing.* **Learning outcome:** The student can identify an early, imperfect idea worth testing in practice and describe how to proceed despite the risk that it might not succeed.
          Rather than waiting until an idea feels fully worked out, you put forward an early version of it and test it in practice, accepting that it might not work the first time. When it does not fully succeed, you record what you learned and adjust it rather than withdrawing the idea altogether. This demonstrates showing creativity and enterprise by applying an idea and working through its early setbacks. **Evidence:** A short record of the early idea tested, what happened and the adjustment made afterwards. *(Grounding: professional: Puts forward and tests ideas without fear of early failure. (Vitae RDF A3: creativity); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/enterprise-and-creativity)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Sharing unfinished work instead of waiting for perfect.* You choose a piece of work you would usually hold back until it felt finished, and share an early, rough version for feedback instead. Drawing on a well-known distinction between fixed and growth mindsets, you reflect on the thoughts that made you want to hold back and on what the feedback taught you. This shows you can notice fear of failure and perfectionism and work past them rather than letting them stop you from sharing early. **Evidence:** A reflective entry with the rough draft, the feedback received and your account of overcoming the urge to hold back. *(Grounding: model: Growth mindset (Dweck 2006))*
        - *Applied: Trying an untested approach and recording what happened.* In a role where you are expected to deliver a planned activity, you try an approach you have not seen used before, knowing it might not work well. It only partly succeeds, so you record what happened, what you learned and how you would change it next time, rather than retreating to a safer, familiar method. This shows you put forward and test ideas without being stopped by fear of early failure. **Evidence:** A short plan and evaluation of the activity, with written comments on how it went. *(Grounding: professional: Puts forward and tests ideas without fear of early failure. (Vitae RDF A3: creativity))*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Mindset reflection on sharing a rough draft.* You choose a piece of work you would usually keep back until it felt perfect and share an early, rough version for feedback. Using Dweck's (2006) distinction between fixed and growth mindsets, you reflect on the thoughts that held you back and what the feedback taught you (for example, sharing an unfinished composition in a music workshop). This shows you can notice and work past fear of failure and perfectionism. **Evidence:** A reflective entry with the rough draft, the feedback received and your account of how you handled the urge to hold back. *(Grounding: model: Growth mindset (Dweck 2006))*
        - *Applied: Trying a new classroom activity on school placement.* On a primary school placement, you try a new activity you have not seen modelled, knowing it might not work. It partly fails, and you record what happened, what you learned and how you would adapt it, rather than going back to safe methods (for example, a role-play to teach fractions that ran over time). This shows you put forward and test ideas without fear of early failure. **Evidence:** Your lesson plan and evaluation for the activity, with your placement mentor's signed observation comments. *(Grounding: professional: Puts forward and tests ideas without fear of early failure. (Vitae RDF A3: creativity))*

**Benchmarks keying in at this tier (match):**

- Psychology 3.16: apply critical-thinking skills to address complex problems to identifying creative and innovative solutions (direct; lands on `creativity/01`, `creativity/02`)
- Psychology 4.10: demonstrated creative thinking when designing psychological research with relevance to contemporary psychological issues (direct; lands on `creativity/01`)
- Universal: Enterprise and creativity (direct; lands on `creativity/01`, `creativity/03`)
- Universal: Critical evaluation of evidence and arguments (indirect; lands on `creativity/01`)

### Intermediate

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 4 | Draw on creativity theory to justify a creative approach | L4 | 4.10.b1 "knowledge of the underlying concepts and principles" | direct | `creativity/07` |
| 5 | Use structured creative problem-solving methods | L5 | 4.12.b5 "to propose solutions to problems" | direct | `creativity/04` |
