**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

4. **Draw on creativity theory to justify a creative approach**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Enterprise and creativity (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Using theory to justify an unconventional approach.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse an unconventional approach against an established creativity theory to justify why it is likely to produce a stronger outcome than convention.
          You choose an unconventional way of approaching a piece of work and set out, with reference to established theory, why that choice is more likely to produce a strong result than a conventional one. You name the theory or model you are drawing on and explain precisely how it supports your reasoning, rather than treating the approach as simply a preference. This demonstrates showing creativity in applying an idea, backed by a stated rationale rather than instinct. **Evidence:** A short written justification naming the theory used and linking it directly to the chosen approach. *(Grounding: ing:doc:bloom-anderson-krathwohl-2001; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/enterprise-and-creativity)*
        - *Applied: Backing an innovative proposal with a stated rationale.* **Learning outcome:** The student can justify an innovative proposal with a clear, step by step rationale that explains its advantage over current practice and how success would be checked.
          When you put forward an idea that departs from how things are usually done, you do not rely on instinct alone. You set out your reasoning step by step: what the current approach lacks, why your alternative should work better, and how its success could be checked. This demonstrates showing creativity and enterprise in applying an idea, with a rationale that someone else could follow and question. **Evidence:** A written proposal with its stated rationale and a note of how success would be checked. *(Grounding: professional: Explains the reasoning behind an innovative proposal.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/enterprise-and-creativity)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Justifying an unconventional format for a piece of work.* You propose an unusual format for a piece of work and justify the choice using theory rather than only personal preference. You draw on a revised model of cognitive processes in which creating, through generating, planning and producing, sits as the highest level, and on a theory of lateral thinking to explain how the format leads you to new connections. This shows you can draw on established theory to justify your approach rather than simply asserting that it works. **Evidence:** A short written rationale citing both theories, attached to the proposal and the resulting approval. *(Grounding: ing:doc:bloom-anderson-krathwohl-2001; model: Lateral thinking (de Bono 1970))*
        - *Applied: Explaining the reasoning behind an unconventional proposal.* In a part-time role, you propose replacing a routine written process with a shorter, more hands-on version. You set out your reasoning in writing: what is not working now, why your idea is likely to help, and how you would judge whether it did, such as fewer questions from people going through the process afterwards. This shows you can explain the reasoning behind an innovative proposal rather than just putting it forward. **Evidence:** Your written proposal, the evidence gathered for it and the written reply it received. *(Grounding: professional: Explains the reasoning behind an innovative proposal.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Theory-based rationale for an unconventional project format.* You propose an unconventional format for a project and justify it with theory. You use Anderson and Krathwohl's (2001) revised taxonomy, where create (generating, planning, producing) is the highest cognitive process, and de Bono's (1970) lateral thinking to explain how the format leads you to new connections (for example, presenting a geography project as an interactive story map instead of a report). This shows you draw on creativity theory to justify your approach. **Evidence:** A 600-word rationale citing both theories, attached to the project proposal and your tutor's approval. *(Grounding: ing:doc:bloom-anderson-krathwohl-2001; model: Lateral thinking (de Bono 1970))*
        - *Applied: Proposal for a new induction format at a leisure centre.* Working part time at a leisure centre, you propose replacing the written induction for new staff with a short, hands-on walk-through. You explain your reasoning to your manager: what is not working now, why your idea is likely to help and how you would judge whether it did (for example, fewer safety questions from new starters in their first month). **Evidence:** Your written proposal, the evidence you gathered for it and your manager's reply. *(Grounding: professional: Explains the reasoning behind an innovative proposal.)*
5. **Use structured creative problem-solving methods**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 1.11: critical thinking, problem-solving, data analysis, ethical awareness (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.16: apply critical-thinking skills to address complex problems to identifying creative and innovative solutions (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Research and enquiry methods (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Enterprise and creativity (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Applying a named method to reach a creative solution.* **Learning outcome:** The student can apply a named structured method, defining a problem, generating options without early judgement, then selecting and refining one, to reach a creative solution.
          Given a problem with no obvious solution, you choose a named structured method, rather than relying on unguided brainstorming, and follow its steps in order: defining the problem precisely, generating options without early judgement, then selecting and refining one. You record which step of the method produced your strongest option. This demonstrates applying critical thinking to a complex problem to identify a creative and workable solution. **Evidence:** A step-by-step record of the method followed, the options it produced and the solution selected. *(Grounding: model: Creative problem-solving (Osborn 1953); benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.16)*
        - *Applied: Solving a complex problem through a structured method.* **Learning outcome:** The student can apply a structured problem solving method to generate and test options against agreed criteria, recording whether the chosen solution resolved the original problem.
          When a problem in your work has no quick fix, you follow a structured method rather than guessing at a solution: you define the problem, generate a wide set of options without ruling any out too early, then apply agreed criteria to choose and trial one. You record the outcome of the trial and whether it resolved the original problem. This demonstrates applying critical thinking to a complex problem to identify a creative, workable solution. **Evidence:** A written record of the method's steps, the options generated and the result of the trial. *(Grounding: professional: Uses structured methods to solve problems and design improvements. (Vitae RDF A3: creativity); benchmark: qaa/psychology-2023/3.16)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Following a structured method to redesign an existing item.* Rather than waiting for inspiration, you work through a design problem using a structured method. You defer judgement while generating ideas, following an established creative problem-solving process, and use a set of prompts for varying an existing item, such as substituting, combining or adapting its parts. You record which prompts produced usable ideas and explain why you chose your final solution over the alternatives you generated. **Evidence:** A method record showing the problem, ideas from each prompt, your selection criteria and the chosen solution. *(Grounding: model: Creative problem-solving (Osborn 1953); model: SCAMPER (Eberle 1971))*
        - *Applied: Working through a recurring problem using a structured method.* Faced with a recurring problem in a setting where things keep piling up unsorted, you work through it with others using a structured method: you define the problem clearly, ask why it keeps happening until you reach its root cause, generate several options and trial one. You then check whether the problem actually eases. This shows you use structured methods to solve real problems rather than relying only on quick fixes. **Evidence:** A short problem-solving record with the cause analysis, the options considered, the trial and its results. *(Grounding: professional: Uses structured methods to solve problems and design improvements. (Vitae RDF A3: creativity))*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: SCAMPER and creative problem-solving record on a product redesign.* You work through a design problem using a structured method rather than waiting for inspiration. You follow Osborn's (1953) creative problem-solving process, deferring judgement while generating ideas, and use SCAMPER (Eberle 1971) prompts to vary an existing product (for example, redesigning a pill organiser for older users in a product design module). You record which prompts produced usable ideas and why you chose your final solution. **Evidence:** A method record showing the problem statement, ideas from each SCAMPER prompt, your selection criteria and the chosen concept. *(Grounding: model: Creative problem-solving (Osborn 1953); model: SCAMPER (Eberle 1971))*
        - *Applied: Structured fix for a charity shop donation backlog.* Volunteering at a charity shop where donations pile up unsorted, you use a structured method with the team: you define the problem, ask "why" repeatedly to reach its cause, generate options and trial one (for example, a sorting rota tied to delivery days). You measure whether the backlog falls. This shows you use structured methods to solve real problems and improve how things work. **Evidence:** A one-page problem-solving record with the cause analysis, options, trial results and the shop manager's comments. *(Grounding: professional: Uses structured methods to solve problems and design improvements. (Vitae RDF A3: creativity))*
6. **Shift approach when a strategy is not working (cognitive flexibility)**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Adjusting an approach once a gap in understanding appears.* **Learning outcome:** The student can analyse how a mistaken assumption about the subject matter can undermine a planned approach, and explain how updated understanding should prompt a change of strategy.
          While working through a task, you find that your planned approach relies on an assumption about the subject matter that turns out to be wrong. Rather than pressing on, you pause, update your understanding, and change your strategy to fit what you now know. You record the original assumption, what corrected it and the new strategy. This demonstrates a well-founded grasp of the subject's core content guiding a change in approach. **Evidence:** A short note recording the original assumption, the correction and the revised strategy adopted. *(Grounding: model: Single-loop and double-loop learning (Argyris and Schon 1978); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/subject-knowledge-and-understanding)*
        - *Applied: Changing course once results reveal a misunderstanding.* **Learning outcome:** The student can evaluate results that reveal a flawed approach, correct the underlying misunderstanding and adjust strategy accordingly rather than continuing regardless.
          Partway through a task, results show that an approach based on your current understanding of the subject is not working as expected. Instead of continuing regardless, you revisit what you know, correct the gap and adjust your approach accordingly, recording both the original plan and the revised one. This demonstrates a well-founded grasp of the subject's core content being used to recognise and fix a flawed strategy. **Evidence:** A short record contrasting the original approach, the misunderstanding found and the revised approach taken. *(Grounding: professional: Adapts approach when circumstances or results change. (Vitae RDF B2: self-management); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/subject-knowledge-and-understanding)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Reviewing and changing a strategy that is not working.* When a planned approach to a piece of work stalls, you write a short review using a distinction between first-level fixes and deeper, assumption-level changes. You record the initial adjustments you tried, then question the assumption underlying your original strategy and change approach altogether. This shows you can shift approach when something is not working, and explain, not just describe, the change you made. **Evidence:** A strategy review note comparing surface fixes and deeper changes, with written agreement to the revised method. *(Grounding: model: Single-loop and double-loop learning (Argyris and Schon 1978))*
        - *Applied: Changing an activity mid-session when it is not working.* Leading a session you are responsible for, you notice partway through that an activity is not working: people are losing interest and the intended skill is not improving. You stop, change the format there and then, and record why you made that choice. Afterwards you plan the next session differently based on what you learned. This shows you adapt your approach when the results are not what you expected. **Evidence:** A session plan with notes on the mid-session change made and a short review of its effect. *(Grounding: professional: Adapts approach when circumstances or results change. (Vitae RDF B2: self-management))*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Double-loop review of a failing research strategy.* When your dissertation data collection stalls, you write a review using Argyris and Schon's (1978) single-loop and double-loop learning. You record the first fixes you tried, then question the assumption behind your strategy and change approach (for example, a psychology study that moved from an online survey to short interviews after recruitment failed). This shows you can shift approach when a strategy is not working, and justify the change. **Evidence:** A strategy review note comparing single-loop and double-loop changes, with your supervisor's agreement to the revised method. *(Grounding: model: Single-loop and double-loop learning (Argyris and Schon 1978))*
        - *Applied: Adapting a coaching session that was not working.* Coaching a junior football team as a volunteer, you notice partway through a session that a drill is not working: players are bored and the skill is not improving. You stop, change the format and record why (for example, swapping a queue-based passing drill for small-sided games). Afterwards you plan the next session differently. This shows you adapt your approach when results are not what you expected. **Evidence:** Your session plan with notes on the change made mid-session and a short review from the head coach. *(Grounding: professional: Adapts approach when circumstances or results change. (Vitae RDF B2: self-management))*

- Psychology 3.16: apply critical-thinking skills to address complex problems to identifying creative and innovative solutions (direct; lands on `creativity/04`)

### Advanced

| # | Capability | FHEQ | Framework anchor | Benchmark | Id |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | Apply creativity and enterprise thinking in professional contexts | L5 | l5/knowledge.1 "to find ways forward in broadly-defined, complex contexts" | direct | `creativity/06` |
| 8 | Turn ideas into action under uncertainty | L6 | 4.15.b4 "an appreciation of the uncertainty, ambiguity and limits of knowledge"; l6/knowledge.1 "to create ways forward" | **UNATTRIBUTED** | `creativity#e2` |
| 9 | Own a creative practice, aware of one's own creative process | L7 | 4.17.b6 "demonstrate self-direction and originality"; 4.17.b3 "originality in the application of knowledge" | direct | `creativity#e3` |

**Benchmark, generic learning outcomes and evidence, per capability:**

7. **Apply creativity and enterprise thinking in professional contexts**
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 1.34: opportunities to develop and apply enterprise behaviours, attributes and skills (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 1.35: Enterprise and entrepreneurship education takes different forms and may be embedded (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 2.16: the opportunity to develop enterprise and entrepreneurial skills, and the opportunity to practice these skills (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 3.16: apply critical-thinking skills to address complex problems to identifying creative and innovative solutions (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 4.10: demonstrated creative thinking when designing psychological research with relevance to contemporary psychological issues (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Psychology 1.33: enterprise and entrepreneurship education supports the development of behaviours, attributes and skills that together comprise a mindset (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Professional practice and employability (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Enterprise and creativity (direct)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Showing creativity applied to a real opportunity.* **Learning outcome:** The student can construct a reasoned case for a genuine opportunity, synthesising its likely beneficiaries, costs and success criteria, and justify a revision based on tested evidence.
          You identify a genuine opportunity connected to an area you know well, and work through how you would turn it into something of value: who would benefit, what it would cost to develop and how you would know if it had succeeded. You test the weakest part of the idea with real evidence rather than assumption, and revise it in light of what you find. This demonstrates creativity and enterprise applied with discipline rather than left as a vague idea. **Evidence:** A short written case for the opportunity, the weakest assumption tested and the resulting revision. *(Grounding: model: Business Model Canvas (Osterwalder and Pigneur 2010); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/enterprise-and-creativity)*
        - *Applied: Turning an identified opportunity into a proposal of value.* **Learning outcome:** The student can construct a costed proposal from an identified opportunity, present it for approval and justify any changes made in response to the feedback received.
          You spot an opportunity within an organisation or venture you are involved with, and take it from idea to a proposal with a stated cost and expected benefit. You pitch it to those who would need to approve or fund it, and record their response, including any changes you would need to make. This demonstrates showing creativity and enterprise by applying an idea to create something of real value. **Evidence:** A written proposal with its costed benefit and a record of the response it received. *(Grounding: professional: Identifies opportunities and turns ideas into value for an organisation or venture. (Vitae RDF A3: creativity; Vitae RDF D3: engagement and impact); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/enterprise-and-creativity)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Testing a venture idea with a business model framework.* You take an idea with potential value and test its logic using a widely used framework for mapping a venture: who it serves, what it offers, how it reaches people and how it would sustain itself. You fill in each part with evidence, identify the weakest assumptions and revise the idea accordingly, such as discovering that people would not actually pay for it and adjusting the offer in response to that finding. **Evidence:** A completed venture map with evidence notes, the assumptions tested and a revised version of the idea. *(Grounding: model: Business Model Canvas (Osterwalder and Pigneur 2010))*
        - *Applied: Pitching a costed idea developed from an external brief.* Given a brief from an outside organisation asking for ways to reach a specific group more effectively, your team identifies an opportunity, develops it into a costed proposal and pitches it to the people who set the brief. You explain the value the idea would create and how its cost compares to the benefit expected. This shows you can identify opportunities and turn ideas into value for an organisation or venture. **Evidence:** The pitch materials, the costed proposal and written feedback on the idea's value and feasibility. *(Grounding: professional: Identifies opportunities and turns ideas into value for an organisation or venture. (Vitae RDF A3: creativity; Vitae RDF D3: engagement and impact))*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Business Model Canvas for a venture idea from your discipline.* You develop an idea from your own subject into a possible venture and test its logic with Osterwalder and Pigneur's (2010) Business Model Canvas. You fill each of the nine blocks with evidence, identify the weakest assumptions and revise the idea (for example, a computing student's app that helps students find quiet study spaces, revised after finding no one would pay for it). **Evidence:** A completed Business Model Canvas with evidence notes, the assumptions you tested and a revised version of the idea. *(Grounding: model: Business Model Canvas (Osterwalder and Pigneur 2010))*
        - *Applied: Live brief pitch to grow a theatre's young audience.* On a live brief from a local theatre, your team is asked to find ways to attract more audience members aged 18 to 25. You identify an opportunity, develop it into a costed proposal and pitch it to the theatre's staff (for example, a student ticket scheme linked to post-show discussions). This shows you can apply creativity and enterprise thinking in a professional context. **Evidence:** The pitch deck, the costed proposal and the theatre's written feedback on the idea's value and feasibility. *(Grounding: professional: Identifies opportunities and turns ideas into value for an organisation or venture. (Vitae RDF A3: creativity; Vitae RDF D3: engagement and impact))*
8. **Turn ideas into action under uncertainty**
    - *Benchmark:* none matched at this tier (**UNATTRIBUTED**)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Reasoning through acting on an idea before certainty exists.* **Learning outcome:** The student can justify a deliberate decision to act on an idea before certainty exists, accounting for available resources, acceptable risk and a response to early setbacks.
          You set out, step by step, how you decided to act on an idea despite not knowing whether it would succeed: what you already had available to use, how much you were prepared to risk, and what you would do if early signs were discouraging. You explain this as a deliberate way of managing uncertainty, not as guesswork. This demonstrates turning an idea into action under uncertainty through considered reasoning. **Evidence:** A short written account of the resources used, the risk accepted and the plan if early signs were poor. *(Grounding: model: Effectuation (Sarasvathy 2001); benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
        - *Applied: Committing to an initial step despite an unclear outcome.* **Learning outcome:** The student can construct a limited first step on an idea under genuine uncertainty, treating its outcome as information that shapes the next decision rather than a final verdict.
          Rather than waiting for enough certainty to guarantee success, you commit to a first, limited step on an idea, using only what you can afford to risk, and treat the result as information rather than a final verdict. You record what that first step taught you and how it changed your next move. This demonstrates turning an idea into action under uncertainty rather than delaying until the risk disappears entirely. **Evidence:** A short record of the initial step taken, what it revealed and the next move it led to. *(Grounding: professional: Acts on an idea under genuine uncertainty and adapts it as real information comes in.; benchmark: none (unattributed; written from capability statement))*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Planning an initiative using effectuation principles.* You plan an initiative where the outcome cannot be predicted in advance, following a set of principles for acting under uncertainty: start with the means already available to you, commit only what you can afford to lose, and build partnerships as opportunities appear rather than planning them all upfront. You record how you acted on the idea without waiting for certainty that it would work. **Evidence:** An effectuation plan setting out your means, affordable loss and partners, with a log of decisions made. *(Grounding: model: Effectuation (Sarasvathy 2001))*
        - *Applied: Starting an initiative without knowing what demand will be.* You set up a small, ongoing initiative without knowing in advance whether anyone will take part or exactly what will be needed. You run a first, small-scale version, learn from how it goes and adjust the next one, such as adding a feature once you notice what people actually want. This shows you can turn an idea into action under uncertainty and adapt it as real information comes in. **Evidence:** An action log across several sessions with uptake figures, changes made and a short organiser's statement. *(Grounding: professional: Acts on an idea under genuine uncertainty and adapts it as real information comes in.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Effectuation plan for a pilot with an uncertain outcome.* You plan a new initiative where the outcome cannot be predicted, using Sarasvathy's (2001) effectuation principles: start with the means you have, commit only what you can afford to lose, and build partnerships as you go. You record how you acted on the idea without waiting for certainty (for example, a social work student piloting a peer-support group for young carers with no data on likely demand). **Evidence:** An effectuation plan setting out your means, affordable loss and partners, with a log of decisions taken as things changed. *(Grounding: model: Effectuation (Sarasvathy 2001))*
        - *Applied: Launching a community repair café with unknown demand.* With a local community group, you set up a monthly repair café without knowing whether anyone will come or which skills will be needed. You run a small first event, learn from it and adjust the next (for example, adding bike repairs after most visitors brought bicycles). This shows you can turn an idea into action under uncertainty and adapt as you learn. **Evidence:** An action log across three events with attendance figures, changes made and a short statement from the group's organiser. *(Grounding: practice: community project)*
9. **Own a creative practice, aware of one's own creative process**
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Enterprise and creativity (direct)
    - *Benchmark:* Universal: Subject knowledge and understanding (indirect)
    - **Generic outcomes from the benchmark:**
        - *Conceptual: Analysing your own creative process against a named model.* **Learning outcome:** The student can critique their own creative process against a recognised stage model, identifying which stages are rushed or skipped and the implications for future work.
          You examine how your own ideas tend to form, from first noticing a problem through to a finished piece of work, and compare this against a named stage model of the creative process. You identify which stages you move through quickly, which you tend to skip, and what that means for how you should plan future work. This demonstrates owning a creative practice through genuine awareness of your own process, not assumption. **Evidence:** A written comparison of your own process against the named model, with implications for future planning. *(Grounding: model: Four-stage model of the creative process (Wallas 1926); benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/enterprise-and-creativity)*
        - *Applied: Describing your own creative practice to someone experienced.* **Learning outcome:** The student can construct an account of their own creative practice, including its triggers and risks, and justify revisions made after testing it against an experienced perspective.
          You put into words how you actually go about producing original work: what triggers your ideas, the habits you rely on, and the risks you choose to take or avoid. You test this description against the view of someone experienced in similar work and revise it where their observations do not match your own account. This demonstrates owning a creative practice through active, checked awareness rather than unexamined habit. **Evidence:** A written practice statement, revised after discussion, with a note of what changed and why. *(Grounding: professional: Describes and refines an account of their own creative method, not only their finished output.; benchmark: qaa-universal/threshold/enterprise-and-creativity)*
    - **Generic outcomes distilled from the exemplars below:**
        - *Conceptual: Mapping your own creative process onto a staged model.* Over the course of a substantial piece of work, you keep notes on how your ideas actually formed, then write a statement analysing your own creative process against a well-known four-stage model: preparation, incubation, illumination and verification. You identify where your own process departs from the model and name the conditions that seem to help you work at your best, using dated notes rather than hindsight. **Evidence:** A creative process statement with dated project notes as evidence, plus the conditions you now plan for. *(Grounding: model: Four-stage model of the creative process (Wallas 1926))*
        - *Applied: Building a portfolio that shows your creative method.* In a role where original work is expected, you build a portfolio that shows not only finished pieces but how you reach original ideas, the methods you rely on and the risks you are willing to take. You discuss it with someone experienced in the same kind of work and revise how you describe your own practice as a result, such as how early you tend to commit to a direction. **Evidence:** A practice portfolio with a written practice statement and recorded feedback from an experienced practitioner. *(Grounding: professional: Describes and refines an account of their own creative method, not only their finished output.)*
    - **The existing practical examples (evidence a student could offer):**
        - *Conceptual: Creative process statement mapped to Wallas' stages.* Over a major project, you keep notes on how your ideas formed, then write a statement analysing your own creative process using Wallas' (1926) four stages: preparation, incubation, illumination and verification. You identify where your process departs from the model and what conditions help you work at your best (for example, a master's chemistry student finding that breakthroughs came after stepping away from failed syntheses). **Evidence:** A creative process statement with dated project notes as evidence, plus the conditions and habits you now plan for. *(Grounding: model: Four-stage model of the creative process (Wallas 1926))*
        - *Applied: Professional creative practice portfolio from an industry placement.* During an industry placement in a games studio, you build a portfolio that shows not only finished work but how you get to original ideas, the methods you rely on and the risks you choose to take. You discuss it with a senior practitioner and revise how you describe your practice (for example, how you prototype quickly before committing to a design). **Evidence:** A practice portfolio with a creative practice statement and a senior practitioner's written feedback on it. *(Grounding: practice: industry placement in a creative studio)*
